They also write the transfer of the blessed to the high gardens, whose ground is the outer surface of the eighth sphere, and of the damned to Hell, which lies below it.

The Highest Pen has already written in the Preserved Tablet everything these pens erase and confirm. The Tablet records the erasure in the other tablets[^V07-62] and what is made firm there. It also records when one confirmed state is erased and another created in its place. The Preserved Tablet itself is beyond erasure. The divine Pen supplies it with all the changing events[^V07-63] and their outcomes, laid out in full and fixed form, by the decree of the Mighty, the Knowing.

The hearts of God’s friends can receive a true vision from God, through which these pens and their writings appear as images. This is a real kind of vision, like the way Paradise appeared to the Prophet on a wall.[^V07-64] That is the end of Ibn Arabi’s words. I have quoted him here because people with insight recognize the truth in what he says. I have not explained his meanings or shown how they fit a philosophical argument. Other passages in this book provide what a thoughtful reader needs, especially the discussion of God’s knowledge of things in general and in detail.

#### Chapter — The Qur'an’s names and descriptions

Now that you know the difference between God’s speech and His Book—roughly, the difference between a command and an act—you know that an act takes place in time and changes, while God’s command does not change. You also know the difference between what has come down as Qur'an and what has come down as Criterion: one is whole and undivided, the other is made clear in parts. We now have four names: Speech, Book, Qur'an, and Criterion.

One of the Qur'an’s names is Light. It is a light of the intellect that reveals the beginning and end of things, shows what they really are, and guides travelers through the dark land of bodies and the sea of souls. It shows them the way to Paradise and away from Hell. God says, “A light and a clear Book have come to you from God. Through it, God guides those who seek His pleasure to paths of peace. He brings them out of darkness into light, by His leave, and guides them to a straight path.” The word “light” points to the Qur'an as one undivided act of knowledge, called the divine Pen. The word “Book” points to detailed knowledge written on the Preserved Tablet.[^V07-65] The same two levels appear in the verse, “We gave him wisdom and clear speech”: wisdom points to the Qur'an, and clear speech to the detailed Book.[^V07-66]

God says, “Was one who was dead and whom We brought to life, giving him a light to walk among people, like one who remains in darkness, unable to leave it?”

Another name is Wisdom, the best knowledge of the best thing known.[^V07-67] Only God is truly wise.[^V07-68] So wisdom is a gift from Him, not something gained by effort alone. It is given to those who leave behind worldly attachments and the lower traits of the self, through true renunciation and freedom from the flaws of created life. As God says after the words, “He teaches them the Book and wisdom,” “This is God’s favor. He gives it to whomever He wills, and God’s favor is great.”

The Qur'an is also called “good”: “Whoever is given wisdom has been given a great good.” It is called “spirit”: “He sends down the spirit of His command upon whomever He wills among His servants, to warn of the Day when all will meet.” It is called “truth,” because it is firm and never changes, and because it is true and matches what is real; it has no doubt in it.

God says, “Those given knowledge see that what has been sent down to you from your Lord is the truth,” “The Holy Spirit brought it down from your Lord in truth to strengthen those who believe,” “Are those who know that what has been sent down to you from your Lord is the truth like those who are blind?” and, “It is the truth from your Lord, so that you may warn people who have not received a warner.”

The Qur'an is called “guidance,” because it leads to what is right and is itself right in another sense. God says, “That is God’s guidance. He guides by it whomever He wills,” and, “Guidance for those mindful of God, who believe in the unseen.”

It is called “remembrance,” because it helps us remember the next life and the beginning and end of things: “It is a reminder for you and your people, and you will be questioned.”

It is called “the great news,” because it tells of the unseen world, hidden things, the secrets of souls, and what lies within hearts: “It is great news, yet you turn away from it.”

It is called “healing,” because it heals diseases of the soul and inner wounds that bring suffering in the next life: ignorance, envy, pride, self-conceit, showing off, hypocrisy, recklessness, lust, anger, love of money and power, and other illnesses that become so settled in the heart that even the healers of the soul cannot treat them. God says, “Say: For those who believe, it is guidance and healing. Those who do not believe have deafness in their ears, and it is a blindness for them. They are called from far away.”

It is also called “mercy.” God says, “We sent down the Book to you so that you may make clear to people what they differ about. It is guidance and mercy for people who believe,” and, “It is guidance and mercy for the believers.”

The Qur'an is called “high and wise.” It is high because its true source is in the higher world of the intellect. It is wise because it contains wisdom. As we have said, the Qur'an has an intellectual level of its own, where the realities of things are present as one undivided whole. The intellect, when fully active, knows and is what it knows. The same is true here: the Qur'an is wise because it is itself wisdom, and wisdom because it is itself wise. It knows things as they really are. God says, “Yā Sīn. By the wise Qur'an,” and, “It is with Us in the Mother Book, high and wise.”

It is also called “the one full of remembrance.” The difference between being a remembrance and being full of remembrance is only a matter of viewpoint, as with “wise” and “wisdom.” God says, “Ṣād. By the Qur'an full of remembrance.”

It is called “sending down,” because it has descended from its general, intellectual level to the detailed level in the soul: “A sending down from the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.” It is also called “the sent-down Book”: “Those given the Book know that it has been sent down from your Lord in truth.” The difference between being the act of sending down and being what is sent down is like the difference we have already explained.

It is called “bringer of good news and warning.” God says, “A Book whose verses are made clear, an Arabic Qur'an for people who know, bringing good news and warning.” It is also called “good news,” as in, “Guidance and good news for believers.” It is called “guide” as well as “guidance,” as in, “It guides to the path of the Mighty, the Praiseworthy.”

The Qur'an is also called glorious, mighty, and great. God says, “Qāf. By the glorious Qur'an,” “It is a mighty Book,” and, “We gave you seven oft-repeated verses and the great Qur'an.” It is called “good counsel”: “Call people to your Lord with wisdom and good counsel.” It is called “a blessing,” because those close to God enjoy it more than worldly pleasures. Compared with the joy of knowing God and wisdom, the pleasures of this world are like pain. God says, “They know God’s favor, then deny it. Most of them reject the truth.” If they truly knew His favor, they would gain lasting joy and would not deny it.

The Qur'an is called “provision”: “Your Lord’s provision is better and lasts longer.” It is called “the clear Book,” because through it the truths of beings, the secrets of both worlds, and the conditions of God’s rule and human service become clear, along with people’s states and their different groups on the Day of Resurrection. God says, “These are the verses of the Book and a clear Qur'an.”

It is called “the Balance,” because it is a true measure and straight standard. It weighs actions, knowledge, and ideas, and shows which are sound and which are false, valuable and worthless, true and untrue. God says, “We sent Our messengers with clear signs and sent down with them the Book and the Balance, so that people may uphold justice,” and, “He raised the sky and set the Balance, so that you do not transgress the Balance.” There are many other names and descriptions. Their abundance shows how great the one named and described is. Only God knows the full majesty of His Speech and Book.

### Station Eight — Divine care and mercy for all things

#### Chapter — On providence

There is no doubt that the Necessary Existent—God—is perfect in His reality and beyond perfection. The same is true of some of the angels brought near to Him and the pure intellects: their essences are complete, and their identities are joined to the identity of the One who is truly One. They do not act to gain some benefit from the conditions of this lower world. In general, the higher causes[^V07-69] cannot act for goals that would benefit them but that they had not already reached before acting. If they could, they would be incomplete, gaining perfection through their effects. That is impossible. So nothing draws them to act, and no passing preference or added desire comes over them. They act only by following the Highest Good and the most complete, highest Light.

The One who is truly One has no goal above Him that He looks toward when He gives good and spreads His all-embracing mercy. Yet we see in the beings of this world—in every part of its order and in all its kinds, especially plants and animals, and even in the great classes of beings such as the heavenly spheres and the elements—wise planning, fine order, care for needs and benefits, and powers and causes suited to their ends and able to repel harm and damage. These things astonish us. No one can deny the remarkable effects found in individual beings, let alone in the whole order of things, as we will show with examples. Consider the benefits built into plants such as date palms and grapevines, and into small, seemingly humble animals such as bees and spiders. These could not come about by chance, without prior planning, sound judgment, care for what is beneficial, and fitting wisdom.

So we must understand providence, as we have said, as follows: the First is aware through Himself of the way existence is arranged in the best order and the greatest good; He is the cause, through Himself, of all the good and perfection possible; and He is pleased with that order in the way described. These three meanings held together in providence—knowledge, being the cause, and approval—are all identical with His essence.[^V07-70] His essence is the knowledge of the good order, its complete cause, and approval of it, which is His eternal will.[^V07-71] Through Himself, His essence is the form of the good order in a higher and nobler way. For He is true existence, with no limit or end to His perfection. He therefore understands the good order in the fullest and most complete way possible, and what He understands flows from Him as an ordered good, in a way that brings the order to its final end and complete form as perfectly as possible.

This is what providence means when it is free of defect and flaw. Those who believe otherwise—those who say the world arose by chance, as some ancient thinkers did; those who speak of a will without wisdom, as the Shaykh al-Ashʿari is said to have done; and those who say God acts for lower purposes that benefit created beings—have gone far astray. They have failed to understand God’s oneness and freedom from imperfection, and have not valued Him as He deserves.

#### Chapter — Good and evil

Good is what every thing longs for and seeks, and what gives it the share of perfection it can attain. Every being is naturally directed toward it: toward what it needs in order to exist, what belongs to its first nature, and what completes its essence, qualities, actions, virtues, and further capacities.

The absolute Good that all things seek, and through which they and their perfections come to be, is God, the One who sustains all things and is necessary in Himself. He is absolute existence, with no lack in Him: pure light, pure splendour, perfect and beyond perfection. Every possible thing loves and seeks Him by its very nature as something that might or might not exist. Every being below Him seeks Him by its nature as something incomplete. Every effect submits to Him because it depends on its cause. Everything besides Him has some share of lack and need. So none of the things that depend on Him is good in every respect; each has some share of evil, in proportion to how far it falls short of the absolute Good, whose goodness has no limit and whose perfection is unsurpassed.

There is another, more usual meaning of evil: the absence of a thing itself, or the absence of a perfection that belongs to it precisely as the kind of thing it is.[^V07-72] In either meaning, evil is a lack, even though it can be said to exist in a limited sense as a quality of something. That is why philosophers say evil has no independent reality: it is either the absence of a thing or the absence of one of its perfections. The proof[^V07-73] is this. If evil were something that existed in its own right, it would either be evil to itself or evil to something else. It cannot be evil to itself, because a thing’s existence does not require its own nonexistence or the loss of any of its perfections. If a thing did cause the loss of one of its perfections, the evil would be that loss, not the thing itself. Besides, how could something cause its own perfection to be lost when all things seek the perfections and ends suited to them? As we have said, divine providence neglects nothing. It directs every thing toward its perfection. So things seek their own perfections and ends by their nature; they do not seek their own loss and deficiency.

Nor can evil, if it were something that existed in its own right, be evil to something else. It would be evil to another thing either because it removed that thing, removed one of its perfections, or for some other reason. If it is evil because it removes a thing or one of its perfections, then the evil is the absence of that thing or its perfection, not the existing thing that caused the absence. If it removes nothing at all, it cannot be evil to what it was supposed to harm. We know directly that anything that causes no loss to a thing or to its perfection is not evil for that thing, since it suffers no harm from it. So if the supposed existent evil is neither evil to itself nor to anything else, it cannot be counted as evil.

The argument can be set out in its simplest form: if evil were something that existed in its own right, it would not be evil; but that conclusion is false, so the starting claim is false too. The reasoning has already shown why the conclusion is false. We can therefore see that evil is a lack, with no independent reality of its own: either a thing is absent, or one of its perfections is absent.

If we examine what people call evil, we find only two kinds. Some things are sheer absences, such as death, simple ignorance, poverty, weakness, deformity, the loss of a limb, drought, and similar states. Other things lead to an absence. These include pain, grief, mistaken belief, and similar states in which something is perceived or felt, rather than there being only a lack of something. The harmful cause that opposes good and perfection and brings loss or disappearance is of two kinds.

### Kind One — Harm suffered directly from the harmful thing

Suppose a person is harmed because a limb is torn apart by a burning, biting heat. The person feels both the loss of connection and health and the harmful heat itself. The power of sensation in the limb perceives the loss of health through the limb’s own nature, and it perceives the painful heat through that same power. So there are two perceptions: one of a lack, like our perception of other absences, and one of something present, like our perception of other existing things.

The perceived heat is not evil in itself. It is evil only in relation to this particular thing. The other perception—the loss of a perfection and the breaking of a connection—is evil in itself, not merely in relation to this thing. It cannot have an existence that is not evil. Blindness, for example, can occur only in an eye, and in an eye it can only be evil. It has no other side by which it could be something good. This differs from the harmful, painful thing that caused the harm. Harmful heat, an irritating fluid, or deadly poison can each exist in a way that is not evil, but good.

The second kind is when the harmful thing does not reach what it harms. Examples include a cloud that blocks the sun from something that needs its warmth to grow, cold that ruins fruit, or rain that prevents clothes from being bleached.[^V07-74] If the affected thing can sense, it feels the loss of its perfection and the benefit it has been denied. But it does not perceive the cloud, the rain, or the cold as the cause of its loss through the very power that feels the loss. It perceives the cause through another power, such as sight.

For example, a person whose body needs the sun’s warmth feels through touch the loss of bodily health and balance. Touch is, in effect, the sense of warmth and bodily well-being, and it also feels their absence. But the power of touch does not perceive the cloud that blocks the sun; sight perceives it. The sense of sight is therefore not harmed by the cloud,[^V07-75] while the sense of touch is. The sense of touch feels only the loss of health and of a pleasing bodily condition. Evil in itself is this absence and loss—but not every absence. A lack of sweetness in hearing or sight is not evil for those senses. The evil is a lack that reaches a thing by taking away a perfection that belongs to its nature.

Existing things are all good, either in themselves and in relation to everything else, or in themselves but with some thing affected by their existence or by one of their effects. These are called evils only because of the harm they do to something else: they remove or block good from something that needs it, or oppose a perfection and the good contrary to them.

This includes blameworthy traits that keep the soul from reaching its intellectual perfection, such as stinginess, cowardice, excess, pride, and vanity. It also includes blameworthy actions such as injustice, wrongful killing, adultery, theft, backbiting, slander, and foul speech. None of these things is evil in itself. They are existing things, and perfections of natural powers in a person—the animal and natural powers. Their evil lies in how they affect a higher, noble power: the power of reason, which should govern the powers beneath it rather than submit to them.

The heat and cold that harm animals, ruin fruit, and spoil food are active qualities and perfections of natural bodies made from the elements. The evil lies in the loss of health and safety, and in the loss of balance in plants and animals. In the same way, bad traits are perfections of a person’s animal powers. They are not evil for the powers of anger and desire themselves. They are evil in relation to a weaker soul that cannot hold those powers back from excess or deficiency, or direct them to obey the higher order that brings lasting happiness.

Likewise, shameful acts such as adultery and injustice are evil in relation to the order of a person’s body or a community. Injustice is evil for the one wronged in the way we have described. But for the unjust person, simply considered as an unjust person, it is not evil—except that the person also has a rational power, and so often suffers from the injustice even more than the victim.

Pain, illness, grief, worry, and other such things are, as perceptions and as effects of their active causes, good perfections. They are evil only in relation to what they involve: absences and losses, or harmful and painful effects. If you examine all the things people usually call evils in this world, you will find that none is evil in itself. Each has its own positive existence but is harmful in relation to something else, as we have explained with this argument.

The examples are not meant to prove the claim by listing cases or comparing them. They are there to answer objections, make the difference between a loss that is itself an evil and something that harms another thing clear, and show that what people call evil always leads back to some lack. The proof is the argument given at the beginning of this chapter. Some say this claim is self-evident, and that the examples are only meant to help us notice and remember it.

#### A difficulty and an answer

Here is a serious problem that has remained unresolved until now, but which, with God’s help, can be solved. Pain is a kind of perception, so it exists and is good in itself, even if what it is directed at is a lack.[^V07-76] It might seem, then, that we should count it as an evil only because of what it harms, as the philosophers do. But we know from experience that there are two evils here. One is the lack itself, such as a limb being cut off and health being lost. The other is the pain, which is something that exists. This particular pain is evil in itself, although what it is directed at is also an evil. The loss of connection is evil whether it is felt or not.

The pain that follows is another real evil, as anyone can recognize. If the connection were broken but no pain followed, this second evil would not exist. And if pain occurred without the connection being broken, the evil would still be there. So some kind of existing thing is evil in itself, and the general rule that everything evil in itself is a lack seems false.

This was the problem raised by the scholar Dawānī in his commentary on *Tajrīd al-Iʿtiqād*. He could not resolve it. He said: “The question does not trouble them if they mean that evil comes from absence. But if they mean that only absence is evil in itself, and that everything else is called evil only by extension, so that in truth there is only one kind of evil—that of absence—and it is assigned to other things only through an intermediary, then the question stands. So understand.”

I reply that the philosophers mean the second thing. The objection is answered because pain[^V07-77] is awareness of something contrary to us and lacking—such as a broken connection—and it is known directly, not through a separate mental image. In direct awareness, the knowing itself is the thing known. So pain does not contain two things:[^V07-78] the breaking or cutting of something, and a mental image formed from it that causes pain. Rather, the presence of that contrary lack is pain itself. Pain is a kind of awareness, but it belongs among absences. It exists in the way that the absence of a capacity exists, as with blindness, stillness, poverty, deficiency, possibility, and potentiality. You have already learned that the existence of each thing is identical with what it is. The existence of a lack is therefore that very lack, just as the existence of a human being is that human being and the existence of a sphere is that sphere. You have also learned[^V07-79] that knowledge of a thing is identical with the thing known in itself. Here, existence is identical with the breaking, separation, or corruption that is a lack; and the awareness of it is identical with that existence, which is itself the lack.

It has therefore been shown that pain, which is evil in itself, belongs among absences. And the absence called evil is always an absence that belongs to some thing,[^V07-80] not absence in general, as we said earlier. So this does not refute the philosophers’ rule that everything evil in itself is necessarily a kind of absence.

To make this clearer, remember that the soul’s powers extend through the body. The soul itself senses through them. The same soul is what touches, tastes, and smells, and it is the natural form that holds the body together as one whole. Whenever something happens to the body, whether it is something present or something lost, the soul is affected by it and feels it through its powers that reach through the body.

A broken connection in the body is certainly evil for the body because its unity is lost and a perfection is gone. If a body were alive and aware when its connection was broken, it would experience the greatest evil possible: it would be aware of its own absence while it still existed. As you have learned, the soul is in some way joined with the body. So what happens to the body while the soul is connected to it is, in a sense, what happens to the soul itself. This is why wounds, illness, and an unhealthy bodily state hurt and pain the soul according to how closely it is joined to the body.

But the soul has other levels of being and exists beyond this bodily life. So the pain it feels from a severe wound, grave illness, corruption, or death is not like the pain felt by a living creature whose life is nothing more than the life of its body.[^V07-81] My dear friend, think carefully about this: evil affects only things that have unrealised possibilities in their nature. This is because bodily matter has an incomplete kind of existence. It is ready to be damaged, divided, multiplied, and changed. Opposing qualities can arise in it, and its conditions and forms can change. The further something is from matter, the less evil and harm can reach it.

Know also that evil reaches matter in two ways. It may be there from the beginning, as soon as the thing comes to exist, or it may happen to the thing after it has come to exist. In the first case, a condition may become established in matter at the beginning and block its natural readiness to reach a certain perfection. This is an evil opposed to that perfection. For example, matter from which a human being, horse, or plant forms may take on a condition that gives it a poorer balance and makes it less suited to receive the perfect form. Then it cannot receive the finest shape, the most complete structure, or the best arrangement. Its form is marred, and its balance or structure is incomplete—not because the giver of form failed to do its work well, but because the recipient was not ready to receive it.

The second kind happens after the thing comes to exist. It is either a barrier that stands between something and what would complete it, or an opposing thing that reaches it and destroys its perfection. For example, thick clouds or the shadow of a high mountain can prevent the sun from helping fruit ripen and reach its perfection. Or severe cold may strike a plant at the moment it is ready to flourish, ruining its special readiness and the perfect form that should follow from it.

#### Chapter — Five possible ways good and evil can be combined

Philosophers have traditionally divided[^V07-82] possible things, at first glance,[^V07-83] into five groups: things that are entirely good and contain no evil; things with much good and little evil; things with much evil and little good; things in which good and evil are equal; and things that are entirely evil and contain no good. The last three groups do not exist in the world. Only the first two do.

The first group—things that are entirely good and contain no evil—consists of beings that have come to exist in full, without missing anything they could possibly have from their original nature, and without anything foreign to their nature mixing into them, either at the beginning or later. Everything that belongs to their nature is already realized. Examples are beings whose life is entirely intellectual, and God’s complete words, which do not decay or diminish.

Next come the heavenly souls, which belong to the same group. Although some of their abilities are still to be realized, they possess within themselves what brings those abilities to completion. Nothing keeps them from reaching the perfection possible for them. Some perfected human souls that join the ranks of the angels brought near to God also belong to this group. This group must come from the Highest Source because it is pure good, and the doer of good must bring about good. The proofs given earlier establish the existence of the world of intellects, and the principle that the highest kind of possible being must exist before the lower kind.

The second group consists of beings with much good and a small amount of evil. They, too, must exist. If we rejected them because of the small amount of evil they bring, we would be giving up a great amount of good. Giving up so much good would itself be a great evil. So the source of all good and perfection must bring this group into existence.

Natural things are an example. They cannot reach the perfection proper to them without sometimes, through accidental clashes and contacts, blocking other things from their perfection or destroying what is perfect in them. Fire is complete in its heat and power to burn. These bring great benefits, but fire may also burn a house belonging to a saint or the clothes of a prophet. Water is complete in its coolness, moisture, and flow, yet it may flood towns and kill people. The same is true of earth, air, rain, clouds, and other things.

This group of possible beings is found only where there can be change, opposition, generation, and decay. But consider one person harmed by one of the four elements. If that person had been free from harm by that element for a whole lifetime, the small harm would not compare with the great benefit. If this is true for one person, how much more so when that small harm is weighed against the benefit received by all human beings, animals, and other beings?

The same is true of plants used as food and medicine, though they may rarely cause harm, and of animals that are good in themselves but may harm other animals through accidental contact, such as snakes, scorpions, predators, and birds of prey. A human being may be ready for the perfection of the soul and intellect, and for both imagined and real goods, yet accidental events may lead to false beliefs, confused thinking, bad character, harmful actions, and sins that bring harm in the life to come. But the people affected by these evils are few compared with those who remain free of them, and the times of harm are few compared with the times of safety and well-being.

#### Chapter — Why the kinds of evil just described exist only in the world of change and decay

This is because there is opposition in that world.[^V07-84] You have learned that the evil discussed here, and counted as evil by convention, is the absence of a thing or one of its perfections. But for one kind of thing to be lower in rank than another kind, or less perfect in its own right, is not evil for it. Beings whose life is entirely intellectual stand below God, whose existence depends on nothing; souls stand below those beings; the heavenly spheres are lower than the stars; and nature is lower than the soul. These are not kinds of evil.

So evil does not exist in the heavenly spheres, in the beings within them, or in the world above them. It exists only below the heavens, in the world of generation and decay, in the matter of the elemental things. The kinds of evil found there are few compared with the good. They arise because matter can receive opposing qualities, and this opposition leads to generation and decay.[^V07-85] If there were no opposition, new things could not come to be through changes that prepare matter to receive forms. There could be no endless series of souls and individual beings either. Souls come to exist only when bodies come to exist, and when the matter of those bodies is ready to receive them. That readiness comes about through the interaction of opposing qualities.

The opposition in this world is what keeps the flow of existence going. It is good for the order as a whole, though it is evil for particular beings.[^V07-86] This opposition is not something a creator has separately made. Qualities such as heat, moisture, and dryness are opposed to one another because of what they are in their material, external existence, though they are not opposed in the world of intellect. The necessary results of things’ existence are not themselves separately made. What is made directly in these kinds is their existence.

Their inherent defects and lacks are not made, as we have already explained. Their capacity for opposition is a necessary weakness in their nature, not something imposed on them by a separate act of creation.

Just as a creator cannot pack round bodies together without any gaps, though this can be done with squares, hexagons, and shapes that can be divided into equilateral triangles, so the source of creation cannot make the basic kinds of elemental things without opposition.

#### Chapter — How evils enter God’s decree

You have learned[^V07-87] that possible essences are not caused by the fact that they are possible or depend on something to bring them into existence. Nor is there a separate cause for their being less complete than God, whose existence depends on nothing. Nor is there a cause for the opposing forms in the matter of changing things, for the damage one opposing thing does to another, for fire to burn, or for the sea to drown. Nor is there a cause for a person absorbed in worldly desires and pleasures to be consumed by the fire of hell and kept from the gardens of bliss. These are necessary results, not things separately made. What is made is what underlies them, and these underlying things are among the good things that exist.

Many perfections that are goals for some things harm or spoil other things. For example, the goal of the power of anger is harmful to the power of reason. You have already learned, in our earlier discussion of final causes, about the necessary results of things’ own ends. These evils follow from good ends. They are like the possibility that belongs to each essence, and the necessary incompleteness of each kind of existence. These degrees of incompleteness differ. A body is more incomplete than a soul; sensation is farther below the First Intellect than imagination is. If all possible things were equally incomplete, all kinds and essences would be the same. Just as the essences of different kinds vary, so do the identities of individuals within one kind.

In short, creation necessarily leaves the created thing less than its creator. Otherwise, neither would have more claim to be the creator than the other. So every possible thing must have some deficiency. Deficiency is greater in the world of souls than in the world of intellects, greater in the world of nature than in the world of souls, and greater in the elemental world than in the heavenly spheres. The decline continues until it reaches common matter, which has no good in itself beyond its potential and readiness to receive things. You will learn that, though it is low and deficient in itself, matter is the means by which all good things come to be. Through it, existence returns from deficiency to perfection, from lowliness to nobility, and from descent to ascent. Matter can receive a form, but it can also be without that form. It cannot receive every form and its opposite at the same time.

So the evils that are absences, deficiencies, or lacks in a thing’s nature are not there because an agent created them. They exist because the agent did not create them. They are not good in relation to something else.

Other evils affect things that are good in themselves but are evil in relation to some other thing. These include fire, water, swords, spears, predators, snakes, and other existing things; qualities such as anger, desire, cunning, and deceit; and actions such as striking, stabbing, and killing. They arise for two reasons. One comes from matter, which can receive forms or their absence and must be able to receive opposite things. The other comes from the agent, which must perform its own action when it encounters matter suited to that action.

A power cannot have two opposite actions as its own actions, nor can it exist without acting at all. Fire cannot provide the benefit for which it exists without burning a human body it reaches. A sword cannot do what it is meant to do without cutting a person’s limb when it strikes it. So the benefit of such things must sometimes bring harm to some material things. Still, the good they are meant to bring is what happens most often and lasts.

Fire benefits more beings than it harms. Even those it harms spend more time safe from burning than suffering from it. And many kinds of things could not survive without fire. So it would not be wise for the eternal will and first providence to give up common benefits and lasting good because of rare harms to individual things. Divine wisdom requires that the lasting good of whole kinds and the benefits received by most beings should not be abandoned for the sake of evils that affect some individuals and are neither lasting nor common. Good is decreed in itself; evil is decreed only by extension.

Know that this kind of evil, which exists as harm to particular things, is not found in the world of divine decree. That world contains all things as intellectual forms, free of matter and its deficiencies. The intellectual fire has no evil there.[^V07-88] Nor does intellectual water, the intellectual human being, horse, lion, or any other intellectual form contain evil. They are all pure good. Evil exists only in the world of destiny: the distinct unfolding of the forms in the world of decree and their embodiment and measurement in known proportions. Bodily existence contains opposition and resistance, which separate what was united and multiply what was one. Matter is the source of this separation. The possibility of matter is the source of matter itself. Bodily matter flows from the active intellects because of their possibilities and deficiencies, not because of their necessary existence and perfections.

Matter is therefore the source of evil and deficiency, and it must exist, as you have learned.

The learned Tusi says in his commentary on *al-Ishārāt*, in explanation of the Shaykh’s words that whatever follows from a thing, directly or through an intermediary, reaches it as its destined share, which is the detailed unfolding of its first decree, and reaches it necessarily, since, as you have learned, what is not necessary does not exist:

“I would explain it this way. All the forms of existing things, both kinds and individuals without number, are present as ideas in the world of intellects through the first act of creation. It is impossible to create all the forms that belong in matter within matter all at once, because matter cannot receive even two forms at the same time, let alone so many. But divine generosity requires that matter be completed by placing these forms within it and bringing into actuality, one after another, what it can receive only as a possibility. So the forms unfold over a time without beginning or end.[^V07-89] Throughout that time, each form exists in its own matter, and matter is made complete through it.”

With this in mind, know that decree means that all existing things are present together as a whole in the world of intellects through creation. Destiny means that they exist in their outer matter[^V07-90], one after another and in detail, after the right conditions have come together. As the Revelation says: “There is nothing whose stores are not with Us, and We send it down only in a known measure.” The intellectual substances and what belongs with them[^V07-91] exist in decree and destiny once, viewed in two different ways; bodily things and what belongs with them exist in each twice. This is the end of Tusi’s words.

The Shaykh of Illumination says in *al-Talwīḥāt*:

“If you looked at the signs of God’s mercy in this world, you would be astonished. Divine mercy cannot stop at a limit while an endless range of possibilities remains. So matter was given an endless readiness to receive, just as the first principles were given an endless power to act. A changing world was also created, so that each new event would bring a new readiness. The heavenly spheres move in cycles toward higher ends. Their movement creates endless readiness in matter. With an unlimited giver and a receiver that is always ready, blessings can descend and good can flow forever. Each receiver gets what it is ready for, while the source that gives does not change.

“If ants were ready to receive a nobler soul, as human beings do, they too would receive a share from the overflowing intellect. Since the noblest thing joined to matter is the rational soul, and since possible souls cannot come into existence all at once or apart from bodies, souls appear through cycles, generations, and varying degrees of readiness. They receive their existence from their giver, and return to their Lord when they are complete.”

It is now clear that the endless number of individuals and souls brought about by eternal providence requires change and opposition in the world of generation and decay. Without opposition there could be no generation or decay; without these, there could be no endless series of individuals, nor of the even nobler animal souls, nor of the still nobler rational souls. The opposing forms and qualities are not separately produced by an agent. They follow necessarily from material existence and from the interaction of material things, which brings about balance and completes their opposition. So without opposition, the flow from the Generous Source could not continue. Its generosity would come to a halt. The elemental world could not receive life, through which its purpose is fulfilled. Most possible things would remain hidden in possibility and buried in pure nonexistence. And those who travel the path would have no way to journey to God and return to Him. God says: “As We began the first creation, so shall We bring it back.”

If you reflect on death, which people generally count among the greatest evils, you will see that it brings much good, which far outweighs the harm it brings to the dead person and to others. As for the good it brings to others: if death were removed, people would crowd together until there was no room to breathe, let alone to move, eat, and drink. A person who remained alive in those conditions would be worse off than someone who had died.

As for the good it brings to the person who dies: death frees that person from this worldly life, which is exposed to harm and hardship, and leads toward mercy, as we will explain, God willing.

#### Chapter — Answering objections about good and evil

One objection is this: if good outweighs evil in the second class of beings, why did God not create them in a way that involved no evil at all, so that every being would be purely good? The answer is that this would make the thing something other than itself. Such a thing cannot exist in this second kind of existence. It is possible only in the absolute kind of existence: the First Director, the source that orders and gives things their being, has brought forth wholly immaterial intellectual forms, soul-forms that are connected to matter only in a limited way, and the measured natures of the heavens, all free of corruption and harm. What remains is this second kind of existence, which can exist only through a mixture of potentialities, lacks, and opposites.

If you ask why fire, one of the kinds in this class, was not made without any evil attached to it, you are asking why fire was not made something other than fire. It is impossible for fire to be fire, touch a worshipper’s clothing, and not burn it when nothing prevents the burning.[^V07-92]

Another objection is that you claim good is abundant in the world and evil scarce. Yet among the kinds of beings, the human being is the noblest; and among human individuals, most seem dominated by evil. Their actions are shameful, their habits and character poor, and their beliefs false. In their practical power, desire and anger usually rule them; in their thinking, they are trapped in compounded ignorance. These two conditions bring pain to the soul in the next life, cause misery there, and keep people from lasting happiness. So evil seems to prevail in the very kind that is the highest fruit and ultimate aim of the world’s creation. The worldly pleasures of desire, amusement, and play—which are really misery—are small compared with the true happiness people lose and the fires of torment they incur.

The answer is that people in the next life, as in this life and in their first development, fall into three groups. In this world, some are sound and well formed; others are middling, and they are the majority, though they differ in degree; and a smaller group are deeply deficient, ugly, sick, or disabled. Compared with the first two groups together, the last group is very small. Souls in the next life likewise fall into three groups. The first have perfected both their powers: they have gained the wisdom that completes the mind and the noble traits that complete action. The second group are in the middle, and they are the greater part, though some are closer to the nobler end and others farther from it. The third are deeply sunk in simple or compounded ignorance and in corrupt character. They are far fewer than the middle group, and very few compared with the first two groups together. In both lives, then, those who find safety and mercy are by far the greater number.

The Shaykh, Ibn Sina, says in *The Directives and Remarks*: “Do not suppose that happiness in the next life has only one form. Nor should you suppose that only one kind of ignorance and vice brings eternal ruin, or that only one degree of vice brings punishment. These apply to only a small number of people. Make room for God’s mercy.”[^V07-93] Although these words, and the argument before them, seem to conflict with the plain meaning of some religious reports, close attention to the principles of faith and reason makes it certain that most people in the next life must be safe and saved.[^V07-94] Those who know through direct spiritual insight have another way of understanding this question, which will be explained later, God willing.

An argument beginning with the causes of things shows that when God creates a natural kind and orders the world, He must bring all or most individuals of that kind to their own proper perfection, unless an obstacle gets in the way on a rare, incidental occasion. We must understand that we are discussing the first two stages by which a thing reaches its proper completeness, not every further achievement.[^V07-95] Nor does the first or second perfection of human beings, simply as members of the human kind, require most people to become sages who know God, His kingdom, His signs, and the Last Day. Most people are not naturally suited to that. Those who are suited to it form a distinct kind of human being, different in their inner nature from the rest. As we have indicated, in their first stage human beings are one kind; in the second, inward stage, they divide into many kinds, each with its own perfection and happiness, and its corresponding misery. We will explain this in the discussion of the afterlife.

Ibn Sina says in *The Healing*: “Know that evil, when it means a lack, is either a lack of something necessary or useful, something close to necessary; or it is not evil in that respect, but only in relation to something possible that would be an additional perfection beyond the first and second perfections, with no natural cause requiring it. This second case is not the one we are discussing; it is the case we set aside. Such a lack is not evil for a kind as a whole, but only by comparison with some further perfection, such as not knowing philosophy or geometry. That is not evil simply because we are human. It is a lack only in relation to a perfection that ought not to be universal. You will see that it is a lack only when a particular person or soul desires it—not because that person is human or a soul, but because they have recognised its value, longed for it, and become ready for it. Before that, the nature of the kind does not move toward it as it moves toward the second perfections that follow its first perfection. If it does not have such an extra perfection, then it lacks nothing that its nature calls for.”

Another objection is this: you admit that sins and vices a person commits are included in God’s decree and determination. So the person must commit them, whether willing or unwilling. If they are necessary and forced, it is unworthy of God, the source of generosity and kindness, to punish sinners for them. To punish a weak, powerless person for something they had to do would violate justice and kindness; it would be wrongdoing and aggression. That is impossible for God, who commands justice, kindness, and care for relatives, and forbids indecency, evil, and oppression.

The answer, according to the philosophers’ principles,[^V07-96] is that God has no need of anyone: neither of the obedience of those who do good nor of the disobedience of those who do wrong. What comes upon the soul after it leaves this world follows from its own failures and from staining its own being with painful, dark, and frightening traits. It is not punishment by some outside avenger who disciplines it and takes revenge for its actions, as ordinary people imagine from the punishments inflicted by external causes in this world. Things in the next life are different. Its punishments follow from its own deeds and actions, and from the results of its corrupt habits and evil character. The soul carries the fuel for its own fire and the kindling for its own hell. When it leaves the body stained with blameworthy habits and degrading traits, the bodily veil is gone. The material for the flames of hell, the tinder for inward burning, and the fires hidden within it are then exposed to its clear sight.

It sees how bitter its worldly desires were. It suffers from the harmful effects of its character and habits. Its bad actions and their results return to it, as the Imam al-Sadiq says: “Your deeds are returned to you.” And it is said: “A moment’s desire can bring long sorrow.” The person suffering because of such traits is like someone with an insatiable appetite who neglects a proper diet: when the appetite grows stronger while the stomach grows weaker, the result is painful illness. The suffering follows from the desire that destiny led the person to indulge; it is not revenge by the doctor who told them to diet.[^V07-97]

Another objection—one of the weaker criticisms of the philosophers[^V07-98]—is that they deny that actions are rationally good or bad,[^V07-99] and deny that God has a purpose in His actions. Since they also say that God acts necessarily, there is no point in their asking why things happen. If God’s actions are necessary, or if they have no moral value, asking why is pointless.

The answer is, first, that the philosophers did not deny that any action has an end or purpose. They denied only that God’s action as a whole, and His first act, have some additional goal beyond His own essence. As for the later and particular acts, they affirmed that each has its own end. Their books are full of discussions of the ends and benefits of beings, as can be seen in their studies of the heavens, bodily balances, compound things, anatomy, medicine, and more.

Second, as you have learned more than once, God’s action follows from His own will and approval. The necessity that follows from them is not the same as compulsion in mindless natural causes or forces.[^V07-100] Third, even if the objection were right, a philosopher could still investigate how actions follow from their own causes in a fitting way, without contradiction. A philosopher could also ask how evil can come from what is good in itself, and show that what first and directly comes from God is good. Evil does not come from Him directly. Rather, the production of much universal good incidentally leads to a few particular evils. Compared with the great goods, those evils are few. So nothing evil comes from God in itself.

This discussion also answers a major objection from the Magians, who believed in two eternal principles: Yazdan, the source of good, and Ahriman, the source of evil. That alone is a great honor of this inquiry. It also answers the seed of doubt raised by Satan, when he objected to the angels—who are among the noblest beings in the heavens, just as philosophers are among the noblest in the gardens of paradise—by asking God, “Why did You create me, when You knew I would lead Your servants astray and turn them from the straight path?” The answer given—“He is not questioned about what He does”—is decisive as a reply to someone arguing with a person who cannot understand a demonstration.[^V07-101] The Qur'an gives three ways to address people: demonstrative reasoning for those who are complete and brought near to God; exhortation for people seeking safety and for the people of the right; and argument to rebut the doubts of those who have gone astray, the people of the left who deny the Day of Judgment.

Another objection says: if philosophers allow a noble being to come from God for the sake of something low—if the world of intellect is created first, then the world of soul through it, and bodily nature through the soul, until the chain ends at matter, which is the lowliest thing—then why do they not also allow what is evil in itself to come from the Good? The answer is that this mistake comes either from confusing a true end with a necessary consequence, or from assuming that the converse of a universal statement is itself universal.[^V07-102] It does not follow that because every end is, in one respect, later than the thing directed toward it, everything that is later in that respect is an end. So it does not follow that common matter is nobler than the heavens, or that the heavens are nobler than the active intellects. “End” can mean the purpose for which an action is done, or the point at which a process finishes. One can say that the chain of eternal things ends at common matter, or something like it, and that a series of actions comes to an end.

The divine generosity has no such end. When philosophers say that God brings things into existence in order, from the intellect down to matter, they mean something like the Qur'anic statement, “He directs the affair from heaven to earth.” They do not mean that matter, or anything like it, is the final aim of His generosity. If that were so, existence would not turn back from this lower dwelling and lowest earth toward the highest perfection, in the reverse order of its first descent, as God says: “Then they will return to Him,” and “As We began the first creation, so We shall bring it back again—a promise binding on Us; We will surely do it.”

Those who talk at length about good and evil and find the issue difficult are often people who imagine that the vast divine works—the heavens and all they contain—were created only for human beings. They also imagine that God’s actions begin with a choice and aim at goals in the same way as our chosen actions do. If this ignorant person, shut off from the insight of those who know, thought carefully even for a moment, they would see that if things were as they imagine, and there were no firm order and true divine knowledge and rules in place from eternity, then God’s friends would not have suffered such severe trials or faced the power of enemies and oppressors. Nor would God’s enemies—tyrants, impostors, and people of corrupt faith, false beliefs, and wicked opinions—have enjoyed the power to spread their false religions, seize people and property, act arrogantly, and show hostility.

If God’s will were newly formed and arbitrary, what would stop Him from making events fit each person’s desires, especially when that person was one of God’s friends or a righteous servant? Someone could then ask why God, with such a will, did not remove every obstacle and grant the requested good. Why did He not combine safety from harm with reward? Why did He not remove disbelief and oppression from the world so that it would always be filled with justice and fairness? Why did He not make the earth unlike this earth—pure, radiant, and free of the stains of disbelief and sin, as it will be in the next life?

If they reply that eternal determination prevented this, we ask whether that determination was necessary or only one possibility among others. If it was only one possibility and God chose it, there must be some further reason for that choice, as they themselves maintain. And choosing universal good would have been better, since disbelief does no good for the unbeliever, nor misery for the wretched. If the determination was necessary, so that things could not exist except as they do, then they have conceded that it must be so. If they say, “He does what He wills, and no one may ask why,” we reply that their idea of forbidding the question—because it supposedly burns the tongue, makes inquiry unlawful, or prevents proof from reaching God—is false in every form. If they claim that one cannot ask why about matters known by reason, then an opponent could use that answer to block every proof, including proofs that the world’s features need a reason, or disputes about whether God’s attributes exist. The opponent could simply say, “No one may ask why.”[^V07-103]

A serious difficulty remains. God is pure good, generous and kind, and has no need of the obedience of the obedient or the sins of the guilty. Why, then, does He punish unbelievers in the next life with fire forever, as the Qur'an says: “They are the people of the Fire; they will remain there forever,” along with other passages that say their punishment has no end? We have already pointed out that a natural kind must, by its nature, reach its own perfection in all its individuals, or at least in most of them, without being blocked forever. As long as a thing remains what it is by nature, it must not be kept forever from its second perfection or from the purpose for which it was made. When a force that holds it back is removed, it must return to that perfection. As the noblest of creatures said, “Each is guided toward what it was created for.” A force that holds something back cannot do so forever.

So either disbelief and the like take a person out of their first nature and make them part of a different kind, or they do not. Either way, eternal punishment seems impossible. If the person leaves their original nature, that is clear: through repeated acts of desire or anger, they become a different kind of being. Their settled character becomes the form of their self and the substance of their nature. Those actions then complete their nature, as satisfying desire completes a beast and overpowering others completes a predator. A being does not suffer from what suits its own nature; rather, it takes pleasure in it, as we see in many people.

If the person remains in the nature they began with, then the barrier that hides that nature from them is accidental and temporary. Once the barrier is gone, they return to their original nature and receive the wide mercy and all-embracing generosity, with no one to prevent it and no veil between them. There is therefore no reason for punishment to last forever.[^V07-104]

The answer will become clear in its proper place, God willing, when we explain the different kinds of souls in the next life. In later chapters we will also give an account that points to the breadth of God’s grace and the reach of His mercy to all His creatures, in this life and the next.[^V07-105]

#### Chapter — Why what people call evils occur in this world

The eternal will has been directed toward what is good for created beings. No one who truly understands the matter can doubt this, as we will explain when we show that the order of the world, as it is, is the noblest possible order, the most complete and best that can be conceived. This is firmly established, and both philosophers and theologians agree on it, whether they believe in an eternal decree or in a renewed act of choice and an additional purpose. Even someone who believes in choice can say that the world could not be made better than it is. If it could, and the free Creator did not know that a better world was possible, His knowledge would not fully encompass universals and particulars. If He knew and did not make it, though He had the power, that would contradict His generosity, which embraces all beings.[^V07-106]

Al-Ghazali says this in one of his books. The complete Shaykh, Muhyi al-Din Ibn al-ʿArabi, quotes him in *The Meccan Revelations* and approves the argument. It is a sound demonstration. God, exalted in majesty, has unlimited power and complete generosity. Whatever has no matter, needs no special readiness, and faces no opposing force flows from Him simply because it is possible in itself, through creation from nothing. The whole order is one universal kind and one unique form, without matter.[^V07-107] Anything without matter has its kind confined to one individual. Its being is therefore not held back by limited readiness or a fixed time; it must be brought into being. So this order cannot be better either as a kind or as an individual.[^V07-108]

If this is so, then the basic elements of possible things in this order are revolving spheres and moving stars, moved by higher longings, and elements arranged in sequence. They are ready to produce many other kinds of beings. This can happen only through changes and transformations in the matter of generated things, brought about by the positions and movements of the heavens. A created thing cannot be made dependent on motion while every motion has the same result. Each motion must have a different effect. If the first effect agrees with one thing’s nature, a second effect may oppose it. Nor can the result of one motion, when it suits one nature, suit the natures of all other things. What is better must be given priority.

For example, the sun’s light and heat may act on a place on earth, softening and transforming it until it takes on another nature and produces a kind more noble than the earth itself. This does not suit the earth simply as earth; yet divine mercy calls for it. If the earth could feel, it would know that leaving its present state, though painful and forced from the standpoint of what it is now, brings great kindness beneath that pain and force: it changes from one form into another, nobler and more ready to receive life and divine mercy.

The divine Word points to this meaning: “Come, willingly or unwillingly.” The two said, “We come willingly.” Coming to God—as in death and similar changes—may at first seem unwelcome because a thing is attached to its particular state. But when it passes from that state into another, what it fled from becomes what it seeks, and what it disliked becomes what it desires. Death, decay, and similar things are therefore necessary parts of the order. It would not have been wise to create this world without creating the conditions that entail some evil.[^V07-109]

Look more deeply into the secrets of created things, and you will see that the whole is good and well ordered. Death, which people dread, is in truth a passage of the soul from a lower state to a higher one. It is one of the natural forms of completion. Every time nature reaches a fuller state, something comes to be and something less complete passes away. When a seed becomes an animal, for example, the seed’s former form disappears. In the same way, when an animal’s sensory form is completed by a form suited to the afterlife—an imaginal or intellectual form—the sensory form must be shed, and the spirit must leave this natural body.

The innate heat, which those who truly understand regard as a heavenly substance in the hand of an angel who draws out souls and carries them from one state to another, does not by its nature exist merely to dissolve and consume moisture until death occurs. If that were its purpose, God would not have given it to us or set it to work in the body. God permits anyone to die—especially a human being—only so that another life may begin in the world of return. You will learn that the souls of animals, and even plants, also pass to that world. The true work of this heat is to regulate the body’s balance, transform it, and warm its matter into a lighter, finer state, so that it can become a suitable vehicle for the soul: a gentle intermediary body that obeys its rider, rather than a wild one pulled first in one direction and then another because it is made from opposing elements. This change of vehicles prepares the soul to leave this home and journey toward God. In this sense death is natural—not in the sense given by physicians and other naturalists, who say death comes when moisture runs out and the body’s powers fail, as we have already explained.

So every kind of death and decay, the dissolving of forms, and similar things that happen by nature rather than by force or chance, has the cause we have described. There is no evil or harm in a lower form being sacrificed for a nobler one, or in a lower and less complete kind becoming food for a higher, fuller kind. The elements feed compound beings; compound beings feed plants; plants and animals feed human beings. In this way human beings gain honor and excellence, and are prepared for the journey to a brighter realm.

Let us now return to another way of explaining the kinds of relative good and evil that lead to a greater good, following the approach found in some earlier books.

#### Chapter — The relative amounts of good and evil

Know that good and evil are spoken of in four ways. One concerns the heavens: the good or bad fortunes associated with the stars.[^V07-110] Another concerns natural things: coming to be and passing away, and the pains and illnesses that affect bodily balances in animals. A third concerns the nature of living things made from the elements: their conflicts and alliances, their affection and hostility, love and enmity, and the struggle and competition built into their nature. A fourth concerns souls governed by the commands and prohibitions of divine law, and the happiness or misery, both immediate and in the afterlife, that follows.

The good attributed to favorable arrangements of the heavens comes from the care and will of the First Reality. The evil attributed to unfavourable arrangements is accidental, not intended in the first place. For example, the sun may shine on and warm one region for a time, then move away and leave it cool for a while, so that animals and plants can grow there. This happens through the care of the First Reality and the demands of His wisdom, for the sake of the general good and common benefit. God says: “Tell Me: if God made the day last forever over you …”[^V07-111] The excessive heat or cold that occasionally kills some animals or plants in certain places and at certain times is not intended in itself.

The good attributed to natural things—the coming to be of animals, plants, and minerals, and the causes that help them grow and reach their fullest state and end—is all approved by God, and comes from the will and care of the Creator who sustains all things. The evils of decay and calamity that befall them after they come to be are incidental, not intended in the first place. They occur as a side effect and consequence.

Since individuals in this world cannot remain forever in matter, divine wisdom and care arranged for their forms and the source of their kind to endure through a succession of individuals. Their nature is preserved by the continual arrival of new examples, even though individual beings are always dissolving and passing away. This is necessary for wisdom and care because there are many great goods and perfections hidden in potential, without end.

They cannot all emerge from potential into actuality at once. Bodily matter cannot receive, all at the same time, every perfection contained within the guiding intellect of a natural kind, the world of its unseen reality, and the source that pours its being into its fixed archetype. It receives these perfections one after another, gradually and through the passage of time. This is true even of one kind of nature; how much more so of all natural lights and their many conditions, qualities, consequences, and effects. If God had created all human beings who ever lived, those alive now, and those who will live until the Day of Judgment at once, the broad earth could not hold them. How, then, could it also hold their animals, plants for food, possessions, and all they need during their lives? For this reason God creates them generation after generation, nation after nation. The earth cannot hold them all, and matter cannot receive them all at once.

This shows that the shortfall lies in matter and its capacity to receive, not in the Creator. An action can fall short because the maker is weak or lacks knowledge, but it can also fall short because the tools and materials needed for skilled work are missing, or because of the limits of place, time, motion, and similar conditions. It can also fall short because of the material itself. Iron is hard to twist into a long cord, unlike hemp or wool; that is not the blacksmith’s failure, but a limit of the iron. Air cannot hold a writer’s letters because it flows away. A carpenter cannot make a ladder that reaches the sky because there is no wood, not because the carpenter is incapable. A wise person cannot teach a child who is not ready to learn; the fault lies in the child’s readiness, not in the wise teacher. Likewise, when some wretched people are not guided by a prophet’s call and warning, the shortfall is not in the prophets, peace be upon them all, or in their ability to guide and warn. Rather, corrupt souls are not ready to receive what would bring them nearer to God and His highest kingdom. The same principle applies to matter’s inability or difficulty in receiving forms: the shortfall is in matter, not in the wise Creator and Director.

There is another reason. These beings begin with the weakest and least complete kind of existence, then gradually rise to their fullest states and ends by means of causes that help them grow and develop. The Creator’s care brings them to their most complete ends. Such causes are called good. Any cause that gets in the way and holds them back is called evil. Yet it is evil only by extension, not in the first place. It is evil in relation to the thing it prevents from reaching its perfection and end, but it may be good in relation to something else, helping that thing reach its own perfection. The first kind of cause was created directly and in itself, not for the sake of the second. So its goodness is essential, while its being evil is incidental.

Now that you understand that an action may fail because matter is incapable or because place, time, motion, or similar conditions are missing, you can see the error in the view of many ordinary people and theologians. They do not understand how matter can be incapable or unable to receive a form, so they blame every shortfall on the wise, powerful, and knowing agent. They may claim that God is unable to do many things, such as remove Satan from His kingdom. Apart from the benefits considered in creating Satan, the failure to remove him comes from there being no other kingdom outside God’s kingdom into which he could be sent—not from any lack of power in the Creator.

They may say that God cannot put the heavens inside a needle. They do not realize that the limit is in the needle and its hollow. They may say that God cannot join opposites, not seeing that the impossibility lies in the opposites themselves.[^V07-112]

When asked what is meant by “God has power over everything,” they say the verse is restricted, even though it is expressed universally and explicitly uses the word “everything.” They do not realize that the impossibilities they imagine are not things outside their thoughts and conceptions. The mind imagines certain notions, treats them as names for impossibilities, and assigns them properties only hypothetically, not as settled facts. This is like hypothetical propositions that are not self-evident, as explained in some places in this book.

As for the good found in the animal nature of living things made from the elements—love, pleasure, companionship, eating, drinking, and sexual union—there is no doubt that God placed these in their nature and planted them in their original make-up. They are good by His care and approval, directly and in the first place. The discord, fear, hatred, hunger, pain, thirst, wounds, illness, aches,[^V07-113] death, and similar things that befall them come through necessity, as side effects and secondary consequences. God gave animals hunger and thirst, the pleasure of what suits them, and discomfort at what harms them because of His wisdom and concern. These things then become causes of their own ends, bringing their bodies to ruin and their structures to destruction, and their souls to misery, as a necessary consequence.

But the wise Creator’s purpose in bringing animals into existence and shaping them is their survival and well-being. He gave them hunger and thirst to prompt them to eat and drink, replacing in their bodies the nourishing fluids that are continually used up as their bodies dissolve and change. He gave them appetites to draw them toward different foods that suit their bodily balance and needs. He gave them pleasure so they would eat the right amount, neither too much nor too little. He gave them pain when their bodies are harmed, so their souls would protect their bodies from injury for a set time. And some animals eat the carcasses of others so that nothing created is wasted.

My dear friend, know that people have been bewildered by the fact that some animals eat others. They have wondered why God placed this in their nature and gave them the tools needed for it: fangs, claws, sharp nails for gripping, holding, tearing, biting, and eating, as well as appetite, pleasure, hunger, and similar powers. They have wondered too about the pain, suffering, fear, and terror the prey experience when they are killed. When people reflected on this and could not see the cause, purpose, or wisdom, their opinions divided and their schools multiplied. Some said that animals’ domination of and eating one another could not be the work of a wise and merciful creator, and so they claimed that the world had two makers, one good and one evil. Others blamed the stars. Some said it was punishment for sins committed in earlier cycles; these were the transmigrationists. Some called it chance. Others said it was best this way. Some admitted they did not know the cause, while insisting that God does nothing but what is wise.

They said all these things because they were searching for a cause and a wise purpose. They failed to find it because they looked at separate cases and asked about each cause in isolation. One cannot discover the universal causes of things by examining individual cases alone. God’s acts aim at universal benefit and general well-being, even when particular harms and hardships sometimes occur. Consider the laws of the true religious order: the law requires retaliation for murder, and says, “In retaliation there is life for you, people of understanding,” although the murderer suffers death and pain. It also requires cutting off a thief’s hand, which benefits the community as a whole even though it causes pain and harm to the thief.

In the same way, God created the sun, moon, and rain for general benefit and the common good, even though they sometimes harm particular people, animals, or plants. The followers of prophets and Imams may also face hardships and pain when establishing the faith and its laws, especially at first. But the Creator’s purpose in making the faith known and establishing its laws is a general benefit and common good for the countless people who will come after them until the Day of Judgment, and for the many blessings and goods those people will receive. Compared with that, the harm suffered by the Prophet, the Imams, and their pure descendants, peace be upon them all, from the attacks of idolaters and the battles and hardships they faced is small.

Likewise, the effort a believer spends in night prayers, fasting during the day, and performing required acts is slight compared with the gardens, companions of paradise, and God’s greatest approval prepared for them. Since the result is the common good, the hardships are small and particular in comparison.

By the same reasoning, the pain animals feel when they are harmed is not meant as punishment or torment, as the transmigrationists suppose. It is meant to urge their souls to protect their bodies and guard them from injury. Bodies cannot seek benefit or ward off harm by themselves. Without pain, souls would neglect their bodies and abandon them to danger before their lifetimes were over. They would all perish at once in a very short time. For this reason animals, unlike plants, were given pain and the natural impulse to defend themselves—by fighting, fleeing, or taking precautions—so they could protect themselves until a fixed time. Once that time arrives, fighting, fleeing, and taking precautions no longer help. They must yield, even if they feel some pain.[^V07-114]

With this in mind, we can say that when God created kinds of land animals, He knew none could live forever. He therefore gave each the longest natural lifespan possible, after which natural death comes, whether they wish it or not. God knows that every day countless animals die on land and sea, in plains and mountains. In His wisdom,[^V07-115] He made the bodies of the dead into food for the living, and a material source of their survival, so that nothing created would be wasted. This benefits the living and does not harm the dead.

There is another wise purpose. If the living did not eat the bodies of the dead, the corpses would remain and pile up over the ages, until the earth’s surface and the bottoms of the seas were filled with them. Their smell would pollute the air and cause epidemics, killing the living. What could be wiser than this? By letting animals eat one another, God brings benefit to the living and wards off harm from all of them, even though some feel pain when they are killed or seized. The one that kills or catches them is not trying to cause them pain; it seeks the benefit of eating them or avoids harm by doing so.

Know that when the wise Creator brought beings into existence and arranged created things, He divided them into two groups: those brought into existence all at once, and those formed through generation. He ordered and arranged both. For the first, ungenerated group, He made the nobler cause of the lower being its source, its support, and what brings it to its fullest end. For the second, generated group, the order runs in the opposite direction: they begin with their weakest state and rise toward the noblest and most complete. The incomplete serves the complete; the lower helps and is directed toward the nobler.[^V07-116]

For example, an individual plant is lower in rank and condition than an individual animal.
