So the plant’s body becomes food and material for the animal’s body, and the plant soul serves and supports the animal soul.[^V07-117] Likewise, animal souls are lower than the human rational soul, so they serve and support it. And when some animals are more fully formed and complete than others, the less complete animals serve and support the more complete. Their bodies become material and food for the bodies of the more complete, helping them survive and reach their fullest end, since the matter of individual beings is always changing and passing away.

This explains the cause that makes animals eat one another. The further purpose is this: when the wise Creator made the things that come to be, either to bring benefit to animals or to ward off harm from them, He left nothing without benefit or purpose. If He had not made the bodies of animals into food for other animals, those corpses would have been useless and would have caused general harm and destruction, as we explained. As for the pain animals feel when seized or killed, we have already explained that God did not make it to torment and punish their souls for earlier sins, as the transmigrationists suppose. It is meant to urge their souls to protect their bodies from harm and not abandon them to danger before their appointed time.

If you ask why animals love life and hate death, there are several reasons. One is that life resembles lasting existence, while death resembles extinction. Beings naturally love permanence and dislike extinction, since permanence is linked with existence and extinction with nonexistence. These are opposites. God, exalted in majesty, is the cause of all beings and remains forever. So all beings love permanence and long for it because it is an attribute of their cause. An effect loves its cause and the cause’s attributes, and longs to resemble them. For this reason, the sages say that the Necessary Existent is the first beloved, longed for by all creatures,[^V07-118] as will become clear, God willing.

A second reason is the pain, suffering, fear, and distress animals experience when their souls leave their bodies. A third is that their souls do not know they can exist apart from bodies. You might ask why their souls are not taught that they can exist without bodies. The answer is that they cannot understand such things. If they did, they would leave their bodies before reaching completion. Then they would remain empty and inactive, without any work to do. Nothing in existence is idle: every existent has its own particular effect. It would not be wise to leave souls inactive and without direction, since the source of all things has never ceased for a moment to direct and preserve the world and its beings. Every day He is at work. If He stopped directing, sustaining, and preserving the world for even an instant, the heavens would collapse, the stars would fall, the world would vanish, and souls and bodies would cease to exist.

Know that when complete and perfected souls leave their bodies,[^V07-119] they are either absorbed in beholding eternal beauty, illuminated by the lights of the First, and joined to the ranks of God’s angels who are lost in wonder at the majesty of the First of all firsts; or they are occupied with helping imperfect souls embodied in bodies, so that these may rise from deficiency to completion. The perfected souls themselves can then rise to a higher and nobler state until they join the first group, the pioneers brought near to God. “To your Lord is the final destination.”

An example in this life is a loving father or devoted teacher who teaches children and students, bringing them from the darkness of ignorance into the open space of knowledge. The students and children learn and grow; the fathers and teachers also become more complete by bringing into action the knowledge, skills, and wisdom that had been potential in their souls. They imitate the Creator, exalted in greatness and majesty, and follow His wisdom: He is the cause of bringing beings from potentiality into actuality, and from hiddenness into open existence.

The more knowledge a soul has, the more skillfully it works, the better its actions and character, and the more it gives to others, the closer it is to God and the more it resembles Him. This is the rank of angels, who do not disobey God’s commands and do what they are told, seeking the way to their Lord and asking which of them is nearest. For this reason the sages defined philosophy as “becoming like God to the extent human ability allows.” The more a person’s knowledge is true, their work sound, their actions good, their character beautiful, their views right, and their benefit to others lasting, the closer they are to God and the more they resemble Him, for God is so.

As for the good and evil attributed to human souls because they are subject to religious commands and prohibitions and to the laws of the divine order, all of them are things that exist.[^V07-120] This is true whether they are counted as good—such as prayer, fasting, pilgrimage, almsgiving, urging what is right and forbidding what is wrong, keeping family ties, visiting the sick, following funeral processions, visiting the graves of God’s friends, and other acts of obedience—or as evil and disobedience—such as adultery, theft, gossip, murder, consuming an orphan’s property, injustice, oppression, transgression, and similar sins.

As existing acts, they are perfections and goods for a person’s powers of animal life, not for the person as a human being—that is, not from the standpoint of the rational part. Reason or religious law condemns them only in relation to the rational soul, which is perfected when it restrains the powers of desire and anger and gains mastery over them. It is diminished when it is overpowered by the body and moved by bodily powers. When the soul is moved by what is lower and base, that is misery, distance from God’s mercy, and lack of kinship with His highest kingdom.

If we set aside the effects that these blameworthy acts have on the nobler part of the human being, then in reality and from other points of view—more numerous than that one point of view—what is blamed would turn into something praised, and what is condemned into something commendable. Desire, for example, is blamed; the adulterer and adultery are blamed. But desire itself is a natural power that runs through the soul. It is a shadow of the longing found in the angels brought near to God, who are absorbed in contemplation. Anger, too, is a shadow in human beings of the power of the higher forces that overcome resistance. Each is praiseworthy in itself.

Consider desire. Its true nature is love. As it takes shape in men and women, it preserves the human kind and brings new individuals into the world; it also gives pleasure. In these respects it is a perfection, praiseworthy in itself. Sexual intercourse is likewise a perfection and would not be blameworthy if we set aside the incidental feature under discussion. In all these other respects, blame turns into praise. What remains blameworthy is desire’s failure to obey reason and submit to its guidance. It is blameworthy only when it turns into disobedience to reason and religious law, and leads to broken family lines and inheritance, neglect of children’s upbringing, conflict, disorder, and strife. These are absences that arise from the limits of created things and their shortcomings as possible beings.

In the end, all existence and necessity, and all that belongs to them—activity, completeness, perfection, permanence, joy, love, and pleasure—are good, praiseworthy, and effective. Goodness is the whole of reality. Nothing that exists is truly blameworthy; it is blamed only in relation to something else. Let us now explain this point with the strongest argument and the clearest inquiry.

#### Chapter — The visible world, like the world understood by the mind, is made as well and as fully as it can be

We have established that God, the Lord of the world, whose existence depends on nothing else, is free from every kind of deficiency. His existence, which is His own essence and reality, is the best and most complete kind of existence. Indeed, He is existence itself and its reality. Everything besides Him is a flash from Him, a trace, or a shadow. That is why we say He is necessary in every respect and is all existence: all existence belongs to Him. There is no cause for Him, and no existence precedes His. Nothing acts upon Him; He has no matter, form, or subject. He has no end beyond Himself, but is the end of all ends, the source of all sources, the form behind all forms, the reality of all realities, the essence of all essences, and the one who brings all things into being.

Anything that comes from such a being comes from the heart of His essence and the core of His own reality. No additional motive draws Him to act, and no goal beyond Himself makes Him act. The existence of what He brings into being does not add a perfection, honor, joy, delight, benefit, desire for praise, escape from blame, or any other imagined or real advantage. He is free of every deficiency and needs nothing in all the worlds. The existence by which His essence is what it is is the very same existence by which He acts. In Him, being what He is and being the source of all things are one reality and one fact—not as with other agents, whose being and acting depend on two different things. A human writer, for example, is a rational being by one capacity and writes by a separate skill. The sun illuminates the earth and fire burns wood through powers different from what makes them what they are. They therefore depend on added qualities and on something able to receive their effects.

God, exalted in majesty, brings things into being from Himself, not from something ready to receive them; He creates both the recipient and what it receives, matter and form together. So whatever comes from Him, from the very root of His reality, must be the noblest possible being and the most excellent created thing that can be conceived. Nothing is better than it except the sustaining Creator Himself.

Since God knows all things through His own essence in the best possible way—the forms of all things in His knowledge are His essence—things have divine, holy forms of knowledge before they exist in the created world. Every divine existence must be perfectly beautiful and radiant. When the likeness of these forms appears in the created world, it must be as beautiful and noble as a created being can be. And when things are arranged, their order must be the best and noblest possible. It begins with the beings whose existence is most noble, such as the active intellects, then proceeds to what is a little less complete, such as the souls of the heavenly spheres, followed by their imprinted forms, and so on, descending gradually to the least complete and lowest beings. The chain of creation ends there and cannot descend further, because nothing lower is possible. This is the final reach of the order, as God says: “He directs the affair from heaven to earth.”

From there, through the mixing of sensory matter, motion, and change, successive natural forms flow into recipients according to their different degrees of readiness and the sequence of movements. Existence keeps rising from the lowest toward the best, and toward nearness to the worshipped One, until it reaches the highest point possible in this ascending chain. As God says: “To Him good words rise, and He raises up righteous action.” Here the order of goodness and generosity reaches its summit, and the circle of existence is complete.

This is the finest way to create: the thing created reaches the good end intended for it. No creator can make anything better than that its end should be God’s own essence, exalted in glory. With this order, the whole world is like one person: its beginning is the Real and its end is the Real. Its form is the form of the Real; indeed, its fulfillment is the Real. It is like a circle whose beginning and end are one point—indeed, the whole circle is that point, flowing from itself, within itself, and back to itself.[^V07-121] Glory to His essence, too great for comparisons and beyond the reach of words.

#### Chapter — In detail, every level of created beings is the best and noblest possible; two approaches, from cause to effect and from effect to cause

#### First approach: from cause to effect

#### First discussion — Everything in the descending order exists in the best possible way

Nothing at any level of this chain can be more noble than it is, either as this individual or as this kind, in the respect in which it comes from its creator. First, each kind in this order must be confined to a single individual.[^V07-122] Multiple individuals of one kind could arise only through motions, chance events, and incidental features that distinguish and multiply them. But before there are motions and chance events, if some individuals of a kind were distinguished by an attribute belonging to the kind or following necessarily from it, then every individual would equally deserve that attribute. It would be arbitrary for one to have it and another not. If the difference came from something outside the nature of the kind, then there would have to be matter, able to receive outside influences in different ways according to its readiness and reactions. But there is no such matter in the world of origination. So each kind there must be confined to one individual.

Second, the principle of the noblest possible being,[^V07-123] whose proof was given earlier, shows that the source of all existence and generosity does not create a lower being while a nobler being is possible. The flow of existence from His generosity must proceed from the more excellent to the less excellent. So He first brought into being the active intellects, the honored forms, the angels absorbed in God, and the overpowering lights: the noblest beings actually possible, given their level. Among those holy beings, the one earliest and nearest to the Light of Lights, exalted in glory, is the most excellent and radiant.

Then come the others in order, down to the final level of this class: the lords of archetypes, or ideal forms of natural kinds, affirmed by Plato and the Platonists. We have revived the teachings of the earlier sages on this doctrine, strengthened it, and defended it by answering doubts and removing objections, with the aid of the Mighty and Wise, as explained before.

The last of these shining intellects is the lord of the archetype of the earth. In the language of Persian sages, he is the mistress of the house of earth, governing the forms of the compound kinds that appear in this world. When the chain of intellects reaches its end in descent, the chain of souls begins with the soul of the outermost sphere, which surrounds the heavens. Next comes the soul of the noblest star, then the soul of each star and sphere in turn, down to the soul of the lowest sphere, the nearest heaven. Beneath these come the forms of the spheres and stars, each as excellent as its existence allows, arranged in the best possible order. Their order reflects the order of the intellects, and each form reflects the intellect corresponding to it, without weakness, failure, fatigue, or exhaustion.

Their bodies are the finest bodies, their shapes the best shapes, and their movements the best movements. First, their simplicity: despite the simplicity of their tangible quality, it contains all the sensory qualities in a finer, purer, and more delightful way. In that one quality are all delicious tastes, pleasant scents, beautiful colors, and uplifting sounds. Second, they are spherical, and elsewhere we have shown that the sphere is the best of shapes. Third, their movements are loving and yearning. Their movers are divine beloveds, intellectual angels, and soul-like powers. The first kind of mover moves as an end, without itself moving—like the beloved moving the lover, or a teacher, or knowledge itself, moving the learner. The second kind moves as an agent acting directly, through renewed desire—like longing moving the one who longs, thought moving the thinker, or new knowledge moving the learner. Each of these movers is itself moved.

In short, their movements are the finest, for they are acts of worship directed to God and rites of the intellect. Longing and love for God stir them. Each heavenly being has its own beloved intellect and its own mover, drawing it toward that guide so it can follow and imitate it. Yet all long for the beauty of the Lord of the Worlds and dance in the joy they receive from union with the First of all firsts. With each moment, their movement brings a new longing and delight. A fresh holy influx reaches their being, and another movement follows, carrying them to another longing and pleasure, and so on without end.

From them flow the perfections and goods suited to this world: direct, reflected, and bent rays of light.[^V07-124] These prepare matter to receive the forms and perfections that the Active Source pours into it at every moment, until God inherits the earth and all who are upon it.

#### Second discussion — The beings and generated things that rise through the world of composition are perfectly ordered

The order of beings in this world depends on the movements and positions of the spheres. The order of the spheres, in turn, reflects the order of the divine decree, which you have learned is complete and perfect. It has also been shown many times that these beings do not come from luck or chance, as Democritus claimed; from arbitrary acts of will, as the Ashʿarites supposed; from an incomplete will and an extra purpose, like our own wills, which depend on motives outside us; or from a nature unaware of itself, let alone aware of what it brings about, as the materialists and naturalists claimed. Rather, the intelligible order that philosophers call providence gives rise to the order we see. So this world has the greatest good and excellence possible.

It follows that, in relation to their causes and principles, nothing in this world is arbitrary or accidental. Everything is necessary and natural in relation to the nature of the whole, whether it happens naturally by itself, as a stone falling down; by force, as a stone driven upward; or by choice, as an animal moving across the earth. Everything that occurs in the world of generation must arise from a cause, for a thing does not exist until its existence is necessary. The chain of causes rises to one source, from which things follow in the way He knows, through His wisdom and care. Nothing that exists is out of keeping with the nature of its causes, which lead back to the One who is truly Real. An effect is suited to its cause; it flows from that cause.

So movements that seem to conflict are harmonious and ordered when seen as parts of the whole. Even an extra finger is natural in the nature of the world as a whole, and every lifespan is natural in relation to the whole, even if it is not natural in an absolute sense.[^V07-125] Suppose you could know each thing through its causes. Suppose your intellect could leave this dark abyss and journey to God and His kingdom. You would witness the wonders and order of the heavens, then rise to the higher kingdom to see the wonders of divine power and know the First Source truly. You would travel through the Real and from the Real back to creation. In His light, you would see the angels who know, then the angels who act by God’s command. You would also see the powers and natural forces of the heavens that move them, and the causes and principles guiding their movements, longings, forms of worship, lights of obedience, and closeness to God. You would see how these bring about changes in the elements and combine them, and how the forms of generated things and the souls of their changing bodies follow. You would then see all things as beautiful, noble, and pleasing, and know through direct experience what you now know through argument. One like you would truly belong among the people of God’s good pleasure and find the joy of paradise.[^V07-126]

#### Third discussion — The world as a whole, in kind and in individual reality, has the best order and fullest completion

First, the whole world, considered as a whole, is one individual with a natural unity. Its unity is not like a collection of different things that happen to make one object by joining together, as bricks make a house or individuals make an army. The parts of the world have an intrinsic connection because they are arranged as causes and effects, from the noblest to the lower, from the higher to the lowest. A whole arranged in this way has an essential unity. You know that a cause completes its effect, and what is more noble completes what is less noble. A thing is more truly one with itself when it is complete than when it is treated as separate from itself; its parts therefore form one unity.

For us, the world’s unity as one individual is established by demonstration. Aristotle the Sage explicitly said that the world is one living being, and that the questions “What is it?” and “Why is it?” have one answer. Whoever knows what it is knows why it is. If this is so, the ultimate goal of the whole world—called by the mystics the Great Human—is the First Reality, exalted in glory. He answers the question “Why does this Great Human exist?” If the answers to “Why?” and “What?” are one, then God is what completes its essence and establishes its reality. No possible reality can be conceived as higher than one whose whole existence and essence are completed by the First Reality. The whole world, then, is the best and noblest possible.

Let us explain this more fully. If the entire world is one individual, it cannot be replaced by another order, whether that order were better than it or equal to it. Any proposed alternative would either differ from the existing order and fail to belong with it to the same kind, or would not differ from it. Neither possibility works, so no other order can be conceived.

The first possibility fails because it is impossible for there to be substances or attributes different in kind from those in the existing world. The intellects, universal souls, forms of the spheres and stars, and their matter cannot have different kinds of counterparts at the same level. Each comes from its source through a single necessary way of acting, without any contribution from another thing such as matter. Body, simply as body, does not differ in its essential reality. The simple kinds of body differ through their specific forms, which come from intellectual sources by necessary modes of action, either directly or with help from earlier forms.[^V07-127] Incidental events have no part in this, since these forms precede motion and readiness. The attributes of simple bodies depend on and are sustained by their substances. If the subjects and the necessary conditions of action and reception are the same, different attributes cannot result. What applies to simple beings applies equally to compound beings and their specific forms, which depend on their matter, combinations, readiness, and necessary attributes, such as the movements and positions of the spheres.

If no substances or attributes other than those that exist could come about, then any other world would have to share this world’s essence and differ from it in no essential respect. The second possibility—that another world could have the same essence but still be a different world—also fails for several reasons. First, whatever has no matter and no prior motion is brought into existence through creation. Such a thing does not vary because of outside conditions, so its kind cannot multiply into individuals. Each kind must be one individual, since whatever distinguishes it is its own essence or something that follows from it, and neither can be multiplied. Second, its existence comes from God’s own essence, with no other source; one cause gives rise to one effect. Third, its individuality comes from God, exalted in majesty, who is individual through His own reality. Every effect is likewise made individual by its immediate, producing cause. It therefore cannot be multiplied. Fourth, God’s knowledge of the good order—which is identical with His essence—requires the world’s existence, so no other world is possible. Finally, as already indicated, the final cause of the world’s existence is the essence of the Highest Source. The cause of its beginning is also the cause of its completion. Anything whose end is the most noble of all things is itself the noblest possible being.[^V07-128][^V07-129]

#### A question and further examination

You may say: if the whole world, the Great Human, is one individual and the noblest possible being because the cause of its beginning and its completion is one and the same—the Real—then the same must be true of the First Effect. It would be like the world in its reality. But then two things would come from the One Real at the beginning of creation, which is impossible. It would also mean there were two beings of one kind above the world, contrary to the principles of philosophy.[^V07-130]

The reply is that there is no real multiplicity here. As we established earlier, a thing’s full reality comes from its final defining feature: its form, which grounds all its other meanings.[^V07-131] You know that a thing is what it is because of its form, not its matter. What comes from God is one thing: the Great Human, the world as one individual. But it can be considered in two ways, as a whole or in detail. The difference between the whole and its details is in how we understand them, not in what is understood.

If you consider the world as one reality, you judge that it comes from the One Real in a single act of creation. If you consider its details one by one, you judge that what comes first is its noblest and most complete part, the First Intellect. As we have said, intellect is all things. Then the other parts follow one by one, in order from nobler to lower and more complete to less complete, down to the weakest and lowest form of existence. So the Great Human and the first created being are one in their essence and reality. To say “the First Intellect” is, in a sense, to say “the whole world”; and to say “the whole world” is, in a sense, to say “the First Intellect.” There is no difference in their essential or causal standing; the only difference is between the whole and its details.

If you ask how God created the entire world all at once when some of its parts exist gradually, such as times and movements, while others exist all at once,[^V07-132] and still others belong to neither class, we reply that the world has another kind of unity, one that gathers and contains all its individual unities. All movements, changes, bodies, and bodily things are included within that unity, as those firmly grounded in knowledge understand. God knows best.

#### Second approach: from effect to cause

We see that beings are connected to one another and benefit from one another. Everything incomplete turns toward its own completion, and everything low seeks to join what is higher, by an instinct and natural impulse placed within it by God. We also see the care each higher thing shows for what lies below it, the concern each stronger being has for the weaker, and the way every soul and intellect directs what is entrusted to it with wise guidance, careful shaping, excellent ordering, and gentle completion. Each is brought to the fullest state possible for it.

Form completes matter by giving it measure, shape, and qualities suited to it. If matter is already in its proper place, form lets it rest there; if it is outside that place, form moves it there by the easiest and shortest route. The soul completes the body’s nature by giving it powers and tools, and by giving it the shapes and organs it needs. It preserves the individual for as long as possible and brings it to full growth through nourishment and development. It preserves the kind by producing more of its kind, and keeps the body healthy by maintaining its balance. These functions are carried out through innate heat, which ripens, cooks, and digests matter, removes harmful waste, and retains the healthy blood and other fluids that nourish and sustain the body—phlegm, yellow bile, black bile, and the rest. It also gives the organs their appropriate forms, shapes, sizes, and positions, and arranges them in the best order. Much more could be said; anatomy and the study of the heavens show it. All this happens by God’s permission and inspiration, as He says: “By the soul and the One who formed it, then inspired it with its wickedness and its mindfulness of God.”

Intellect likewise perfects, straightens, refines, and purifies the soul from material impurities and bodily stains. It gives the soul knowledge and wisdom, lights it with understanding and guidance, brings its undeveloped abilities to full realization and carries it from darkness into light, strips away its bodily coverings, and raises it from decayed graves and crumbling resting places to the world of resurrection, where it stands before God. The care, guidance, creation, kindness, mercy, generosity, and grace of the First Reality toward the intellect, soul, nature, and all other things are greater than all of this. Let us now explain the point in more detail.

#### Chapter — A few signs of God’s wisdom and care in creating the heavens and earth

Even the finest human minds cannot fully grasp the realities of heaven and earth, the subtlety of their design, the wonders of nature, or the signs of care and wisdom found in them.[^V07-133] Most people cannot even fully understand the soul that is their very self: its many states, how its powers work through the body’s senses and organs, how the organs are arranged and shaped, or what each part does and what its purpose is. What anatomists have discovered through careful thought is only a little compared with the subtle features, wonders, and wisdom in God’s design that remain beyond their notice.

If we cannot fully understand our own soul and body, how could we grasp everything in the physical and spiritual worlds? We cannot even examine the true nature of most things that exist, let alone understand them all. Yet, despite this natural limit, while we are strangers in this world, we must still reflect on the wonders of creation and the marvels of nature. God calls us to do so: “Have they not reflected within themselves?” and, “Have they not looked[^V07-134] into the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and all that God has created? Perhaps their appointed time is near. What message after this will they believe?” He says: “In the creation of the heavens and the earth are signs for the believers,” and: “Have they not looked at the sky above them, how We built it and adorned it, with no gaps in it? And the earth We spread out, setting firm mountains upon it and bringing forth every lovely kind of plant—a sight to behold and a reminder for every servant who turns back to God.” He praises those who reflect on the creation of the heavens and earth, and condemns those who turn away from its signs: “How many signs there are in the heavens and the earth, yet they pass them by and turn away!” And He says: “Woe to every lying sinner who hears God’s signs recited to him, then persists in arrogance as though he had not heard them. So give him news of a painful punishment.”

We must therefore offer a few examples of the wisdom found in the physical world and of the harmony and fine order of its parts. From them we may learn something of the Maker’s greatness, the breadth of His mercy, the abundance of His generosity, and the subtlety of His care.

Think, then, you who are intelligent and attentive to God’s work and the signs in His Book. The Maker’s power to give goodness and mercy is without limit. His generosity cannot stop at some boundary, leaving the endless possibilities beyond it unrealised. That would be impossible. Yet the proofs show that an infinite number of beings cannot all come into existence together, all at once. They must come to be one after another. So God’s work must include a substance through which new and changing things can continue to come from Him, while His own being remains far above change. That substance must have an unlimited capacity to receive what comes from another, just as God, exalted be His name, has an unlimited power to act independently.[^V07-135]

Since new events arise as things become ready to receive them, this requires something that changes by nature and comes into being in itself. Its own changes and coming to be can then give rise to new events and to changes in what changes, linking these events to the timeless, unchanging Maker. Out of His generosity, He brought into being noble, luminous bodies[^V07-136] whose natures change and whose movements—of their own souls, by nature, and in relation to one another—never cease. Their movements serve higher spiritual purposes and wise intellectual ends. These ends bring forth physical lights and visible forms, which prepare matter to receive an endless variety of forms. In this way, an agent with unlimited power to affect things gives its gifts to a receiver with unlimited capacity to receive them, so that goodness may continue to flow and blessings may never cease. As God says: “If you tried to count God’s blessings, you could never number them.” The Sustainer, who knows the unseen, does not withhold His gifts. He gives to every prepared receiver according to its readiness. Even tiny ants, lizards, and geckos would receive rational souls if they were ready for them.

You have learned that the noblest beings connected with matter and bodies are rational souls. All the rational souls that could exist cannot become actual at once: not with bodies, for that would require bodies and dimensions without limit, and not without bodies, since souls first come into being through their connection with bodies. Matter has an endless capacity to become ready, through successive cycles and periods, to receive the flow of souls from the intellect separate from matter. Those souls that reach completion return to the intellectual world, their true home. Those that do not reach completion remain for a shorter or longer time in some level of the in-between world of images, according to how thick or thin the veil is and how many or few wrongs they have committed.

Now consider how the heavenly bodies are arranged for the benefit of earthly things. If all of them shone with light, their brightness would burn up the material of everything that comes to be. If none of them shone, the world below the heavens would be left in deep darkness, under a long, desolate night. If their lights stayed still or followed only one circular path, they would have too great an effect on some places and too little on others. If they had no swift movement, they would have the effects of stillness. If the stars did not have a shared swift motion as well as slower individual motions, and if the circles followed by the slower movements did not tilt away from the path of the swift movement, their influence would not spread north and south across the earth. Their benefits would not reach all its regions. And if the sun’s path did not differ in this way from the path of the swiftly moving heavens, there would be no four seasons. The seasons bring about generation and decay and bring the climates and regions of the earth into balance. The divine Book points to this[^V07-137] when it says: “Say: Have you considered? If God made the night last forever until the Day of Resurrection, what god besides God could bring you daylight?” Then it says: “Say: Have you considered? If God made the day last forever until the Day of Resurrection, what god besides God could bring you a night in which to rest? Will you not see?”

The moon acts as the sun’s deputy, helping ripen and break down things. So its path was made to differ from the sun’s, moving north and south across the seasons. In winter the sun is to the south and the moon to the north, so that neither of their functions is lost; in summer it is the other way around, so that their heating effects do not combine. Since the sun’s path moves north in summer and south in winter, its farthest point from the earth was placed in the north and its nearest point in the south. Its greater distance offsets the heat and light when it moves north, so they do not become too strong; its nearness offsets its southern position, so that its heating power does not become too weak. See how God gave the stars, along with their shared swift movement, other slower movements that carry them north and south along paths that differ in size, speed, and distance from the earth, depending on their farthest and nearest points. Their differing conditions serve created things and the order of the world. God says: “The sun, the moon, and the stars are made to serve by His command”—that is, they follow the paths He has set for them, rising and setting, sometimes moving north and sometimes south.

At times the stars are at their farthest points, at times their nearest; they also appear to move backwards, pause, or move forward, and they occupy their own stations, where they are said to be at home, in decline, exalted, or fallen. Their positions also depend on whether the constellations they enter are fixed, changing, or two-bodied, and on other conditions described in outline in books of astrology. Only God, exalted be His name, and His angels brought near to Him—the intellectual lights and divine rays—know all their details.

Have you not considered the kingdom of the heavens, the spirits of the spheres, and the souls of the stars: how their being is sustained, their light shines, they obey their Maker, and they keep moving without rest, out of love and longing for Him? Consider, too, the wonders of wisdom and the subtlety of care in the arrangement of the elements. Earth is at the center of all things, where its density and darkness suit its distance from the source of light. If it were beside the heavenly bodies, it would burn from the heat of their constant motion. And even if it remained there, no animal could live on it and no plant could grow from it. Fire, by contrast, is placed beside the sphere because its lightness and brightness suit that position. If another element were placed there, it would be heated by the sphere’s continual motion until it became fire; then the heat of fire would be added to it, burning the other elements until everything became fire and all life was destroyed.

From the beginning, God’s care called for rational and non-rational souls to exist. They needed animal and plant bodies, most of whose material is dry. Dryness helps preserve forms and shapes. So the earth had to be cold and dry, with parts that hold together, so animals and plants—whose bodies contain much of this element—could live beside it. For the same wise reason, water does not completely surround the earth: most animals and plants need to breathe air and draw it in. Air is placed beside fire because they share heat and lightness; water lies between air and earth because it shares lightness with air and coldness with earth, preserving the balance among them.

Consider also this: if the layers of elements and spheres above the earth were opaque and colored, rather than clear, the light of the stars would stop at their surfaces or be reflected upwards, away from the world of change and decay. Those living in the depths of matter would be left in a terrible darkness. So the stars were made bright, and the spheres and the other elements clear. If all the elements could receive light, their heat would grow so intense that everything in this world would burn.

#### Chapter — An example of His care in creating compound beings

Can you not see, seeker of knowledge, how God gave the elements heat that dissolves, refines, and sets things in motion; cold that steadies and holds things together; moisture that can receive shapes and be divided; and dryness that preserves the shapes and order it receives? He formed living things from mixtures of the elements, gave each kind of perfection a fitting balance of qualities, and granted every prepared thing the perfection it was ready to receive. He did not stop there. He also gave each thing guidance toward further perfections and ends, so that it could rise along the upward path to higher levels. This is what our Lord means when He says: “Our Lord is the One who gave everything its form, then guided it.”

Consider the bodies of plants and animals. They are always breaking down, so God gave them a power to take in food, digest it, and change it into a substance like their own. Since plants and animals cannot reach their full size in an instant, He also placed in them a power of growth. It increases the size of the nourished body in each direction according to the proportions suited to it.

Rejoice, seeker of knowledge, and remember your Lord’s mercy. Praise Him for how the generosity of the true Giver completes what would otherwise be missing from lasting existence. Some things last as individuals, while others last only as kinds.[^V07-138] He makes up for the short life of each individual by preserving its kind through a succession of individuals. In this way, the natural world is ordered through both kinds of endurance. Consider plants and animals, which generally continue only by reproducing. God placed a power of generation in their reproductive organs. It takes what is left over from the material that formed one individual and uses it to begin another, so that the kind continues even though the individual must perish.

Consider how God created a desire for food and the pleasure of sex in beings whose actions are voluntary rather than purely natural or forced. Such a being is like a worker who will not do the work without being paid. The desires that move an animal’s soul to perform the actions needed to preserve its own life and its kind are like wages for a worker or payment to servants and helpers. They prevent the animal from neglecting the purpose of these actions.

Consider, too, how the power that takes in food has four powers to help it: one draws food in; another breaks it down and prepares it for the body to use; a third holds it in place while it is being changed; and a fourth removes what cannot be made like the body. Animals are nobler than plants, so they have further powers: the ability to perceive and the ability to move. The animal soul senses and chooses how to move its body.

The human body has a finer balance of qualities. God gave it a spiritual reality: when it obeys its Lord and is completed through knowledge and action, it rises into the world of spirits, draws near to its source by cutting its ties to the bodily world, and becomes like the highest company of angels and the people of the world of power. If you think carefully about how the soul governs the body, how they become attached to one another, how the soul longs to act through the body, and how painful their separation is—even though the body is heavy and dense while the soul is subtle and light—you will be astonished.[^V07-139] How can light and darkness, what is free from matter and what is material, be joined? How can the higher realm, which God honors by saying, “We raised him to a high place,” and, “The record of the righteous is in the highest place,” be joined to the lower realm, which He describes by saying, “The record of the wicked is in a prison”? The difference between them is clear to anyone who reflects.

Most people—and perhaps everyone except a very few—are unaware of, or even deny, this connection and union between soul and body. Even many who claim to be learned deny that the soul exists apart from the body. Most who do believe that it does imagine that a person’s humanity comes from another species-form impressed on the body, and that the two together make a human being. They take the “I”—the rational soul—to be something else, merely attached to the body because it is the tool of the soul’s bodily actions. In our view, they have not understood the soul or its stages of descent and ascent. As we explained earlier, the soul is the same being that takes nourishment, grows, moves, rests, eats, drinks, touches, smells, and tastes.

See the Maker’s wisdom: He makes what is subtle dense[^V07-140]—or rather, through His wise care, makes what is dense subtle. From the finest part of the elements He formed the dense body from which the human being is made, beginning with the substance of semen. From its finest part and cream He formed the cone-shaped heart, and from the finest part of the blood He formed the pure blood.[^V07-141]

From the purest part of that blood comes the vital spirit, a fine vapour that flows from the body’s chief organ. In its clarity and balance,[^V07-142] it is like a distant sphere, far removed from conflict. It is balanced between opposites while gathering qualities from both ends, like the seven firm heavens. Its brightness is like the stars that shine before our eyes. Because it is clear and smooth, it becomes a mirror in which the forms of real things shine and sensory images appear. These images stand between the two worlds. In a way, this substance is the soul’s home and is united with it when its own life-bearing substance rises from the power of the soul to the level of intellect. The soul, in turn, is the material for intellect and is united with it when its own being rises from potentiality to actuality. The vital spirit is a mirror in which the soul appears, and the soul is a mirror in which intellect appears.

The human being passes through these stages in its own being, changing little by little from semen to intellect. It brings together the two worlds, the visible and the spiritual. In it there is something like a sphere, a mirror for perceiving individual things, both present and absent; and something like an angel, a mirror for perceiving universal truths that do not change. Consider this power and wisdom, then this subtlety and mercy. The wonders of God’s presence will leave you amazed. If you want a fuller explanation, listen.

#### Chapter — Signs of God’s wisdom and care in creating the human being

Human beings begin as the lowliest things, made from the least complete material and the weakest bodies. They begin as matter that has the capacity to become something but is not yet any definite thing, then come to be from dark dust and clinging clay, and then from semen. The divine Book points to the first stage when it says: “Has there not been a time when the human being was nothing worth mentioning?” It points to the next stages when it says: “We created the human being from a mixed drop to test him,” and, “He is the One who created you from dust, then from a drop.”

After these stages, the human being develops the powers and organs of plant life. Then comes animal life, as God says: “We made him hearing and seeing.” The human being gradually acquires all the powers of animal life, beginning with touch and ending with the power that makes judgments about particular things. Then another creation begins, as God says: “Then We brought him into being in another form. Blessed is God, the best of creators.”

Look, you who study the signs within and around you, at the semen: a dirty, watery fluid whose balance would break down if it were left for even an hour. See how the Lord of lords brings it forth from between the backbone and the ribs, protects it from dispersing, and places it in a secure resting place. He changes it from a white fluid into a red clot, then into a lump of flesh. He divides its similar parts into bones, nerves, veins, tendons, flesh, and other simple organs. Then He joins those simple organs into more complex ones—the head, hands, feet, and other parts—and shapes each for the work it is meant to do, as the principles of anatomy and other sciences make clear. He creates all this in the womb, within three layers of darkness.[^V07-143] If the veil were lifted and your sight could reach inside, you would see lines and shapes gradually appear, though you would see no tool at work. Glory be to the One who shapes, without any tool or finger visible in His shaping.[^V07-144]

When the growing body reaches the stage where it draws in food and grows, God gives it plant life, with many powers to serve it. Angels are appointed to these powers,[^V07-145] and through them the body is nourished and grows. See how God appoints angels to keep your affairs in order—even your nourishment. Those who see with the inner sight of their hearts say that each part of the body is nourished only with the help of at least seven angels, perhaps ten, a hundred, a thousand, or more.[^V07-146] Nourishment means that a part of food takes the place of a part of the body that has worn away. To do so, the food changes through several stages, until at last it becomes blood, then flesh and bone.

Food is a body. It does not move or change by itself through all these stages. Wheat does not become flour, then sifted flour, then dough, then a round loaf baked in an oven without the work of makers. In the hidden world, when a work serves a wise purpose, its makers are angels, just as human beings are the makers of visible things. God has given you His visible and hidden blessings, though people often overlook the hidden ones.

A person of insight knows that seven angels are needed. The first draws food to the organs. The second holds it beside the organ. The third strips away its former shape and changes it into blood. The fourth gives it the form of flesh, bone, and other tissues. The fifth removes what is left over. The sixth joins new flesh to flesh and bone to bone, so nothing remains detached. The seventh oversees the amounts and proportions as the parts are joined.[^V07-147]

You might ask: why not assign all these tasks to one angel? Why are seven needed? Wheat also needs one person to grind it, another to sift it, a third to add water, a fourth to knead it, a fifth to divide it into round pieces, a sixth to roll them into flat loaves, and a seventh to place them against the oven. Why do the angels’ hidden tasks not work like the visible tasks done by people?

The answer is that angels are made differently from human beings. Their being is simple and has no mixture of parts, and their actions do not conflict with one another. Their qualities and beings do not compete, and each angel has only one task. God points to this when He says: “There is not one of us who does not have a known place.” So they do not quarrel or struggle, and their actions and qualities do not conflict. Their order is like the order of our senses: sight does not compete with hearing, whose task is to perceive sounds; smell does not compete with sight or hearing in perceiving scents; taste and the other senses do not interfere with one another either.

The senses are unlike the body’s limbs. The toes can grasp weakly; some people write with their toes, and others walk on their hands. You might strike someone with your head, making it compete with the hand, which is the usual tool for striking. Nor are angels like a person who grinds, kneads, and bakes the bread alone. Such a person’s work is disordered and falls short of what is right because human qualities and motives differ. A human being does not have one single way of acting: sometimes people disobey God, and sometimes they obey Him. Angels cannot do this. They are made to obey, with no possibility of sin: “They do not disobey God in what He commands them, and they do what they are commanded.” They praise Him night and day without tiring. The angel who bows is always bowing; the one who prostrates is always prostrating; the one who stands is always standing.

Here is a subtle point. Someone might say: if every angel is assigned one task and cannot move on to another, then it is limited and unable to do many good things. The answer is that every angel’s being is connected to the being of the angel above it, which sustains it and gives it life[^V07-148]—not by joining together like bodily parts, but in another way. Each angel is one in being and one in its task, yet many different actions can issue from it. They follow one another in an ordered sequence that never changes. You have learned that order gathers many things into one. So the angels’ actions rise toward a single action from which their many effects branch out. In the same way, their many beings return to one being with which they are united. Each angel lives and knows through the life and knowledge of the angel below its command and care. This is unlike our body parts, which are unaware of themselves and of what they do.

The angels stand in relation to those under their command as our powers of perception stand in relation to the rational spirit within us. As we have explained, each sense and each image present to the soul is alive through the soul’s life. Yet each sense has its own task, distinct from the others, with no mixing or confusion. The senses come from the higher, active side of the soul, not from the receptive side, like the limbs and similar parts.

Let us return to our subject. Plant life, together with the helpers God created for nourishment and reproduction, makes a human being more complete than stones, crystals, iron, copper, gold, silver, and other minerals. But plant life is still incomplete. Food may not reach a plant or touch its roots, and the plant may dry out because it cannot seek food or move from one place to another. Seeking requires two things—knowledge and power—and plants lack them. If human existence stopped at this level, it would be incomplete and helpless in what it could do. See how God raised the human being beyond this stage by giving it another form of life, setting it apart from plants and lifting it above lifeless things. In one more step, He brought it nearer to the Lord of lords and the source of light, away from the depths of darkness. He gave it the tools of sensation and voluntary movement, so it could seek food. The Book points to this: “We made him hearing and seeing,” and, “We showed him the way, whether he is grateful or ungrateful.”

Now consider God’s wise arrangement of the five senses. Touch comes first. It is the least complete sense, since it detects only what touches or is joined to the animal, not what is far away. Sensing distant things requires a stronger, more complete sense. Touch is found in all animals. Some lowly animals, close to plant life, such as worms in the soil, have only touch and cannot seek food. If you had been given no sense beyond touch, you would be incomplete like a worm. You therefore needed another sense to detect what is far away, and God gave you smell. But smell does not tell you which direction a scent comes from, so you might wander around for a long time before finding its source.

Then God gave you sight. But sight cannot detect what is hidden behind a wall or another barrier, and it detects only what is present before you. You cannot learn about what is absent unless someone tells you about it. You receive that report through speech and signs heard by the sense of hearing. So your need for hearing became great. See how God blessed you and set you apart from other animals by giving you the ability to understand speech. Yet all this would not help you if you had no sense of taste. When food reaches you, you would not know whether it suited you or harmed you. You might eat it and die, like a tree that draws in water that dries it out because it has no taste to warn it.

Even all this would not complete human life without an inner power that receives the forms of the five senses, preserves them, and recalls them. Otherwise, you would have to learn every sensible thing again whenever you encountered it, because its image would not be stored within you. So God gave you other inward senses to preserve what you perceive, bring it back to mind when you wish, and work with it. Animals share these powers with you. If you had no more than these, you would still be incomplete, because you could not understand the outcomes and rational purposes of things. God distinguished and honored you above many of His creatures by giving you another power: reason. It can recognise what is harmful and what is useful in the long term, and grasp true goods and future harms.

This is one example of God’s gifts to you in the realm of perception. As for the other group of powers—the powers that move you, which are also among God’s forces—and how they are arranged within you, consider this. If you could perceive the things needed to develop and preserve your life in this world, including food, whether near or far, but had no natural inclination, appetite, or longing to move you towards them, and no bodily ability to act, then your perceptions would be useless.

You therefore need an inclination toward what suits you, called appetite, and an aversion to what conflicts with you, called dislike. God placed in you an appetite for food to preserve your individual life and a desire for sex to preserve the human kind. These desires press you to eat and act like a claimant demanding payment.

If appetite did not subside once you had enough of what you wanted, you would overeat and destroy yourself. So God gave you dislike when you are full, and when you have enough in similar ways, so that you stop eating and doing other things. A plant, by contrast, keeps drawing in water as it flows to its roots until it is ruined. It needs a human being to give it only as much food as it requires.

You have also learned that you have a natural power, shared with plants, that spreads through the whole body and begins in the liver; another power, shared with animals, that spreads through the organs[^V07-149] and begins in the heart; another, more specialised power of the soul, shared with some animals and beginning in the brain; and a still higher power, which animals do not share—only angels do. Each of these gives you a way to approach or withdraw from what benefits or harms you. The two responses belonging to the first power are called attraction and repulsion; those belonging to the second are inclination and aversion; those belonging to the third are desire and anger; and those belonging to the fourth are will and avoidance.[^V07-150]

God made the last pair answer to reason, which recognises the consequences of actions. He made desire and anger answer to sensory perception. This allows you to make use of reason. Simply knowing that something will harm you or benefit you is not enough to make you avoid or seek it. You also need a will that follows what you know, or an aversion that turns you away from it. Through this will, God set you apart from animals, honoring human beings, just as He set you apart from them through your ability to understand consequences.

#### Chapter — God’s care in creating the earth and what it contains for human benefit

God says: “He created for you everything on earth.” Now that you have seen God’s care in creating you and your body, look at the signs of His mercy in what lies outside you. The nearest thing outside you is the ground on which you rest. Consider the earth, the home of your body and the mother of the body that was first within her, then born from her, and will return to her again. God says: “From it We created you, to it We will return you, and from it We will bring you out once more.” Then consider its benefits.

The earth is like a bed and resting place on which you can sleep, and a surface on which you can walk, as God says: “He made the earth a bed,” and, “God made the earth spread out for you, so that you may travel along its wide paths.” A bed does not have to be flat. A very large sphere can also provide a good surface for rest and walking. But for that to be possible, the earth must remain in its natural place, at the center of the heavenly spheres. God points to this when He says: “God is the One who made the earth a place of rest.” Heavy things naturally move downwards, while light things move upwards. “Up” on every side is toward the sky, and “down” is toward the center.

Another of God’s gifts is that He did not make the earth as hard as iron or stone, so that food could grow from it, wells could be dug, and rivers could flow. Nor did He make it as soft and yielding as water, so that we could sleep and walk on it, farm it, and build with it. He did not make it so fine and transparent that light would pass through it and it could not be walked on. He also let part of it rise above the water, even though its natural tendency is to sink into it, so that we and the land animals could live there. One reason for the exposed land is that God made the earth and water, through the blowing of winds, the flow of rivers, and the movement of seas, not perfectly round. When water settles into the lower parts, some land remains uncovered, and the earth and its surrounding water together form a sphere.[^V07-151]

We know that the earth is round because stars rise and set earlier for people in the east than for people in the west. From north to south, the visible pole rises higher for those traveling north, and the reverse happens for those traveling south.[^V07-152] Travelers moving along paths between these directions see both effects. There are other signs of the earth’s roundness too, including the way eclipses begin and end at different times in different places. A particular eclipse begins at the start of night in one place, an hour later in another, two hours later in another, and after a quarter, a third, or half the night elsewhere. These differences follow a measured pattern based on how far apart the places are, both east to west and north to south. The same is true for people traveling over land and by sea.

The earth also brings forth minerals, plants, and animals. It draws down water from the sky, as God says: “We sent water down from the sky in due measure and let it settle in the earth.” It has springs and great rivers, as He says: “We spread out the earth.” It opens up to plants and other growth: “By the earth that splits open.” Its life and death remind people of their own death and resurrection. God says: “A sign for them is the dead earth; We bring it to life,” and, “We bring the earth to life after its death. That is how the dead will be raised.” He also says: “Look at the signs of God’s mercy: how He brings the earth to life after its death. He will surely bring the dead to life; He has power over all things.” And: “Among His signs is that you see the earth lying still; then, when We send water down on it, it stirs and swells. The One who brought it to life will surely bring the dead to life. He has power over all things.”

The earth also produces many kinds of animals and spreads every sort of living creature across it. Some are food; others are livestock, whose wool and hides keep you warm and whose other benefits serve you. God says: “He created livestock for you. In them you have warmth and many benefits, and from them you eat.” Some animals are for riding and adornment: “Horses, mules, and donkeys, for you to ride and as an adornment.” Some carry heavy loads to places you could reach only with great effort: “They carry your loads to a land you could not reach without great hardship. Your Lord is kind and merciful.” Some bring comfort and beauty, when they come home in the evening and go out to pasture in the morning. Some are created as mates: “God made spouses for you from among yourselves.” Others provide clothing, shelter, and household goods: “He made homes for you from the hides of livestock, easy to carry on the day you travel and the day you settle; and from their wool, fur, and hair, furnishings and useful things for a time.”[^V07-153]

The earth also grows plants of many kinds: “We made every lovely kind of plant grow in it.” Their different colors are a sign and a mercy; their different scents offer guidance and care. Some plants feed people and others feed animals: “Eat and graze your livestock.” They are a provision for you and your animals. From plants come food and seasonings, medicines, and fruits: “He grows grain, olives, date palms, grapevines, and every kind of fruit.” Plants also provide clothing: cotton and flax, and, from animals that live on plants, hair, wool, silk, and leather.

The earth yields stones of many colors and degrees of clarity. God says: “Among the mountains are white and red paths, of different colors, and others intensely black.” Some stones are used for adornment; others for buildings. Compare the common stone that gives us fire with the rare red ruby. The common stone is used far more, while the rare gem is used little. The earth also holds precious metals such as gold and silver. Human beings have developed delicate crafts and great industries, brought fish up from the bottom of the sea, and drawn birds down from the heights of the air. Yet they cannot make gold and silver. The reason is that their chief use is as money, and that use depends on their being rare and hard to obtain. If people could make them freely, their purpose would be lost. That is why God has closed the door to making them. As the saying goes, whoever seeks wealth through alchemy ends up poor.

The earth is also generous by nature: it takes one seed and returns seven hundred, as the Qur'an says. People have disagreed, based on reports, about whether heaven or earth is nobler. Some say heaven is nobler because it is the place where angels worship and no part of it has disobeyed God; its inhabitants are protected from error and disobedience. When our father Adam sinned, he was sent down from the garden. God says: “He does not let those who disobey Me remain in My presence.” He calls heaven a protected roof and praises its constellations in many verses. Others say the earth is nobler because God has called certain places blessed: “The first House established for people was the one at Bakkah, blessed,” and, “Glory be to the One who took His servant by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque, whose surroundings We have blessed.” He also says: “The east and west of the earth, which We blessed,” meaning the land of Syria. He describes the whole earth as blessed: “He placed blessings in it and measured its provisions.” Someone might ask: what blessing is there in empty, deadly deserts? We reply that they are homes to creatures known only to God. This is why He says: “On earth are signs for those who are certain.”

The truth is that heaven is adorned with stars, the stars with spiritual angels, and the earth with forms and animal souls. The earth’s adornment includes human spirits, which make use of its resources, and their adornment is their divine knowledge, which they share with the angels brought near to God. Heaven’s outward aspect is nobler than earth’s, but inwardly the two are the other way around, because the end is nobler than what leads to it.[^V07-154] The earth is also like a gift and a pearl containing Adam and his children. God taught Adam what they would need and said, in effect: “I will not leave you in need of anything beyond this earth, which is like a mother to you.” He says: “We poured down water in abundance, then split the earth open,” and: “He sent water down from the sky and brought forth fruits as provision for you. My servant, the things you value most are gold and silver. If I had made the earth out of them, could it have provided these benefits? I made these things available for you in this world, though this world is a prison for you. What, then, will it be like in the garden?”

#### Chapter — The wonders of God’s work in the celestial bodies and the lights of the stars

Now that you have seen an example of the earth’s benefits, raise your head to the sky. Look and reflect on how the heavens were made, on their stars, and on their ordered movements, their rising and setting, and their different points of sunrise and sunset. The sun, moon, Jupiter, and Saturn move constantly, seeking God and longing to meet Him without weariness, delay, or change in their courses. They travel through measured stations, according to a precise reckoning and a written record, until their appointed time.[^V07-155] God says: “We have set the moon through stages.” It neither goes beyond them nor falls short, until God folds them up like a scroll.[^V07-156]

Consider the number of stars, how many bright ones there are, and how their colors differ. Their colors are not like the changing, decaying colors we see on earth. Their shapes are of a higher kind, as are their tastes, scents, and sounds.[^V07-157] They descend from the heavens to the earth, so there they are more subtle, pure, and simple. Every form on earth has a higher likeness in heaven. God has many purposes for each star: in its creation, form, size, shape, position, relation to other stars, and distance from the center of the sky. Compare the wisdom shown in these arrangements with the care given to the organs of your small body. The heavens are incomparably greater than a human being. God says: “Are you harder to create, or the heaven? He built it.” Even within the earthly world, compare the differences in size and subtlety between things with the differences in their wonderful arrangement, fine order, and remarkable natural forms.

Have you not considered the kingdom of the heavens and the stars within them, how their being is sustained, their light shines, and they obey their Maker through their movements, out of love and longing for Him? Do you not hear how God speaks of their greatness and honors the stars in His Book? Many chapters describe the greatness and glory of heaven, and God swears by it: “By the sun and its brightness,” “By the moon as it follows it,” “By the star when it falls,” and, “I swear by the positions of the stars—if you only knew, that is a tremendous oath.”

God praises those who reflect on the heavens and earth: “They reflect on the creation of the heavens and earth.” He condemns those who turn away from them: “They turn away from its signs.” He made the heavens a protected roof, raised high above us, and kept devils from entering because heaven is a place of worship and purity, inhabited by angels who praise and glorify God. Defiled souls stained by disbelief and idolatry cannot enter its sacred place, just as people stained by idolatry are not allowed to enter the Sacred Mosque on earth. God says: “The idolaters are impure, so they must not approach the Sacred Mosque.”[^V07-158]

Look into heaven’s spiritual realm and you will see wonders of might and power. Let your thought travel far through the visible heavens. Perhaps its gates will open to you and you will enter the garden, leaving this dark pit for the world of light. Let your heart travel through its regions until you stand before the Throne of the All-Merciful. Then we may hope that you will reach the highest rank—but only after passing beyond the levels of this lower world. The signs of heaven’s nobility are too many to count, as are its benefits for human beings.

Among them is the wise purpose and sound end in its creation. God says: “Our Lord, You did not create this without purpose,” and, “We did not create the heaven, the earth, and all that lies between them in vain. That is what those who disbelieve suppose.” He adorned it with lamps: “We adorned the nearest heaven with lamps.” He placed the moon among them as a light and the sun as a lamp. He called heaven a protected roof, seven layers, and seven strong heavens. He made it a path by which good actions rise and good words ascend—the perfected souls, complete in knowledge and action. God says: “A good word is like a good tree, whose roots are firm and whose branches reach the sky,” and, “Good words rise to Him, and He raises up righteous deeds.”[^V07-159][^V07-160]

Heaven is also the direction toward which prayers rise and the source of light and radiance. God brought lights and colors down from it into the earthly world. Its colors, shapes, sounds, melodies, and other qualities are the finest, and its places are the highest because they are far from opposition and decay. They benefit God’s servants. Its stars are signs that guide travelers across the darkness of land and sea. The sun rises at a time that lets people go about their needs in every region, and sets at a time that brings calm and quiet, so that they may rest, digest food, and let it reach their organs, or escape their enemies. God says: “We made the night a covering,” “We made the day for work,” and, “He made the night a time of rest.”

If the sun never rose, the waters would freeze, cold and density would take over, and the body’s natural warmth would weaken and die. If it never set, the earth would grow so hot that every animal on it would burn. The sun is like a lamp brought into a house for as long as its inhabitants need it, then taken away so they can settle down and rest. Light and darkness, though opposite, work together for the well-being of those who live on earth.

It is as though God were saying: if the sun stayed on one side of the sky, north or south, a rich person might build a house on a hill above a poor person’s home and block the light. But I move the heavens and guide the sun so that the poor receive their share of daylight just as the rich do.

As we have seen, the sun’s height and descent bring about the four seasons. In winter, heat retreats into trees and plants, where food and fruit take shape, and animals grow stronger because their natural warmth gathers inside them. In spring, nature stirs, the substances formed in winter come forth, trees grow bright with leaves and flowers, and animals are driven to reproduce. In summer, the air grows hot, fruits ripen, excess moisture leaves the body, and the earth dries, ready for buildings and farming. In autumn, cold and dryness set in, fruits mature, and bodies gradually prepare for winter.

The moon follows the sun and acts as its deputy. It helps us count years, measure time, and set the religious times for prayer,[^V07-161] fasting, and pilgrimage. It also helps plants grow and thrive. Its rising and setting, and its changing shapes from crescent to full moon, serve many purposes known only to God.

Al-Jāḥiẓ said: “If you reflect on the world we live in, you will find that it is like a house equipped with everything needed. The sky is raised like a roof, the earth spread out like a carpet, the stars set in place like lamps, and the human being is like the owner who manages the house. All kinds of plants are prepared for human use, and all kinds of animals are put to work for human benefit.” I would add that if you look at the heavenly world, with its vastness and countless stars, you will find an inhabited house,[^V07-162] one of those houses that God has permitted to be raised, where His name is remembered. It contains many kinds of worshippers: some remain prostrate and never bow; others remain bowed and never stand upright.[^V07-163] They praise God without weariness. Their eyes do not sleep, their bodies do not tire, and forgetfulness does not overcome them.

A house need not be made of physical things in order to be a home. God says: “The home of the next life is truly life.” Nor must a place of worship be built from clay, stone, and wood. God says: “Only those who believe in God and the Last Day, and keep up prayer, maintain God’s mosques.” A place of worship need not even be a physical building. Wherever worship, remembrance, praise, and sanctification take place, there is a house of worship.

Consider how the Maker, exalted be His name, built the heavens as a place of worship for angels who praise, glorify, and remember God. He holds them up without pillars that you can see, and without a rope or support hanging them in place. It is astonishing that someone can fail to look at or reflect on a house that God built by His power, made and adorned by Himself, and filled with all kinds of decoration and images.[^V07-164] Forgetting himself and failing to be present in heart, he forgets his Lord and thinks only of food, sex, status, and reputation. It is astonishing that he can enter a rich person’s house, see its painted and gilded walls, and never stop marvelling at it or praising the one who built and decorated it—yet remain unaware of God’s great house and of the angels who live in His heavens.

He gives heaven no thought. He knows no more about it than an animal knows about the roof above it, or an ant knows about the ceiling of its nest. He knows no more about the angels of heaven or their wondrous forms than an ant knows about the minds of those in its home or the patterns on its walls. How deep is this carelessness,[^V07-165] this sleep that overwhelms most people, this intoxication, this great ignorance, this misery that surrounds them! Come, seeker of knowledge, let us praise and sanctify our Lord. Come, seeker of truth, let us proclaim His oneness and greatness. Let us call upon the One who sustains the world, orders the seen and unseen realms, joins the human world to the divine, and possesses all majesty and power—with grieving hearts, present minds, remembering tongues, chests opened by the light of knowledge, and flowing tears. Hurry, seeker of knowledge, with an eager spirit and a pure soul, in love with its Lord and returning to Him. Let us call to Him quietly and plead with Him in the darkness of night. Let us pray as lovers who sigh, as the humble who bow, and as those who long for Him and know their need. Glory be to You; there is no god but You. Save us from the fire of separation and the pain of being cut off. You bring the dead to life and raise spirits from their worn-out graves. You give good things and send down blessings. Pour the gleams of Your blessing upon our souls and the bright rays of Your goodness upon our spirits. You are the One whose presence makes all things bow; You sprinkle Your light upon dark beings and shadowed bodies in the deepest night, giving them light and form, stirring in them longing for Your beauty and leading them to seek Your presence.[^V07-166] You cast the spark of Your longing upon the heavens, setting them in motion and making them circle. You set all who are in them to move in love, leading them to circle the Ancient House once every seven thousand years.[^V07-167] Free us by Your light from the darkness of the grave.

Do not torment our souls with their ties to the bodies of this deceptive world, the source of delusion. Keep our feet firm on the bright and straight path, the true and upright way. You are generous and kind, the giver of goodness and mercy.

This is one example of God’s subtle care and kindness in the visible world. No one can hope to know every detail of the mercy and care hidden in His unseen realm, or to explore every wonder of His work and wisdom in the beings around us, even over a long lifetime. The knowledge of the learned is slight compared with what the prophets and saints know; their knowledge is slight beside that of the angels brought near to God, and of the highest people among humankind when they stand before Him. When all the knowledge of angels, spirits, and human beings is compared with God’s knowledge, it hardly deserves to be called knowledge. It is closer to bewilderment, confusion, helplessness, and limitation than to understanding and wisdom. True wisdom is knowing things as they really are.
