As we have already explained, to know a thing fully is to have its very being. Only its Maker and source can encompass it. No one is truly wise except God alone. Wisdom and knowledge are applied to others only by comparison and metaphor. That is why everyone is addressed by God’s words: “You have been given only a little knowledge.”

#### Chapter — All beings love God, long to meet Him, and seek His home of honor

Know that God has given every being—intellectual, spiritual, sensory, and natural—its own perfection. He has placed in each one love[^V07-168] and longing for that perfection, and a movement toward completing it. Love without longing belongs to intellects that exist apart from matter and are fully actual in every way. Other beings lack some perfection and have the potential and readiness to gain it. They therefore have love and longing, either by choice or by nature, in different degrees. A movement then follows, matching that inclination. It may be a movement of the soul or of the body. Bodily movement may be a change in quality, as in natural compounds; a change in size, especially in animals and plants;[^V07-169] a change in position, as in the heavenly bodies; or a change of place, as in the elements.

Here is the proof. You have learned that all existence is good, valuable, and pleasant, while its opposite, non-existence, is evil, repellent, and something we flee from.[^V07-170] Existence is one simple reality in itself. It differs only through limits and levels, which give rise to the different kinds and classes of possible things. Existence varies only in being more or less complete, strong, or weak. Its highest perfection is the Necessary Being, the Real, whose perfection has no limit. Every caused being receives a share of that perfection from Him, and has a limit because it is caused. Existence itself, simply as existence, does not require lack, limitation, or change. If it did, the Necessary Being could not have limitless power and perfection. Lack and limitation therefore belong to caused beings. An effect cannot equal its cause in rank; if it did, neither would be the cause rather than the other.

The one who is not caused by anything else—God, the Necessary Being—has no lack. He is pure existence and pure good, and so the greatest source of joy and love. He loves Himself and all the good things that belong to Him. The more a being depends on causes and intermediaries between itself and the pure Real, the less complete it is. The closer it is to God, the more complete it is. So every being has some share of divine love, divine care, and God’s love for it. If it were without this for even a moment, it would vanish and perish. Every being loves existence, seeks to complete its existence, and turns away from non-existence and lack.

Whatever is sought can be preserved and continued only through what completes it. An effect lasts only through its cause, because the cause completes it. Heat lasts only through stronger heat, and light becomes complete only through stronger light. Uncertain, incomplete knowledge becomes complete when a proof grounded in reason turns it into lasting certainty.[^V07-171] In the same way, an incomplete being can become complete only through something stronger than itself: its cause. The cause sustains it and makes it what it is. Matter is completed only through form; form, only through what gives it form; sensation, only through the soul; the soul, only through intellect; and intellect lasts only through the Necessary Being. Every incomplete thing therefore turns away from its lack and reaches toward its perfection. Once it gains that perfection, it holds on to it. Every being must love its perfection and long for it when it lacks it. A being therefore always loves its perfection, whether it has it or is without it.

Longing and inclination arise only when a being lacks its perfection. Love, then, runs through all beings, while longing belongs only to those that can lack something. We have also explained more than once that life runs through all beings because existence runs through them. Existence is one reality, and it is the same as knowledge, power, and life. Just as nothing can exist without existence itself, nothing can exist without some kind of knowledge and action. Whatever knows and acts in any way is alive. So, in the view of those who know these things directly, everything is alive. Most people, however, use the word “animal” only for a being with outward senses and the ability to choose where it moves.

If every simple or compound being has some life and awareness, it must also have love and longing. Look at beings and their levels of existence. Each is either complete in every way or lacking in some way. A being complete in every way is complete either through itself or through something beyond itself. In the first case, the being and its perfection are one and the same, both in reality and in how we think of them. The first is the Necessary Being; the second is the intellectual beings. Neither kind is without love, and for both, love is their very being. The Necessary Being loves only Himself: He is beloved by Himself and by everything other than Himself. Intellectual beings love Him, not themselves except insofar as they are His effects. Their love for themselves is lost in their love for the First. Their light of existence is likewise swallowed up in the light of God’s oneness. In truth, there is one connected love with different levels, some surrounding others. God encompasses all that lies beyond them.

The second group—beings that are not complete in every way—either have what they need to rise from lack to perfection within themselves and their own cause, or they do not. The first are the souls of the heavenly spheres. They have perfection in one respect and lack it in another, so they love and long without end. They feel the pleasure of connection and the pain of separation. Their pleasure does not belong only to one time and their pain only to another. Instead, their pain is mixed with pleasure, and because it comes from the experience of love, it is a pleasurable pain. To describe these souls’ longing for intellectual perfection and spiritual beloveds, philosophers used a distant comparison: the state of someone who has an itch or is being tickled.[^V07-172]

The second group is either incomplete in every way or incomplete in one respect but not another. A being in the latter class may have no perfection beyond the basic nature of its body. These are the lowest bodily kinds, such as the four elements, whose own natures are limited and opposed to one another. Or it may have some added perfection beyond its bodily nature.

The first kind, because its own substance is incomplete, can be combined with other kinds. When combined, the elements’ opposition is reduced, and they receive another kind of life. This life preserves their structure for a time, keeping one opposite from destroying the others. If the form that preserves them from destruction by opposites is their only perfection, the result is an inanimate thing. If it also seeks individual growth and the continuation of its kind through reproduction, it is a plant. If it goes further and receives forms that are not material in themselves, while its senses and tools draw those forms from material things, it is an animal. Animals gain strength through sensation and imagination, just as plants gain strength through nourishment and growth.

Each of these incomplete beings clearly seeks the perfection that completes its existence. The form that holds a compound body together becomes more complete when it gains the power to increase the individual’s size. It draws in food, changes it into a substance like its own, and joins it to the body in the right proportions. It also increases the number of individuals in its kind through reproduction.

Once it reaches this perfection and, with it, comes a little closer to the First, who is perfect in every way, it seeks a fuller perfection, one closer to true completeness. It becomes able to sense, then to imagine, then to remember. It becomes able to understand basic truths as a settled ability, then to grasp further truths through thought. It then becomes an intellect that is simple and fully active in understanding. At last, it returns to God, content and accepted by Him. These stages come through a series of longings and experiences: each love is a cause that leads toward the next beloved, which becomes the end of that earlier love. This continues until the being reaches the Beloved of all beloveds, the end of every pleasure and desire, and the final goal of all movement.

The first kind in the second group, which is incomplete in every way, is bodily matter. In itself, it is the potential for everything, and has no existence apart from the forms that enter it. Its very nature is need and dependence—in fact, a longing for every form of perfection that has become actual. The Shaykh, Ibn Sina, strongly denied in the natural-science section of *The Healing* that matter longs for forms, as earlier thinkers had said. Yet in his treatise on love and its spread through all beings, he admitted that it does. He explained this love by saying that matter is never without a form, but is always clothed in one. He explained the love of the simple elements and compounds of minerals by their natural movement to their proper places, and the love in plants by their powers of nourishment, growth, and reproduction. He did not claim that these beings have awareness or life.

To say that something loves without being alive or aware is only to give it a name.[^V07-173] In the First Journey, when discussing cause and effect, we proved that matter loves form. We also showed that life and awareness are present throughout all beings.[^V07-174] This is the key to the present discussion. Ibn Sina did not establish it, nor did anyone after him up to our own time, except the mystics who know by direct insight. Through their own experience and by following the light of the Book and the Prophet’s teachings, they saw that all things are alive, speak, remember God, praise Him, and bow before Him. The Qur'an says: “There is nothing that does not praise Him, though you do not understand their praise.” It also says: “To God bows everything in the heavens and the earth.” By God’s grace, we have come to know this through both proof and faith. This insight has been given to us through God’s favor and kind guidance.

What the Shaykh, one of the greatest philosophers of this Islamic age, managed to say about love in simple beings that he thought were lifeless is found in his treatise. He says that there are three such simple beings. The first is prime matter; the second is form, which cannot stand on its own; the third is an accident. Form differs from matter because it makes a substance what it is. For this reason, the early thinkers who believed in God kept form among the kinds of substance: it is part of substances that exist on their own. They did not deny that form is a kind of substance just because it cannot exist by itself. Prime matter is in the same position, yet it is not strange to count it among substances because it is part of substances that exist on their own.

Form also has a greater claim to being called a substance than matter does, because form makes a substance into an actual substance. Wherever form exists, an actual substance must exist too. So form has been called a substance in the active sense. Matter, on the other hand, is only able to receive substance. It is called a substance in the potential sense: the presence of matter does not mean an actual substance must be present. This explains what form really is. It should not be treated as an accident.

Once this is clear, we can say that each simple being thought to be lifeless is joined to a natural love that never leaves it and is a cause of its existence. As for matter, it is always reaching toward form. It lacks form in itself, but is made into different kinds of things through form.[^V07-175] When it is stripped of one form, it quickly receives another because it fears remaining in complete non-existence. Matter turns away from non-existence. When it has a form, only a relative absence remains in it; without form, it would be covered by complete non-existence. There is no need here to explain the cause of this in detail. Matter is like an unattractive woman afraid to reveal her ugliness: whenever she lifts her veil, she covers her face with makeup. This establishes a natural love in matter.

Natural love in form is clear for two reasons. First, form makes every effort to remain in the matter that supports it and resists being stripped away from it. Second, when it has reached its natural place, it tries to remain there; when it is moved away, it moves with longing back toward that place. This applies to the forms of the five simple bodies[^V07-176] and to compounds made from the four elements.

The only other forms are accidents. Their love is shown by their effort to remain in their subject, even as they follow one another when that subject changes.[^V07-177] So none of these simple beings is without a natural love. That is the Shaykh’s argument.

Anyone thoughtful can see how weak and incomplete it is. It does not prove that these simple beings love in any real sense; at most, it compares their behaviour to love. Ibn Sina’s account of matter’s love is only an image, not a proof. He has simply replaced other words with words that suit his claim, such as “striving,” “fearing,” and “hurrying.” His claim that matter hurries to exchange one form for another is also false. Matter is something that receives forms; it cannot act. It does not demand or seek anything. It only receives and obeys.

We have already shown, by the method we use to prove that love runs through all beings, that matter is itself a potential for longing and love. It is not something that already longs or loves a particular form. If it did, it would already be an actual form, not matter.

As for Ibn Sina’s account of the form’s love, he says that form strives to remain with its subject, matter, and to remain in its natural place once it reaches it. When it is moved away from that place, it moves back toward it with longing. But this does not prove the point by sound reasoning. It only uses words such as “striving” and “longing,” assuming what it is meant to prove. Simply existing in a subject does not show love for that subject. Nor do stillness in a place after arriving there, or movement back toward it after leaving, prove longing for it. That would require showing that bodily nature is aware of itself and its actions. Ibn Sina did not establish this in that treatise or elsewhere. The same applies to his claim that an accident loves its subject, which he calls obvious even though it is one of the least obvious things. If every necessary relation between two things were love, then every essential property, relation, and connection would love what it belongs to. So would surfaces and boundaries love the bodies whose dimensions they define. That conclusion is plainly false.[^V07-178]

Here is an important point. The thinkers who say love runs through all beings mean that each thing loves and longs for what it is, or for its own perfection. It does not love what is lower or less complete than itself, except indirectly. So Ibn Sina’s claim that form loves the matter that supports it is wrong.[^V07-179]

Whenever a nature, soul, or power acts, it does not first seek the action it performs or the things it acts on. It seeks something greater: its goal, or its own full perfection. Nature does not seek matter for its own sake; it makes matter a means to gain another perfection. Nature is itself a perfection of matter; matter is not a perfection of nature. Nature therefore takes up and shapes matter so that it can reach a higher perfection. In the same way, the soul does not govern the body and create bodily tools because it loves the body or the tools. It does so because it loves itself and longs for its own perfection.

In short, anyone following the mystics’ path must arrange the loves of beings in an order. The love of each lower being leads upward to love of a higher one, until all lead to love of the Necessary Being. Then all things will be seen to love Him and long to meet Him. The higher being must also love the lower one—not to gain something or become more complete, but as a result of its care and generosity toward it. Since nature completes the body, the body loves what completes it: nature. Since the governing soul completes nature, nature loves the soul. The soul is completed by intellect, so it loves intellect. Intellect is completed by God, the Necessary Being, so intellect loves Him. Through Him, the whole is complete. He is all things in their unity. He rejoices in Himself, not in anything else, for there is nothing other than Him[^V07-180] that could stand apart as an equal or partner. He is the Mighty, the Overpowering; He rules over His servants, and all things lie within His reach. In His love for Himself, His love for all things is gathered together, just as His knowledge of Himself includes His knowledge of all things. We explained earlier, in the discussion of knowledge, how this can be so without dividing His absolute oneness. All heavenly and earthly bodies are contained within the souls’ longing for themselves and their intellectual perfections.

The souls’ longing for themselves and their perfections is likewise contained within the intellects’ love for their highest source. And the intellects’ love for their highest source is contained within the splendour of the First Light, the fullest good, the greatest beauty, and the highest majesty, for He is the return and final goal of all things.

#### Chapter — Another way to show that love runs through all things

The early philosophers gave another general argument for this point. They said that just as all beings receive their existence from causes that give it to them, they also receive their perfections from those causes. These causes do not intend to create any particular effects or their perfections, and what is higher does not look down toward what is lower, or toward its perfection. So divine wisdom[^V07-181], care, good ordering, and the world’s fine arrangement require every being to have love within it. Through this love, it holds on to the perfections it has gained and longs to gain them when they are absent. This produces both the order of the whole and the careful arrangement of its parts.

This love must be a lasting feature of every being. If it could leave, another love would be needed to preserve the first love, which was supposed to preserve the being’s perfection while it had it and restore it when it lost it. If that second love could also leave, we would need another love to preserve it, and so on without end. If the second love could not leave, then one of the two loves would serve no purpose. That is impossible. So love must run through all beings and all their parts.

That is their argument. It is sound, but the one we have already offered is stronger, clearer, and gives a fuller account of why it must be so. We have shown that existence is one reality, but differs in how complete it is; that an effect belongs to the same order of reality as its cause; and that the cause completes the effect. We have also shown that existence is good and pleasant. Every being therefore loves itself and its own perfection. But its perfection is completed by its cause. Since its perfection comes from what is its cause and source, the cause—though it does not intend the effect and does not look toward it, being far above it—must love itself and desire its own being. That very being is what perfects and completes the effect. The effect follows from this completeness.[^V07-182]

So every effect is preserved through its love for its cause, and every lower being is ordered through its love for what is above it. If there were no such bond between higher and lower, beings could not endure, and the order could not remain so complete. Disorder and corruption would enter it. The world cannot be held together by each being’s love for itself alone. Nor is love for one’s own perfection enough unless that perfection comes through the being of its cause. To love its own completion is therefore also to love the cause that gives it being.

Nothing can preserve itself, just as nothing can bring itself into existence. Otherwise it would have to come before itself. Preservation means that a thing continues to exist. A possible being depends on a cause other than itself not only when it first comes to be, but also to keep existing. A thing does not preserve its own perfection; in fact, the reverse is true. Its perfection is what gives it its being, makes its matter into a particular thing, and defines its kind.

From all this, we learn that the love that preserves each effect is its love for its cause. This means that its existence is linked to and depends on that cause. Through this link—this dependent existence—the effect keeps its identity and continues through the source that gives it being.[^V07-183] This is what people mean when they say, “Without the higher being’s love, the lower would vanish.”

We have also learned that love runs through all beings in the same order as their existence. Just as each stronger level of existence gives rise to a weaker one, down to matter and bodies, each lower love gives rise to a higher love, until it reaches love of the Necessary Being. According to the perfections suited to them, all beings seek the perfection of the One who is perfect through Himself and resemble Him as they gain that perfection. God, whose names are holy, is the final goal of every being and the end of every level. Love and longing bring beings into existence with all the perfections they can have, and keep them in existence. Without love and longing, no event could occur in the bodily world and nothing could come to be in the world of generation and decay.

#### Chapter — The beloved is one in the end, but each kind has its own near beloved

We have already explained that all beings naturally turn toward the First Reality, in an ordered way. They love to reach Him and gain from His being, from the lowest level to the highest and from the least complete state to the noblest. Just as every being seeks the fullest good and loves the Real according to its own level, the fullest good—the true Beloved—reveals Himself to those who love Him. Each receives that revelation and the light of His beauty according to its capacity.

The closest approach is to receive His real self-disclosure without any intermediary, like seeing the beloved’s image directly, with no mirror between. This is the state of the First Effect, the Universal Intellect. What other beings receive of that beauty is like seeing the beloved in one mirror or in several mirrors, apart from the eye that sees them. Yet the beloved is still what appears in them, not the mirrors.

The mirror has no image of its own; it is empty.[^V07-184] The image belongs to the person, and it is the person who appears in the mirror. Many mirrors and many receivers make the image less clear, so it appears dimmer and has less effect on the one who receives it. That is how possible beings receive the self-disclosure of the First Reality and the light of God’s beauty and majesty. Nothing other than God has power to act, and nothing is acted upon by anything other than Him. His command is one, and His act is consistent. His self-disclosure is one, but different beings receive its effect in different degrees. The beings nearer to Him are lit by it more fully and clearly than those farther away.

Ibn Sina says in his treatise on love that every passive thing is affected by an active cause through an image of the cause that enters it. An active cause acts on a receiver in the same way, by placing its image within it. He supports this by examples: fire affects a body by giving it its own image, heat; the same applies to other qualities and powers. A rational soul acts on another rational soul by placing its own image within it, and so on.

I say that Ibn Sina did not find the proof we have given here. He relied on examples. He then says that the Active Intellect receives God’s self-disclosure directly, without an intermediary. It does so by knowing itself and the other intelligible things within it through its own active and lasting being.[^V07-185] Beings that grasp ideas without sight, the senses, or imagination understand later things through earlier ones, effects through causes, and lower things through nobler ones. The divine souls also receive this disclosure directly when they reach it.[^V07-186] The Active Intellect helps them move from possibility to fulfillment, gives them the power to imagine and hold ideas, and helps them settle in what they have understood. Below them come the animal powers, then the plant powers, and then the natural powers. Each level that receives something is stirred by it to become like it in its end. Natural bodies move naturally to imitate it by remaining in a certain state, as when they reach their natural places; they do not imitate it in the beginning of this end, which is movement. Plants and animals act in their own ways to imitate it in their ends: preserving an individual or a kind, or displaying power and ability. They do not imitate it in the beginnings of these actions, such as sexual union and feeding. Human souls perform intellectual actions and good deeds to imitate it in their ends, which are wisdom and justice, though they do not imitate it in the beginning of these ends, such as learning. Divine souls imitate it by preserving the world of generation and decay, its fields, and its offspring.[^V07-187] Animal and plant powers resemble the First Reality in the goals of their actions, but not in the conditions from which they begin. Those conditions are states of readiness and potentiality, and the fullest good is free from such states. The ends, by contrast, are actual perfections.[^V07-188] The First Cause has complete actuality, so beings may resemble it in their ends but not in the potential states from which those ends begin. That is where Ibn Sina’s argument ends.

God’s wisdom and care have joined every level of beings to every other and arranged them in a single intelligent order. As we have explained, some beings are effects of others, some first and some later. The effects are naturally drawn to and long for their causes, while causes are kind and generous toward their effects. We see the same bond between parents and children, adults and young people, and the strong and the weak. The weak need the strong to help them. The reason is that members of one kind feel drawn to one another, an effect is almost a part of its cause, and the cause completes its effect. The same is true of anyone in need and the one who can meet that need: the poor cling to and long for the rich, while the rich show them mercy and give from what they have.

Know that love has many kinds, and beloved things cannot be counted, as there are many kinds and individuals. One kind is the love animals feel for mating, through which their kind continues. Another is the love of leaders for their position: their desire to gain it, observe its customs, and hold on to it, as though this love were planted in their nature. Its purpose is for the soul to rise above the powers it governs.[^V07-189] Chosen actions imitate natural actions: a craftsperson follows the example of nature in making things. That is why people say that craft imitates nature. If the desire for rank and leadership were not rooted in the soul, most people would not want to be obeyed by their community or strive so hard to lead.

Another kind is the love of merchants and wealthy people for making money, their wish to gather and store it, and to keep cash and goods safe. This too seems built into their nature, because it benefits others and the people who come after them. Another kind is the love of scholars and wise people for discovering knowledge, writing it down, sharing it, investigating its hidden questions, uncovering its secrets, teaching it to learners, and making it clear to those ready to understand. This, too, seems built into them because it brings souls to life[^V07-190] and wakes them from the death of ignorance, the grave of nature, and the sleep of heedlessness and forgetfulness. Another kind is the love of craftspeople for showing their work, completing it, improving it, and making it beautiful. This seems natural to them because it benefits people and helps keep life in good order.

The higher and nobler a soul is, the more subtle, pure, beautiful, and splendid are the things it loves. Here is one example. The plant powers have three main functions: taking in nourishment, growing, and reproducing. So the love found in plant life has three forms.[^V07-191] The first belongs to the power that nourishes. It begins the plant’s longing for food when its material needs it, and for food to remain in the plant after it has changed into a substance like its own. The second belongs to the power of growth. It begins the plant’s longing to increase naturally and in the right proportions in all directions. The third belongs to the power of reproduction. It begins the plant’s longing to produce the starting point for another being like itself.

It is clear that these actions of plant life do not happen by chance. They have ends and purposes that suit the plant and goods that fit its nature. Anyone who reflects on them can see that they come from inclinations and aims. Plants therefore love these ends. They bring their powers to completion, finish their work, and move from what they lack to what completes them, turning abilities not yet realized into something real.

Now look at animals, whose souls are nobler than the souls of plants. Their appetites produce the same three functions found in plants: nourishment, growth, and reproduction. Yet they do so in a subtler and more splendid way. Animal appetite is the clearest sign of love and longing that most people can see. In most animals, except rational human beings, it loves the same things as the plant powers do. The difference is that plant love produces action only by nature and in a lower, less complete way. Animal love produces eating, mating, and reproduction through choice and desire, in a nobler way[^V07-192], with greater pleasure and more complete longing, using the senses and imagination.

It is clear that imagination works better than the outward senses, and the outward senses better than nature. The power of imagination receives the image of what it loves without the dark matter of this world, in a pure form safe from decay and non-existence, as long as the image stays in the imagination. When attention is drawn elsewhere, from within or without, the image disappears, but not because it has decayed. The senses also receive the image they love apart from the matter, but the matter must be physically present. Nature, by contrast, is immersed in matter. It cannot separate its beloved from matter or hold it before itself in any other way. The foods and other things it receives remain mixed with the dark coverings and material absences that overwhelm its own being and what it takes in.

A non-rational animal acts and loves in a way nobler and more beautiful than a plant. Its beloved things are brighter and finer, though, as we have seen, its appetite loves many of the same things as a plant’s. But an animal’s rank is below the rank of a rational being. It lacks the power of reason that reveals universal truths and rational ends. It cannot reach an intellectual purpose, a true good, or a universal goal through its actions. Its longing is limited to particular objects and passing pleasures. Its appetitive power is therefore like the plant’s in being limited to individual aims and kinds of love.

The way these actions and appetites support individuals, preserve kinds, and improve the order of the world comes from a greater and higher agent,[^V07-193] who has a nobler purpose. It depends on God’s care and the angels who govern the affairs of creatures in the wisest order.

When a higher power joins each animal power, the lower power gains beauty, brightness, and greater purpose. Its actions last longer, bring greater benefit, and reach a higher rank than they could on their own. They also produce more effects, or stronger ones, and do so with better coordination and a clearer path to their goal.[^V07-194] Each higher power can support, strengthen, and refine the lower one. It keeps the lower power from harm and protects it from opposing forces. The lower power then receives greater perfection and beauty from the higher one.

The higher powers also direct and use the lower powers for their purposes, giving them grace, beauty, and splendour. Animal appetite supports plant life, while animal anger protects it from losing its substance before it has reached its full growth and being harmed. The rational power helps the animal powers reach their aims, lending them grace and brightness as they serve its purposes. That is why a human being’s senses and desires can sometimes rise above animal behaviour and act in a way like the higher angels. They can pursue purposes that belong only to reason.

For example, a person’s animal soul alone may produce feelings and actions such as sensing, imagining, eating, sexual union, and fighting enemies in a low and crude way, if it is not guided by the noble rational soul. This is how the lowest people act, resembling wild animals. But when the rational soul gives these powers light, subtlety, and grace, they act in a wiser, fuller, nobler, and better way. A thoughtful person chooses foods with a good balance of qualities and prepares the body to carry out acts of worship and seek good things and virtues. The imagination is used for subtle and useful purposes that may almost match the work of clear reason and insight. Anger is directed in ways that help overcome opposition and reach a worthy aim.

Some actions also come from the rational part of the human being working together with the animal part. The rational power directs thought toward fine discoveries and searches for the steps connecting premises to conclusions and the starting points needed for proof. It directs the senses to gather particular images from material things. Then, by examining these separate cases, imagination helps draw out general ideas and intellectual forms. In this way, a person moves from first principles to later conclusions. They come to understand intellectual realities and the roots of existing things, reach holy principles, and know the source of all sources and the return of all things.

The rational power may also direct sexual desire toward more than bodily pleasure. It can seek to imitate the First Cause by preserving the fields and offspring and keeping species alive, especially the human kind, which is the fruit of the created world and the finest of bodily forms. It can guide the appetite for food so that a person eats well, enjoys the food, and strengthens the body. The body is like a riding animal that carries the soul on its journey to the next life and toward God. It can direct anger toward fighting strong opponents and defending a good city and a righteous community.

The deepest actions can come from pure reason: understanding intellectual truths, joining the highest company, loving death, longing for the next life, knocking at the gate of divine acceptance, and living near the All-Merciful.

#### Chapter — On the separate forms that serve as models

Keep this point in mind. People develop through levels, rising from plant life and animal life to rational life. They also differ in their aims. Some people act from appetite and anger only for the pleasure of eating, drinking, and sex, or for the pleasure of defeating an enemy and satisfying anger and hatred. They do not consider a wise purpose or an intellectual goal in any of this.

Yet reason and proof show that God’s care in creating plant powers and animal senses is not limited to their immediate acts and particular goals.[^V07-195] Those acts serve higher purposes too: preserving kinds, completing the order of the whole, and leading to the highest good and the highest world. These are also the aims of the complete human being, through the high rank of the rational soul.

Look, too, at the actions of lifeless things and plants. They help produce a well-ordered whole, though they are not aware of this purpose. Their rank is below that of animals, whose actions are aimed only at gaining what suits their particular appetites. This careful reflection shows that each bodily nature must have a director above it. The bodily natures themselves cannot understand how their actions serve the order of the whole. Nor can the individual animal souls, which know only what suits their particular and changing lives and pleasures. So these natures must be guided by intellectual directors and holy movers who have higher aims.

The complete human being uses the high power of the soul to direct the body and move its natural and animal powers. This is like the way intellectual directors move the bodily natures toward their own places when they have been moved away, and keep them settled there when they arrive. It is also like the way these directors guide other natural actions: fire heating, water cooling, trees taking nourishment and growing, and plants reproducing. These and similar actions of nature bring great good and broad benefit.

When a human being is guided well, they rise through their powers and aims in their natural order. After completing each lower power, they reach the perfection of a higher one. They move from power to power and from one aim to another until they reach the divine intellect, which has gained the highest purpose, joy, and delight. As far as it can, it understands and acts upon the parts of the world, their causes, and their ends. The greater world is like this too.[^V07-196] It contains natural beings in their different ranks, souls in their different levels, and intellects in their different orders. Their beings and substances are ranked by nobility, some higher and some lower. Their pleasures and joys are ranked too: some are intellectual, some belong to animal imagination, some to the senses, and some to natural inclination. The beloved things of beings differ, but they are arranged in levels. The lowest belong to natures within bodies; the highest to the angels brought near to God and those who love Him as they behold the light of His beauty and majesty.

We have shown that bodily natures change and move within their very being. All these natures move toward the highest source, rising step by step from the bodily world to the intellectual world. This is like a human being, who rises through the changes within their being from the lowest level to the highest—from semen to complete intellect—over a lifetime. The world also has a natural lifetime, as a human being does. The world’s natural lifetime is about fifty thousand years.[^V07-197] As the revealed Book says: “The angels and the Spirit ascend to Him on a day whose measure is fifty thousand years.”

#### Chapter — Why refined men and youths love beautiful faces

Know that philosophers have held different views about this love and what it is. Is it good or bad, praiseworthy or blameworthy? Some condemn it as a vice and list its faults, saying it belongs to the idle and useless. Others call it a virtue of the soul, praise it, and speak of the good qualities of those who feel it and the nobility of its end. Some do not understand what it is, its causes, its meaning, or its purpose. Some call it a sickness of the soul; others call it a kind of divine madness.

Careful reasoning about causes and purposes shows that this love is found naturally among most peoples, without planning or artifice. By it, I mean the intense pleasure taken in a beautiful face and the deep affection felt for someone with delicate features and well-proportioned limbs. It is therefore one of the arrangements established by God, with benefits and purposes that follow from it. It should be considered good and praiseworthy, especially when it comes from good beginnings and leads to noble ends.

As for its beginnings, we find that most people in nations that study the sciences, crafts, arts, and fine manners—such as Persians, Iraqis, Syrians, and Romans—have this gentle love, which begins with admiration for a beloved’s qualities. We have not found anyone with a delicate heart, refined nature, clear mind, and compassionate soul who has never felt this love at some point in life. By contrast, we find that people with coarse souls, hard hearts, and harsh natures—such as Kurds, Arabs, Turks, and Zanj—are generally free of this kind of love. Most of them know only men’s desire for women and women’s desire for men, driven by marriage and mating, as in the nature of other animals. Nature set this desire in them to preserve their kind and keep their forms within matter, since individual beings are always passing away and changing.

Among refined and sensitive people, this love serves a purpose: to educate boys, raise them well, and improve their character. It also leads them to teach practical knowledge—grammar, language, rhetoric, geometry, and other sciences—as well as delicate crafts and good manners. They teach fine, measured poetry, beautiful music, stories, histories, unusual tales, traditions, and other excellences of the soul. When children no longer need to be raised by their fathers and mothers, they still need teachers. They need their teachers’ kind attention and care. For this reason, God’s care placed in adult men a desire for boys and an affection for handsome youths. It moves them to educate and refine these boys, complete their still-incomplete souls, and bring them to the ends for which they were created. Otherwise, God would have placed this desire and love in most refined and learned people for no purpose at all. So this love, found in sensitive souls and gentle hearts, must have a wise benefit and a sound purpose. We see these purposes taking effect. This love in human beings must therefore be counted among virtues and good qualities, not among vices and sins.

I swear that this love empties the soul of every worldly concern except one: longing to see a human beauty in which many signs of God’s beauty and majesty appear. God says: “We created the human being in the finest form,” and, “Then We brought him into being in another form. Blessed is God, the best of creators.” Whether the “other form” means the complete outward form or the rational soul, the outward form points to the inward one, and the body is a bridge to the truth.

For this reason, love for another person can count as a virtue when it does not begin in excessive animal appetite. It must begin instead in admiration for the beloved’s character, balanced body, good temperament, graceful movements, actions, charm, and manner. It softens the heart, sharpens the mind, and wakes the soul to perceive noble things. That is why some spiritual teachers tell their followers to begin with love.[^V07-198] It is called chaste love, and it is said to soften the soul and bring light to the heart. The traditions say: “God is beautiful and loves beauty.” Another saying is: “Whoever loved, stayed chaste, kept it secret, and died, died a martyr.”

Human love is of two kinds: true and metaphorical. True love is love of God, His qualities, and His actions as His actions. Metaphorical love is either spiritual or animal. Spiritual love begins in the likeness between the soul of the lover and the soul of the beloved. The lover is most moved by the beloved’s character, because it comes from the soul. Animal love begins in bodily appetite and the search for pleasure. The lover is most moved by the beloved’s outward appearance and color, and the shape of the limbs, because these belong to the body. This second kind usually goes with sexual wrongdoing and excessive desire. It makes the rational power serve the animal power. The first kind comes from the soul’s refinement and its qualities. The second comes from the lower self, which urges a person toward bodily desire.

The first kind makes the soul gentle, eager, longing, sad, tearful, soft-hearted, and thoughtful. It seems to seek something inward and hidden from the senses. It cuts the soul off from worldly distractions and turns it away from everything except the beloved, making all its concerns one concern. For this reason, it can make it easier to turn toward the true Beloved. The soul does not need to turn away from many things; it only needs to turn from one beloved to another.

But keep this in mind. Although this love may be counted as a virtue, it is a virtue between pure intellect and the animal soul. A virtue of this kind is not always noble or praiseworthy, at every time and in every situation, or for every person. It should be used in the middle stages of the spiritual path, when the soul is being softened, roused from heedlessness and the sleep of nature, and pulled out of animal desire. When the soul reaches full perfection through divine knowledge, it becomes an active intellect. It understands universal truths and can remain connected to the holy world. At that point, it should no longer occupy itself with beautiful bodies and human features. Its place is higher than that. This is why people say that metaphor is a bridge to truth. Once you have crossed the bridge into the world of truth, it is wrong to return to what you crossed from.

The early philosophers may have differed over whether to praise or condemn this love for the reason just given. They may also have confused chaste spiritual love—based on a refined soul’s admiration for balanced limbs, a balanced temperament, and a well-shaped body—with animal desire, which comes from one particular power of appetite.

Those who say this love belongs to idle and useless people say so because they have no knowledge of hidden matters or subtle truths. They know only what appears to the senses and outward faculties. They do not know that God places nothing in the nature of the soul without a wise purpose and a great end.[^V07-199]

Those who call it a sickness of the soul or a kind of divine madness say so because they have seen what happens to lovers: sleepless nights, thinness, a wasted body, a rapid pulse, sunken eyes, and deep sighs. They thought these were signs of sickness, caused by an imbalance in the body and an excess of black bile. It is the other way around. These conditions begin in the soul and then affect the body. When a person thinks and reflects constantly on something inward and gives it their whole attention, the body’s powers turn toward the brain. Too much activity in the brain creates great heat, which burns up moist fluids and healthy nourishment. The organs become dry, and the blood turns to black bile. This may lead to melancholy.[^V07-200]

Those who call it divine madness do so because they could find no medicine or drink that would cure the lover’s distress. They could only pray to God, give charity, or seek help from the prayers and charms of monks and priests. This was the way of Greek philosophers and doctors. When they could not cure a sick person, they took them to a temple, told them to pray and give charity, offered a sacrifice, and asked priests, monks, and religious leaders to pray for their recovery. If the person recovered, they called it divine medicine and the sickness divine madness.

Some say love is a powerful current in the soul, drawing it toward something that matches the body’s nature[^V07-201] or resembles its own kind.[^V07-202] Others say love begins when two people are born under compatible astrological signs. Their signs or degrees may match, they may share a ruling star, or their signs may be linked in some way, such as by element or aspect. Astrologers describe these and similar conditions. These people then fall in love.

Others say love is an excessive longing to unite. This is a good start, but it needs to be explained: what kind of union do they mean? Bodies can join by mixing and blending, but souls cannot join in this way. And even if a lover’s body and a beloved’s body touch while they are unaware or asleep,[^V07-203] it is clear that this does not achieve what love seeks. Love belongs to the soul, not to bodies. Only one kind of union is possible: the kind we explained when discussing intellect and what it understands. The knowing soul becomes one with the form it understands; the sensing soul becomes one with the form it senses. In this way, the soul that loves someone can become one with the image of the beloved. Repeated meetings,[^V07-204] seeing, thinking, and remembering make the beloved’s features and character take shape within the lover, until that image is firmly present in the lover’s being. We have explained this path so clearly that no thoughtful person should have room to deny it.

Stories of lovers show this. It is said that Majnun of the tribe of Amir was once so absorbed in love that Layla came and called to him: “Majnun, it is me, Layla.” He did not turn toward her. He said: “My love for you is enough; I no longer need you here.” For true love is the image that exists within the soul. That image is the beloved in the fullest sense, not the person outside, who is beloved only indirectly. What we know most directly is the image of knowledge within us, not what lies outside our thought. In the same way, a lover who has become one with the image of the beloved does not need the beloved’s body to be present. A poet says: “I am the one I love, and the one I love is me; we are two spirits[^V07-205] living in one body. When you see me, you see him; when you see him, you see us.”

Union between two things can happen only in the way we have described. It belongs to spiritual realities and states of the soul. Bodies and bodily things cannot unite in this way. They can only be beside, mixed with, or in contact with one another. In truth, in this world no one reaches complete union with another. This is so for two reasons.

First, when we examine one connected body, we find that it still contains absence and separation: each part is separate from the others. The connection between its parts is also a kind of separation. We call them connected and one only because there is no separate body or empty space between them, and no surface has appeared between them. But this is not a pure unity. If a body does not have full presence or unity even within itself, how could something else become one with it, or fully join with it?

Second, even if we set that aside, two bodies can meet only at their surfaces. A surface is not the body’s own reality or what makes it a body. Nothing in the lover can therefore reach the beloved’s body itself. The thing that might reach it is either the lover’s soul, the lover’s body, or an attribute of the soul or body. It cannot be an attribute, because attributes cannot move from one thing to another. It cannot be the body either, because two bodies cannot occupy the same place; they can only touch at their edges. This kind of contact does not heal the sick person who longs for union or quench their thirst.

Nor can the soul reach the beloved’s body. If a soul were joined in its being to another person’s body, it would become that person’s soul. One body would then have two souls, which is impossible. This is why,[^V07-206] when a lover gets what they had wished for—being close to the beloved and spending time together—they ask for still more. They long to be alone with the beloved, without anyone else present. If that is granted, they long to embrace and kiss. If they can do that, they long to share a bed and hold one another as closely as possible. Yet the longing and burning in the soul remain just as they were, or grow stronger. As one of them says:

> I embrace her, but my soul still longs for her;
> can anything be closer than an embrace?
> I kiss her mouth, hoping my heat will fade,
> but the fire of longing grows stronger.
> It seems my heart will not be healed
> until our two spirits become one.

The reason is that the beloved is not truly the bone, flesh, or any part of the body. Nothing in the bodily world is what the soul truly longs for. The beloved is a spiritual form that exists in another world.

#### Chapter — The things beings love differ with their levels of existence

Know that souls and natural powers love different things. Their beloveds vary with their levels of existence and knowledge. Each power reaches completion only through something like itself. Each sense takes pleasure only in the things that belong to it. Sight longs for colors and light; hearing longs for sounds and melody. Smell takes pleasure in scents, taste in flavours that suit the body, and touch in things with a balanced mix of opposing qualities. The same is true of every inward and outward power, whether natural, spiritual, or intellectual. Each power belongs to the kind of thing it can act on. Each sense is of the same kind as what it senses, and each intellect of the same kind as what it understands. Through these things, each power reaches completion. Its undeveloped abilities become fully realized; what it lacked it comes to possess, and becomes one with what it seeks, as we have explained before.

Among created beings, the human being is like a gathering of all the kinds and levels in the world, though at first these abilities remain undeveloped.[^V07-207] Each kind of being and each world—intellectual, spiritual, or natural—has something within a human being that begins incomplete and ready to grow. Everything incomplete longs for its perfection. Since a human being contains the beginnings of all these kinds of incompleteness, the whole person longs for a being that gathers every possible perfection. Only God has all these perfections in full.

So the being most worthy of love to those who know Him fully is God, with His most beautiful names. Next in worth is the whole world, because it displays God’s names and qualities. It contains the Throne, the Footstool, the heavens, the sun, the moon, the stars, the earth, all kinds of animals, plants, and minerals, the seas and their wonders, the winds, clouds, rain, snow, lightning, and shooting stars, and the movements of the heavens and the earth. It also contains the sources and ends of these movements: universal and individual intellects and souls, and both noble and lowly natures. All of this is arranged in the finest order. Anyone who understands how it is arranged is amazed by its beauty and completeness.

After God and His greatest display—the whole world, which is like a greater human being—only an individual human being can be fully loved by someone whose senses and powers, whether still developing or fully active, can all find what they seek in that person.[^V07-208] Nothing in creation draws such intense love and longing, robbing a person of rest and patience and bringing sleepless nights, grief, sorrow, and tears, except another human being. In a person, the lover finds something that answers all the desires of their powers. The other parts of the world each answer only one kind of desire. An idea pleases the intellect; a purely sensory thing pleases only the senses. Color and light please sight; sound and speech please hearing. But a human being contains simple and complex things, substances and qualities, spiritual powers like the angels of the faculties, and bodily powers like the limbs and organs. In short, a human being brings together the visible and hidden blessings of the whole world. Anyone who sees this complete being longs to meet and love them, because they see in them the work of their maker, the display of His signs, and a mirror of His beauty and majesty.

The fully realized teacher Ibn al-ʿArabī says in *The Meccan Revelations*:

> God created the world with perfect skill and order. As Abū Ḥāmid al-Ghazālī said, nothing more beautiful than this world could be created. God created Adam in His image.[^V07-209] The human being gathers the whole world, and God knew the world through Himself, since nothing existed but Him, His act, and His qualities. So the human being had to be in His image.[^V07-210] When God brought the human being into existence, the human became His mirror. God saw His own beauty there, and loved beauty. The world is God’s beauty, and He is beautiful and loves beauty. Whoever loves the world in this way loves only God’s beauty, since the beauty of a work belongs to its maker, not to the work itself. The beauty of the world is God’s beauty, and its forms are His beauty.

In another place he says:

> Whatever a person can imagine is the person’s own image, not God Himself. We can picture God only through the form in which He appears to us, according to what we are able to receive.[^V07-211] Adam is the whole world gathered into a small form. The world contains more than a person can take in at once, but we can understand the human being through its form, its parts, and its spiritual powers. God placed in the human being everything that exists outside God. Each part is linked to the divine name that brought it into being.[^V07-212] All the divine names are present in Adam; none is left out. Adam therefore reflects the name “God,” which includes every other divine name.[^V07-213] The same is true of the human being: though small in body, a person contains every meaning.[^V07-214] Even if a person were smaller still, they would remain human. It is no more impossible for the whole world to fit into a person than for a camel to pass through the eye of a needle.[^V07-215] Being small or large does not change what a thing is; they do not change what a thing is. God could create a camel so small that it could pass through a needle’s eye. This gives hope that people too may enter the Garden. In the same way, even if a person is much smaller than the world, they can gather within themselves all the realities of the greater world. Wise people therefore call the world a “greater human being.” Everything that could be in the world has appeared there; it also appears in the human being in a condensed form. Knowledge is the mind’s image of what it knows. So Adam was created in God’s image.

We have seen that the love which gathers all the things beings love takes three forms: greatest, middle, and smallest. The greatest is love of God. Only those who have reached spiritual perfection and passed beyond themselves completely can feel it. These are the people meant by the words, “He loves them, and they love Him.” In truth, He loves only Himself. The lover and beloved at both ends are one.

The middle form is the love of scholars who study the realities of existing things and keep reflecting on the creation of the heavens and earth. They are described in the Qur'an: “Those who remember God standing, sitting, and lying on their sides, and reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth: ‘Our Lord, You did not create this without purpose. Glory be to You! Protect us from the punishment of the Fire.’” The Fire here is the pain of separation and being veiled from the signs of God. The Garden is the joy of seeing His acts.

The smallest form is the love one person feels for another. An individual human being is also a small model of the greater world. The whole world is God’s complete book, in which He has shown His own perfections and divine meanings. The human being is a short version of that book, containing signs of the clear Book. Whoever reflects carefully on these signs can more easily read the greater book of the world and understand its meanings and secrets. When those meanings become clear, the person can rise to behold God’s beauty and the majesty of His oneness. Then they see everything gathered within His greatness and fading beneath the rays of His light.

#### Chapter — People differ in what they love

Know that people differ more from one another than members of any other kind. This is because human beings are capable of acquiring many different ways of living and acting.[^V07-216] It can rise from the level of animals to the highest rank of the angels brought near to God. Some people live at the level of animals, ruled by appetite. Some live at the level of wild beasts, ruled by anger. Some are like devils, and some are like angels. Each of these four broad groups contains many kinds.

You know that each person loves what resembles them. God has planted in beings and human souls a love of lasting life and of remaining in the best state they can reach.[^V07-217] The fullest state of the appetite-driven soul would be to exist forever, enjoying food and sex without interruption or anything to spoil the pleasure. So this soul loves only eating and sex. The fullest state of an anger-driven soul would be to rule over others, defeat its enemies, and take revenge without being stopped or disturbed. So it loves domination and revenge.

The fullest state of a devil-like soul would be to trick, mislead, and lie: to make false things look true and lies look honest. So it loves schemes, deception, whispers, false promises, and empty hopes. God says of the Devil: “He promises them and raises their hopes, but the Devil promises them only to deceive.”

The fullest state of an angel-like soul is to know the truth as it is, believe in God, His angels, His books, His messengers, and the Last Day, turn away from worldly concerns, seek quiet time with God, reflect on His works and kingdom, and accept His decree. Such a soul loves divine knowledge and being with God without barriers. It takes joy in remembering Him, dislikes being caught up in this world, and longs to die and leave this temporary life for the next, hoping to be near God and see Him. Only a few good people, the closest to angels and the honored messengers, have these qualities. Most people cannot imagine them because they know too little and their minds have been clouded by matter. They are content with bodily forms made of the elements—bodies of flesh, blood, and dark, decaying fluids—and want to remain with them forever. This is because their nature is incomplete and their souls lowly. God says: “They are content with the life of this world and at ease in it, and they are heedless of Our signs.” Many verses in the Qur'an say the same.

Every lover of a worldly thing longs for it when it is absent. When the lover gets it, they enjoy its closeness and feel satisfied. But sooner or later, they must part from it, grow tired of it, or see it change and lose its sweetness. The pleasure fades. What they loved may even become a burden, a source of pain or illness. We see this when people eat too many sweets or rich foods, or have sex too often. A human beloved may become an enemy and seem hateful to the person who once loved them.

This is not so for those who love God, believe in His signs, and long to meet Him. Every day they draw closer to their beloved, their longing grows stronger. As their longing grows, so does their nearness, and this continues forever. God says: “Those who believe love God most deeply.” He also says: “Their light will shine before them and on their right, and they will say, ‘Our Lord, complete our light for us.’” He describes the state of other lovers with the words: “Like a mirage in a flat desert, which the thirsty person thinks is water. When he reaches it, he finds nothing.”

Then God turns to those who love Him and describes their state without naming them directly: “And he finds God there, who pays him in full.” To a clear-sighted knower, the forms of this world are like mirrors in which holy realities and divine qualities appear. A person who knows how a mirage works understands that what appears in it is an image of the sky, not water. In the same way, a person with true insight knows that the existence seen in the world is only the many ways God reveals Himself and displays His names and perfections through created things.

Know that the highest purpose and finest wisdom in placing love in sensitive people, and making them love beautiful bodies and graceful forms, is to wake them from heedlessness and ignorance. This love trains the soul for a time, helping it move from possibility to fulfillment, from bodily concerns to spiritual ones, and from these to lasting, universal beauty. It stirs the soul to long for God and the joys of the next life. The beauties we see on bodies are signs of spiritual beauty and examples of it. When individual souls see them, they long for what they point to. When they meet them, they look closely and reflect on them, until they draw their images into the soul, free of other things and stripped of the coverings that hide them. When the physical person disappears from sight, the image remains in the soul. The beloved forms and features remain there as clear spiritual images, free of matter.

When the soul grows stronger and reaches the level of intellect, those images rise with it. They become lasting and good: universal intellectual forms, joined to the soul. They do not leave, fade, change, or decay. Consider someone who loved another person in youth, then lost them or grew apart. Years later, they see that person again, changed and no longer as beautiful as before. The old image of their beauty remains in memory, unchanged. The lover sees that the features and colors once seen in another person now exist within their own soul, apart from the person outside. The wise person then realizes that the true beloved was never something outside the soul. The beautiful image once seen on that person’s body is now present within the lover, drawn into the soul and made part of it.

When an intelligent person understands this, they wake from heedlessness and ignorance. They learn that the true beloved is not found in bodies, dense matter, or flesh and blood. They can then rest from the struggle, hardship, and distress that come from holding on to someone outside themselves. As the poet says:

> My heart has come to its senses about Salma and left its empty hopes behind;
> the horses and camels of youth have been stripped of their gear.

#### Chapter — Divine love belongs especially to perfected mystics and saints who have reached God

We have seen that human love has three forms: greatest, middle, and smallest. The greatest is longing to meet God and the desire to know His being, qualities, and actions as His own. No one has this desire except those who know God. The rest of us, in our longing for our beloveds, are like children who enjoy playing with a ball and stick, compared with adults who pursue serious aims and take pleasure in leadership. If you felt a desire for God and a longing to know His beauty and majesty, you would choose this—one of the truest desires and greatest pleasures—above every other good. You would choose the Garden of knowledge, with the orchard of wisdom and its fruits, over a garden that offers only bodily or imagined pleasures. But this desire is not given to ordinary people, or even to most of those who consider themselves virtuous. It is found only in a few people firmly grounded in knowledge.

Just as grown people have sexual desire or a desire to lead while children want only to play, the knower is amazed that you and people like you are absorbed in status and leadership. To the knower, the whole world is a game and a diversion. When God gives the knowers this love, their longing matches their love and their desire matches their knowledge. Their longing for knowledge has no comparison with bodily pleasures, in this life or the next. Their pleasure in knowing God does not fade or grow dull. It continues to increase with their knowledge and attention. Other desires do not last this way.

Yet this love arises only after a person has passed through many stages and changed greatly. They must rise from animal life to angelic life, just as an animal grows from a drop of semen through the plant stage to animal life. In the same way, the mind must move from possibility and readiness to active understanding. He compares those who have not developed this delight in wisdom to children and women beside a fully grown man. In his account, someone without this longing is either still a child whose understanding has not matured or a person whose original capacity has been damaged by worldly desires. Those who have received this desire for knowledge and the joy of seeing God’s beauty and majesty live as if they were in a Garden wider than the heavens and earth.[^V07-218] They look at people absorbed in low desires as adults look at children playing with bright toys. That is why they withdraw from most people, prefer solitude, and love to remember God. They avoid money, status, family, and children when these distract them from the joy of speaking with God. People laugh at them and call them confused or mad. The knowers, in turn, are amazed that people settle for the temporary pleasures of this world. They say: “If you mock us, we will mock you, just as you mock us.”

The knower is busy building a ship to save themselves and others. They know how dangerous the return to God will be when the Great Event comes. They laugh at those who ignore God’s punishment, the questions and reckoning, the penalty of the next life, and the bad end awaiting them when the veil is lifted, inner secrets are revealed, what is in people’s hearts is brought out, the dead rise from their graves, and Hell and its people and the Garden and its people appear. They are like an adult who laughs at children playing when a powerful ruler enters the city to judge its people, kill some, honor others, imprison some for life, and bring others close to him.

How foolish is someone whose road leads to the next life, but who spends this life gathering money and status and trying to outdo others in gold, silver, livestock, crops, and children—while dangers and deadly obstacles lie ahead! The truthful Imam said: “A moment’s desire can bring long sorrow.” Junayd was asked what a lover is like. He answered: “They think constantly about the beloved, spend much time alone, and mix little with others. When they look, they do not see; when spoken to, they do not hear; when they speak, they do not understand. They do not grieve when they suffer or rejoice when they succeed. They look toward God in solitude, find comfort in Him, and speak to Him in secret and in the open. They do not compete with people for worldly things. They seem sad, afraid of losing what they hope for, and worried that they will fail to reach what they seek. Their minds are stunned by the sight of God’s majesty. They sleep and eat little and grieve constantly. They live among people differently from others. When alone, they sigh; when apart, they moan. They drink from the cup of love and feel estranged from everyone.”

What he describes is the state of lovers at the beginning of their journey toward God, when they are being pulled away from people and toward Him. Once they reach God, their hearts open and expand through His light. They become among the happiest and most generous of people, rejoicing in the sight of God’s majesty and seeing Him in everything. The Prophet, before his mission, used to withdraw from people and devote himself to worship in the Cave of Ḥirāʾ. Then God opened his heart and made his character great, and sent him to all people to guide them and teach them the Book and wisdom. This is what God means when He says: “Did We not open your heart?” The rest of the chapter explains this matter; we will return to it later, if God wills.

A scholar says: Do not think that the knower’s joy in the gardens of knowledge is less than the joy of someone in the Garden who satisfies their desire for food and sex. They cannot be equal. Some knowers may want to open the doors of knowledge even more than most people want food, drink, clothing, and sex. How could this desire be weaker, when it lets them share in the highest company of angels, who have no need for food, drink, or sex? Animals may enjoy these things more than people do. If you think it is better to share the pleasures of animals than the joy of the highest company, you are deeply mistaken. Your worth is measured by what you long for.

We should also understand that, just as every being receives its existence from its maker, and the cause has what the effect has in a higher and nobler way, so every pleasure and beloved thing in the material world is a trace of something in the world of souls,[^V07-219] the Garden, and the home of life. The Qur'an says: “There you will have whatever the soul desires and whatever delights the eyes, and you will remain there forever.” The pleasures of souls, the forms in the Garden, its trees, rivers, chambers, companions, palaces, and birdsong are themselves traces of the pleasures and joys found in God’s presence. Those who remain near Him receive joys beyond our ability to describe—things no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and no human heart has imagined.

Whoever leaves the world behind gains the next life and the pleasures of the Garden. Whoever gives up even the pleasures of the next life gains nearness to God, the honor of reaching Him, and the joy of meeting Him. Beside this, every other pleasure seems small.

### Station Nine — God’s creative act and the existence of beings whose existence does not depend on physical matter

#### Chapter — Preliminary principles for identifying the first things that come from God and the existence proper to each

To establish this point, we first recall some principles explained in the First Journey, in the general discussion of what applies to existence simply because it exists.[^V07-220] Here they are, in brief.

The first principle is this: the direct opposition between “yes” and “no”[^V07-221] is a relation between two meanings, one of which means exactly that the other is absent; it means nothing else.[^V07-222] For this reason, this opposition can only be between two sides. Reason cannot allow a middle state between them. This is also called contradiction. Some logicians, trying to make the word “contradiction” mean that each side acts against the other,[^V07-223] said that two claims contradict when the truth of one necessarily makes the other false.

They heard the saying, “The opposite of a thing is its denial.” So some changed this wording[^V07-224] to “The denial of a thing is its opposite,” and treated each side as an opposite of the other. But denial belongs directly to the negative side, not to the affirmative one. Others said that contradiction means that each meaning denies and is denied by the other, making it a relation that goes both ways.[^V07-225] The better explanation is that the two sides oppose each other in different ways: one is directly the denial of the other; the other is its denial only indirectly, as the denial of a denial. This is enough to account for the relation going both ways. Anyone seeking the truth will see that the details of the wording do not matter.

At any rate, one side must mean exactly the denial of the other. The other side need not have any one particular meaning. It may be “human,” “horse,” “stone,” “white,” or anything else. Its only condition is that it not itself mean the denial of the first side. It can still be something that the denial applies to.[^V07-226] So, in a true opposition between affirmation and denial, one side must be the denial itself. The other is an affirmative claim in relation to it, whether that claim is a genuine positive statement or another kind of denial.

This opposition occurs not only between full statements but also between single terms. It can concern what a particular thing is—such as “human” and “not human”—or what it has, as when we say something is white or lacks whiteness.[^V07-227] A particular thing cannot be outside both sides. It is either white or not white. Likewise, it either has whiteness or does not have it.

As the direct opposite of a single term is its denial, the direct opposite of a statement is its negation. The negation of a denial is an affirmation.[^V07-228] For example, the contradiction of “Every human is an animal” is “Some human is not an animal.” The contradiction of “No stone is a human” is “Some stone is a human.”[^V07-229] So logicians say that the opposite of a universal positive claim is a particular negative claim, and the opposite of a universal negative claim is a particular positive claim.

Another principle: we must distinguish two ways of saying that something is or has a property. A property can be said of the thing itself, as in “this is human”; or it can be said to belong to the thing, as in “this has whiteness.” For this reason, “blackness” and “movement” can both belong to one thing, even though one is not the other. But “black” and “not black” cannot both be said of one thing in the same sense. “Blackness” and “not blackness” are direct opposites, as are “black” and “not black.” But “has blackness” and “has a non-black quality” do not always contradict each other. A thing can be both black and moving. Movement is one kind of “not-blackness” when we speak of what a thing has, but it does not mean that the thing itself is not black.[^V07-230] If “not-blackness” means only the bare denial of blackness, with no other quality meant, then “has blackness” and “has not-blackness” do contradict. Keep this distinction in mind; it will help with the question we are discussing.

Another principle concerns the different “respects” in which we speak of a thing and the different kinds of reasons for a claim. We must distinguish them, or we may mistake many things for one, or one thing for many. People have made this mistake when they argued that many effects could come from the one true First Cause, or denied that God has qualities such as knowledge, power, and will within His own being. They did not see that these qualities have different meanings but do not make the One many.

A “respect” can either help define the thing or explain why a claim about it is true. The first kind is treated like a part of the thing: for example, being rational is part of what it means to be human; being a writer defines a certain kind of compound thing.[^V07-231] The second kind is outside the thing, as when we explain that something acts as a cause or is an effect.[^V07-232] The wording may include the thing itself, but the respect is still a reason for the claim rather than part of the thing.[^V07-233]

Sometimes a phrase such as “in itself” or “without condition” does not add a condition to the thing. It only tells us how to consider it.[^V07-234] For example, “humanity considered in itself” means humanity without adding whether it exists or does not exist. This differs from taking “unconditioned” as a feature in its own right, in contrast to being conditioned in one way or another.

Different explanatory reasons do not make the thing itself many; they add reasons from outside it. People commonly think that different defining features do make the thing itself many.
