But careful thought and proof show that there is a difference between features that differ in meaning and features that differ in reality and existence.[^V07-235] Every concept differs from other concepts in meaning, but some concepts can still be one in reality. “Existence” and “being an individual,” for example, differ in meaning but are one in reality. “Knowing” and “being known” differ in meaning, but can belong to one simple being. God’s knowledge and power differ in meaning, yet are one in His being, as we have explained.

Some differences really do give a thing distinguishable parts or powers. Some differ from one another but are not direct opposites—for example, shape, size, color, taste, smell, heat, movement, and relation. Others are directly opposed: black and white, knowledge and ignorance, earlier and later in relation to one thing, or existence and non-existence. A further principle applies to all these features, whether essential or added: the reason why a thing has one feature is not the same as the reason why it has another.[^V07-236] A person is not rational because they can write, nor able to speak because they are an animal. If it were otherwise, every animal could reason, every human being would already be a writer, and every shaped thing would be moving.

This is true both in existence and in meaning. Being able to know and being something that can be known do not mean the same thing; otherwise they would be two words for one meaning. Earlier writers did not always distinguish the two kinds of difference. They said that a thing’s qualities cannot come from different respects unless still earlier, different causes explain them. But their point does not mean that one thing cannot have several qualities unless those qualities first multiply the thing itself. Opposed qualities are a special case: one thing cannot really have both sides of an opposition at once unless there is a real difference in its parts or powers. A difference created only by thought, such as dividing a human being into animal nature and rational nature, does not let the same thing be both knowing and ignorant, or both black and white.[^V07-237] The two opposites must belong to two distinct things.

Other defining features, such as blackness and movement, can both belong to one natural thing. But this is possible only when they come from different powers or sources, whether or not those powers always act together. The plant and animal powers within a human being, for example, produce different kinds of action.

Another principle follows. A thing can have a quality in one respect or at one level without having it in every respect or at every level. If we say that Zayd exists, it is enough that he exists in some way; he need not exist through himself, without any cause. If we say Zayd is black, we do not mean he is black because he is human, a writer, knowledgeable, generous, or brave. Those features have nothing to do with his color. He is not black in those respects, but that does not stop the statement “Zayd is black” from being true. A thing is established if it exists in any way; it is absent only if it is absent in every way.[^V07-238]

We can now say this: if a subject is simple in every respect, whatever is true of it must be true of it in every respect and at every level of reality.

We said earlier that descriptions which oppose each other when stated directly of a thing can still occur together in one subject. An apple, for example, has both a smell and a color, although color itself has no smell. This is possible because the apple is not absolutely simple. What gives it its smell is not precisely the same as what gives it its color. If those were one and the same, saying that the apple has a smell but its color has no smell would amount to saying that smell is not smell.

The same distinction applies to movement and its absence. Two opposed descriptions can be connected with one existing subject,[^V07-239] but not through exactly the same aspect of that subject. If the aspect that gives rise to movement were identical to the aspect that gives rise to non-movement, movement would have to be non-movement. Sound reason recognises this even before a formal proof.[^V07-240] The same aspect would both be and not be itself; indeed, the very same subject would both be and not be that subject.[^V07-241] Both conclusions are impossible.

So opposite descriptions may belong to one subject in different ways, but only when they do so through different aspects of it. They cannot arise through one and the same aspect.

#### Chapter — The first thing to come from the First Reality must be one

The principles we have recalled are enough for reason to show that the One, the pure First Reality, and anything considered simply as one, can produce only one thing insofar as it is one.[^V07-242] A many-sided thing cannot produce many effects as many, nor can two first effects come from it at once. One thing must come first, then another, until different respects and causes appear and the door to many good things opens. This principle may be one of the truths that a sound mind sees naturally. Yet those who oppose the people of truth have strongly denied it,[^V07-243] so the way to wisdom will not open to them.

They also deny the existence of separate lights, intellectual beings, and forms that do not depend on matter, bodies, or dimensions.[^V07-244] Their nature is too heavy to rise to another level and live near those who dwell in God’s holy sanctuary. They sit down with those who sit idle. Had they wanted to leave, they would have prepared for the journey; but God did not will their setting out, so they were held back and told, “Stay with those who stay behind.” They seem, in their hearts, to be enemies of spiritual beings and the angels brought near to God, even if they do not say so openly. God says: “Say, ‘Whoever is an enemy to Gabriel—he brought it down upon your heart by God’s permission.’” So the people of truth have tried to prove this claim decisively and answer objections, to protect those seeking truth from the doubts of people who deny spiritual beings and God’s action.

As we explained in the First Journey, when discussing principles of cause and effect, if A and B both came from the One considered simply as one, A would be something other than B; so the same aspect of the One would have produced B and what is not B. That would make contradictions true together. We have already answered some objections to this proof. We also discussed an objection raised by a leading critic of philosophy, who used logic against its own master, and showed where the confusion lay.

The argument continues: from what we have established, it is clear that producing something other than B is the same, in the respect from which it is produced, as not producing B. Otherwise “not B” would be B. In the wider reality, however, the existence of something other than B does not always mean that B is absent. A body may be fragrant and also have no fragrance, if “no fragrance” refers to its motion, which is a different quality. But the body cannot be fragrant and not fragrant in the very same respect.[^V07-245]

In short, “B” and “not B” can belong to one subject in a claim that it has a quality only if they come from different respects, not the same one.[^V07-246] This is close to the argument we used against a critic who said that when the simple cause produces A, it must be acting as the cause of A through its own distinct nature. It cannot do so merely because that nature can be named as “the source of A”; the same nature is also what the cause actually is.[^V07-247] If that same nature produced B as well,[^V07-248] it would then be both the source of A and the source of something other than A. These two descriptions differ in meaning, so the source nature itself would have to be different from itself. That is impossible.[^V07-249]

A similar point appears in Kātibī’s commentary on *The Epitome*. After explaining the main author’s argument, he says that we can deny that producing “not A” means not producing A. Even if we accept that link, he says, there is no contradiction between “A came from Him” and “A did not come from Him,” since both claims are unrestricted. If one is made a lasting, universal claim, it becomes false.[^V07-250] I say that both unrestricted claims can be true only if they apply at different times. But when they apply at the same time, they cannot both be true. In this discussion, he treats different respects as if they were different times. There is no time difference here. By “unrestricted,” he means that the claim does not cover every respect; by “lasting,” he means that it does. So both claims can be true only if the respects differ. If the respect is the same, they cannot both be true. This is clear.

At this point, the criticism Imam al-Rāzī made of the earlier philosopher turns back on al-Rāzī himself.

#### Chapter — Another proof that the first thing to come from the First Reality must be one

Bahmanyār gives another version of this argument in *The Book of Attainment*. He sums up a point Avicenna makes more than once, especially in *Notes on Philosophy*:

> A being that has no parts cannot cause two things at once as its first effects. Nothing comes from it until it is necessary for it to come. If A produces B only because B must come from A, then B would not be necessary if A produced C instead. For if C came from A in the very respect that makes B necessary, A would produce something other than B through that same respect. So B would not be necessary after all. Therefore, the first thing to come from any simple being must be one in its nature.

Dawānī objects in his commentary on *The Guide to Belief*: “It does not follow from the assumption that A produces C through the respect that requires B that B is not necessary. It follows only that B is necessary through the very respect that requires C. Is that not exactly what the argument denies?”

I say that adding the word “necessary” to this version of the argument shows that a cause that must produce an effect has a particular feature in relation to that effect, a feature it would not have in relation to a different effect. That feature is what makes this particular effect follow from the cause. “Feature” or “respect” here does not mean a general idea that can be repeated for different things. It means the real, particular ground of the effect: what it is in the cause that makes this effect come from it.

Every possible thing depends on an earlier necessity: it must be produced by its cause. This necessity belongs to the cause, as we explained when we said that every possible thing is surrounded by two necessities. Although the necessity belongs to the possible thing, the cause gives it to that thing. Zayd’s necessity of existence differs from ʿAmr’s, just as their existence differs. Two distinct things cannot share one and the same necessity that comes before them both. In the same way, two different effects cannot have one prior necessity as their source.

So an effect must follow from the First Source either because of its own nature or because of something else. If it depends on something else, it does not come from the First Source directly. But if it comes directly, the source’s own nature must be what makes that effect necessary. Since the source has one real nature, it cannot necessarily produce two effects at the same level. Some mistaken thinkers have said that one pure being could have a single natural fit with two things, and that this one fit could make both come from it at once. That claim falls apart on examination.

A general fit is not enough to explain why one particular effect comes from a cause. The two effects must share something in common, but each must also have something that sets it apart from the other. The particular feature that explains one effect cannot also explain the other. That feature is the true ground of that effect and what makes it a real effect of the cause. The shared feature by itself is only a general idea. It cannot explain why this particular being comes from the cause.

The real effect, then, would be one shared thing—provided it is one complete reality, not an unfinished general idea that still needs a particular feature to make it real. It is impossible for one simple cause to produce two different effects at the same level through one and the same feature.

Avicenna gives another proof in *The Healing*, *Pointers*, and *Notes on Philosophy*. The idea “this cause produces A by its own nature” differs from the idea “this cause produces B by its own nature.” If both ideas were parts of what the cause is, the cause would have parts. If both were features that follow from it, they would themselves need causes, and the same question would arise again. If one were part of the cause and the other followed from it, the two effects would not stand at the same level: the cause would produce only one first effect, while the other would depend on that first one. If every feature that follows from the cause needed another feature to explain it, there would be an endless chain of such features. That would also make it impossible for anything to have a feature at all. In every case, producing two first effects would make the cause complex, either in what it is or in something that follows from it.[^V07-251][^V07-252][^V07-253]

#### Chapter — Objections to this rule and replies to them

A critic of the philosophers writes in *Questions on the Book of Attainment*: “We hold that one cause may produce more than one effect, despite the philosophers’ and the Muʿtazilites’ claim to the contrary. A body, for example, brings about both being in a place and the ability to receive qualities.”

He then gives the philosophers’ proof: the idea of being the source of one effect differs from the idea of being the source of another. If both ideas are parts of the cause, the cause is composite. If they both follow from it, they are effects themselves, and the same question returns. If one is part of the cause and the other follows from it, the cause is composite and there is still only one first effect, since a part of the cause is not itself an effect.

I say that this is Avicenna’s proof, with “part of the cause” substituted for “what makes the cause what it is.” This change leaves out a fourth possibility: one of the two ideas could be the cause itself, while the other follows from it. The critic should have discussed that case and shown that it leads either to a contradiction—the supposed effect would have to be the cause—or to the claim that two effects are really one.[^V07-254]

The critic then says: “A thing’s being a cause is not a positive quality, as we have shown. So it makes no sense to ask whether it is part of the cause or a feature outside it.”

Tusi replies in his critique of the book: “The Ashʿarites say that one quality cannot produce more than one result. But they did not say this of one being, since they do not believe that anything except qualities causes effects. The Muʿtazilites and the philosophers apply this rule to beings as well. The author disagrees with all of them. But being in a place is a real feature, caused by a body; receiving qualities is not a real feature according to him.[^V07-255][^V07-256] The philosophers do treat it as real, but as something received rather than produced. They do not deny that one cause may both act and be acted on. So this example does not prove the point. The philosophers’ proof does not depend on treating causal power as a positive quality. It says that one cause’s power to produce one effect cannot come from the same respect as its power to produce another. The question is whether these two respects are parts of the cause or not.”

The critic then argues: “A point on a circle can be opposite one point without being opposite another. The meanings differ, but that does not make the point composite. Likewise, ‘A is not B’ differs in meaning from ‘A is not C,’ but the two denials do not make A composite.”[^V07-257]

Tusi answers: “Relations and denials do not exist in one thing in the same way. The philosophers say one cause cannot produce two things through the very same respect; they do not deny that it may produce two things for two different receivers. So relations and denials do not refute their argument.”

I will explain later how denials can be true of God and in what sense many denials may be true of the One, and I will show where the critics’ reasoning falls short.[^V07-258]

The critic also offers several versions of the same objection. One thing can be said not to be many things: “This is not a stone and not a tree.” It can have many qualities: “This person is standing and sitting.” It can receive many qualities: one body can be black and moving. Surely these different claims do not make one thing many, he says. If they did, we would have to say that one thing can be denied only one thing, have only one quality, or receive only one quality.

Tusi answers in *Pointers*: denying one thing of another requires both the thing denied and the thing it is denied of. Having a quality requires both the thing that has it and the quality. Receiving a quality requires something that receives it and something received. Different received qualities also require different powers: a body receives blackness because it can be affected by something else, but it receives movement because it can change from one state to another. But producing an effect is different. To explain one effect, we need only one cause. If it required more than one, all effects could not ultimately depend on one source.[^V07-259]

All that is right except the answer about denials.[^V07-260] Someone can say that denying a quality of a thing does not require that the quality itself exists. The One, God, can be said to lack every other possible thing, at any level we consider Him. Many denials are true of Him even before those things exist; they all come from the One alone.[^V07-261]

This is a serious objection, and it has two parts: many denials come from one source, and both affirmation and denial seem to apply to it. The answer I have given addresses both parts.[^V07-262][^V07-263] All these denials concern what is lacking or absent. To deny a lack is to affirm the complete and perfect existence that gives rise to every being, completes every existing thing, and is the goal of every perfection. In that reality there is no lack; there is only the absence of absence. There is no denial except the denial of denial. This is the full, single existence that is necessary in itself.

Dawānī gives another answer in his notes on *The Guide to Belief*. He says denial can be understood in two ways. First, it can be a bare denial, in which case it is not something added to the cause and does not make the cause many. It simply means that the cause exists and other things do not. Second, denial can be treated as something with a kind of existence that is added to the cause. But it takes this form only after many things have come to be, so it cannot make the first thing many.

This answer does not help. The difficulty is that many denials are true of God Himself: whenever the mind considers one of these things, it sees from the outset that it is absent from God, even before anything else exists. The first kind of denial that Dawānī describes may not need another thing to be added to the cause, but for that denial to be true, something must be denied of its subject.[^V07-264][^V07-265] His claim that it means only “the cause exists and other things do not” confuses two meanings. “The cause exists” and “other things are absent from it” are different ideas. The fact that a thing exists cannot be the same respect in which another thing is absent, even if the two ideas concern the same subject.[^V07-266]

A leading scholar has proposed a different answer. He says the objection does not apply. A bare negative claim does not need a cause to be true. Its truth depends on there being no cause for the matching positive claim. The other objections also fail: a thing can have many qualities or receive many effects only through different reasons, so there is no conflict with the rule. These points would matter only if all the different claims came from one and the same respect.

I say that a bare denial can either be the denial of a whole statement—“It is not the case that A is B”—or it can be part of the statement itself, as in “A is not B.” In the first case, the statement only denies a claim. It does not need a thing in the world that matches the denial or even a subject that exists in that respect. In the second case, it is an affirmative statement with a negative quality.[^V07-267] Once the subject exists, the two forms are always true or false together. For example, if “A is a human, not a horse” is true, then “A is not a horse” is true. So this kind of denial needs an existing subject that matches it. An affirmative statement joins its subject and predicate. The denial in “A is not a horse” therefore has to be grounded in the subject itself, especially when the statement says what the subject is, rather than merely describing it.

To say “a human is not a horse” is to say that a human is made up of what makes them human and what makes them not a horse. Humanity is not the same respect as being non-horse.[^V07-268] So when we ask how many denials can be true of God, we must use the explanation given above. Some scholars say that every denial of God comes down to one denial: that He is not possible or dependent.[^V07-269] Possibility is a negative quality that includes every lack, limitation, readiness, and absence. To deny possibility of God is to deny all these things. Since possibility itself means that existence and non-existence are both open, denying that openness affirms necessary existence.

The same problem applies to God’s many relative qualities, such as knowing, power, and will. It is no answer to say that these relations arise only after the things they relate God to exist. We can ask what gives rise to those relations in the first place. Most, perhaps all, of them have a source in Him: knowledge, power, will, and the like.[^V07-270][^V07-271] Our knowledge of Zayd, our power over him, and our will toward him are not just relations between us and him. Something in us gives rise to each relation. Our knowledge, power, and will toward Zayd also differ from our knowledge, power, and will toward ʿAmr.[^V07-272] Yet the philosophers hold that God knows every possible thing in detail from eternity, before those things come to exist.

The true answer is that God’s relations to things do not multiply His being, any more than His real qualities do. His knowing each effect is the very same reality as His power over it and will for it. The number of His relations follows the order of the effects: His relation to the first effect is the source of His relation to the second, which is the source of His relation to the third, and so on through the ordered chain of creation. This ordered series of relations does not divide God. In the same way, the order among created beings allows many things to come from the One; the order of the relations allows Him to have many relations without becoming many.

A doubt may arise: if an effect comes from a cause through a particular feature, the cause is a cause because of that feature, not through its own being. Then the cause has two things in it—its being and that feature—and is no longer truly one. The same problem would arise for a cause producing only one effect. It could be composite, because it has both the cause’s nature and the particular feature that makes it act.

The answer is that “particular feature” means the real ground that makes one effect come from the cause rather than another. We use this phrase because ordinary language is limited. When one thing comes from the First Source, the ground for its coming must be the First Source’s own being. But if several things came from it at once, each would need a different ground, unless they all came from one general feature. In that case, each particular effect would still need something that sets it apart. If all the effects had exactly the same relation to their source, they would also be the same in every respect, including existence and individuality. There could not be several distinct beings.

Tusi explains that “coming from” can mean two things. One is a relation between a cause and an effect that exists only after both exist. That is not the kind of coming from at issue here. The other is the cause’s being such that an effect must come from it. This comes before the effect and before the relation between them. For one effect, this ground is one thing. If a cause acts through its own being, the ground is its very being; if it acts through another feature, the ground is a state that belongs to it. But if there are several first effects, the grounds must differ, which makes the cause many.

A discerning teacher of ours raised a serious objection to this reply. He says that the First Source, through its one being, causes the first effect. But the cause’s being such that this particular effect must come from it is a different feature, and comes before the effect and before the relation between them. This feature is not God’s very being; it follows from His being. God’s own being, he says, is instead the ground for the outpouring of all good and the order of existence as a whole. So there are three meanings of “cause”: God’s own being as the source of all existence; the particular ground from which the first effect must come; and the relation of cause and effect that exists only when both exist.[^V07-273]

The teacher gives three reasons for this distinction. First, the feature that makes the first effect necessary comes after the effect is possible but before it exists. How can something that comes after the effect’s possibility be God’s very being? Second, if the first effect is one in number, then its cause, in relation to that effect, must also be one in number. But God’s unity is beyond number. Third, a quality belongs to God Himself only if it is a complete perfection of existence itself. God’s being such that this particular effect comes from Him is not such an absolute perfection. What belongs to His perfection is that He is, through Himself, the source of every good, every existence, and every perfection. His perfection does not depend on the relation to one particular being. He is complete in His creative power even before possible things exist, and remains so when they exist, just as His knowledge is complete before and after the things He knows come to be.[^V07-274][^V07-275]

I find several problems with this distinguished teacher's account. First, on his view, two things would proceed first from the God, whose existence depends on nothing: the first intellect and the additional feature that makes the intellect come from Him.[^V07-276] That is impossible. Someone may object that they do not come at the same level: the feature exists before the intellect. But then the feature, not the intellect, is truly the first effect. To explain its coming from God, we would need another feature. On the teacher's own account, that earlier feature would also be additional to God's being. The same question would arise again, with no end.[^V07-277]

Someone may reply that the extra feature is only a distinction made by the mind, not something separate in the outside world.

But God is not a being whose essence can be divided by analysis, as things may be one in reality yet contain several distinct features when we consider them. God is free from every kind of multiplicity. Just as there is no division between a thing and an added attribute in His actual existence, we cannot introduce one merely by changing how we consider Him.[^V07-278]

Second, the respect in which God produces the first intellect is the very same respect in which He produces all goodness and the whole order of the world. The first intellect contains the later order in a unified way, just as a thing’s defining form makes its parts one thing. We have shown that a thing is what it is through its form, not its matter. The form is the thing’s completion and true self. The intellect is, in a higher and simpler way, all the things that come after it.[^V07-279][^V07-280] To speak of the first intellect is therefore almost to speak of the whole world. It comes from God in a compressed, complete way; the whole world comes from Him in a detailed way. The detailed order is the same reality as the complete order, viewed in a different way.

So God’s being the source of the first intellect, His giving the whole order of the world, and His knowing all things are one and the same reality in Him. The different descriptions do not add separate features to His being.

Third, saying that the cause makes its effect necessary after the effect is possible describes how we think about the effect’s nature. It does not prevent the cause’s own real ground from being God’s being. We can consider a thing’s nature first, then say it is possible, then say it needs another thing to exist, and then say that its cause makes its existence necessary. In reality, however, the cause’s act is prior to the existence it gives. So there is no conflict between saying that God’s being is the ground of the effect and saying that the effect is possible when we consider its nature by itself.[^V07-281]

The same consideration applies if we speak of the whole world rather than the first effect: does not the whole world possess an even richer and higher unity than that first effect?[^V07-282][^V07-283]

Numerical unity is not the only kind of unity: members of one kind may share a unity even while being individually distinct. Such a distinction can be thought of within a general nature, but it does not follow that the cause of a simple being must possess that same kind of unity. The intellect, considered as a simple being, is not composed of an existence added to an independently existing nature. It is a particular way of existing, and its difference from God lies in its dependence, lower degree of completeness and place in the order of existence.[^V07-284]

Fifth, the claim that God’s making this particular effect is not an absolute perfection does not hold. The first intellect’s existence is a perfection of existence itself, and so is the real ground that makes it come from God.[^V07-285]

A later writer, the author of *The Garden of Heaven*, raises a related objection. If the feature that makes one effect come from God is God’s own being, then it cannot be absent when we consider other effects. But the special feature that links God to one effect is absent in relation to a different effect. So it must be something added to God’s being. He answers that the feature is God’s being only in the sense that it makes that particular effect what it is, without adding anything to God. I have already given the sound answer.

Another clever critic argues that if two things, A and B, came from the One, then producing A would not be producing B. So the One would both produce A and not produce A. The answer says that not producing A is not the same as producing something other than A. The critic then says that producing B is also not producing A, so the One would both produce A and not produce A. I reply that if this reasoning worked, it would also mean that the One could not produce even one thing or exist as one.[^V07-286] Every existing thing can be described through different ideas, such as being one, being a thing, and being this individual. The critic’s argument is therefore a verbal trick.[^V07-287]

The proof itself is sound. Its starting points are necessary truths, not matters of persuasion, debate, poetry, or trickery. Its form is valid too. It runs as follows: if what is truly one produced both A and B, it would have to be described as producing A and not producing A. Those two claims contradict each other. But that consequence is impossible. The original assumption—that a truly single source produces two effects—must therefore be false. In this kind of proof, rejecting the consequence requires us to reject the assumption. The reasoning can also be set out as a series of linked conditional statements.[^V07-288]

If a proof is sound in both its premises and its form, an apparent exception does not justify attacking the proof, as many disputants do. Someone seeking truth should investigate the apparent exception until it becomes clear.[^V07-289]

The critic’s examples—existence, unity, and being a thing—do not make a subject many just because they differ in meaning. The same is true of God’s qualities. Knowledge, power, will, life, hearing, and sight are not merely different words for the same idea, and they are not said of God by metaphor. Yet they belong to His one being, with one existence and one individuality. God’s one existence is the reality behind all these ideas. Their truth of Him does not divide His being.

For example, when we say fire is extended and hot, its being extended is shared with other bodies, while its heat comes from its particular form. These two features show that fire has matter and form, and so is composite. A human being’s life and ability to speak likewise follow from different parts of the definition of a human being. In cases like these, different qualities reveal real parts in the thing.[^V07-290]

A line can be described as equal or unequal in size because of its quantity; as having all its parts at once because it is continuous;[^V07-291] and as straight because of its shape.[^V07-292] These descriptions point to different elements in the line’s definition, even though it exists as one thing. It can also be a side of a triangle, a diagonal of a square, or the curved edge of a cone. These roles bring different relations and causes into the description.[^V07-293]

God’s complete qualities, such as knowledge and power, do not make His being many, either in what He is or in anything that belongs to Him. This is true in existence and in meaning, in the world and in thought.

The same applies to qualities we describe through relations, such as being known, being a cause, being the source, or being first. Some relative ideas do imply a difference in the thing, such as being possible or necessary, beginningless or originated, earlier or later. A possible being is composite in the broad sense: it has both its nature and its existence. But the ideas of God’s knowledge, power, and will do not divide Him.

The differences the critic points to—being one, being a thing, and being understood—are only differences in meaning. They do not create real differences in God’s being, qualities, or relations. A person who understands God’s unity should understand His real qualities, relative qualities, and denials in this way. Otherwise they may fall into a kind of divided belief about God, or deny Him qualities altogether.

A critic might object that if God’s knowledge, power, and will are one in reality, then everyone who knows must also be able, and everyone who is able must also will. Every existing thing would then know, be able, and will. But stones and other lifeless things do none of these things.

I answer that if you could see without the veils that hide reality, you would see that everything in the heavens and earth—intellects, souls, forms, bodies, and qualities—is alive, knowing, able, and willing, though each to a different degree.[^V07-294][^V07-295] These qualities rise and fall with the strength and weakness of existence itself.

A material body is filled with absence in two ways. First, it extends through space, with each part in a different place. Since its position is its way of existing, the difference in place between its parts is also a difference in their existence and individuality. Each part is present only where the other parts are absent. This is part of what a body is. Its being is therefore made up of absence and concealment: its parts are hidden from one another, and it is hidden from itself.

Second, its nature keeps changing. It flows from one moment to the next like running water. Each moment it passes away and takes on a new state. These two features show that a bodily form does not have lasting awareness, either for itself or for something else, unless it is represented by a matching form in a higher world of images or intellect.[^V07-296] At one time, what was present in a body is no longer there at another time. If it were itself a knower, it would be different from moment to moment; if it were alive, it would also be dead or destroyed at other times.

Material things are therefore weak in awareness, life, and will, just as they are weak in existence. Their forms are like images of knowledge clouded by absence and limitation: knowledge that is almost not knowledge, life like death, will like aversion, power like helplessness, hearing like deafness, sight like blindness, and speech like silence.[^V07-297] The Qur'an says that everything praises God, though we do not understand its praise, and that all in the heavens and earth bow to Him. Mystics have also said that every particle of the world, even stones and plants, is alive, speaks, bows, and praises its Lord.

This can be understood in either of two ways. First, existence and its seven qualities—life, knowledge, will, power, hearing, sight, and speech—always go together. Anything that exists has these qualities in some degree, though ordinary people do not see them in bodies.[^V07-298][^V07-299] Second, each kind of natural body may have a separate, immaterial form that guides and sustains it by God’s permission. These forms relate to natural bodies as souls relate to bodies. They are truly alive and aware, not merely by comparison. A body, considered as matter, differs from a body considered as a kind: as a kind, it is brought to completion by its defining form. Both explanations are subtle, but the first applies more broadly.

#### Chapter — Another argument that the first thing to come from the First Reality is simple and one

The cause that gives existence to an effect must have a natural fit with that effect, a fit it does not have with other effects. Fire fits burning, water fits cooling, and the sun fits giving light. Fire does not fit cooling; water does not fit burning; earth does not fit shining. If two things came from one truly simple cause, they would have to come either through one respect or through two. They cannot come through one respect, because the cause would then need to match two different realities at once. To match something in this way is to share its nature: if two qualities match, they are alike; if two things match, they share a reality. But one truly simple being has no qualities beyond its own being. It cannot match two different realities and still be one. Nor can it produce the two through two respects, because then it would not be truly simple. This proof resembles the one we gave earlier.[^V07-300][^V07-301]

A clever critic objects: the fit a cause has with an effect does not require the whole nature of the cause to match the whole nature of its effect. The fit can come from one special feature within the cause. Fire burns because of its fiery form, not because all of its nature matches heat. So a cause could fit two effects through its own nature without being the same as either of them.

I say that when a cause is simple, the feature through which it fits its effect is its whole nature. That is not true of complex things such as fire and water. Their power to produce an effect may belong to one part of their nature, such as fire’s form when it burns, or to a quality added to them, such as heat added to water when it is warmed by force.

The critic then asks whether God is really simple in every way. The philosophers say that God is pure, simple existence with no separate nature and no parts. But they also say He knows, has power, and wills, and that He is both the knower and the known in different ways. If these descriptions are allowed, why could He not be the source of several things? The rule that only one simple, immaterial thing comes first would no longer follow. A body cannot be the first effect, they say, because it is made of basic matter and form; matter needs form, and form needs matter and other features to become an individual. An accident needs a subject. The first effect must therefore be an immaterial intellect.

I say that anyone who thinks God’s knowledge, power, and other qualities make Him many in any real way has not escaped divided belief about God. We have shown that each quality in Him is, in reality, the same as every other. His knowledge is His power; His power is His will. His being the knower is His being the known, as Avicenna says. This does not make two beings or two points of view in God. The being and the way we consider it are one, though our words put the ideas in a different order.

Someone may say that being the knower and being known are relative to each other, and that two relative terms cannot belong to one thing in the same respect. I answer that not every relation creates an opposition between two things.[^V07-302][^V07-303] A relation simply means that understanding one idea leads us to understand another.[^V07-304] Some relations do involve opposite states, such as moving and being moved, parent and child, earlier and later, greater and smaller, higher and lower. But this does not apply to every relation. “Relation” is one kind of opposition; not every relation is itself an opposition.

More precisely, one of the four kinds of opposition is the opposition between certain related things. It is not the case that every relationship is an opposition.

The critic then offers two more objections. First, philosophers say form is part of the cause of matter, so form must come first. Why not say that form comes from God first, then matter comes through form, as they say the heavenly spheres come from the nine intellects and the matter of the elements from the tenth intellect? Second, why must every soul come into being with a body? Why could a separate heavenly soul not come first and then produce the sphere it governs?

Both objections fail. The first confuses the kind of form that helps produce matter with an individual bodily form. Matter comes to be through a universal form, a form that is still vague and needs a special readiness and features to become an individual form.[^V07-305] Such readiness can occur only in matter as it changes, with one form following another. So bodily or natural forms cannot be the first effects, before changing things exist. A particular form has a certain size, shape, and position. These features either make it the individual thing it is, or mark it so closely that removing them would remove that individual. They cannot all belong to the form by itself, apart from matter. If they did, every body of the same kind would have the same size, shape, and position; there would be no whole and parts. That is impossible.

A vague, unindividualized form could not first come from God and then produce the matter it needs in order to become individual. Every cause, whether near or far, a condition, a part, or an instrument, must be a particular being before it can cause one particular thing.[^V07-306] When philosophers say that form helps cause matter, they mean that form belongs to the changing world, where forms can replace one another. This applies to bodily forms and natural kinds, even to heavenly forms in some respects. We have shown that every natural form changes and comes to be.

So a bodily or natural form cannot be the first thing to proceed from God, existing before the changing world. Every particular bodily form must have a limit, a shape, a definite size, a position, and other features.[^V07-307] These features either make it the individual thing it is or necessarily accompany that individual existence. If they disappear, that individual ceases to be what it was.

Such features cannot belong to the form through its nature alone, apart from matter and the changes matter undergoes. Otherwise, every body of the same kind would have exactly the same size, shape, and position—even a part imagined within a larger body would have to be identical in these respects to the whole. We could then make no distinction between a whole and its parts. That is plainly impossible.

How, then, could an unrestricted form that is not yet an individual be the first thing produced, and become the cause of the very matter it needs in order to exist as an individual? Whether a cause acts directly, from a distance, as a condition, as a part, or as an instrument, it must exist as a particular thing before the effect it helps produce.

The philosophers' claim that form shares in causing matter[^V07-308] is valid only when that form belongs to the world of motion and change. There it can be replaced by another form, and matter can receive the next form as the conditions change. That account does not make a bodily form the first thing to come from a source that has no parts.

You have also learned from our account of how the world comes into being that every natural form is continually renewed. Even the older philosophy describes a bodily form as having two aspects: through one it completes matter, while through the other it depends on matter and undergoes change. Such a form cannot be the first thing to come from God, independent of the matter it needs.

If one form came first and matter came through it, then either the form would be one or there would be many. If there were many, many things would come directly from God. If there were one, some bodies would have to cause other bodies, which proof rules out.

The critic’s comparison with the philosophers’ account of the spheres and elements also fails.[^V07-309] In the first case, one heavenly sphere—a natural whole made of matter and form—comes from one intellect. The intellect itself can be analyzed in thought as having a kind of form and matter, or existence and nature, or completeness and limitation, or independence and need. So a composite comes from a being that can be described as composite, though the effect is more composite and its unity is weaker. This is not like a composite coming from what is simple in every respect.

In the second case, shared matter comes from an intellect with the help of the continuing motion of the heavens. Every moment, that motion has a new state; matter too has a new readiness for the next form. So a series of physical things and movements is really the cause of the shared matter. At most, one vague shared aspect within this series can be traced back to one numerically single cause. That is no comparison to the One who is before every many, with nothing else at His level of existence.[^V07-310]

The second objection is also mistaken. A soul’s being a soul is its way of existing; it is not a relation added after it exists, like being a parent or a ruler. The popular comparison of a soul to a king ruling a city, or a captain steering a ship, has been disproved. A soul cannot exist as an individual before the body it belongs to, either in time or in its own being.[^V07-311] So if it caused the body, it would have to exist before it, which is impossible.

Even by the usual philosophical account, a soul is a being that needs matter in order to act. A being that acts without matter must also exist without matter, so it would be an intellect, not a soul. A soul cannot produce the matter it needs in order to act, or any other matter, before that matter exists. It also cannot produce a particular arrangement in matter before the matter exists. At most, it can cause qualities that come to belong to matter after matter exists. The general rule is that a bodily power, or a form that depends on matter, cannot produce matter—whether it depends on matter to exist or only to act.[^V07-312] So the first thing cannot be a heavenly soul that produces its sphere.

If the other heavenly spheres existed at the same level as that soul, many things would come first from one. If the soul were the outermost sphere containing the others, there would have to be empty space for them to move into. If it were contained within the others, a sphere higher than it would have to exist before or after it began to change. That again requires empty space and would make a lower thing produce a higher one.[^V07-313][^V07-314] These are all guesses with no sound basis.

#### Chapter — The principle that the nobler possible being comes before the lower one

Here is another principle: a possible being that is nobler must come earlier in the order of existence than a less noble possible being. If the less noble one exists, the nobler one must already have existed. This is a powerful principle with many uses. We have made much use of it, by God’s grace. Aristotle’s school used it often in *Theology of Aristotle* and *On the Heavens and the World*. Avicenna built his account of the order of existence and of the outward and return paths on it. The Illuminationist philosopher Suhrawardī developed it in many books. He used it to prove the existence of intellects and of immaterial forms—the guardians of natural kinds.[^V07-315][^V07-316] Muhammad al-Shahrazūrī later explained the principle in detail.

Here is the proof, following Suhrawardī. If a lower possible being comes from God, a nobler possible being must have come before it. If it came at the same level, two things would come directly from the One at once, one nobler and one lower.[^V07-317][^V07-318] If it came after the lower being through that being, the effect would be nobler than its cause. If it could not come before, at the same time, or after, then a possible being would never exist. But if it is possible, there must be a cause for its existence outside its own nature. The cause cannot be lower than God or any of the effects that come from Him. Otherwise it would have to be nobler than the cause that produced the lower being. So the possible nobler being must come from God before the lower one, and the chain of causes must proceed from the noblest to the lower, step by step.[^V07-319]

Suhrawardī’s commentator arranges the choices this way: Suppose a lower possible being exists but a nobler one has not preceded it. Four unacceptable consequences may follow. If the lower being exists through the nobler one, the original supposition is false.[^V07-320] If both can come directly from God, many things come from the One in the same respect. If the nobler one comes through a lower effect, its cause would be less noble than its effect. If it can come neither from God nor from His effects, something nobler than God would be required. Every choice is impossible. So the nobler being must already exist before the lower one. Nothing is nobler than God or His act; therefore, the noblest possible effects are closest to Him, and the causes between Him and lower beings must follow this same order.

A key note on this principle: some writers say it applies only to beings of the same kind, and only to beings above the changing physical world. But existence is one simple reality, with differences of greater and lesser perfection. These differences can give rise to different kinds of things, or to different individuals within one kind. The second kind of difference occurs only among changing things that can develop new abilities. Since all possible beings share the one reality of existence, the rule can prove that every higher level of existence must come before a lower one, even when they do not belong to the same kind.[^V07-321]

The second limit does matter. Otherwise we might wrongly conclude that every person must receive every kind of perfection, even though many people do not reach their full intellectual or bodily abilities. This rule applies to possible beings whose existence lasts as long as their causes last, beyond the effects of heavenly motions and physical conditions. It applies where nothing blocks the cause. In the changing world, other causes can prevent a being from reaching a higher state. A physical thing may be capable of many perfections in itself but be kept from them by other things, depending on its readiness and on the motions of the heavens. But for lasting, immaterial beings, their higher or lower rank follows the rank of their cause. This explains where the principle applies and where it does not.[^V07-322]

A lower thing in the changing world can come before a higher thing in time and in its natural development. A seed comes before a tree, an egg before a chicken, and an embryo before a mature animal. But when we look at what causes them to exist, the higher thing comes first. What is actual comes before what is only possible. The more perfect being is prior in the order of making; the less perfect one comes later. The same is true in other pairs: necessity before possibility, the particular before the general, the defining feature before the broad kind, the individual before the kind, form before matter, unity before multiplicity, continuity before separation, existence before non-existence, good before evil, and truth before falsehood.[^V07-323]

A difficult objection is raised. The proof seems to mix what is possible or impossible in itself with what is possible or impossible in relation to something else.[^V07-324][^V07-325] Perhaps a being that is impossible in itself could still be possible in relation to a nobler possible being, without being made possible by that being. The higher level that lies beyond God’s own perfection may be impossible in itself, the critic says, yet possible in relation[^V07-326] to a nobler possible being that calls for it.

Dawānī states the objection this way in his commentary on *The Temples*: the proof fails unless the possibility of an effect requires the possibility of its cause. But it seems possible for the first effect not to exist, even though the nonexistence of its cause, God, is impossible. Dawānī answers that considering the effect by itself shows that nothing in it makes it impossible. But this answer does not settle the question.

He then gives another version: if a possible nobler being did not exist before a lower possible being, it would not be possible. For if it did exist, it would either come directly from God, though the lower one already does; or it would come through the lower one, which would make the cause lower than its effect. Both are impossible. So the higher being’s possibility would make it impossible—a contradiction. The same difficulty remains: the answer works only if “impossible” includes “impossible because of another thing.” If it means “impossible in itself,” it does not work.[^V07-327]

I answer that possibility follows from existence. What is made real by a cause is the existence of a thing; then the mind considers that existent thing and forms the idea of its nature. We call that nature “possible” when we think of it apart from existence and non-existence. So kinds of things and their possibilities follow the order of what actually exists. Every chain of existence ends in God. There can be no possible thing whose existence, if it were real, would depend on something impossible in itself. That would be a baseless guess.

There is another way to answer. God, who exists necessarily through Himself, cannot lack any degree of perfection or nobility. Every real perfection and every active, positive feature of existence must be in Him in a higher and nobler way.[^V07-328][^V07-329] If we imagine a rank of goodness or perfection above what belongs to God, careful thought shows that it must already be in Him. There can be no possible or impossible feature in God alongside His necessary existence, because that would make Him complex. Every perfection that a possible being can call for must therefore be in God or in something that depends on Him. God is necessary in every respect of existence that can be found in anything. His one reality is complete and beyond complete in every perfection, with unlimited strength and creative power—far beyond what any intellect can grasp.

No holy intellect can fully survey these ranks of perfection, long for all of them in detail, or contain them in its own understanding. Reason can prove that they belong to God, but it cannot picture their full extent.

We cannot imagine any degree of greatness, honor, or endlessness—whether in strength, number, or duration—that is absent from God's simple reality. If a perfection is found anywhere, it must exist in Him in a higher and nobler way, through the necessity of His own being. As argued earlier, every positive aspect of existence belongs to Him not by addition or chance, but necessarily through what He is.

This shows that the proof does not confuse what is possible in itself with what is possible in relation to something else. Dawānī’s own answer to the familiar objection—that a possible effect could fail to exist, even though that would mean its cause did not exist—also fails. Possibility in relation to another applies only when there is no necessary link between the two things. A cause must exist in relation to its effect, and the effect must follow from its cause. Each is necessary in relation to the other.[^V07-330]

The true answer, as I have explained, is that an effect is a way of existing that follows necessarily from its cause. Possibility belongs to a thing’s nature when we consider it by itself, and that nature is not linked to its cause as either existing or not existing. Its possibility therefore entails neither that its cause exists nor that it does not. But when we consider the existence of the thing, that existence is necessarily dependent on God. To imagine that it does not exist would be to affirm both existence and non-existence of the same thing. So a possible nature does not entail an impossible result; the supposed impossible result is impossible in itself.

A puzzle once troubled me about this principle. The intellects are pure lights, all sharing the simple reality of light but differing in strength and weakness, as the Persian and Illuminationist philosophers say. In my own account, they are pure forms of existence, sharing one reality and differing in strength. Either way, there would have to be infinitely many levels between any two intellects, and between God, the Light of Lights, and the first intellect. Since they share one reality, there should always be another rank of light between any two ranks: stronger than the lower one and weaker than the higher one. The rule of the nobler possible being would require every such rank to exist. That would place endlessly many intellects between each pair of intellects, all ordered in levels. This seems impossible. It would also make one endless chain lie between two fixed points.[^V07-331]

I put this puzzle to many learned people of our time, but no one could solve it. Then God lit my heart and opened my sight to the kingdom of heaven and earth through knowledge of myself. Knowledge of the self is the key to the hidden world. When I looked within, I found a simple, self-aware existence. It was not a body, an organ, or a bodily spirit, and it did not contain a mental idea or an abstract nature. All these things can disappear from my awareness; my own self cannot disappear from itself. I also saw that my self was not limited to one fixed rank. Though one and simple, it can understand ideas, imagine images, and sense things through itself.

The mind does not understand or imagine only through tools that act in its place. If the eye or ear were the true knower, then the person using the eye or ear would not know what it was doing. The eye and ear would be separate animals joined to our body. The senses help cause particular experiences, but the self is what knows through them. We will explain this more fully in the discussion of the soul.

The human self, though one in its existence, has levels: intellect, imagination, and sense. Each level has degrees of greater or lesser strength. One person’s sight may be stronger than another’s; one person’s imagination clearer; one person’s intellect more complete. So a simple being can contain many levels without being many beings.

The intellects are even more clearly like this. Each intellect has, within its one existence, many possible ranks and limits. The highest rank is just below the lowest rank of the intellect above it; the lowest rank is just above the highest rank of the intellect below it. These are not separate beings arranged between two others, but levels held together in one complete existence.[^V07-332] If you ask what these intellects are in relation to God, they are all ranks of the divine order and lights of His presence. Their many effects—heavenly spheres and other simple or complex beings—do not divide them. Their degrees of strength, nearness, and perfection explain why each produces higher or lower kinds of nature and bodies. Their lasting existence through God does not prevent the changing world from beginning in time.

The intellects have no beginning in their own order of existence. But in relation to the new things they produce, they change along with those things. A cause exists with its effect in the respect in which it causes that effect. As new effects come to be, this relation is renewed. The way something that does not change in itself can be related to changing things is difficult for many people to grasp, but clear to those whom God has helped.

Here is another point. Existence has two extremes: God, whose perfection and power are without limit, and first matter, which has the least perfection and the greatest readiness to receive form. Existence cannot reach first matter without passing through all the levels between them. Nor can it rise from matter toward God without passing through every middle level in order. Once this is clear, we can prove many middle levels not only through the rule of the nobler possible being but also through a second rule, which we may call the rule of the lower possible being.

By the first rule we can prove that earth and the other simple elements have an intellectual guide and a form in the world of images.[^V07-333] By the rule of the lower possible being, we can then show that they also have plant-like souls, life, and imagination in the world between intellect and matter.[^V07-334] The same rule shows that a body capable of direct movement comes after the animal soul and before circular movement, which is an added quality.

Plants also have a basic nature that preserves their structure and balance. This nature works on matter through the plant soul. These powers are not qualities added to a body, as people usually think,[^V07-335] but beings whose existence is joined to what they can do.

#### Chapter — What follows from the principles established so far

God’s being is perfect, fully active, and necessary, with no trace of absence or lack. So the first thing to come from Him must be the noblest being that has no trace of absence or lack. It must belong to the world of intellect, not the world of souls, still less the world of nature.

A soul contains two kinds of absence. One is an absence that exists only when the mind analyzes it. It has no separate reality outside the mind. The mind considers any created being, except the fully simple reality of God, and separates its existence from the particular limit that gives it this form. A soul can then seem made up of existence and non-existence, completeness and lack, good and evil, light and darkness, necessity and possibility, nature and individual existence. But in reality its absence is swallowed up by its existence, its evil by its good, its lack by its completeness, its darkness by its light, and its possibility by necessity. Its nature is held within its actual existence. These are only ways the mind considers it; there is no real absence, evil, lack, darkness, possibility, or separate nature in God.

The second kind is real absence: the lack within a thing that is still developing. A soul is actual in one way, but still has a perfection it may gain. If it had no perfection left to reach, it would already be an intellect, not a soul. So a soul is partly actual and partly only possible. It cannot be the first thing after God.

Natural forms that depend on matter are even further from being the first effect. They contain the two kinds of absence found in souls, and a third: their existence depends on matter in two different ways. Matter is the readiness through which a form comes to be, and the form is one of the causes that makes matter actual. The first kind of absence is the potential existence of what has not yet come to be; the second is the matter that comes before the form. The first is prior in nature;[^V07-336] the second is prior in its own being.[^V07-337] A natural form is surrounded by these two kinds of absence at every moment[^V07-338] because it comes into being and continues through stages.

A soul differs from a natural form[^V07-339] because it remains the same in its own nature while its link to a body changes. It has both an intellectual, lasting aspect and a changing aspect through which it governs the body. A natural form, by contrast, is itself a changing link to matter.[^V07-340]

A body’s measurable shape has even more kinds of absence. It lacks the fullest perfection, it lacks the perfection it may later gain, it is absent from itself at different times, and it is absent from itself in different places.[^V07-341] Matter considered before it receives any particular form is the capacity to receive one.

Aristotle counts the absence of a form among the three principles involved when things come into being, alongside form and matter.[^V07-342] The absence before a new thing comes to be is joined to the earlier part of its matter.[^V07-343] Matter itself is actual as what has the readiness to receive a new form, but only potentially what it will become. In this sense it exists in itself, while also being absent in relation to what may come next.[^V07-344]

A feature that depends on something else must come after the thing it belongs to, just as matter depends on form. Such a feature is even more dependent than matter, since matter at least has some priority in relation to form. So no dependent feature can be the first effect. God has no separate quality, whether eternal or newly added to Him. The Ashʿarites and Karrāmī theologians, for example, claimed that His will could be eternal or newly formed. The first thing after God must therefore be one being, independent in both what it is and what it does. It can only be a pure intellect, since matter has no actual existence, body has no unity, form and dependent features require something else in order to exist, and soul depends on something else in its action.[^V07-345]

There are many ways to prove that this separate, holy being stands between God and the created world and carries His goodness and generosity to all things.[^V07-346]

The first is through prophecy and inspiration. The Prophet called it “the first thing God created,” the Intellect, and “the first thing God created,” the Pen, because it draws the forms of things and orders the world, just as a pen draws words and orders them. He also said, “The first thing God created was my light,” and, “I and ʿAlī were created from one light.”[^V07-347]

The second proof comes from the rule that many things cannot come directly from the One.[^V07-348] The nearest thing to God must be one, simple, free of physical readiness, and independent in both what it is and what it does. It must be able to carry the gift of existence from the true Giver to all other things. It cannot be a body, matter, form, or soul. It must be an intellect and a holy, immaterial being.

The third proof uses the rule of the nobler possible being. Intellect is nobler than every other possible being.[^V07-349] It is one level in the single reality of existence. So even if it is not a separate kind in the way we use that word for things in the mind, it is still a higher level in the same reality. The highest intellectual being must therefore come before every lower being.[^V07-350]

The fourth proof is the natural fit between a cause and its effect. The fit between the first cause and its first effect must be the closest and most complete possible. The first effect must therefore be the noblest intellect and the simplest, brightest immaterial being after God.

The fifth proof concerns how a soul reaches the knowledge it has only in potential. When something has the ability to reach a perfection but has not yet reached it, something else must bring it from possibility to actuality. Otherwise, it would make itself complete, which is impossible: if it could do that, it would already be complete. It would also be both less complete and more complete than itself at once.

So the human soul cannot bring itself from basic, potential understanding to actual understanding, even when it has prepared itself through learning and practice.[^V07-351] It needs an immaterial teacher and giver of forms between God and the souls that receive knowledge. This giver must be free of potential and change, or it too would need another being to complete it, leading to an endless chain. It cannot be God Himself without an intermediary, since there are many souls but God is absolutely one. So there must be an intellectual being between God and the souls that receive from Him.

The active intellect relates to the soul’s readiness for understanding as sunlight relates to the eye. Sunlight makes sight actual, and makes the things seen visible. The eye and what it sees both become actual through the light.[^V07-352] In the same way, the soul and what it understands become actual through the intellect that gives knowledge.

The sixth proof comes from the relation between matter and form. For matter to exist, it needs a form in general, not any one fixed kind of form. It also needs an immaterial intellect.[^V07-353] When one form passes away, this intellect supplies another in its place. Matter can therefore remain one and the same individual through a changing succession of forms. The forms provide one kind of continuity as they replace one another; the enduring intellect provides another, unchanging kind. We explained this earlier when discussing how matter and form depend on one another.

The seventh proof comes from changing nature. Nature changes in its individual existence, though its kind remains the same. The cause of this change cannot be God directly, for He is one in every respect. Nor can it be another changing nature: that would leave us asking the same question again and lead either to an endless chain of causes or to causes circling back on themselves. It cannot be a soul, because the soul, insofar as it is tied to the body and develops through it, also changes and passes from one state to another. Nor can the cause be matter, which receives rather than produces forms. It must be an immaterial intellect, a divine form and a noble angel.[^V07-354]

The eighth proof comes from desire and purpose. Every natural kind is drawn toward a final perfection that belongs to the world of intellect. Otherwise, the natural desire to reach completion would be pointless, and nature does nothing in vain. Some things may be prevented from reaching their goal, but such obstacles are not usual or lasting. The final perfections toward which things move cannot be souls, which still long for a more complete state, or God, who is one while these perfections are many. They must be intellects. Just as an intellectual being must stand between God and created things at the beginning of their descent, so intellectual beings must stand between created things and God at the end of their return.

The ninth proof starts with beings whose nature is complete enough to receive existence without first becoming ready through matter.[^V07-355] Their own possibility is enough for the Giver to bring them into being.[^V07-356] They therefore come before time and before things that move, occupy places, or depend on bodily conditions. This is a priority in being, not an earlier date. Nothing in their nature prevents them from receiving existence, and nothing can prevent the Giver from giving it. They therefore receive existence without interruption.

Other kinds of things need matter to be ready before any particular individual can exist. Their individuals depend on particular materials, places, and times. Yet their natures, considered as kinds rather than as individual members, also receive their existence continually from the Giver. The giving is constant even though its recipients, considered one by one, come into being and pass away. Thus the forms of different kinds flow from God continually,[^V07-357] while the bodily world and the things within it arise in time after not existing.

Since the First Reality is one, there must be immaterial intellects between Him and the different kinds of material things. These intellects have distinct ways of acting through which different kinds can receive existence. Matter can make many individuals of one kind, and different material conditions can distinguish those individuals. But matter and its readiness cannot, by themselves, account for the existence of different kinds with different natures.[^V07-358]

This also proves that an intellectual world exists and contains many forms. These forms do not stand apart from God’s being; they are divine knowledge within the realm of His rule.

The tenth proof comes from the motion of the heavenly spheres.[^V07-359] Anything that moves must be moved by something other than itself. Otherwise, the same thing would be both what is moved and what moves it, and what completes it would itself need completion. If the mover also moves, another mover is needed, and so on without end. We must reach a mover that does not move at all, an actual being that does not change by nature, force, divine direction, or choice. It moves the sphere either by giving it its first impulse or by serving as the beloved model that the moving sphere seeks to imitate.

Second, the circular motion of the heavenly spheres cannot be natural motion; it must come from a soul that knows and chooses. But the spheres do not have bodily appetites or anger. They need no food and have no enemy. Their movement must aim at an intellectual good. It cannot be a low or uncertain goal, and it cannot be a bodily benefit. It must be a high intellectual perfection. The spheres move not to become God, but to imitate many beloved intellects. All seek the First Beloved in their shared circular movement, but each also has its own beloved, which guides its particular movement and helps it draw nearer to God.

Third, the heavenly motions never stop. Their source must have unlimited power. No bodily power, whether in a soul or in a form within matter, can produce endless motion. So their source must be an immaterial power. These three arguments show that there are intellects, not just one.[^V07-360]

The eleventh proof comes from the agreement between true judgments in our minds and what is true in itself. We know that some judgments are true and match reality, while others held by ignorant people do not. This match can occur only between two different things that agree in the relevant respect. Both kinds of judgment exist in the mind, but only true judgments also match something outside our minds. That reality is what we call “what is true in itself.”

This reality outside us must either stand on its own or exist within something else. If it stands on its own, it is either physical or not physical. It cannot be physical. These judgments do not belong to any place or time, but everything physical has a place and time. We can also know that they match even when we know nothing about a physical thing. And we grasp these judgments through intellect; we grasp physical things through the senses. The reality cannot be a nonphysical being standing on its own either, since that would be Plato’s separate Forms.[^V07-361][^V07-362]

So the truth our judgments match must exist within something that does not divide. It cannot exist there only as a possibility, even if some judgments are only possible in our minds. A possible truth cannot match a truth that is already real. Nor can it change from possibility into actuality, because these truths must be true forever, without change or limits of time and place. Their source must also exist in an unchanging way. So there must be an independent, nonphysical being that contains all the truths our minds can reach—the truths that remain unchanged forever.[^V07-363]

That being cannot be God, who is absolutely one. It must contain many truths, and God cannot contain many things as parts or as separate objects of knowledge. So there must be another being, which we may call the Universal Intellect. The Qur'an calls it the Preserved Tablet, or the Clear Book that contains all things, fresh and dry. This is what I wanted to establish.

We accept Plato’s separate Forms, as we have already explained, but this proof does not depend on them. It establishes a world of intellect in which the enduring truths are present, whether they exist within an intellectual being or stand on their own.

The twelfth proof comes from the difference between complete and incomplete beings. In thought, things fall into four groups: complete or incomplete; the complete may be more than complete or simply complete; the incomplete may be able to reach completion by itself, with something that belongs to it, or only with something outside it. Material things are incomplete. The heavenly spheres can reach their own completion. God is beyond complete. There must therefore be a complete being between God and material things: the intellect. If God, who is beyond complete, directly created something incomplete, there would be no fit between the Giver and what receives existence. There would also be a gap in the order of existence. Our rule about the lower possible being has already shown that no level can be skipped.

As support, Aristotle’s school says in the tenth book of *Theology of Aristotle*:

> The One, who has no parts, is the cause of all things. He is not any one of them, but their beginning. He is not the things, but all things are in Him. They all flow from Him, are held together by Him, and return to Him. If someone asks how all things can come from the simple One, who has no two-ness or multiplicity in any respect, we answer: because He is absolutely one and simple, with none of the things in Him. Precisely because He is absolutely one, all things flow from Him. Since He has no separate nature, all natures flow from Him.

In short: because the One is not any one thing, all things are seen to come from Him. Yet the first nature to flow from Him without an intermediary is the nature of intellect. From it, all the natures of the higher and lower worlds flow. The pure One is beyond complete perfection; the world of sense is incomplete, made by the complete intellect. The intellect is complete because it comes from the One who is beyond complete. What is beyond complete cannot make something incomplete without an intermediary, and one complete being cannot make another equal to itself. The maker must be lower than what it makes.[^V07-364][^V07-365]
