You are now prepared to understand this teacher’s words. When he says that “no separate nature” flows from the One, he likely means no separate existence, not no general nature. A general nature is not made. He means an individual existence that is distinct from other individual existences. Created beings each have existence limited by a degree of perfection, so each differs from the others. God’s existence is unlimited in power and intensity. It contains every level of existence and has nothing outside it to be set against it. Where God is, all things are in Him[^V07-366]; where any created thing is, God is with it. The Qur'an says, “He is God in heaven and God on earth,” “He is with you wherever you are,” and “There is no private talk among three without Him being the fourth, or among five without Him being the sixth.” A saying reliably attributed to ʿAlī puts it this way: “He is with every thing, but not by being beside it; and He is other than every thing, but not by being apart from it.”

These are the proofs for the separate intellect and its world. I will later explain how many intellects there are, how well ordered their world is, and how it contains every kind found here, with many further perfections. I have already mentioned this in the discussion of nature.

We have another way to show that intellectual forms are stored in the world of intellect. God says, “There is nothing whose storehouses are not with Us,” and, “To God belong the storehouses of the heavens and earth.” We may know something, forget it completely, and need to learn it again. Or we may lose sight of it for a time, then remember it without learning it again. In both cases, the image of the thing is no longer present in our active awareness. If it were there, we would know it, since knowing means that the image of a thing is present to the knowing power.

But the two cases differ. When we lose sight of something but can remember it without new learning, its image has left our active awareness but remains in a power that keeps it. When we forget it completely, it has left both. In the second case, the mind has lost its readiness to connect with the power that gives knowledge. The senses can be divided, so one place in the brain may receive images while another keeps them. This is the common account. But our intellect cannot be divided into one place for receiving knowledge and another for keeping it.

So a separate intellectual being must store the images our minds understand. When the soul turns toward it, it receives an image that fits its nature. When the soul turns toward the body or to another image, the earlier one disappears from awareness. It is like a mirror that has been cleaned and turned toward a picture: while it faces the picture, the image appears in it, or the light from the picture reaches it. This is how two philosophers explain the relation between the mind and the intellectual world: one says the image shines into us, the other that it flows into us.[^V07-367] I have already said that neither account is the one I accept. I say that the soul joins with the intellectual being itself.

If the mind has been cleared and the heart made pure, it keeps the ability to reconnect with the image it once understood. It can recall it without learning it again. The image was out of mind, but not forgotten. The mind has kept its connection and its readiness to receive it. If the mind becomes clouded again by the body and nature, it needs new effort to clear the barrier and reconnect. So there is an intellectual being that keeps all the forms our minds can understand. This is what I wanted to prove.

#### Closing clarification — Resolving a difficult objection

This argument has an objection about lapses of attention and forgetting.[^V07-368] Some thinkers found it so difficult that one of Tusi’s students could not answer it. Ḥillī recounts the exchange in his commentary on *The Guide to Belief*:

> While I was studying with him, I once asked what people mean when they say that a judgment in the mind is true when it matches what is true in itself. What is true in itself is either something in the mind or something outside it, but he had rejected both. He replied that “what is true in itself” means the Active Intellect. Every image or judgment in the mind is true if it matches the images impressed in the Active Intellect; otherwise it is false. I objected that the philosophers themselves must then say false images are impressed in the Active Intellect. They argued that it exists by pointing to the difference between losing sight of an idea and forgetting it. Losing sight means an idea leaves the knowing intellect but remains in the power that keeps it; forgetting means it leaves both. This can happen with sensory images, they say, but in the case of ideas it happens when the mind loses its readiness to receive knowledge. Yet we can lose sight of and forget false ideas too.[^V07-369]

> He did not give a satisfactory answer.[^V07-370]

Dawānī answers that the Active Intellect stores both true and false ideas, but it agrees with and affirms only the true ones.[^V07-371] It keeps false ideas only as pictures, without accepting them as true.[^V07-372]

I say that ideas in the Active Intellect are more settled and certain than those in our minds. When two things are linked in our minds, the link may be weak because its cause or proof is weak. We may not know whether they truly belong together, or the link may be false. But when they are linked in the Active Intellect, their connection is firm and necessary, like the connection between things in the world.[^V07-373][^V07-374]

Second, imagining and affirming are two kinds of knowledge that arise in our changing, bodily minds. The higher sources of knowledge, and God’s own knowledge, are neither pictures in a mind nor judgments. They are the presence of the knower and the known to each other. Their necessary qualities are present with them, without any further act of learning or creation, as our knowledge of ourselves and our own qualities is present with us.[^V07-375]

To settle this objection, I must first explain that every lasting ability in the human soul—whether it is a way of knowing or a practical skill such as writing, trading, or farming—comes from a special link between the soul and the Active Intellect. It comes from the Active Intellect through one of its active features. Different kinds of things cannot arise only because their receivers differ or can receive different things. They must have either many separate intellectual causes, as Plato’s followers say, or many active features in the last intellect, as Aristotle’s followers say.

The Active Intellect contains all these different abilities in a higher and simpler way. This is true even of things called bad, since their badness lies in the loss or absence of something else. Acts such as adultery and theft are real actions, even though they harm what ought to be protected. A lie and a firmly held false belief are also real states in the soul; in themselves, they are among the things a living creature can do. They become harmful when compared with a higher soul that needs steady knowledge and truth. In themselves, they are positive states, but in relation to that higher soul they are faults.

Once this is clear,[^V07-376] not every ability or quality in the human soul must exist in exactly the same form in the Active Intellect. It is enough that something there fits each one. Repeated attention to true knowledge connects the soul to one side of the Active Intellect. In the same way, a false belief or a strong focus on it can connect the soul to another side. The side it reaches does not need to be the very same false statement. There only needs to be a feature that fits it. This is what it means to say that the intellectual world stores the forms of things and that the soul can recover them.[^V07-377]

We have explained more than once that images in the simple intellect are not stored as many separate images, as they are in physical matter or in the detailed imagination. The world of intellect is too full and complete for that kind of separation, absence, and division.

### Station Ten — God’s unceasing generosity and eternal power

Nothing outside Him ever keeps God from acting, even though the world is always changing, coming into being, and passing away. The only things that never perish, diminish, or run out are God’s complete words and lasting knowledge. As we have often said, these do not belong to the world or to anything apart from the Real.

#### Chapter — Why this question matters, and why God’s unceasing giving is compatible with a world that comes into being and changes

This is one of the most important questions in both philosophy and religion.[^V07-378] We cannot know God, affirm His oneness, or understand Him as free from multiplicity and defect unless we grasp it clearly, in a way that fits sound reasoning and the teachings of the prophets. Many people with little understanding imagine that philosophy and rational inquiry conflict with divine law and the prophets’ teachings. They also assume that the great philosophers of old taught that the whole world is eternal: that the heavens, stars, their forms and matter, and even the matter and kinds of earthly elements have always existed as the same individual things, without ever passing away or coming into being. As we explained when discussing substance and its kinds, this is a false charge against those early philosophers. Their view was not the crude one attributed to them, which conflicts with the prophets and their followers.

They did hold that God’s generosity is eternal and His outpouring never stops.[^V07-379] But the world itself is renewed with every breath. This is one meaning of the verse, “Every day He is at work”: His acts and the appearances of His names are always new, as we have already explained. A changing effect can depend on an eternal, unchanging cause only if it is continually renewed: things come to be and endure gradually, while change and loss continue without a break. People commonly say the same about motion: it is a gradual process that exists bit by bit and passes away bit by bit.

In our view, however, motion is not itself an independently existing thing. It is an idea we use for the gradual passage from possibility to actuality. Like other ideas we form by considering real things, it describes an actual thing whose existence unfolds step by step. That thing’s existence is therefore gradual; as it comes to be, it also passes away. Each moment ends as the next begins, and that unbroken succession is what we call continuity. Motion itself does not move.[^V07-380][^V07-381] It means that something else is coming into actuality little by little. Otherwise, motion would need another motion to move it, and another after that, without end.

What changes gradually, in itself, are certain kinds of things. The usual account lists four: quantity, quality, place, and position. A closer account adds substance as a fifth, because material natures and souls joined to natural bodies change in their very being, as we have proved. Every bodily thing in this world—simple or composite, form or matter, heavenly or earthly, soul or nature—has a time before which it did not exist. Each particular existence is preceded by a past without that thing. At the beginning of time there were no bodily individuals or natural beings. Taken one by one, together, or as a complete collection, every one of them was preceded by absence. Each is therefore something that came to be. None is one individual that has existed continuously with an unchanging identity.

The so-called “universal nature”—what a kind of thing is, considered without choosing any one example—does not have its own existence, unity, plurality, continuity, or beginning apart from its individual examples. It follows those examples in every respect: it exists through their existence, is one through their unity, many through their plurality, old through their age, and new through their coming to be.

Once reason shows that every individual example came to be, the universal nature must also be new in reality. It may not be new when we consider only what it means in itself, but for the same reason it is not eternal in itself either. This reveals that a common claim among philosophers—that kinds are eternal because their individual examples succeed one another—is mistaken.[^V07-382] It confuses the idea of a thing with the thing itself.[^V07-383] It also confuses being one in number with being one in meaning.

Many people think this means that one and the same thing remains in existence while different features come and go. When one feature disappears, the underlying thing remains and receives another feature. They imagine it as one body that is white at one time and black at another, hot at one time and cold at another. The body, they say, is the same throughout all these changes.

But this is a serious mistake. Avicenna criticized those who claimed that a universal nature exists outside the mind as one thing, multiplied by its attachments, appearing in many places and at many times. He said that a universal meaning does not relate to its individual examples as one father relates to many sons, but as different fathers relate to different sons. A person does not merely share one separate “humanity” with everyone else. Each person’s humanity is a distinct individual, different in number from everyone else’s. What people share exists only as a meaning in the mind. We discussed this earlier when examining what things are.

A further point follows. Theologians tried to prove that motion, time, and the chain of events have a beginning and an end by comparing their parts and relations. Some followers of the philosophers objected that these successive events never exist together, either in the world or in the imagination, so they cannot be compared all at once or judged to have an endpoint. But they missed a key point: if the whole chain of events does not exist as one thing, we cannot call that whole eternal or without limit either. How, then, can they say that motion and time are eternal, or that events have no beginning?

The right answer is that “eternal” and “without limit” do not apply to the chain as one existing thing. Nor do “begun” and “finite” apply to that nonexistent whole as though it were a single object.[^V07-384] We can, however, say positively of each particular motion, moment of time, and event that it began and has an end. This does not prove that the collection as a whole began.[^V07-385] What is true of every member does not always transfer to a collection as one thing.[^V07-386] Nor does it prove that the whole collection is eternal, as some claim, or that the universal nature is eternal.

Someone might object: if the whole has no end, then it must be infinite; and if a universal nature is not new in itself, then it must be eternal in itself. In answer to the first claim, something that does not exist cannot have any property. A positive claim needs an existing subject. So how could we say of a nonexistent whole that it has some feature, whether that feature is something real or the absence of something—such as blindness, lack of sight, or lack of writing?

A nonexistent thing lacks sight, but it also lacks the condition “not seeing.” The opposite of saying that a thing sees is not to say that it positively has “not-seeing”; it is to deny that seeing belongs to it at all. In the same way, both writing and not-writing are denied of what does not exist. Neither is a property it possesses.

As for the second claim, when we say that a universal nature is not new in itself, we mean that the idea of what a thing is does not include its having come to be. We do not mean that it never comes to be in reality. In fact, it does come to be, because each individual example of it does. Whatever is true of each example is true of that nature as it exists through those examples.

Even if we grant that a nature, considered in itself, is neither new nor old, it does not follow that it must be old or eternal. As we explained earlier, when we consider what a thing is by itself, we deny both sides of a pair of opposites. The kinds whose individual examples come and go are, in reality, new; they are not eternal or everlasting. If a kind were eternal in reality, at least one of its individual examples would have to be eternal, since the kind exists only through its examples. A kind has no feature apart from the features of its examples. Since none of them is eternal or everlasting, neither is the kind.

If we ask whether the kind is eternal or new in itself, we must deny both: in itself it is neither eternal nor new, neither without a beginning nor beginning, neither endless nor finite. It has these features in reality only through its existence in individual examples.

#### Chapter — Another proof that bodies come into being

Here is another way to prove that bodies came into being, different from the argument we gave earlier when discussing substance.[^V07-387] Anything described by a quality must not, in its own existence and in the state in which it can receive that quality, already have the opposite quality. Otherwise one thing would have two conflicting qualities at once, either in thought or in reality.

The first kind of example is what a thing is, considered in itself, and its relation to existence. If a thing’s nature were already nonexistent in itself, existence could not come to belong to it. To receive either existence or nonexistence, its nature must first be undecided between the two. The second kind is a bodily size or extension received by basic matter. Matter cannot already be fixed as having no size or extension; otherwise it could not receive extension and become measurable. The same goes for position and place. If a thing gains a position or place, it cannot already exist in a way that rules both out. Something that exists without size, position, or place is an intelligible, nonbodily thing. How, then, could it become a body and take on these features?

So what can receive such features must first be incomplete and open to possibility. Compared with the features it may receive, its state is like weakness compared with strength or lack compared with fullness. For this reason, we say that matter, in itself, is neither joined nor separate, neither one nor many, neither in a place nor outside place, and neither positioned nor without position.

We must also distinguish between conditions that are necessary for a thing’s existence and identity, and conditions that it can lack in reality. Size, position, place, and time are necessary for a body.[^V07-388] Blackness, heat, writing, and similar qualities are not. The subject that receives a necessary condition cannot have a complete existence without a form that gives it that existence. So the matter that receives body, size, position, and the rest cannot exist fully without a form that brings those features with it.

This differs from receiving a changeable quality such as blackness. The thing that receives blackness must not already be black, white, or fixed in some opposite state. It must be open to either quality. But it can still exist without depending on a color-form to make it what it is. Being black is not its way of existing, nor is blackness necessary to its identity. A body without color is possible; so is an element without felt heat or cold.

With these points established, we can say that existing in time, and being described in terms of when it exists—whether directly or indirectly—is part of how a thing exists. The same is true of existing in place and being described in terms of where it exists. Sound judgment tells us that no thing that exists in time or place can, in its particular existence, be separated from every time and place and remain unchanged, with no difference between moments or locations. Anyone who claims otherwise is going against common sense.

So the body’s being subject to change, with past, present, and future unfolding for it, must come from a form that is part of what makes it exist. The body is not fully real in itself, ready-made before it changes. It can receive these changes only because it is incomplete and open to them. In one respect of reality it can possibly have change or its opposite, but in actual reality it is embodied and extended, with place and position as consequences of those features.[^V07-389][^V07-390][^V07-391]

We have already distinguished between not having a quality in reality and not having it at one particular level of reality.[^V07-392] The first does not imply the second. The same distinction applies to having a quality in reality and having it at every level. A nature may exist in reality without existing in itself, considered apart from its examples. Likewise, matter is bodily and extended in reality, yet it is not a body or an extension in itself, in the state where it can receive those features.[^V07-393]

Anything that belongs to a thing considered in itself must belong to it in reality. The reverse does not always follow. This is especially clear when we deny a quality: something can be true of a thing in reality while not being true of it at the level where we consider it simply as a possibility.

It is now clear that change and renewal are not qualities that a body can simply do without, like blackness or heat.[^V07-394] Change is a basic part of the body’s form and identity, or of the conditions that make its existence and individuality possible. So the form of every body must itself be changing and renewing. Its nature, which completes it and makes it the kind of thing it is, must unfold over time. It comes into being and passes away, again and again. This is true of bodies as they exist in the world, even if it is not true of the abstract idea of a body or of matter considered only as the possibility of receiving a form.

In short, the features people assign to motion and time at the level of ideas—passing away, coming into being, continuing to renew themselves, and enduring through a sequence of new moments—also belong to natural bodies in their actual, individual existence. Every thing in this world exists in time and change, just as it exists in a place. Both “when” and “where” apply to it.

Some features of a body come after change and time, such as color, heat, motion, and other states that arise after a thing did not have them and later pass away. Other things exist along with change and time, such as the natures of the heavenly bodies. Their relation to time is part of their own existence.[^V07-395]

The body’s relation to time is not like that of an unchanging intellect to time, as many people have thought. They say the body is always moving or resting, and both involve change: motion changes directly, while rest changes only in thought. If a resting body were moving instead, they say, its motion would have taken some amount of time. So even when the body itself does not change, it is “with” time rather than “in” time, as the intellect is. But the body is then in time only because it can move or rest, they say.[^V07-396]

They have missed that something whose existence and identity are truly unchanging, like a separate intellect,[^V07-397] cannot be a subject of change or motion. Something can be neither moving nor resting only when we consider it as an unfinished idea in the mind, or as matter in a state that is not yet complete in itself. This allows it to be open to either change or rest in reality. But if a body were fixed in its nature and identity as an actual thing, it could not be described as changing or moving. It could not even be described as resting, because rest is the lack of motion in something that can move.

The mistake comes from failing to understand existence and actual identity. These philosophers treat existence and individuality as ideas in the mind with no real counterpart outside it. They overlook that existence is the actual reality itself, not an idea that represents some other thing.[^V07-398]

When they compare what a body is with what motion and time are,[^V07-399] they see that motion and its duration are not part of the definition of a body. So they treat them as qualities added to it. They make the same claim about existence: it is something added to what a thing is. This leads them to deny that existence is real outside the mind. Their reasoning is that a quality cannot exist before the thing that has it; but if existence is added to a nature, that nature must already exist before existence itself. Then we ask what gives existence to that earlier thing, and the argument circles back or continues forever. They conclude that a thing’s nature has existence only in the mind.

But this does not solve the problem. The same endless chain or circle arises whether the added quality is said to exist in thought or outside it.[^V07-400] The truth is that existence is not a feature added to a finished nature in the way color is added to a body. It is the real presence that lights up the heart of anyone God wills.

In short, these philosophers did not distinguish between features of a thing’s definition and features of its actual existence. A feature can belong to a thing in reality even if, in thought, its concept is outside the thing’s definition. That does not prevent the feature and the thing from being one in reality. Existence itself is such a feature: it belongs to a nature because that nature exists, not as an extra feature added to an already existing thing. The same applies to a species-making feature in relation to a broader kind, and to individuality in relation to a kind.

So although we can think of a body’s gradual existence as something separate from the idea of a body, it does not follow that it is separate from the body’s actual individual existence.

Here is the argument in brief. What undergoes change and motion must be one of three things: something that stays fixed through time; something that changes; or something that is neither fixed nor changing. The first is impossible: a thing separate from every time cannot receive a change that makes it belong to one particular time.

The second option divides into two cases. If change exists as something added to its subject, we must ask about the subject again, and an endless chain or circle follows. If the change is not added, it must be either identical to the thing’s nature or identical to its existence. The first is impossible, since if change were simply the definition of motion, the subject of motion would itself be motion. Nothing can be a quality of itself.[^V07-401][^V07-402] We have also shown that motion itself does not move and that there cannot be motion within motion. So the change must be identical with the thing’s actual existence. That is the conclusion we want.

The third option is that what undergoes change is the thing’s form or its matter. It cannot be the form, because what is already actual and changing cannot have a form that is unchanging and fixed.[^V07-403] It must be the matter. Matter is pure potential: in itself it is neither changing nor fixed, neither moving nor resting, even though in reality it changes through the form it receives.

Someone might suggest a fourth option: perhaps the whole thing, matter and form together, is what changes.[^V07-404] But we have explained many times that a thing is made complete by its form, not by its matter. The form has the same standing as the whole, especially if matter and form are understood as joined into one.[^V07-405]

We have now proved that every body is continually coming into being in its very existence. Its form is itself a process of change. Each body has come into being in time and can pass away. Neither individual bodily identities, universal natures, nor general concepts last forever. A universal idea has no existence outside individual examples. A universal nature exists only as those many individual examples, each of which has come into being. The many examples do not exist together as one collection outside the mind, so there is no single collection that can be called new or eternal. If the collection did have an existence separate from its members, it would have even more reason to be new.[^V07-406] In truth, it exists only when the mind imagines the members as one thing. But the imagination cannot hold an unlimited number of things all at once.[^V07-407]

A universal nature differs from a collection. It exists in each individual, so it can be described as existing and as new. A collection has no existence of its own, because existence goes with unity; the collection exists only as the many existences of its members. Nor does it exist in any one member. So it cannot be described as new or eternal, any more than it can be called an existing thing.

We do accept that general intelligible forms exist through the separate divine forms, as Plato and other wise philosophers taught. But those forms are not part of the world or anything apart from God. They are forms of God’s knowledge and His complete words,[^V07-408] which never perish or run out. As the Qur'an says: “What you have will pass away, but what is with God remains”; and “If the sea were ink for my Lord’s words, the sea would run dry before my Lord’s words ran out.” The world, then, with all its parts—the heavens and stars, simple and composite things alike—comes into being and passes away. At every moment, whatever exists is a new creation, as we explained earlier in our discussions of nature and what lies beyond it. God knows best.

#### Chapter — The theologians’ arguments and some of their views on this question

As we explained in the general study of nature, things need a cause because their essence is possible and their existence is incomplete—not simply because they began to exist, as some people think.[^V07-409] The philosophers argue that if God, by His own nature, is the reason possible things exist—either by His nature alone or together with an attribute, as theologians who accept God’s eternal attributes say—then He is prior to all possible things, since He is their cause. If the cause is eternal, its causing must also be eternal. Whatever makes God the maker of the world—a time, the removal of an obstacle, a condition, a will, a natural disposition, a power, or any other state proposed by theologians—must be either eternal or new.

If it is eternal, the world must also be eternal, since an effect cannot fail to follow from a complete cause. If it is new, it must have a new cause; otherwise it would have come to be without a cause. Then we must ask what caused that new cause, and what caused the cause before it. This chain would continue without end, so there would be events with no first event.[^V07-410]

As we have shown, the Necessary Being is one in every respect,[^V07-411] unchanging and unaltered, with no change in His state.[^V07-412] If nothing ever came from Him and His state remained as it was, then the world would never come to be. If a new state in Him brought the world into existence, that is impossible: in sheer nonexistence there is nothing that makes the world’s existence more fitting, or God’s act of creating it more fitting, or some other state more fitting that would then require creation. His state does not change. This is the philosophers’ argument that the world is eternal.

I say that all these premises are true, but they do not prove that the world is eternal. A nature whose existence is renewed has its stability through that very renewal. What it has already become is bound up with what it can still become; its existence includes successive states that pass away, and even its completeness remains limited. It depends on its fully active cause—the source that always gives existence and never stops causing—because of what is stable, actual, existing, and complete in it, not because of its renewal, unfulfilled capacities, deficiencies, or lack. These last features belong to its own nature and are not made by a cause, as we have explained of features that follow necessarily from what a thing is.

An effect need not share its cause’s kind of existence or stability. It is an effect because its existence falls short of the existence of its active cause; absence enters into the effect’s being. If coming into being were an extra quality added to an existing thing, one could ask why that quality belonged to it at one time and not another. But if coming into being and renewal belong to the thing’s nature, without being separately made, then they are like other natures that come from a cause whose own existence and giving are eternal. God’s creating a human being does not make God human or make the human being God. In the same way, an eternal cause producing a being that is new in its very identity does not make the new being eternal or the cause new. The coming-to-be of a continually renewed being belongs to its own nature, as a defining feature belongs to what a thing is; no separate act of creation lies between the thing and that feature. This is the right answer to philosophers who deny that the world and everything in it came into being.

The theologians of the religious communities fall into two groups. The first deny that things truly cause one another and say that God acts by sheer choice. Scholars have no argument to make with them, because all inquiry depends on causes and effects and on true premises leading necessarily to their conclusions. If a valid argument did not necessarily yield its conclusion, knowledge would be impossible. Without knowledge, we could not trust that anything is real, have confidence in anything, or pursue any goal. Religions, beliefs, and human efforts would all be pointless: an action could lead to the opposite of its aim, or an effort could bring harm instead of benefit. This view comes close to the Sophists’ denial that anything can be known.

The second group accepts causes and effects and rejects choosing one outcome without a reason for choosing it. Some of them say that God has attributes beyond His essence, such as knowledge, will, life, and power. They say God creates the world by His will[^V07-413], and that some factor must explain why He wills it. Some say this factor is a benefit for the world. As we explained when discussing causes, that does not work. What benefit is there in not having many more revolutions of the heavens before the number that actually occurred?[^V07-414] Others say the deciding factor is the time itself. That also fails: the reason that selects a time must exist before the time selected, so the same question arises about it.[^V07-415]

The Karrāmiyya say that a new will exists in God. This would make God subject to changing events, which is impossible for two reasons. First, it would mean that His essence has both an active side and a receptive side. These two sides would make Him composite, as we have explained. But God, whose existence depends on nothing, is not made up of parts. Second, if a new will arose in Him[^V07-416] and later ceased, either His essence caused it—in which case it should never cease—or something opposed to it ended it. That opposition would itself need a cause, and the will’s end would need another new cause. Its cause would never stop being new, and the cause that ended it would never stop being new either. Such a series is motion that never ceases: philosophers place it in the rotating heavenly body; we place it in nature as it exists in the physical world. Either way, its subject must be bodily matter. If these changing wills existed in God Himself[^V07-417], He would have to be a body that moves continuously in a circle. He would also be continually changing and coming into being, and would need another god to preserve Him through an endless succession of similar states. Exalted is the One who sustains all things, far above these misleading fantasies and the errors of imagination. If these wills existed outside God,[^V07-418] they would be among His effects, and the Necessary Being would be continually acted upon by a changing effect. That too is impossible. Once we reject a new will in God, we must also reject the claim that He has infinitely many new wills, each making Him act, until one special will finally brings this world into being. That claim does not avoid an endless chain of events; it says only that the world, including minds, souls, and bodies, came into being through one of those new wills.

Among those who say that will comes into being are Abū ʿAlī al-Jubbāʾī, Abū Hāshim, Qāḍī ʿAbd al-Jabbār al-Hamadhānī, and their followers. They say that will exists without being in a subject. That is impossible: if willing is a feature that exists in something else, it needs something in which to exist. How, then, could it exist in itself? Perhaps they mean by “will” some other, substantial thing. But then it would be part of the world, and the question of its coming into being would return.

Others say that will is eternal. They claim that God’s eternal will caused the world, and that although His will is eternal, He willed the world to begin at the time it did. His will was not directed to its beginning at another time. They say it is wrong to ask why He made the world then rather than at another time, because His will by its nature selected that time. A feature that belongs necessarily to a nature, they say, should not be explained by anything outside that nature.[^V07-419]

But will needs a reason that directs it. All times are alike, and in sheer nonexistence nothing differs from anything else. Nothing there makes one time more suitable for the world’s creation. Whatever someone places before the world as its cause—a new will, a time, the removal of an obstacle, a new object of knowledge, a benefit, or any other state—must itself have come into being and need a reason. The same question returns, and the chain of events has no first member.

Some theologians report the philosophers as saying that God’s bringing this world into existence at one time is no more fitting than bringing it into existence at an earlier or later time, so it could not exist at the time it did. This report is mistaken.[^V07-420] The philosophers agree that God is prior to all possible things in His own being. Time is itself one of those possible things: for them, it is measured by the heavens’ circular motion; for us, by nature’s self-renewing existence. Time, motion, and their subject cannot be prior to every possible thing, or a thing would be prior to itself. That is impossible. We will later discuss how God is prior to all possible things in time.

A reminder: no one can say that God’s eternal will made the world exist at the time it did, while before then the same eternal will did not require the world to exist.[^V07-421] Consider our own will: if today we decide to do something a year from now, our present will requires it to happen then, not now. But this comparison fails in God’s case. Before the world there is nothing that could make His intended act wait for a certain time,[^V07-422] as changing conditions may prevent us from acting until next year. If someone with enough power could bring about now what he intended to do a year later, and could bring it about together with the time he intended for it, he would do so at once, without waiting.

Before the world there is only sheer nonexistence, the same throughout. Nothing there distinguishes the time selected by God’s eternal will from a similar time. If His will can be directed to this time, it could also be directed to the time before it.[^V07-423] What made this possible time the one His will selected? What set it apart? A thing differs from another only through a reason that selects it, and in sheer nonexistence there is no such reason, as we have explained many times.

What we have said shows that the generosity of the One who gives without limit, and the making and giving of the true Creator, are eternal: He gives to things from beginning to end, always in the same way.[^V07-424] But this does not make possible things eternal—not the whole world, any part of it, any kind, or any individual.[^V07-425] If the philosophers had stopped after proving that the Creator is one, fully active, complete in power, and always generous and merciful—never holding back His care or closing His hand against giving—their view would have been sound.[^V07-426] But later philosophers went further and claimed that the world, the heavens, stars, simple bodies, their forms and souls, and composite beings and their kinds had always existed. They said that kinds of composite beings last forever, though not each individual. With help from God and His higher kingdom, we have proved that this view is false: no created thing and no possible being is eternal.

#### Chapter — Some arguments used by theologians and religious communities to show that God’s outpouring stops

Theologians and religious communities have offered many arguments for their view.[^V07-427]

**First argument.** If there were infinitely many past events—for example, revolutions of the heavens—then each revolution would be preceded by an endless series of times when it did not exist. All those past absences would have to exist together from eternity, without order, since order belongs to things that exist, not to absences. If existing things were present alongside that collection of absences, then what came earlier would be together with what came later, which is impossible. If no existing thing accompanied those absences, then the whole collection of existing things must have had a beginning.

They also put it this way: either some of those events could have existed in eternity or none could. If some could exist then, and no earlier event came before them, the whole series must have a beginning.[^V07-428] These versions do not depend on claiming that what is true of every member is true of the whole. But sometimes they do make that claim: since each motion and event was preceded by nonexistence, the whole series was preceded by nonexistence; or since every event entered existence, the whole series entered existence and must be finite.

The first version is answered by asking what “the preceding absence has no beginning” means.[^V07-429] If it means that the absence is not preceded by any event at all, we deny it.[^V07-430] The nonexistence of each motion is preceded by another event, without end. If it means only that every event is preceded by some absence, but not by one and the same event,[^V07-431] then it does not follow that all those absences existed together from eternity. To be eternal means to have no prior thing at all.[^V07-432] Not being preceded by one particular thing does not mean that nothing precedes it; absence of a narrower claim does not prove absence of a broader one.

The second version is answered by asking whether the common meaning “motion” is counted among the individual motions and events. If it is, we can grant that some motion existed in eternity. That common meaning is what existed. It does not follow that it was the first motion or event; that would follow only if it were the first individual motion. If the common meaning is not counted among the individual events, we can instead say that none of those individual motions existed in eternity. But it does not follow that the whole series had a beginning.[^V07-433] As we explained, each motion and event is preceded by another like it without end, while the common meaning of motion remains through their succession, forever.[^V07-434][^V07-435] There is a difficulty in this answer, so keep it in mind.

We have already shown that the claim “what is true of each is true of the whole” is false unless carefully stated.[^V07-436] Every possible thing apart from motion might be able to exist all at once, but not all possible things can exist at once: some only come to be in a sequence. Likewise, either of two opposites might exist in one place at one time, but both cannot exist there together. A more basic answer is that past events can never form one whole collection. They are gone, and what does not exist cannot make up a whole. Nothing can be stated of that nonexistent whole. At any given time, only one event or a finite number of events exists.

**Second argument.** If past events were infinite, each event could exist only after an infinite number of events had passed. But nothing can exist only after an infinite number of events has been completed. So if past events were infinite, no event could ever exist. That is plainly false.

The answer depends on what “only after” means. If it means that when something does not yet exist, its existence must wait for something else to happen, then the thing that cannot wait is one that would have to follow an infinite sequence that has not yet been completed. Such a thing cannot come to be in the future. But in the past there was never a time when the infinite sequence an event supposedly awaited did not yet exist and then later finished. At every time we choose, infinitely many events already came before it. Every event that depends on motion depends on an endless past.

If “only after” means that no event can occur until an infinite number of events have happened, that is just the point in dispute: our opponent says that every event is preceded by events with no first one. He cannot use this disputed claim to prove itself. That would assume what he is trying to prove, the mistake of assuming the very thing that needs to be proved. In short, if “only after” has its ordinary meaning, the argument does not show that events depend on a future infinity. If it means that an event cannot happen unless infinitely many events came before it, the link is accepted, but the claim that this is impossible has not been proved.

**Third argument.** Past events have a final event, and anything with a final event must be finite. Therefore, past events are finite. The first claim seems clear: the present moment is the last of the past.

The answer is to ask what “last” means. If it means that no time comes after the present, we deny the premise, because the other side accepts endless future moments and times. If it means only that the present is last from our point of view, it does not mean there is no later moment. In the second premise, if “having an end” means that events have a first point, we deny it. If it means having a final point, then the conclusion says only that events with a first point are limited in number from their final point. That does not show they are limited from their first point. The question is whether the past has a beginning, not whether it has a last moment, so this argument misses the issue.

**Fourth argument.** This adapts the claim that physical lengths cannot go on without limit. Take one sequence of past revolutions, from this year back to eternity. Then take another sequence, beginning one year earlier and extending back to eternity. Match the nearer end of one sequence with the corresponding end of the other. Either the shorter sequence must have fewer members at the far end, or it must not. If neither has fewer, though one includes an extra year, they are equal; then a thing would be the same as itself and as something beyond itself, both impossible. If one has fewer members, it must stop after a finite distance from the start of the other. The extra segment is finite, so the longer sequence must be finite too. This would show that past motions and the events that accompany them are finite, with a beginning in eternity.

The answer is that the motions in these two sequences no longer exist. What does not exist cannot form a whole collection. So it is impossible to suppose that both collections existed together in eternity. The impossible assumption that all these successive motions are gathered together does not prove that the past motions stopped. Events that can exist only one after another never exist together as one collection.

**Fifth argument.** If God is the complete cause of the world, then whenever God exists the world must exist, and whenever God does not exist the world must not exist. So if God and the world always imply each other, they must be equal in existence. That is impossible.[^V07-437]

The answer is that the link between a complete cause and its effect does not work both ways. If the complete cause is absent, the effect cannot exist. But the effect’s absence does not by itself make the cause absent. Instead, an absent effect shows that the cause must already have been absent. These are two different kinds of dependence.

**Sixth argument.** In *Al-Muṭāraḥāt*, the philosophers report this argument on behalf of the theologians: You agree that rational souls come into being with their bodies and remain after their bodies die. Once they leave their bodies, they are together. This collection of souls must either have come into being or be eternal. It cannot be eternal, they say, because it is made up of individual souls that came into being. If the causes of a collection are new, the collection must also be new. So the total collection of souls that survive after their bodies die must have come into being. But if past events had no beginning, there would have been infinitely many souls, each born with a body, and no first soul. Then their collection could not have come into being. Yet, as just explained, the collection of souls in the past did come into being. Therefore, past events had a beginning.

The first answer is that the collection of thinking souls from the past may have come into being, but that does not prove past events had a beginning. Every group of individuals makes one collection; add one more individual and a different collection results.[^V07-438] The souls that have come into being may now be thought of as one group, but that group changes continually as new souls arise and join it. At every time a different collection forms.[^V07-439] The same is true of all beings: add a new event to the earlier ones, and the collection changes.

This does not prove that souls have a first member. Every moment brings a new collection, but it does not follow that there was a time when no rational soul existed.[^V07-440]

The second answer is that souls surviving after death do not form one real collection. They are not linked so that we can count or enclose them all. So they do not prove that past events are finite.

The third answer grants, for the sake of argument, that the souls in the past are finite and came into being. Even so, it does not follow that past events are finite. A kind of animal might have appeared at one unusual arrangement of the heavens, though no example of that kind existed before or appeared afterward.[^V07-441] Human beings might be such a kind. Then the human kind would have come into being at one point, though events in the past could still have no beginning.

This answer is deeply troubling, and it is strange to hear it from a wise person. Could divine care fail to preserve the noblest kind of natural being through endless ages, while it preserves less noble kinds by producing one individual after another? The rule of possibility applies to kinds as a whole, even if their individual examples change. Every kind also has an intelligible form in the divine realm. God’s care should preserve its subtle realities and their reflections. For the mystics, that form is a divine name, a manifestation of the First Reality, “who is at work every day.”[^V07-442][^V07-443]

**Seventh argument.** Any number, whether its members are finite or infinite, must be either even or odd. Every even number can be divided into two equal parts. Half is less than the whole, and whatever is less than something else must be finite. So if half is finite, the whole must be finite. If the number is odd, subtract one and what remains is even, so the same reasoning proves the whole finite. Thus the past souls must be finite in number, and past events must have a beginning.

In *Al-Muṭāraḥāt*, the divine philosopher answers that souls separated from bodies do not have one actual number in the outside world. Number is an idea we form; it does not exist as one thing outside the mind. So souls cannot be called even or odd, fewer or more, equal or unequal, or divided into roots and powers. There is no numerical whole of them outside the mind, because they are not joined in reality or gathered in thought. The mind cannot count and picture them all one by one. Even if we grant that they have a number, it does not follow that this number is even or odd. Evenness, oddness, equality, sharing, difference, square roots, and similar features belong to finite numbers, not to number without qualification.

Even if every number must be either even or odd, it does not follow that whatever is less than another number must be finite. That rule fails for potential infinities: an endless series of halves is less than an endless series of thousands. Theologians also say that God’s possible acts are fewer than His known things, even though both are unlimited, since they count impossible things among what God knows but not among what He can do.

I say there are problems with this answer. First, it is not right to say without qualification that number does not exist. This philosopher treats number as an idea because he also treats unity, existence, individuality, possibility, “thingness,” and being known as ideas in the mind. Since number is made by combining units, and he treats units as ideas, he concludes that number is only an idea.[^V07-444] But we have proved that existence is the very reality of each thing that exists, and that a nature exists through it. In thought, existence is real, while a nature considered apart from existence is only an idea. Unity is existence itself, so it is real. The claim that number is only an idea because it is made of conceptual units therefore fails.

Second, even if number is an idea, it does not follow that it is false and has no match in reality. Otherwise, it could not be divided into even and odd, or compared as equal or unequal. These qualities first belong to number, and through number to the things counted. This is clear.

Third, “infinite” has two meanings. One is potential and unending: something can always be divided or increased further. The other means an actual number of infinitely many things. When theologians say God’s possible acts are unlimited, they mean the first kind, not a completed infinite number. They deny an actual infinite, whether the things are ordered or scattered, successive or gathered together. Only a potential infinite can be more or less, as with endless nested halves and quarters that philosophers say can be divided within a body; the second endless series can be half the first.

An eastern reminder. As we said before, unity matters to every thing just as existence does. If many things have no real link with one another, we cannot take them together as one existing thing and make claims about that whole beyond what we say about each member. Each member is real, but the members do not exist as one being; they are many separate existences, each with its own properties.

From this point of view, the argument about the souls is answered: souls separated from bodies have no shared external unity that would let us call them even or odd. The mind can group some of them together, but every group it can picture in detail is finite, since it cannot hold endlessly many things before it one by one. Any finite group of souls is either even or odd. This does not prove that the souls or their events have a beginning.

Some complete souls unite with the Active Intellect; some incomplete, wretched souls descend to Hell and do not rise to the realm of separate souls. Some philosophers, including Avicenna in one of his works, even held that incomplete souls perish after death, a view found in some reports from the Imams. Other souls are scattered among the levels of the next world. So they do not all gather in one place, time, or intelligible bond.[^V07-445]

Here is another point. When two sequences are arranged in order, as lengths or chains of causes and effects are, each has a structure. If we match the first item in one with the first in the other, their remaining members can be matched by that order. We do not need to compare each pair in detail; a general view is enough. But with unordered things, or purely imagined collections, we must compare each item with its counterpart one by one. The mind cannot do this for infinitely many things. Some compare the first two sequences to ropes or long boards: match their starting points and the rest line up. The second kind is like scattered pebbles with no order. The matching argument works only for existing things that have an order.[^V07-446]

The theologians’ comparison arguments fail in several ways. Some replies show that the comparisons do not apply to past events, numbers, or the separate souls that philosophers say survive. Other examples also contradict them: the heavens’ existence from eternity to the Flood is shorter than their existence from eternity to our time, yet both are called endless; God’s knowledge includes impossible things as well as possible ones; and every existent has infinitely many linked qualities, since one quality can itself have another, and so on. A final objection is that the mind cannot survey an infinite series to match its members.

**Review and analysis.** One sharp-minded writer says that an actual infinite number is impossible, whether its members are ordered or not. If infinitely many things existed, he says, their whole would depend on what remained after removing one member; that remainder would depend on what remained after removing one more, and so on.[^V07-447] There would be infinitely many wholes that could be matched with one another. Someone might reply that only many separate things exist in reality, and that the mind alone makes them one whole, so there is no real order for matching. The writer answers that if ten things exist, then nine, eight, and seven are also included in them. The nine, considered as nine, are one group even if the mind does not make them one. That is enough to allow a comparison. End of his argument.

I say, first, that a collection of many things exists only through the existence of its members. It does not have an additional unity of its own. Careful philosophers therefore say that the unity of a number is just its multiplicity: it is a weak kind of being whose form is its matter, just as the actuality of basic matter is its potentiality and readiness. A collection or number has unity only in the mind’s way of viewing it, with no matching unity outside the mind. So no real thing depends on such a collection; what has no real existence cannot depend on anything.[^V07-448]

Second, even if ten exists and depends on its parts, that does not mean it depends on one particular group of nine. Nine plus one makes ten, but so do eight plus two, seven plus three, six plus four, or five plus five. There is no reason to choose one particular nine as the needed starting point. As Avicenna says in *The Healing*, a number depends on units, not on any fixed arrangement of smaller numbers.

Third, even if ten depends on the idea of nine, it does not depend on one particular nine. From any ten objects, we can remove any chosen one and leave a different group of nine. There are ten possible groups of nine, and none is more entitled than the others to be the starting point. Picking one without a reason would itself be an ungrounded choice. The matching argument needs a definite order at every level, as a careful reader will see.

**Eighth argument.** Everything in the world is possible in itself, and every possible thing that exists must have come into being. Therefore the world came into being. A possible thing needs a cause, and the cause must affect it either while it exists, while it does not exist, or at a time that is neither its existence nor its nonexistence. The first is impossible, because it would mean making what already exists. The second is impossible because it would join existence and nonexistence. So the cause must affect it at the moment it comes into being.[^V07-449] Whatever has a cause, then, has come into being; therefore the world came into being.

A better version is this:[^V07-450] the cause affects a thing when it does not exist, when it comes into being, or while it remains. The first two options prove the claim. The third cannot be right, because it would mean the cause is making what already exists. We answer by choosing the third option: the cause acts while the thing exists. Does that mean the cause makes it exist all over again or gives it a new period of existence? No.[^V07-451] The cause brings it into being through this very act, because the effect follows its cause in existence and depends on it. A cause’s influence on a thing means that the thing’s existence depends on that cause.[^V07-452]

There are several reasons why a cause must act while its effect exists. First, if it does not act while the effect exists, it must act either while the effect does not exist—which would join existence and nonexistence—or at some time that is neither of those—which would add a third state. Both are impossible.

Second, possibility is a complete reason for every possible thing to need a cause. If the thing did not need a cause while it continued to exist, either its nature would have changed or an effect would have ceased to depend on its complete cause.

Third, many who use this argument are Ashʿarites, who say God’s attributes are distinct from His essence and that He brings them into existence through His essence. Those attributes are a counterexample: God’s act gives them existence while they already exist.

Fourth, if a possible thing no longer needed a cause once it existed, the world would not cease to exist even if God were removed. They would also have to say that nothing that came into being could ever cease to exist. That is a serious and ugly conclusion. Some accept it, saying God creates “annihilation” to end the world. They would do better to say that since time consists of successive moments, and the body exists in each moment, God creates a new world at every moment.[^V07-453][^V07-454]

Fifth, their proof also shows that things that are eternally nonexistent need a cause to remain nonexistent. A possible thing cannot bring itself into existence, and it cannot make itself cease to exist either. So their view would require causes for nonexistent things.

**Ninth argument.** A later theologian argues that everything apart from God is either immaterial, a body, or something bodily. An immaterial being cannot exist, he says, because if it did, it would share something with God and need a distinguishing feature. Then God would be composite, which is impossible.

A body or anything depending on a body, he says, must either move or remain at rest. Both are new. Motion means changing from one state to another, so it must be preceded by another state. If motion were eternal, it would both have and lack a prior state, which is a contradiction. If every revolution of the heavens were eternal, each would have to be preceded by another, and all those revolutions would be preceded by the absence of earlier ones. Then all those absences would exist together in eternity, so none of the revolutions could exist then. Or, if some motion existed in eternity, it would be prior to every other motion and so would be the first motion. In either case, motion began.

Rest, he says, is remaining in the same place. Remaining there is a positive state of existence, and if it were eternal, it would have to be caused by the Necessary Being. Its complete act would then make it impossible for rest to end. But rest can end, because any body can move.[^V07-455] A simple body’s parts resemble one another, so each could move into the place of another; and a composite body’s simple parts can also move, which would require the whole body to move.

We answer this argument in several ways. First, the argument against immaterial beings would prove that nothing besides God exists. The theologians themselves reject that conclusion.

Second, it conflicts with the arguments that establish the existence of immaterial beings and intelligible forms.

Third, simple beings differ from one another in degrees of completeness within the very existence they share. They do not need an added feature to distinguish them. The popular reply that being immaterial is merely a negative quality, and that sharing a negative quality does not make things composite, is not enough. Immaterial beings share a real kind of existence that differs from bodily existence. Calling knowledge “not being absent from oneself” uses negative words to describe it, but knowledge is still a positive reality.

Fourth, this argument assumes that immateriality is a kind or broader class that belongs essentially to God.[^V07-456] Either way, it would give God a nature, which we have proved impossible. The shared reality of existence, in our account, is not a natural kind or broader kind, nor one of the five kinds of universal concept. A concept that we draw from reality and that exists only in the mind is not an essential part of a thing.

Fifth, at most, the argument would show that God has conceptual parts. The theologians have not proved that this is impossible. This answer challenges them using their own assumptions.

Sixth, even if motion means being preceded by something else, or passing from one state to another, it does not follow that the very idea of motion must itself be preceded by something else. A thing’s nature does not have to be one of its own examples. Saying that a thing is itself is true by its basic meaning, not by the ordinary way we apply a general term to an example. So this nature requires each individual motion to be preceded by another, but that does not prove that the nature or its individuals had a first beginning. A nature’s dependence on something earlier does not mean that it or its individual examples must have a first moment.

Seventh, it has been said that this argument proves only that each separate change began, not that the ongoing process of motion began—the process that remains through an endless succession of separate changes.

Eighth, it has been said that the argument proves only that each particular motion began, not that motion as a kind began. I find both replies doubtful and, in the end, incorrect. Motion considered as a passage between a specific beginning and end has particular agents and subjects, but in itself it is an indeterminate reality with no independent lasting existence.[^V07-457] It lasts only as basic matter lasts,[^V07-458] depending on forms that complete it in kind and in each individual case. A universal nature that appears in successive individuals has no lasting existence in itself either. This discussion goes beyond the stated proof, but points to the real issue.[^V07-459]

The debate would be strong if the theologians could prove that the continuous succession of individual motions leaves one enduring, rotating, bodily subject. We have already explained why they cannot. The sound argument would be: every body and bodily thing, in its very nature, is never free from new events; and everything whose own nature cannot be free from new events has itself come into being. Therefore every body and bodily thing came into being. If they cannot establish this, the proof does not follow.[^V07-460][^V07-461] Perhaps this was what the earliest theologians meant, closer as they were to the time of prophecy and revelation: their view agrees with what the greatest philosophers learned from the light of prophecy.

The ninth reply is that saying every revolution is preceded by eternal nonexistence means only that all their absences are grouped together in this judgment. It does not mean that they occurred at one particular time.[^V07-462] We can also choose that no motion existed in eternity—not that eternity was a particular time when no motion existed, since that would prove nothing.[^V07-463] Or we can choose that some motion existed in eternity. They say this motion would have come before every other motion and so would have had a beginning. We answer: yes, it was first in the sense that no other motion came before it, not in the sense that some prior absence came before all motions. Its being eternal rules out that meaning.[^V07-464]

Those who argue that the world began have other proofs, which I would present with their replies if I were not afraid of making the discussion too long. My purpose is to let readers see the main arguments on both sides, understand what we noticed and why we followed this path, and not mistake our view for a bare appeal to spiritual insight or religious authority without examining proofs. Spiritual insight alone is not enough to guide us without proof; reasoning alone, without spiritual insight, is a serious shortcoming. God is our help.

#### Chapter — How to reconcile revelation and philosophy on God’s unceasing giving and the world’s coming into being

We have often said that true philosophy does not conflict with divine teachings. Both aim at one thing: knowing the First Reality, His qualities, and His acts. We reach this knowledge sometimes through revelation and the prophets’ message, which is called prophecy; at other times through spiritual discipline and learning, which are called wisdom and spiritual authority. Only those who do not know how to understand religious language in light of philosophical proof say that the two paths conflict. A person must be guided by God, fully learned in philosophy, and familiar with the secrets of prophecy to bring them together. Someone may excel at philosophical reasoning yet know little of scripture and religious law, or the reverse.

A clear-minded person who considers the matter fairly, without bias, stubbornness, or evasion, will see that pure minds and cleansed souls have spoken honestly. They were not stained by the ignorance of earlier ages or by selfishness. They did not leave the path of honoring God, speak falsely, or deceive people. God supported them in knowledge and action with remarkable signs and events beyond ordinary experience, without magic, trickery, or fraud. Yet they firmly taught that this world would come to an end: its roof would split, its heavens collapse, its stars fall and lose their light, its earth be laid waste, its seas burst open, and its mountains move and crumble.

They insisted on this even though, on the surface, belief in an eternal world would not prevent belief in individual souls surviving their bodies,[^V07-465] or in reward and punishment after death. So we must be sure they did not speak from whim, but from true knowledge and sincere belief. At the same time, certain rational proofs show that the maker of the world is one and self-sufficient, free of defect or change. He does not shift from one state to another, grow tired of acting, hold back His giving, or wait for a new will or a suitable time.[^V07-466]

Anyone who creates something to gain a benefit—even for someone else—is incomplete and needs something outside himself. If the benefit made no difference to him, it would not explain why he acted. If it did make a difference, he would gain a perfection he lacked. God is therefore complete in His action and will. His essence contains no added purpose or newly arising will. He has always given, and will always give, mercy and existence without limit. The limitation belongs to us, the children of this world of bodies and matter, a passing home whose inhabitants are unjust to themselves.

We can reconcile philosophy and religion on this great question only through the insight God gave us: things in the physical world are continually renewed, and their own existence is never free from new events. God’s giving remains; the world changes and passes away at every moment. It lasts as one likeness takes the place of another, like the breaths that sustain a person’s life. We fail to notice this because similar things follow one another so smoothly that we mistake them for one continuous thing.

A note of warning. A mystic writes in *The Essence of Realities* that saying “the world is eternal in time” is empty talk. Ask what “the world” means. Does it mean all bodies—the heavens and the elements—or every being apart from God?[^V07-467] If it means every possible being, bodies and everything else, including minds and souls, then most things called “the world” do not depend on time for their existence. They must exist before time. How can the world be eternal in time if most of what it includes exists before time?[^V07-468]

If “the world” means only bodies, it still makes no sense to say bodies have existed for as long as time has existed. That suggests that time existed before bodies.[^V07-469] But bodies exist before time, while time follows from them, even if only in rank and dependence. That is the mystic’s argument.

I say that this argument has a flaw: two things can exist at the same time while one is prior to the other in its own being.[^V07-470] Yet after close thought, the mystic’s point proves sound.[^V07-471] The relation between time and body[^V07-472] is like the relation between matter and form: each depends on the other in a different way. Time helps make a body a particular individual,[^V07-473] just as matter’s readiness helps make a form particular. Studying the relation between matter and form shows that both are renewed in existence. A form brings matter into being and completes it, as an active cause brings about its effect. Matter, through its readiness, allows a later form to follow the first. The form needs matter; matter prepares for another form. The form causes matter by acting, while matter causes form by preparing to receive it. So both are renewed in existence.

The same is true of time and body. Time helps make a body a particular, changing thing, while a body sustains time and allows it to continue. Zaytūn the Elder, whose words we quoted in our discussion of nature, says that matter and form are both renewed: things last because new forms keep appearing, and they pass away because an earlier form gives way to the next. Passing away belongs to both form and matter.

The mystic continues: “If you say bodies existed from the moment God existed, you make a grave mistake. Many scholars think they have judged more honestly than anyone before them. But you must understand that bodies do not exist where the First Reality exists—not now, not before, and not after. Whoever says the world exists now alongside God is deeply mistaken. Where God is, there is no time or place. He encompasses time, place, and every other being. His existence is prior to the world just as it is prior to these written words; there is no difference between the two.

“Anyone who distinguishes between these cases is still trapped in doubt and has not freed God from likeness to created things or from time. Such people avoid saying He is in a place, as ordinary people do when they imagine Him as a body among other bodies. But they have not reached the true knowledge that comes to a mystic at the beginning of the path. God is prior to past time just as He is prior to future time. A true mystic knows this with certainty. Scholars cannot grasp it by reasoning alone; otherwise, they would not say that the world’s existence is equal to God’s, any more than they would say that the written letters on this page are equal to God’s existence.”

Here is the author’s reply. It is correct to say that the world does not exist with God at the level of God’s own existence. But proof, supported by direct spiritual insight, shows that God exists with the world and with every part of it. The same is true of every complete cause in relation to its effect. Because an effect is limited and depends on its cause, it does not exist at the level of the cause’s own complete being. But the cause is present with its effect at the level where that effect exists, without losing any of its own completeness. Anyone who understands this deeply will gain much. The Qur'an supports this: “He is with you wherever you are”; “He is God in heaven and God on earth”; and “Wherever you turn, there is the face of God.” Other verses and prophetic reports say the same.[^V07-474]

So we return to our point. God, who is beyond time, is present at every time, but is not limited to or tied to any time. God, who is beyond place, is present in every place, but is not confined to it, as those who picture Him like a body claim, nor separate from it, as some scholars claim in trying to show that He transcends place. They have not reached the mystics’ level of knowledge and do not see that their way of separating God from the world can itself limit Him and make Him resemble created things. They imagine that the source of the world’s existence is confined by being stripped of some kinds of existence and set apart from the world.

Yet the Necessary Being must be necessary in every respect. The One who encompasses all cannot have any state of possibility or need. He is with every existent in every respect, without limitation or multiplicity. He is in everything, yet He is not a thing; He is in every time, yet not in time; He is in every place, yet not in a place. He is all things, yet He is not the things themselves.

Praise belongs to God, first and last, and peace be upon our master Muhammad and his pure family. Here ends the second part of divine philosophy and with it the Third Journey of the Four Journeys of the Intellect. We have worked to correct and refine this book as carefully as we could. By God’s grace, it is free of errors except those our eyes missed and our attention failed to catch. May God open the way. — Ibrāhīm al-Amīnī, 1389.

## Notes

[^V07-1]: This way of describing speech is clear when the speaker is a possible being. But God, the Necessary Speaker, speaks through His own essence. He is therefore the source of every word that brings things into being, as well as spoken words. His speech is an inner attribute—His own transcendent essence—and it has effects: all complete words and the levels below them come from His essence and express what lies hidden in Him, the deepest of all mysteries. If someone says that God speaks by creating speech, and means by “speech” the intellects, which are His complete words, that is a sound view. The same is true of the view that God has inner speech, if this means that His most holy, simple existence contains all words in one simple reality. This also gives a sound meaning to the saying, “Speech is in the heart; the tongue is a guide to what is in the heart.” — س قده

[^V07-2]: Calling an attribute a “source” means that it gives rise to the attribute. The argument that speech involves effects because its root means “wound” is a play on words, not a proof. Calling speech an inner, effective quality hints at treating it not as an action that follows God’s essence, but as an essential attribute identical with His essence. This is done by defining speaking as the essence affecting another by making what is within it known. In reality, this reduces to power, an essential quality identical with God. But the same argument applies to every active attribute—creating, bringing things into being, giving life, providing, showing mercy, forgiving, and more.
