If an action comes from a distinctive feature of its agent, one might describe the agent by that feature: “Creator” and “Provider” could refer to the essence from which creation and provision come. Or one might use those names for the agent as it acts, so the attribute is taken from the action and is additional to the essence. The sound view is that God’s essential positive attributes are life, power, and knowledge; hearing and sight return to knowledge, while the rest are active attributes. We can then explain how the active attributes return to the essential ones by tracing them back to the source from which their actions come. — ط مد ظله

[^V07-3]: Speech as we use it is a shared sign that grew from social need. People need to tell one another what is in their minds. A person living entirely alone would have no need to speak. Animals also use sounds to communicate according to their social needs, such as mating, feeding or raising their young, and defending themselves. Speech may first have appeared through an action meant to show something. A hen picks up a grain in front of her chicks; they follow and peck at it. She then adds a call to the action. Later, the sound alone is enough for the chicks to understand what she means. Something similar happened with human beings. Sounds came to express different aims, words separated, combinations of words developed, and languages appeared. Through repeated use, spoken signs became so clear that a speaker could convey an idea without first showing the action, and a listener could grasp it without seeing it. This is a brief account of how speech arose among us. Words exist as agreed signs for their meanings, and speaking creates a sign for what is in the speaker’s mind so that someone who knows the language can understand the intended meaning. The sign is conventional because its meaning depends on an agreed convention. But an action truly shows the distinctive nature and perfection of its maker. In this sense, every possible being is God’s speech: it reveals what is within Him. Its existence is like a command addressed to itself, as God says, “When He wills a thing, He says to it, ‘Be!’ and it is.” In relation to other beings, it is like a statement. When a being is complete, it is a complete word of God. God says, “There is no changing the words of God,” and calls Jesus “a word from Him.” — ط مد ظله

[^V07-4]: Here he means speech as the act of speaking. In the second and third definitions, he means speech in its ordinary sense: what the act produces. — س قده

[^V07-5]: This hints at Mulla Sadra’s settled view: speech means the complete words, the intellects that exist through God’s creative act. In fact, everything exists through Him. On the Ashʿarite view, which defines a speaker as one in whom speech exists, it is no problem for speech to exist in God, since that view treats all His attributes as additional to His essence. — س قده

[^V07-6]: “Exists in” is used here because union assumes that there are two distinct things. Their distinction comes from one being related to what receives it. — س قده

[^V07-7]: That is, existence without letters or sounds. The Commander of the Faithful said that when God wills something to exist, He says “Be!”—not as a sound that strikes the ear or a call that can be heard. God’s speech is His act. The author also uses “word” for the fixed realities of possible beings before they receive actual existence. Their being heard is their established presence, as explained in the discussion of knowledge. — س قده

[^V07-8]: The first difference is between existence and what a thing is, as the mind distinguishes them. The second is their unity in reality. — س قده

[^V07-9]: According to the list in Sayyid’s *Jadhawāt*, the intellect, the soul, the nine spheres, the four elements, the three kinds of offspring, and the world of images make nineteen levels—the same number as the letters in the phrase “In the name of God, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.” Add the nine kinds of qualities, and the total is twenty-eight. — س قده

[^V07-10]: The intellects are absorbed in existence, like a letter that cannot stand alone as a meaning. Souls are like names: their existence is connected to their own selves and does not disappear into another. Flowing natural powers and changing bodies are like verbs, or acts. — س قده

[^V07-11]: One might object that this is the highest kind of human speech, yet the person here is listening and receiving, not speaking. First, he speaks with God by asking through his readiness to receive knowledge and understanding from Him; conversation goes both ways. Second, even though these words are first and directly attributed to God or to an angel, they can also be attributed to the soul, as something is attributed to what displays it. They descend from the soul’s inner self to its outward side, the intellect. — س قده

[^V07-12]: This refers to the prophets. For others, the Prophet said, “Among my community are those who are spoken to and inspired.” — س قده

[^V07-13]: Taken broadly, Ibn Arabi’s words include the general intellectual knowledge of the rational soul. But his way of speaking and the context suggest that he means the knowledge of people of the heart and the divine promptings received by those on the spiritual path. They describe four kinds of inward prompting. A divine prompting, sometimes called a “knock on the heart,” never errs; it can be recognized by its strength and the way it cannot be pushed aside. An angelic prompting, or inspiration, urges a person toward something required or recommended. A prompting from the self, or an impulse, serves the person’s own interests. A satanic prompting, or whisper, urges a person away from what is right. God says, “Satan threatens you with poverty and commands you to do what is shameful.” These promptings can be hard to distinguish in matters where either choice is allowed. What goes against a person’s own desire is more likely to come from the first two; what agrees with it is more likely to come from the last two. By God’s help, a sincere person with a clear heart and attention turned to the truth can tell the difference. — س قده

[^V07-14]: Far from it! A report from the noble descendants of the Prophet says: “The Holy Spirit tasted the first fruits from the gardens of Ṣāqūra in our gardens.” — س قده

[^V07-15]: By “the Book,” he means the lowest level of the books written on heavenly tablets: the world of bodies. He reads “There is nothing fresh or dry that is not in a clear Book” as referring to literal opposites such as fresh and dry. — س قده

[^V07-16]: This means the tablet of breath—*nafas*, with a vowel on the *f*—and breath or air taken without restriction, as belonging to the soul—*nafs*, with the *f* unvowelled. In the first case, the book was treated as something separate from the soul. In the second, the forms written on the air or on paper are counted as existing through the rational soul. — س قده

[^V07-17]: It may seem that the passage first calls the breath or air a writer, then says the rational soul is the maker. The two statements fit together: “soul” here means the breathing soul, the soul as it breathes. At this lower level, the breathing soul makes the images and writes them on what receives them. — س قده

[^V07-18]: When the light of reality appears, nothing else has independent existence, much less a relation to God of either kind. Any relation that assumes separation is only the relation of reality to appearance, or else no real relation at all. This is why Avicenna says that the First has no relation to things; rather, things are related to Him. If things are the subject of the statement, God is their necessary ground, so they must be related to Him. But if God is the subject, things vanish because their existence is borrowed, and no relation remains. The verse “His are the creation and the command” does not prove an ordinary relation. It may mean that He is the preserved source and foundation of all, or that all things receive existence from Him. The better proof is that the one who gives something cannot lack what He gives. — س قده

[^V07-19]: This is also reported from the Commander of the Faithful, ʿAlī. The sages say that times and things in time relate to higher principles as the present moment does, and places and things in place as a point does. Events that follow one another in time all exist together in eternity. They explain this with a rope or piece of wood, colored differently along its length, passing in front of an ant whose narrow field of sight cannot take it all in. The colors appear to the ant one after another, but you see them all at once because your sight can take in the whole length. This applies when we look at the world’s forms and what receives them. But if we look at its meaning and existence, there is no extension or sequence at all. Existence has no parts, so it has no size, quality, measure, or number that could divide it into past, present, and future. There is no absence or presence, years or eternities, beginning or decay, return or recurrence. All of these apply only to the things that receive existence and to the forms in which it appears. The Persian verse says: “The water in this stream has changed many times, yet the moon and stars remain in its reflection.” — س قده

[^V07-20]: In both the outer world and the human inner world, there are four things: an inward Throne and Footstool, and their outward forms. In the outer world, the inward ones are the universal intellect and universal soul; the outward ones are the outermost sphere and the sphere of the fixed stars. In the human inner world, the inward ones are the inner heart, or detailed intellect, and the inner chest, or imagination. Their outward forms are the pine-cone-shaped heart and the brain. — س قده

[^V07-21]: The effect that reaches the storehouse of imagination is the image in the faculty itself. The lower effect mentioned here is the image in the subtle bodily spirit, which carries the imaginative faculty and displays it. — س قده

[^V07-22]: This refers to the circular path of the words. When the ear hears what the tongue says, the words return to their source and circle back on themselves, like the creative words through which things come to be. For this reason, some may prefer speaking remembrance of God aloud to silent remembrance, though silent remembrance is better in some respects. — س قده

[^V07-23]: “Good words” means the complete words: the simple, higher intellects in the upward order of existence. Good action—the religious law and the path of life—raises them. But the meaning that best supports the author’s point is that all beings are God’s words, considered in their luminous aspect. “Good action” then means their inner and outward movements toward greater completion. In this sense, creation itself worships God, as He says, “Your Lord has commanded that you worship none but Him.” This does not conflict with the fact that some beings turn away from Him in their religious conduct. — س قده

[^V07-24]: Just as a servant in the ordinary sense owns nothing, and everything in his hands belongs to his master, so the true servant’s being, attributes, and actions belong to the Lord of all masters. This follows from the unity of God’s essence, attributes, and actions. God is called the All-Merciful here because His mercy reaches everyone, while His special mercy is for those who believe in and know His oneness. “He has counted them one by one”: just as a final sum gathers all the separate numbers, God’s knowledge gathers all scattered beings in a simpler, higher way, and nothing lies outside it. “Each will come to Him alone on the Day of Resurrection”: when beings rise from the things that receive them and stand before the One, each is stripped of what is foreign to its own reality. At a minimum, this means leaving behind the worldly body and its attachments—in short, the limits of nature. Complete freedom from matter belongs only to the closest servants of God. A Persian verse says: “Today, yesterday, the day before, and tomorrow—all four become one in tomorrow.” This return to the All-Merciful takes place only in human form, since the human being is the gateway to God and the straight path to Him. The Qur'an guides people to what is most upright: to reflect God’s greatest name. Since God’s giving never ends, every natural being reaches the natural human being and enters through this gateway; all strongholds reach the greatest stronghold, and all villages reach the mother city. Mulla Sadra adds another way to understand this: beings enter the human being through their many relations to him, in his imagination and in the connected worlds of dominion and intellect. In short, when the perfect human being reaches the highest end, all beings reach it with him. This does not mean that each kind of being fails to reach its own Lord and is not gathered to God along with that Lord. — س قده

[^V07-25]: This passage concerns detailed knowledge, which gives rise to many things within unity, and mercy as an attribute, which gives rise to mercy in action. His words about the appearance of God’s names and about what He first brought into being point to this. He is not speaking of God’s essential knowledge at the level of absolute oneness, or of His essential speech, which is simple and one. Although that knowledge contains all good things in a single, complete act of knowing, that is not the subject here, as his words make clear. — س قده

[^V07-26]: “Like them” may refer only to the number of earths matching the seven heavens, or it may mean that the earths are heavens too. This is understood when the soul learns what the realities of the heavens are, whether they exist, and why they exist, through descriptions that match them. Things are present in the mind through their own realities; what distinguishes one thing from another is also connected to what they share. The phrase may instead refer to the soul, heart, intellect, and the remaining levels of the human being, which are counted as seven, as will be explained later. In that reading, the unseen world of God’s command descends between these levels. — س قده

[^V07-27]: “Two days” may mean the moment that has passed—the moment of passing away—and the moment that is coming—the moment of coming into being. Every changing thing in nature is in this state. The phrase may also mean a bright day, seen from God’s side, and a dark day, seen from the self’s side; the days of form and matter; or a timeless day in the world of images and the day of natural time. “He revealed to each heaven its command” means the spiritual word that belongs to the unseen world of God’s command. — س قده

[^V07-28]: The Tablet may be the Tablet of Decree, where detailed knowledge is held—the world of the governing principles of different kinds, each letter containing all the letters of creation. It may also be the Tablet that records existence. Each letter of creation, as an item of knowledge, is greater and higher than Mount Qaf as an object known. — س قده

[^V07-29]: “Whoever knows himself knows his Lord” can be understood in several ways. Whoever knows the Prophet, who has a greater claim on believers than they have on themselves, knows his Lord, because the Prophet said, “Whoever has seen me has seen God.” The Prophet’s spiritual reality is the Universal Intellect, the fullest manifestation of God; in his all-embracing nature, he displays God’s Greatest Name. Or it may mean that whoever knows his own soul, created in the image of the All-Merciful and able to embody all the divine names when fully developed, knows his Lord. Knowing an effect as an effect, or a sign as a sign, is knowing its cause and what it points to—especially when the effect is a mirror of its source and has no independent standing. Or it may mean that whoever recognizes his own helplessness and knows that all power belongs to God knows God’s power and its reach. Whoever sees his own ignorance—once an empty mirror and blank page, later adorned with knowledge from God, the Wise and Knowing—knows God’s knowledge of all things, general and particular: “They do not encompass anything of His knowledge except what He wills.” Whoever knows himself as a servant, poor and owning no existence, quality, or action of his own, knows God as Lord, Master, and Self-Sufficient. A fourth reading is that whoever sees how his small world contains all levels—spiritual and bodily, angelic and satanic, with something in him like an angel, a sphere, a plant, an animal, and a devil—knows how the great world contains every level, from the smallest speck to the tiniest particle. A fifth reading describes something impossible: if a limited, ever-changing person could know the full reality of his own self, whose states and aspects cannot be counted, he would know the full reality of his Lord. But that cannot happen. In short, God has a hidden reality that no creature can grasp, and a manifest side known through Him. The soul too has a hidden reality beyond our grasp and a manifest side through which its powers and levels are known. A Persian verse says: “Knowing your soul is no small matter; know God, and you will know what it is.” — س قده

[^V07-30]: This difference between the Qur'an and the other books follows the difference between their messengers: God gave some messengers a higher rank than others. Since our Prophet is the last in both the order of existence and the line of prophets, no intermediary remains between him and God. The Qur'an is therefore God’s speech, and Gabriel’s role in bringing it down in portions does not contradict this. Other prophets had one or more intermediaries. The two conditions that make speech direct—its independence from what receives it and its direct dependence on the true speaker—were fully and steadily present in the Prophet, so no possible being could surpass him. God says, “Remain upright as you have been commanded.” — س قده

[^V07-31]: “God’s own speech” means that “God’s” qualifies “speech.” It does not mean that the Qur'an is speech but not a Book. As we have already seen, every speech is also a Book in one respect. — س قده

[^V07-32]: The earlier distinction between speech and Book was based on whether they depend on matter. This distinction between Qur'an and Criterion is broader: it concerns what is one and what is many. The many may be different kinds of things or ideas, as with different intellects or divine names and attributes; or it may lie in different levels of existence, which vary in strength. The word “Qur'an” comes from a root meaning “to gather,” as in the verse, “Its gathering and its recitation are upon Us; when We recite it, follow its recitation.” The speech sent down to our Prophet is the Universal Intellect, his station and spiritual reality: the whole order of intellects that move the heavens and earth, from their beginnings to their ends. This speech is Qur'an in that it gathers things together; it is Criterion in that it distinguishes their kinds and levels. — س قده

[^V07-33]: Whatever appears to the senses is perceived, whether it comes from outside or from within. Some followers of the philosophers go beyond saying that the Prophet’s reality connects with the angel’s reality. They say he also connects with the angel’s subtle form through imagination. They do not reduce this to an empty dream: they say the image comes down from the higher Tablets and reflects what is there, like the images in a true dream. Yet this account still falls short. The inner powers are like mirrors facing one another. When an image appears in imagination, it is also reflected in the common sense, which is like a mirror with two faces: one receives images from the outward senses, and the other receives what comes from the higher Tablets or from the imagination. Once we know that the image passes from the inner worlds to the illuminated imagination, then to the common sense, and from there to the senses, how could it fail to be truly seen? The Prophet’s intellect is above the other intellects, and his senses are more luminous than ordinary senses. — س قده

[^V07-34]: That is, these relational forms give rise to the angels of the Tablets. I suspect the text originally read, “From them come the written angels after they have been measured,” matching the description of these forms as created and measured. The next words refer to the angels of the Tablets. — س قده

[^V07-35]: The angel also has a subtle aspect that relates to the lower world. Its reality at the high station appears to the Prophet as a pure form in the world of subtle images. That is what is meant by its ascent and descent; it does not mean that it moves through space. The Qur'an says, “The angels and the Spirit descend,” and, “The command descends between them.” Many similar verses express the same idea. — س قده

[^V07-36]: When the holy spirit of a saint, or someone below a prophet, speaks with angels while awake, the person’s powers do not follow the spirit as fully as a prophet’s powers do, because the saint’s spirit is not as strong as the Prophet’s. The powers remain caught up in sensory distractions, and the spirit cannot pull them away completely. When the Prophet’s spirit meets the angels in sleep, by contrast, his powers follow it, despite his seeming daze, and see what it sees. He is also awake in the natural world: he sees the angel’s form with his senses and hears its voice, recognizing it as an angel. This is one of the marks of prophecy. — س قده

[^V07-37]: Written, spoken, mental, and outward existence are all appearances of one reality, whether in its full form or its subtle counterpart. The nature of a thing remains itself, and existence has one root; the differences are in strength and weakness. A shadow is not separate from what casts it, a name is not separate from what it names, and a subtle form reflects the reality it represents. In brief, the Qur'an has three forms: the written copy between its covers, and the created signs in the outer world and within the soul. Whoever reads any one of these properly is, in a sense, reading the other two. — س قده

[^V07-38]: This applies if Mount Qaf means the mountain said to surround the world—the world of subtle images, which branches into Hurqalya, Jābalqā, and Jābarsā. It does not apply if Qaf means God’s power or the heart, since nothing is greater than the heart, as the sacred report says: “My earth and My heaven do not contain Me, but the heart of My faithful servant contains Me.” The mountain may also point to the sacred existence of the final Prophet, Muḥammad. Ibn ʿAbbās said that Qaf is a mountain in Mecca. In this reading, it points to the Prophet’s great standing with God, his firmness on God’s path, and his place between the oneness of God and the many things in creation. — س قده

[^V07-39]: This applies to every form of the Qur'an, written and created, in the outer world and within the soul. Each can be called by one of its noble names, depending on how it is viewed. — س قده

[^V07-40]: This means that actual beings are also gathered in the written dot, if the written dot is understood as a subtle counterpart and appearance of the real point in the world of writing. — س قده

[^V07-41]: This refers to the saying, “Read and rise.” A person moves from the outward meaning to the inward one, and from the inward meaning to its source. He rises from attachment to things, to taking on the qualities of truth, and then to realizing the truth itself. — س قده

[^V07-42]: In the language of those who know God, a “point of arrival” is the station where the speaker is witnessed while someone recites His verses, as God reveals Himself through the quality expressed in each verse. Our master al-Ṣādiq said, “God has shown Himself to His servants in His speech, but they do not see Him.” One day, while praying, he fell unconscious. When asked why, he said, “I kept repeating a verse until I heard it from the One who spoke it.” Another version of the report says that every verse has an outer meaning and an inner meaning; every letter has a limit, and every limit has a point of arrival. The author may have quoted the meaning rather than the exact wording. — س قده

[^V07-43]: The “breast,” usually identified with the imagination, may have been added by a copyist. Without it, the list has seven levels. Another level, the deepest hidden one, may be added at the end; alternatively, one might leave out the intellect or nature. Nature would fit best here, since it is the outermost level. In short, these are terms used by those who follow the inward path. Philosophers often use “reasoning soul” for all or most of these levels. Those on the inward path often do not define them because, they say, they know them by direct experience. They explain them only as far as the subject allows. I gave their terms at the start of my notes on the first journey. — س قده

[^V07-44]: This means: know God through a light that comes from Him to your hearts. Draw near to Him until these words are true of you: “Through Me he sees, and through Me he hears.” — س قده

[^V07-45]: This means through the kind of prophecy that teaches people about God and His names. There are two kinds of prophecy: one that teaches God’s being, qualities, and names; and one that does all this while also bringing laws, teaching good character, and guiding public life. Some reserve the word “messengerhood” for the second kind. If so, the saying means inheriting the first kind of prophecy, as perfected people do. Even jurists can be its outward signs. A report says that whoever memorizes the Qur'an carries prophecy between his sides. Another report says that God has servants who are not prophets but whom the prophets envy. The Prophet also said that his community has people who receive insight and speak with angels. — س قده

[^V07-46]: This means becoming one of those entrusted with authority—not only people who know the truths, but also people able to act on them and bring about God’s commands in the world. — س قده

[^V07-47]: This means that the souls of beings such as luminous intellects and angels are not drawn to their own possibility, because the light of God’s eternal existence hides that possibility. The same is true of the intellects that rise toward God and lose sight of themselves in Him. Most people lean more toward possibility, and the effects of being many are stronger in them; outwardly or inwardly, they resemble those who deny the truth. Those who lean more toward existence, with a stronger sense of oneness, are among the prophets and saints who draw near to God. Those in whom the two sides are balanced are the moderate believers. — س قده

[^V07-48]: A beautiful line says: “Among those who love is a secret no word or pen can reveal.”

[^V07-49]: This refers to our prior being in the world of intellect. We are called “letters” because intellects do not exist independently; their existence depends entirely on God. They have no matter, or even a separate nature of their own, according to those who have understood this point. They belong to the divine realm, exist through God, and remain through Him. — س قده

[^V07-50]: This refers to our later being in the soul. — س قده

[^V07-51]: This means that the letters were like lines, extended in length and running straight down. Ibn Arabi says something similar: “We were lofty letters, not separate things, on the highest peaks.” The Commander of the Faithful said to Abū al-Aswad al-Duʾalī, “A word is a noun, a verb, and a letter,” pointing to the same truth. God’s “word” is His act, as the title of the chapter in *Nahj al-Balāghah* indicates. The word “Be!” refers to the existence that spreads through all things. It divides into the high letters of the intellect, the names—which are the lasting bodily beings—and the actions linked to time, which are changing qualities. In the author’s view, bodies also change in their very being. “Names” here means souls, which stand between still intellects and changing nature. — س قده

[^V07-52]: Different kinds of things, each with its own nature, are separated from one another and freed from foreign features. The individual things within each kind are then gathered into one whole. This is like the mind grasping the pure meaning of a thing, stripped of unrelated features. At that level, the meaning is one. The mind also separates qualities, amounts, positions, and other features that are mixed together in ordinary things. — س قده

[^V07-53]: This means the intellects, which are God’s speech and true word. — س قده

[^V07-54]: This means how the intellects become universal souls, which are God’s Book, and how they descend into open hearts. The universal soul is like a subtle copy of the intellect’s reality within the soul. It becomes simple images, without any loss or separation from its source. Another reading takes the three stages as knowledge held in one whole, knowledge unfolded in the soul, and images in the imagination. In short, “We made the word clear to them” refers to intellects becoming clear to other intellects; “a Book whose verses are clear” refers to the soul or imagination. — س قده

[^V07-55]: This includes the Qur'an as it exists in the intellect, in the soul, and in other forms, including God’s speech as it exists in His providential knowledge. — س قده

[^V07-56]: The point is that the world beyond the senses contains everything found here, including all five senses, in a form suited to that world. Each sense is reached by a matching inward sense. Through inward sight, the Prophet or saint sees what ordinary people cannot see; through inward hearing, he hears what they cannot hear. In the same way, he can taste, smell, and touch what ordinary people cannot. The Prophet said, “I spend the night with my Lord; He feeds and gives me drink.” He also said, “I can smell the breath of the Most Merciful from the direction of Yemen,” and, “I felt the coolness of his fingertips between my breasts.” These reports point to it. In short, when a prophet or saint leaves ordinary awareness, he perceives through inward senses, each suited to what it knows. — س قده

[^V07-57]: The pens here are not the intellects in the middle ranks, below the Highest Pen—whether that means the first intellect or the whole order of intellects. They are not the universal souls that govern the heavens either; those are the Preserved Tablet. The pens are the individual souls of the heavenly bodies. When viewed in relation to their bodies, they are tablets of erasure and confirmation, because their nature changes. When viewed as linked to their universal souls, they are pens that write on those tablets. — س قده

[^V07-58]: The Prophet’s connection with these souls is not always the same. At one time, he connects with the souls that know the positions and effects of the heavenly bodies, sees one set of forms, and draws one conclusion. At another time, he sees different forms and draws a different conclusion. — س قده

[^V07-59]: The author has said before that God’s activity has different levels. Ibn Arabi points to this by calling the changing movement of a heavenly soul “hesitation,” and linking it to a divine name such as “the Swift” or “the Shaper.” — س قده

[^V07-60]: That is, without the unseen, intellectual record of erasure and confirmation, there could be no actual erasure and confirmation in the changing world. — س قده

[^V07-61]: This explains “fixed within them.” The hesitation in people would be raised above them and become a fixed decree that could not be changed. Or it means that just as change comes from a divine reality, change in human souls is itself fixed by that reality. — س قده

[^V07-62]: As explained earlier, what makes us speak of something as not yet existing is its prior existence; what makes us speak of it as no longer existing is its later existence. Nonexistence is nothing in itself, so erasure does not mean that something is made into absolute nothing. Rather, one stage of a changing thing is erased and another is established, then that stage is erased and replaced. The Preserved Tablet and Highest Pen do not change. — س قده

[^V07-63]: Change takes place only on the two Tablets of Erasure and Confirmation: the unseen record and its visible counterpart. — س قده

[^V07-64]: The earlier “remarkable unveiling” is direct knowledge gained by thought and proof. The vision described here is an image seen through inner senses as a result of good actions and character. Every meaning has an image, and every reality has a subtle form. Those who follow the Prophet’s way can see these images through the inward senses. Some people, especially those who combine deep knowledge with spiritual practice, can bring together both kinds of vision. — س قده

[^V07-65]: This applies to the Qur'an as it exists in the world and within the human soul. In the revealed Qur'an, “wisdom” points to the fact that the whole Qur'an is contained in al-Fātiḥah, in the fourteen separate letters, in the bāʾ of “In the name of God,” and even in the dot beneath that letter. “Clear speech” points to its many levels: each chapter and verse has its own meaning. — س قده

[^V07-66]: This is clearer when applied to the Qur'an within the human soul than when applied to the written Qur'an, though the verse can include both. People were spiritually dead until God brought them to life by sending the Prophet, completing the work through the saint, and sending down the Qur'an. They were guided by its light. — س قده

[^V07-67]: This definition appears in *The Healing* and other works on divine philosophy. Wisdom is the best kind of knowledge because it gives certainty, not mere imitation. Other fields often rely on assumptions about their subjects, if not in every step then at least in accepting that their subjects exist. Divine philosophy has a leading place because its subject is existence itself, which is pure good; it studies God, His qualities and acts, the angels brought near to Him, and other beings that support the order of existence. Its goal is to make a person’s mind like the world and to reach true happiness. Its starting points are proofs that give lasting certainty. Its subject, goal, and starting points are all the best. The things it knows are therefore the best things to know. — س قده

[^V07-68]: This applies if wisdom means the highest form of direct knowledge, in which what is known is present to the knower. God alone has this knowledge in its fullest form, so wisdom is His gift. — س قده

[^V07-69]: The author relies here on the rule that higher causes are not completed by lower things, as his wording suggests. So “they had not already reached before acting” should mean prior in any sense, not only earlier in time. Separate higher causes do not gain perfection from actions that necessarily follow from their own nature. Their actions are blessings that flow from their being to what is below them. They have no goals in lower things for their own sake; if they did, those effects would complete them. Their goal is their own essence if nothing stands above them, as is true only of God, or a perfection above them, as is true of the separate intellects. Through their actions, the intellects imitate God by giving good, without needing the action or its results. This is pure generosity. That does not mean they are forced to act. They know their actions and approve of them because those actions fit their natures. An action of this kind is not coerced. Even if their actions follow necessarily from their natures, they still arise from knowledge and approval and are therefore chosen. — ط مد ظله

[^V07-70]: In short, providence brings together three things: knowledge, being a cause, and approval. We can clarify this by looking at what it means for us to take care over something. A person takes care to make an action happen, or to make it as complete as possible, without letting some other concern—more urgent, more loved, or more important—distract them from doing it. When we are busy with major matters, we may neglect small ones, either by not doing them at all or by not giving them all the care they need. If we give a small matter the same attention as a great one, or carefully manage all its needs and details as we would in a great matter, that is providence. We neglect small matters because we compare things. If two actions are required, one more important than the other, we may compare them, treat the more important one as the only real duty, and then neglect the less important one. Yet without that comparison, both would still be duties, and we would attend to the small one just as we attend to the large one. When we do not compare them, we know each action as it is: something required of us and suited to our nature. That is providence. It includes knowing the thing, causing it, and approving it because it fits the doer’s nature. God knows each thing as it is, without comparison or anything else weakening or changing that knowledge. He is the highest cause of every cause and its effects, and those effects fit His essence. So He has providence over all things. As this explanation shows, providence is normally an attribute of action: the fit between an action and its doer is understood from the action. But because that fit can be traced back to something in the essence, it can also be described as an attribute of essence. We can say that God’s essence knows things and causes them in a way that makes their effects fit Him. — ط مد ظله

[^V07-71]: We have already discussed eternal will. Tracing approval back to the special kind of causality by which effects fit their cause is more natural than explaining it as a separate will, which the author has already traced back to knowledge of the best order. — ط مد ظله

[^V07-72]: If the lack that belongs necessarily to every effect simply because it is possible were counted as “evil” in the technical sense, then the intellects—and even the heavenly spheres—could not be called wholly good when philosophers divide things into five groups to answer the dualists. On that usage, evil would be found only in the elemental world. The author will return to this technical meaning later. — س قده

[^V07-73]: This proof defies the critics, such as Imam Fakhr al-Din al-Rāzī and his followers, who said that philosophers had not proved that all existence is good and evil is a lack, but had relied only on isolated examples. The proof was given earlier by the scholar Shīrāzī in his commentary on The Wisdom of Illumination, before the author presented it here. It is a sound argument that is simple to follow, so the author accepted it. — س قده

[^V07-74]: Someone may ask: how can clouds blocking sunlight and rain preventing clothes from being bleached be examples of a harmful cause that does not reach the thing it harms? “Not reaching” means that the affected thing does not perceive the cause. And what is being harmed? The owner of the fruit or clothes; the harm is the loss of the benefit they need. The cause itself is perceived by touch or sight. — س قده

[^V07-75]: The power that perceives the harmful cause is not the power that suffers from it. The person’s sense of touch, which needs the sun’s warmth, cannot perceive the cloud. It perceives only the loss of health and warmth, and it judges that loss to be bad. Sight perceives the cloud, but the cloud does not harm sight. So the cloud is not itself judged evil by the power that perceives it; what is judged evil is the loss of health and warmth. The sense that can see the cloud is not harmed by it, while the sense that is harmed does not perceive it. If the cloud were evil in itself, it would make the perceiving sense suffer. This shows again that evil belongs to a lack. — س قده

[^V07-76]: This takes an unnecessary detour. Pain’s existence is known directly from experience; there is no need to prove that it exists by first saying that it is a kind of perception. — س قده

[^V07-77]: The reply does not seem to resolve the problem. First, the objection is broader than direct sense perception. The severed limb is only an example. The claim that pain is direct awareness of a lack does not explain pain caused by knowing that loved ones have died, or by other thoughts and judgements. Second, it is hard to say that sense perception is always direct awareness of the thing itself when the senses often make mistakes about the outside world. Saying that all knowledge is direct in some deeper way does not help, because that concerns knowledge as it is in itself, not whether it matches what exists outside us. The problem can instead be answered this way: the mental image that causes pain is not evil insofar as it perfects the person’s knowledge. In that respect it is something good that exists. But insofar as it represents what has happened outside us—such as a limb being severed or a connection being broken—it is tied to a lack. The evil is in that lack, not in the mental image. — ط مد ظله

[^V07-78]: The scholar Dawānī did not say that the only thing perceived is a broken connection, so it is not enough to answer that pain is a kind of direct perception and that perception is identical with what it perceives. He can say: even if direct awareness is identical with its object, we have not agreed that the only object perceived is the broken connection. That absence is perceived, as other absences are, but what we call pain is the single, present state of hurt that is felt. It is not the broken connection itself—especially when pain comes from an unhealthy bodily condition. How can that felt state be a lack? A lack, simply as a lack, has no awareness or effect, yet this painful state has both. The author himself says in his discussion of motion and rest that each thing has its own kind of existence, and that motion and relations exist in the world. If motion, relation, and other weak kinds of existence are still existents, why should pain not be an existent too? Some philosophers say pain is caused by a broken connection; others say it is caused by an unhealthy bodily state. Feeling the broken connection is awareness of the cause of pain, while feeling the hurt itself is awareness of the painful state. Since pain exists, they also question how an absence, such as a broken connection, can cause something that exists. They have tried to avoid this problem by treating the broken connection as something that exists. The true answer to Dawānī’s objection is this. What pain makes us directly aware of may be either a lack, such as a broken connection, in which case evil is a lack, as the author says; or a positive state, as the objector proposes and as we also allowed as a possibility. In that case, pain as an existing hurt is good in itself. A thing can be good in itself without being pleasant to a particular person. The fact that someone’s weakness makes a state unpleasant to them does not make it evil in itself. How could that existence be evil in its own nature when every kind of existence fits its own nature? A body calls for existence with potential multiplicity; a separate number calls for many actual units; a continuous whole calls for a connected existence; something that changes calls for an existence that changes; fire calls for an active, penetrating existence; snake venom calls for a biting, painful existence. None of these is evil in itself or by its nature. The same is true of pain. We cannot call these things evil in their own nature because a thing is evil only when it harms the existence or perfection of something already present. But here we are speaking of what each kind of thing calls for in itself from the beginning, in the fixed realities of things, which are known through God’s pure giving. There, pain is not evil in itself. It is evil only in relation to those who receive it and are affected by it. This is shown by the fact that, in God’s knowledge, which is direct and identical with what is known, pain is not called evil: God’s knowledge is active and wholly good, and He is not affected or changed. We can see that pain is called evil in our knowledge not because it is an act of awareness or an existing thing, but because it affects us and changes us. Pain also brings many further goods. It can help the blessed and those brought near to God reach high states of patience, approval, and surrender. Seeing pain also lets us understand the condition of those who suffer, so that we can help them and be helped. These benefits, as well as its existence, show that the argument needs to be answered by the distinction given here. — س قده

[^V07-79]: The author uses three claims to show that pain is a lack like a broken connection: first, that awareness of what opposes us is direct, not through a mental image; second, that a thing’s existence is identical with what it is; and third, that knowledge of a thing is identical with the thing known in itself. Even if all three claims are true, they answer only one of the two perceptions: awareness of the broken connection, not the pain caused by it. The objection assumes that the two can occur separately. The real answer is the one given here: pain and hurt are positive things that exist, and are good in themselves, even when they are unpleasant to the person who feels them. — س قده

[^V07-80]: If what is missing is the thing itself, then the thing’s essence is what is described as absent. If what is missing is one of its perfections, then it is the essence of that existing thing that is described as lacking that perfection. — ط مد

[^V07-81]: Someone might say that a human being feels more pain than an animal when struck with a stick. The answer is that the author means the soul, as a soul, is less harmed, while the living animal body, as a body, is harmed more. He added that condition to distinguish the soul from an animal’s soul, which has some freedom from matter through imagination. And if human beings seem to suffer more, it may be because their bodies are more delicate and their senses sharper than those of animals. — س قده

[^V07-82]: This division is attributed to Aristotle. It does not conflict with the claim that evil is a lack, since, as explained earlier, evil is a lack that belongs to a particular thing and therefore has some connection with existence. — ط مد

[^V07-83]: The author gives the division before returning to the proof. The proof later shows that some of these groups do not exist and so are not among the kinds that actually occur. Here, good and evil may mean either what is good or evil in itself, or what is good or evil in relation to something else. A passage in The Glimmers suggests the second meaning. It first says that the first group contains no evil “in relation to anything,” and then gives as examples the intellects, which have no potentiality and do not harm any other being. — س قده

[^V07-84]: The author may have realized that tracing evil to absence would mean that it occurs in every possible essence, since an essence can be the source of absences and losses. It would also mean that evil occurs at every level of existence: each lower level lacks some perfection of the level above it. So it seems that evil must be more common than good in the order of creation. He replies that the “evil” discussed here, in the agreed technical sense, means the absence of a thing or one of its perfections. A thing must have the potential to be absent, or to lose its perfection, and that is matter. Evil is therefore found only in the material world. But defining the word this way does not solve the real problem. The analysis still seems to show that absence and evil exist at levels beyond matter. The author has repeatedly said that possibility, essence, every lack, and every limit are privative in nature, and that absence is evil. The sounder answer is to admit that possible existence always includes a kind of evil, though some evil is only relative: for example, a possible thing is lacking when compared with the Necessary Existent, and each level of existence is lacking when compared with the level above it. This kind of lack is part of what the thing is and does not come from divine decree. A different kind of lack, such as death or losing a perfection after having it, is limited to matter. The author answers that kind in the main text. — ط مد ظله

[^V07-85]: This explains why evil occurs according to the philosophers’ view of generation and decay, where one form replaces another. In the author’s view of substantial motion, one basic form does not lose any perfection when another form follows it, whether the later form is higher, such as an animal form following a human form, or another form at the same level. Evil is the loss of a stage or limit in the moving matter, when compared with that matter’s continuing potential. This loss is a necessary part of change; it is not separately caused. Qualities that depend on basic forms change along with them, so the same reasoning applies to the loss of these qualities. — ط مد

[^V07-86]: This prepares for the next chapter’s explanation of how evil enters God’s decree only by extension. It gives two reasons: these evils help the flow of existence continue, and they are necessary results of existing good things rather than things created separately. — ط مد

[^V07-87]: This refers to evil in both senses: the usual and the technical sense. The two kinds enter the divine decree only by extension. Defects and possibilities that belong to essences are included only because the essences themselves are included by extension. What is made in itself is existence, which is good. Existing things that oppose one another are also good in themselves and become evil only in relation to what they harm. God creates them for their own good purposes, not in order to destroy a thing or one of its perfections. Fire, for example, was made to support the existence of composite things and to cook food, not to burn the clothes of someone who has done no wrong. Anger was placed in us so we could overcome what blocks our path to God and defend a good community, not so we could destroy what desire and imagination happen to crave. Imagination was given to protect us from danger, not to make us fear the dead or worry about tomorrow’s food before tomorrow comes. — س قده

[^V07-88]: The universal fire that exists in the intellect does not oppose universal water there. Only the particular fire and water that appear in the imagination oppose one another. — س قده

[^V07-89]: A circular movement was also set out in parts, so that events could be linked to what has no beginning or end. — س قده

[^V07-90]: Someone may object that the Shaykh’s words, “it reaches it as its destined share,” mean that destiny is the known forms within the heavenly souls, not actual things in matter: the thing that comes to be is not the same as what it comes from. Tusi answers by saying that the connection is indirect, through causes that come before it in time. There may also be a direct chain of higher causes. Perhaps Tusi is hinting at the truth as he sees it: that God has direct knowledge of particular things. — س قده

[^V07-91]: Tusi’s words seem to raise two problems. First, destiny was defined as each thing’s detailed existence in matter, but intellectual substances are not in matter. How, then, can they be in both decree and destiny once? Second, decree was defined earlier as all the intellectual forms of things flowing from God at once into the world of intellects. If so, decree applies to intellectual substances, not destiny.

    The answer is that “decree” and “destiny” may be used here in broader or different senses. All intellectual substances may exist together in God’s knowledge: as a whole they are known simply, which is decree, and in all their detail, which is destiny. Or “intellectual substances” may mean intellects other than the First Intellect; the “world of intellects” in Tusi’s explanation may mean that First Intellect, which holds all their forms together in a simple way. The forms are one there in their simplicity, but their different natures can also be distinguished. This use of “destiny” is not the usual technical one.

    It is not difficult to understand intellectual substances as being in decree or destiny, since a thing may be “in” something in different senses. Motion is in time in one sense, and time is in motion in another. Their existence in both realms at once may mean that the difference is in how they are known, not in the things known. Bodily things, by contrast, are present in decree as a whole in the world of intellects, and separately in destiny in the material world, because matter has a strong hold on them. Their detailed bodily existence is different from their unified existence in the world of intellects.

    The terms may also be used in their standard senses, if we understand the eternal archetypes as part of the world of created intellects. The relation of changing things to the unchanging is timeless, so they are present in both realms together in an eternal sense. When the whole that contains all parts is considered, the separate parts have no independent status. Bodily substances, however, are considered together with their matter and are not contained in what is above them. Their detailed material existence is therefore a second kind of existence, different from their unified existence in the intellectual world.

    As the author of The Judgments says, “Intellectual substances exist in decree and destiny once, because they exist only in eternity, but from two points of view: as a whole and in detail. Bodily forms and qualities exist in both realms twice: once in eternity as a whole, and once in the endless sequence of time in detail.” This may seem compressed, but the explanation is as given above. — س قده

[^V07-92]: Another way to put the argument is this: if no evil occurred in the material world—if forms never decayed or changed and never lost a later perfection—then every good would necessarily come to be and become actual. There would be no possibility of losing either an earlier or a later perfection. Matter would therefore disappear, material things would become immaterial, and every immaterial kind would exist in only one individual. The question “Why did God not create a material world without evil?” would then mean “Why did God not make do with an immaterial world, instead of creating a material world that contains evil?” The answer is that the material world contains much good. This also shows that happiness, benefit, obedience, reward, and the like could have no meaning unless misery, harm, disobedience, punishment, and their opposites were also possible. — ط مد

[^V07-93]: The answer is given by the Shaykh, Ibn Sina, in *The Directives and Remarks*, and later writers followed him. — س قده

[^V07-94]: I do not see a conflict here. The reports that seem to oppose this view are countered by reports like them. Nor does the verse “Few of My servants are grateful” prove the point at issue: falling short in gratitude does not mean that someone is excluded from salvation altogether. “The grateful” may mean those at the highest level, since the Arabic word is an intensive form. The small number of the grateful is also counterbalanced by the honoring phrase “My servants.” — س قده

[^V07-95]: By “perfection” here, I mean the perfection required by a kind’s nature or by an individual’s readiness. Each thing has a particular perfection proper to it: a lion’s might, for example, or a gazelle’s speed. The later perfections are not of this kind. Wisdom, for example, is not required of most people. It is not part of human nature in general, and the matter of most people is not ready for it, so lacking it is not an evil for them. In the author’s example, the straightening of the letter alif is wanted, while the curvature of the letter dal is welcome. The perfection that every member of a kind must reach is the first and second perfection, not later perfections. This reading fits the previous sentence best; the other reading fits Ibn Sina’s quotation that follows better. — س قده

[^V07-96]: This refers to explaining reward and punishment as the results of actions. There is another answer: apart from whether divine decree applies to them, the difference between voluntary actions and actions done under compulsion or necessity is self-evident. Only someone obstinate would deny it. If divine decree applying to a voluntary action made that action involuntary, it would contradict itself. In fact, decree applying to an action as voluntary confirms its voluntariness; otherwise, what was decreed would fail to occur, which is impossible in God’s decree. The author seems to confuse being subject to God’s ordering with being compelled. A voluntary act, precisely as voluntary, is subject to God’s ordering: God wills it and it necessarily occurs. He does not will the bare act while leaving out its voluntary character. — ط مد

[^V07-97]: This is what the religious teaching calls the embodiment of deeds: repeated actions give rise to settled traits, and these traits, by the permission of the all-powerful Creator, shape forms. The material of a trait and the trait itself are one; the water of existence flows through each valley according to its capacity, and light takes on the color of the glass that receives it. Traits therefore cast shadows and take forms that resemble them: the greedy trait, for example, takes the form of ants; the harmful trait, the forms of snakes and scorpions. The rest can be understood by analogy. Well said:

    > If a thorn has wounded you, you planted it yourself;
    > if you wear silk, you spun its thread yourself.

    — س قده

[^V07-98]: The objector is Imam Fakhr al-Din al-Razi. — ط مد ظله

[^V07-99]: The charge that philosophers deny rational good and evil is a slander. How could those who are children and offspring of reason deny that reason can judge actions? The confusion comes from the objectors’ belief that philosophers define good and evil only as agreement with, or opposition to, a purpose or interest. Since they think philosophers deny purpose, they accuse them of denying good and evil too—as though they meant that a wise person should deny them.

    This question has long been disputed, so it is useful to set out the main views. Philosophers, the Imamis, and the Muʿtazilites say that good and evil are known by reason; the Ashʿarites say they are known only through religious law. The Muʿtazilites themselves differ. The earliest say actions are good or evil in themselves, not because of qualities in them. Some early thinkers say that a real quality causes both goodness and badness. Abu al-Husayn holds this only for badness: goodness needs no special quality, but badness does. Al-Jubba’i says actions are not good or evil because of real qualities, but because of points of view and relations; an act can change its moral character with the circumstances, as when striking an orphan is either discipline or abuse.

    To say that good and evil are rational means that reason can recognise that one action is truly praiseworthy and another blameworthy, even without a revealed law; or that reason can understand why an action is good and the law commands it, or why it is bad and the law forbids it. Not understanding the reasons for every action does not refute this view. We know in general that a wise person should not command or forbid an action that has no benefit or harm.

    To say that good and evil are religious means that reason cannot know whether an action is good or bad, whether its doer deserves praise or blame, or what makes any action good or bad, either before or after revelation. Another definition says that the rational view means an action really contains a feature that makes it good or bad, whether or not reason can recognise it; the religious view denies this. On the rational view, religious law reveals and explains a goodness or badness that is already true of the action. The law cannot command an action and forbid that same action at the same time, though it can do so at different times, as when a ruling is abrogated. On the religious-only view, the law itself makes an action good or bad; it does not reveal a quality that was already there. The reversals just mentioned are possible, and there is no necessary connection between prayer and entering paradise, or between wrongfully consuming an orphan’s property and consuming fire within. On that view, God could send a faithful, devoted worshipper to hell and an unbeliever to paradise. The sounder view instead holds that a necessary connection, known by reason, links good actions to delightful forms and evil actions to painful forms. The Qur'an says, “As a reward for what you used to do,” and, “As a reward for what you used to earn”; the Prophet says, “They are only your deeds, returned to you.” So the claim that cause and effect are unrelated and that God merely acts by habit is false.

    The truth is that good and evil are rational, as we know necessarily that justice and kindness deserve praise, and oppression and aggression deserve blame. Every rational person knows this, even without following a religion. Those who deny revelation, such as the Brahmins, judge this way too. Also, judging that what God commands is good and what He forbids is bad presupposes that lying is bad and that God does not lie, and that commanding what is bad or forbidding what is good is foolish and unworthy of Him. If these judgements come from reason, then reason is not shut out; if they come from revelation, the argument goes in a circle. — س قده

[^V07-100]: Since God acts with prior knowledge and approval, and no one above Him forces Him to act or competes with Him, He acts freely. The necessity philosophers speak of—“a thing does not exist until its existence is necessary”—is a necessity understood from the existence He bestows on possible effects. It is impossible to turn that into a necessity imposed on God, making Him compelled to act. — ط مد

[^V07-101]: The reply seems at first to be an argument that silences the objector, but it can be understood as a demonstration. There are two ways to make the point. First, when we ask an agent why they acted, especially if harm followed, we are asking about the compelling benefit that made the action worthwhile despite its harmful consequences. The principles and rules by which we judge our own actions are abstracted from the world’s actual order. Asking about a motive is asking about the order that made the action appropriate. Our actions follow a benefit; that benefit follows the actual order. God’s act is the actual order itself, and the benefit or wisdom we identify follows from it, as an attribute follows from what it belongs to. There is no point in asking God to make His action fit some benefit: His action is the measure of every benefit and the source from which it is understood. Nothing stands above Him to judge Him or His action.

    Second, an agent is answerable for an action only when it involves something that does not belong to them. We ask about an action when it may or may not be within their control. God owns everything in every respect, so nothing He does lies outside His ownership. This does not erase good and evil, make benefit irrelevant, or allow arbitrary action. On this account, an action is good because it belongs to the agent in a way that agrees with benefit. God’s act—the existence that comes from Him—is itself beneficial, so it belongs to Him and is good in itself. To will what is good and beneficial is not arbitrary. One may still ask what the wisdom or benefit in a particular divine act is, meaning one may seek detailed knowledge of it after knowing generally that it has a benefit. But one cannot ask whether there is any benefit at all. — ط مد

[^V07-102]: It is as if someone said of those who believe in Platonic Forms that the Forms were created for what lies beneath them, their images, so that they could serve as patterns for making those images. Both objections come from misunderstanding their teaching. — س قده

[^V07-103]: The point is not that we cannot ask why because God acts from an arbitrary will. That would destroy the rational connection between things and would first of all defeat the very claim that arbitrary will makes questions of purpose impossible. — ط مد

[^V07-104]: Those who believe in eternal punishment can answer the argument by choosing either option. If a person leaves human nature and becomes a new kind through their habits, that new form is a settled trait whose true nature is attachment to matter and aversion to holiness. Once the person takes on that form and its effects appear, the soul leaves the body and the truth of the matter is revealed. The effects that follow from the soul’s own nature then cause it pain, with no way to avoid them. They are perfections that suit it, but they are painful because the person now sees what they truly are. This is like someone who has developed a harmful habit that undermines life, then learns its danger only after the habit has taken hold: while doing the act, they suffer from the very thing they enjoy.

    If the person remains human in nature, the painful conditions that torment them are forced upon them, but none is everlasting or occurs for most of the time. They recur and change, as one torment interacts with another, while the soul continues along its path of misery and becomes more miserable without end. This is suggested by the verses, “Whenever its flames die down, We increase them,” and, “Whenever their skins are burned away, We replace them with other skins so that they may taste the punishment.” In short, the torment is a kind of compulsion, but the kind of torment may continue forever even though no particular torment does. The philosophers do not deny that a kind of compulsion may last forever or occur most of the time; the author himself says so when explaining why the earth is continually forced into an uneven shape, though its natural shape is a sphere. — ط مد

[^V07-105]: The answer that comes to my mind now is to choose that the person remains in the original human nature. The objector says that this nature was hidden by temporary, accidental conditions. We reply: it was hidden by settled, lasting traits and essential forms that do not pass away. The person has become like a mixture of substances such as milk and vinegar, or like an animal tied to the same post as an angel. This matter is among the secrets that most minds cannot understand, and that may harm and destroy them. It is reported that the Messenger of God once passed through a street in Medina with his companions. A woman asked them to enter her house. They saw a blazing fire, with her children playing around it. She said, “Prophet of God, is God more merciful to His servants than I am to my children?” He replied, “God is the Most Merciful of those who show mercy.” She asked, “Would you ever want me to throw my child into the fire? How, then, would God put His servants into it, when He is more merciful to them?” The narrator says that the Messenger of God wept and said, “This is how God has revealed it to me.” — س قده

[^V07-106]: This also implies that the less worthy is preferred. — س قده

[^V07-107]: The world is not one in the strict sense of having one single kind or essence; it has a kind of unity, but its parts include different kinds. — ط مد

[^V07-108]: The totality of matter and material things has no matter of its own; likewise, the totality of the world of places and things in places has no place, and the totality of times and temporal things has no time. — س قده

[^V07-109]: This is a poetic rather than demonstrative argument. — ط مد

[^V07-110]: This means the effects of the stars’ supposed unfavourable influences on particular beings in the elemental world. Saturn and Jupiter are equally fortunate in the heavens themselves, since there is no opposition or conflict among them. So there is no evil in the heavenly realm. — س قده

[^V07-111]: In interpretation, day is the light of the Real, the existence of the heavens and earth. Without the night of essences, the splendour of His face would burn away everything reached by sight, and the rays of His light would dazzle the eyes and inner vision of those who see. Indeed, there would be no one to see. If the nights of essences were endless, without the light of existence; or if the nights of bodily matter had no lights from the guiding heavenly beings and the human and animal souls that give matter life, all would remain in the deep night of nonexistence, the dark night of death and shadow. Darkness would be layered on darkness, dread on dread, terror on terror. He brings night into day and day into night, sheltering us in His mercy, giving us life by His command, and comforting us with His light. A spirit from Him is entrusted to us, and His light is a trust in our keeping; one day the trusts must be returned. If you saw the complete poverty and need of every part of the Great Human—from head to foot—you would understand the great calamity that would follow: all life, all light, all existence itself belongs to God and returns to Him. “To whom does the kingdom belong today? To God, the One, the All-Conquering.” — س قده

[^V07-112]: This agrees with the report from the Imams that such a thing cannot happen; but God must not be described as powerless. — س قده

[^V07-113]: These aches are a kind of sensory, tactile awareness. If animals were not made to feel them, they would be seriously deficient; they would have no sense of touch and no animal balance. — س قده

[^V07-114]: If this protective impulse does not occur, or does not help an individual whose body is ruined by an illness before it reaches full growth, providence does not concern itself with that rare case; the aim is universal benefit, as explained above. — س قده

[^V07-115]: You might say that neither this explanation nor what follows explains why living animals eat the bodies of other living animals. We reply that the reason is clear when we combine it with what was said earlier: the cause must be sought at the level of the whole, since the aim is common benefit and universal well-being. So the incidental urging of living animals to eat other living animals is negligible beside the protection and care given to their bodies, and the direct provision of carcasses for food. They are also given alertness, quick perception, and tools for fighting or fleeing. As the poet says: “You taught the fierce lion to attack, and gave the gazelle of Khotan its speed.” Look, people of insight, at what the wise Creator has done in this world, home to worms and a place of struggle, conflict, and change: He directs each thing toward its purpose and fulfills its true aim, as it journeys toward self-effacement in the One it worships. This is truly complete and excellent. The beauty of His work overwhelms the mind, and the precision of His ordering amazes the heart. — س قده

[^V07-116]: The relation is reversed in the world of origination: there it is active causation; in the world of creation it is receptivity. It is also a relation of sustaining what depends on another. — س قده

[^V07-117]: The plant soul continually uses its powers to grow, nourish, strengthen, and balance its own matter—its taste, smell, and other qualities—so that it can provide material for the animal soul. Likewise, the animal soul continually prepares its own matter for the human soul. Through its powers of sense and movement, it seeks what suits it and avoids what does not, preparing its matter so that it may come to your door and enter your presence. Attar points to this when he says: “The angels’ service is all for you; paradise and hell are the reflections of your kindness and severity.” — س قده

[^V07-118]: This is how things are in reality. From the longing soul’s point of view, and according to what it senses, it may seem an advantage, a perfection, or a low and harmful state. If it does not recognise what is truly sought, who is sought, and at least what manifestation or appearance is being sought, it loses out: it confines absolute light and pure beauty to the receiver, unaware of the reality and occupied with its outward form. This way of seeing gives rise to multiplicity. When reality appears and borrowed forms disappear—as God says, “The Real has come, and falsehood has vanished,” and, “When he came to it, he found it to be nothing, but found God there”—then the person is left empty-handed and cries, “Alas for what I neglected in relation to God!” — س قده

[^V07-119]: This is voluntary death, not natural death. If the wording is left unrestricted, it could include natural separation as well. The ascent of the souls that help others should be understood like sorting grain from its husks in a pile, not like sowing seeds and growing a crop. The latter kind of ascent is not possible, since this world alone is the field in which to sow: wheat yields wheat, barley yields barley. — س قده

[^V07-120]: This needs qualification. The sinful aspect of actions is privative. Every sinful act has a corresponding act of the same kind that is obedience; what distinguishes them is that one agrees with a religious or rational command and the other disobeys or abandons it. Adultery and marriage, taking another’s property by force or with consent, and killing unjustly or in lawful retaliation are examples. The disobedience and abandonment are the sinful aspect of the act, and they are absences, not existing things. This is why the Qur'an generally attributes things to creation and goodness, but attributes disobedience to evil and calls it wrongdoing: “God is the Creator of everything,” “Who perfected everything He created,” and, “How evil is the name of disobedience after faith.” Perhaps the author means that the acts described as sins exist, as the end of his argument suggests when he traces blame for gossip and adultery to disobeying a command and abandoning obedience. — ط مد

[^V07-121]: This is the meaning behind the saying: “A point moved lengthwise and became a line; the line moved sideways and became a surface; the surface moved in depth and became a body.” It is an image for what was described: the flowing point draws all things, just as the flowing instant draws time, and the middle movement draws the completed movement. Each of the three is like a spirit for what it draws. This is because everything is a sign of the Real, as the verse says: “In everything there is a sign that He is One.” Because the comparison might mislead, the author immediately adds a statement of transcendence, following the way the Qur'an joins these two claims: “Nothing is like Him, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing.” — س قده

[^V07-122]: This is the familiar proof that each immaterial kind is confined to one individual. It applies to the immaterial intellects in the descending order, but not to the other beings—such as the souls of the spheres and stars, and their bodies—because these are connected with matter. — ط مد

[^V07-123]: We discussed the Platonic Forms and this principle in Volume I. The account here of the order of creation—the souls and bodies of the spheres and stars, and their benefits and perfections—is based on the views of earlier astronomers and natural philosophers. Modern research does not confirm it. Since these detailed questions do not fall under general philosophical demonstrations, philosophy took them from related sciences as established assumptions. Now that they have been shown false or inadequate, the right course is to use the findings of modern research as assumptions and build the discussion on them. Those sciences are responsible for establishing their details. But the central claim—that the order of origination displays the most careful making and noblest arrangement—stands firm. The immaterial world reflects the divine order, the best and noblest order, and is more complete than what lies below it. The imaginal world reflects the intellectual world, which contains the fullest possible existence and noblest order, and is more complete than the material world below it. The material world—with its galaxies, suns, planets, and moons; their movements and cycles of generation and decay; its elements, their compounds, and their atoms; the fixed suns and moving planets within them; its particular systems and the universal order governing all of them according to constant principles, unchanging measures, and enduring guidance—all of this astonishes the mind with its beauty and fills the inner vision with its radiance. The wonders of creation, subtle care, and forms of good that human beings have discovered are only a drop in a vast ocean. — ط مد

[^V07-124]: The rays that descend from the sun to earth may travel in a straight line, reflect back along another line, or bend along a different line, forming an angle. Some rays fall on the waters around the earth and bend toward land. For example, when the sun is in the east, some of its rays reach the earth directly while others bend toward it, because the upper northern quarter of the earth is exposed above the water, while its lower northern quarter and both southern quarters are covered by water. — س قده

[^V07-125]: Every moving thing may fail to reach one end when considered from one point of view, yet reach an end when considered in relation to the causes and capacities that lead it there. That is the end its mover actually directs it toward. Each being turns toward one of God’s names and worships it; only the perfect human being, the greatest gate and straightest path, worships God in the fullest sense. A supposed failure to reach an end is relative to some other end that partial, imaginative minds expect, and that expectation is only imagined. A being can reach the highest end by coming to the threshold of this greatest gate. The Qur'an says, “This Qur'an guides to what is most upright,” and, “Enter houses by their doors.”
    +
    +Likewise, the claim that a human life naturally lasts one hundred and twenty years means that a natural kind is realized when at least one individual realizes it. Individuals with weaker constitutions and less balanced bodily conditions have a natural lifespan suited to their own matter and circumstances. To say they did not reach their “natural lifespan” means only that they did not reach the lifespan expected of the kind in an individual who lives longer. It is a relative and imagined comparison. The same applies to movements toward ends: expecting every individual to attain the highest end is based on the kind or class, not the individual, and is only an imagined expectation. Understand this well. — س قده

[^V07-126]: As in the saying, “Beware the insight of the believer, for he sees by God’s light.” Especially so for the person who travels through the Real and from the Real back to creation. A Persian verse says: “Look again with the pure light of the King, lest you imagine you see with a short-sighted eye.” The “angels who know” are the universal intellects; the “angels who act” are the universal souls; the powers that directly move the heavens are the imprinted souls and the celestial powers and natures. — س قده

[^V07-127]: If “preceding” means earlier in time, this would conflict with the claim that the specific forms of simple beings are created without matter and are prior to motion.
