The meaning must instead be either that earlier bodily forms help as receptive matter for later specific forms; that prior forms help the later forms in a timeless order, with “directions” meaning the luminous features in the lords of the kinds; or that earlier celestial forms help bring about the elements in an essential order. Some say the Active Intellect causes the four elements through the four groups of spheres. The last interpretation best fits the school of Aristotle. — س قده

[^V07-128]: The difference between this reason and the one before it is like the difference between unity and individuality in concept: unity is the broader notion. — س قده

[^V07-129]: This reason is specifically aimed at denying that there could be another individual of the same kind. If it were broadened to appeal to the unity, individuality, and knowledge of the cause, it would return to the earlier reasons. The author’s words, from “the final cause of its existence” to “the noblest possible being,” restate the point and indicate a proof without spelling it out, leaving the reader to recognise it. The explanation is that the argument seeks to establish the unity of the act, after establishing the unity of the agent and the end. If another world were proposed, it would have the same single, necessary cause; when viewed in that light, it would simply be this world, especially since we have already seen that the answer to “What is it?” is the same as the answer to “Why is it?” — س قده

[^V07-130]: You might ask how the First Intellect and the Great Human could be of one kind: the First Intellect is a separate substance in its essence and activity, while the Great Human includes the whole body, soul, and intellect. The questioner points back to the earlier claim that the two are “like each other in reality.” Given your principle that the answers to “What is it?” and “Why is it?” are one, the questioner argues that the answer to “Why does the Great Human exist?”—God—is also the answer to “What is it?” The same is true of the First Intellect. If the answer to “Why?” is the same for both, then the answer to “What?” is also the same, and they are alike in kind. — س قده

[^V07-131]: Every complete being includes the perfections of all that lies beneath it. After God, the First Intellect is the world’s form, its face and unity, and what brings it into actuality. Since it is simple reality, it must contain all the lower forms of existence in a unified way; the world is the place where that unity is unfolded into multiplicity. We have already learned that a kind is defined by its last and decisive feature, while the other defining elements prepare for it. What was first a condition becomes, in the end, a condition of completion. A thing is what it is through its form. The First Intellect is therefore the final defining feature and form of the Great Human: what makes it actual. A thing is also what it is through its completeness; what is incomplete is contained within what is complete, just as intellect is contained within the light of the Light of Lights. — س قده

[^V07-132]: Some things are gradual, such as durations, sequences, and contacts that occur at an endpoint in time. Some lie outside both classes, such as immaterial beings. Or one might say intellects are instantaneous in a timeless sense, while souls are instantaneous in their essence but gradual in their activity. Another possibility is that souls are instantaneous and intellects, which belong to the divine realm, are neither gradual nor instantaneous in a timeless sense: they remain through God, not by being continually preserved, and belong to the eternal. The first interpretation is clearest. — س قده

[^V07-133]: If you ask about the truth, every work of God is subtle and every form of nature is a wonder. But because we perceive these things gradually, we do not marvel at all of them. Also, when something exceeds a limit, it can have the opposite effect. The signs, proofs, and wonders have become so numerous that people no longer notice them. Each being, by virtue of its own nature, can do something or several things that others cannot. The complete human being alone is more wondrous than all wonders. One of our leaders is reported to have said: “The Holy Spirit in the gardens of the celestial sphere has tasted the first fruits of our gardens.” If this is the state of the Holy Spirit, what do you think the state of others is? You can see the truth of what we have said about the presence of wondrous signs if you return each existing thing to its first state: a shapeless material or an inert element, without any decoration or form. Compare that first, empty state with what has been adorned and completed by its first and later perfections, and you will see the difference between the head of a gathering beneath domes and dust, between the Lord of lords and earth. Then even the movement of the lowly earthworm becomes a wonder, and understanding it a greater wonder. The flight of a fly, which resists the air, is strange; stranger still is its care in touching its head and wings with its legs and cleaning them so they do not get dirty or weigh it down and prevent it from flying. If these things are so wondrous, what of the nobler beings—and especially the noblest being, the human being, who once passed through a time when he was nothing worth mentioning, a dirty drop and impure blood, then changed through stages into an embryo and grew until he came out of his mother’s womb, still one of the weakest creatures? He then grew to maturity and learned to distinguish guidance from error. If God’s care reaches him, he may become a person who knows God, a powerful angel, a leader who displays knowledge and power, a saint, or a prophet and God’s deputy. In the end, he gathers beings into himself, inherits them, and folds them into his own existence, just as the First Intellect is God’s deputy, giving existence to beings according to their readiness and unfolding them in their kinds at the beginning. If you are amazed, this is what is most amazing; if you are moved, this is what is most moving. So rise and sing along with the nightingale in the sanctuary of love and the songbird in the gardens of knowledge and guidance: “The unseen voice pours wine into our cup; let the musician play, for the world has gone our way. We have seen the beloved’s face reflected in the cup, though you know nothing of the pleasure of drinking without end. We delight in the eyes of our beloved; for this reason, our reins have been handed over to intoxication.” This visible human being and the wonder he displays are described in the verse of the author’s spiritual guide. — س قده

[^V07-134]: This verse strongly urges people to seek knowledge. It shows that the soul becomes fully actual and truly lives through knowledge, since every breath is in God’s hand. If people fail to reflect and die before gaining true knowledge and becoming actual through it, what teaching or inward belief could they trust to complete them after they have left the world where they could grow and become complete? The verse also shows that growth cannot continue in the next life: the next life is harvest time, while this world is the field in which it is sown. — س قده

[^V07-135]: The author means that among substances, prime matter is the weakest, and among attributes, motion is the weakest. Matter can receive all created things, and the circular motion of the heavens connects these events to the timeless First Cause. If even these two weakest things contain countless purposes and benefits, imagine what is found in stronger kinds of being. — س قده

[^V07-136]: In place of the celestial spheres described by the author, we should put the flowing nature of the whole world, divided into the simple and compound bodily kinds, whose parts are its changing movements. In place of the outermost sphere and the measure of its movement, we should put universal time, which measures this flowing nature. This follows from what was said earlier about how weak and inadequate the old theory of the spheres is. — Ismail and T. M. (editor).

[^V07-137]: Interpret “night” and “day” more broadly than their usual meanings, so that the verse includes the case where the paths of the stars coincide with the path of their shared swift movement; or take the verse as an example of what came before and what follows. — س قده

[^V07-138]: Reason judges by means of real distinctions. In nature, a universal kind either continues as one individual, as with the spheres and their stars, since individuals of a bodily kind multiply by being divided, and this cannot happen to a sphere or star; or it continues as a kind through successive individuals, as with the elements and the things made from them, which can be divided. — س قده

[^V07-139]: For this reason, when a traveling mystic was asked whether he had seen anything wondrous in his long journeys through many lands, he answered: “I have seen nothing more wondrous than myself.” — س قده

[^V07-140]: That is, the image of the meaning. The guiding spirit first takes an imaginal form, then a natural form mixed with matter. It is like pure air struck by cold until a thin cloud forms, made only from the air; then vapour enters it and makes the cloud dense. This passage refers to the spirit’s earlier state, when it was a universal intellect—or rather, in God’s knowledge—and to the soul’s priority over the body in time, being, and rank. The next phrase, “making the dense subtle,” follows the author’s established view that the soul begins through the body but endures as a spirit. — س قده

[^V07-141]: The pure, finest blood in each receptive organ takes on that organ’s nature, so in the heart it becomes something like the heart’s subtle essence. Most blood, of course, flows through the veins. Nature sends a good portion to the heart; much of it enters the heart’s right chamber and a little the left, where it becomes a vital vapour. — س قده

[^V07-142]: That is, the spirit mentioned above. Just as this vapour is the sphere of the little human world, the sphere is like the vital vapour of the great human world. This is why God calls it “smoke” in His Book. — س قده

[^V07-143]: The three layers of darkness are the darkness of the placenta, the womb, and the abdomen; or the darkness of the abdomen, the world of bodies, and prime matter. The spirit, too, has three layers of darkness: the natural form, the in-between form, and the form of the next life. It must leave all three if it is to cast off the two worlds and both sandals, even if forms are only shadows to which it pays no attention. — س قده

[^V07-144]: “What an artist, whose overflowing beauty has captured the image in its net!”

[^V07-145]: This phrase may mean that the powers are the subject of “appointed,” with the angels as the object. Or the angels may be the subject: the powers, as they face God and are connected to Him, are angels; as they face downward and connect with matter, they are powers and natures. God’s aspect is dominant over the soul’s aspect, and the powers are appointed to it. The first reading treats these two aspects as one in a certain respect. — س قده

[^V07-146]: The word may mean “or” or may be a way of moving to a new point. The number of angels cannot be counted; the reference is to the natures and spiritual powers of things both below and above. The author names one, ten, a hundred, and a thousand to suggest what was said. A similar tradition says there are one hundred and twenty-four thousand prophets. God’s words and blessings do not run out or perish, and His blessings cannot be counted. — س قده

[^V07-147]: You might say that we have no power that oversees the amounts. I think the author means the nutritive power. By “the fourth gives it the form of flesh, bone, and other things,” he may mean that the third strips away its earlier form, and that digestion is the third power. — س قده

[^V07-148]: The active principles and powers are angels insofar as they are connected to what is above them. Each higher being is like a body for the spirit above it, so every one is noble through the nobility of what is above it—indeed, radiant through the glory of God, the Praiseworthy and Glorious. They are like His seeing eyes and working hands, or levels of His active power. Since the questioner’s words suggested that a single task would be a lowly state, the author first establishes the angels’ nobility, then shows that many actions can issue from them in an ordered sequence. The universal intellects and souls—the angels brought near, who stand in rows and direct affairs—have many actions in this way. The active principles in the imaginal world do too. The powers and natures working in the hidden world have the same kind of many actions as what is above them, since a thing is what it is through its form. The questioner’s claim concerns all angels. — س قده

[^V07-149]: This is the animal power in the terminology used by doctors. It prepares the organs to receive sensation and movement. It is also the power that expands and contracts the heart and arteries, renewing the vital spirit. In short, it maintains the vital spirit. The term “animal power” is sometimes used for the brain’s powers discussed later, but that is not what is meant here. — س قده

[^V07-150]: In this terminology, an animal moves through appetite, not through will. But “will” has two meanings, one narrow and one broad. In the narrow sense, it applies to immaterial beings and refers to an intellectual inclination or something similar. In the broad sense, it applies to animals and refers to sensory and appetitive inclination. — س قده

[^V07-151]: The earth is first divided in half by the equator into northern and southern halves. A circle passing through its four northern, southern, eastern, and western points then divides it into quarters. Roughly three quarters are covered by water: the northern and southern parts below that circle, and the southern part above it. The exposed part is roughly the upper northern quarter. — س قده

[^V07-152]: The wording needs to be corrected by restoring a phrase left out of the sentence: as people travel north, the visible pole rises higher and the hidden southern pole sinks lower; the reverse happens for those traveling south. Otherwise, instead of “the reverse” the text should say “for those traveling south.” — س قده

[^V07-153]: By listing women among the animals, the author hints that women’s reason is weak, that they remain fixed on particulars, and that they desire worldly adornment, so most of their conduct is like that of animals. Yet, he says, they were given human form so that men would not be repelled by their company and would want to marry them. He uses this to explain why Islamic law gives men authority over women in many rulings, such as divorce, disobedience, and cases where harm is compounded—unlike some rulings in other religions. “How far apart are the Pleiades and the bright star!” — س قده

[^V07-154]: The animal souls meant here are those on the path toward human beings, since something is adorned by what is connected to it. The same applies to the plant souls connected to animal souls on that path, and to what lies above the rational soul, such as the divine universal soul. Separate beings are also adorned by the complete human being, who reflects them in the visible world. Natural powers reflect his nature; plants his plant life; animals his animal life; embryos and devils his capacity for cunning; working angels his practical reason, purity, and protection from sin; and angels who know his theoretical reason, wisdom, and knowledge. The sphere, which the divine Book calls “smoke,” reflects his vital spirit, form, and beauty. In short, the powers and natures of all the worlds are gathered within him in a unified way. A Persian verse says: “When we sent Adam out, we spread our beauty across the plain.”

[^V07-155]: Everything enters existence through seven things: knowledge, will, decree, measure, a record, permission, and an appointed term, as a hadith says. The “record” means its measured imaginal form. — س قده

[^V07-156]: This describes the vertical sequence of beings as they turn toward the inward realm; it does not describe events happening at particular times, since time itself is folded away. Countless moving things reach countless ends over countless periods, and all are gathered into the ultimate end. — س قده

[^V07-157]: It is easy to understand how sound might exist in the heavens, through bodies striking or touching one another as they move. As for tastes and pleasant scents, the author has hinted at how they may be there: whoever gives a perfection must possess it. Yet other beings do not receive these qualities by tasting or smelling them as we do. The clearer account is that, if God made them reach such a being, it would become a sphere—unless this were done as a miracle for a prophet. Such a being would then possess what it received, because the spheres act upon things made from the elements and have stronger active powers than fire. Another explanation, which the philosophers in Aristotle’s school would accept if they listened, is that the spheres possess these bodily perfections in a higher way, through the forms in their senses—their own inward images. These forms are present through active knowing, not passive reception, just as a human soul may create the image of a beautiful face in its shared sense even when the body it inhabits does not have a beautiful face. A scholar would judge that this person possesses the beautiful image in a higher and more complete way than the matter in which it is impressed. The ignorant person would not, because he did not see the beauty on the body. The same applies to taste, smell, and other senses. Since the spheres perceive through strong, active knowing, the author holds that their inward images are senses, as our imagination is a sense. The sphere itself, however, is simple, with similar parts, so it cannot contain different powers and natures in the way a body does. — س قده

[^V07-158]: Impure souls cannot enter heaven as particular individuals, nor can their bodies, which are unlike heavenly bodies, though the kind of human soul may be able to enter. The verse “Do not let them approach the Sacred Mosque” applies to such souls by the rule that governs their nature. — س قده

[^V07-159]: The interpretation is that holy souls belong to the higher heaven, but because they are connected to the natural world they also belong to the nearest heaven. Intellects are different: their being and attention are fully raised above it. Heaven is adorned with lamps because it is lit by knowledge, its qualities, and its actions. Among these actions are the universal divine souls, the greatest signs. Its stars drive away devils by using the light of proofs to remove doubts. — س قده

[^V07-160]: The “firm roots and branches reaching the sky” mean that the spoken word is a bounded sensory or psychic form whose effect reaches the depths of the heart and becomes a simple intellect that encompasses things. In the inward, creative word, the firm root and its other parts mean that its natural level is still while its spiritual level unfolds—or that its preserved root remains firm while its aspects spread out. — س قده

[^V07-161]: This includes the prayer during a lunar or solar eclipse and the night prayer. If the moon is near the horizon at the beginning of the night, it is high in the middle of the sky at midnight. The same applies to other nights. Many recommended prayers are set by lunar times, as those who observe them know. — س قده

[^V07-162]: This is taken from the noble traditions: the Inhabited House is truly the place where God is remembered and known. It is the station of the souls in heaven, that is, in their matter and form. — س قده

[^V07-163]: The first group are the universal intellects sought by the movements of the heavens. They are compared to the heavens. Their prostration is their complete disappearance and erasure in witnessing the transcendent Real. The second group are the universal and particular souls impressed in matter, which seek the universal intellects as manifestations of God and His overpowering lights. They bow because their existence has not vanished as completely as that of the first group; they are also directed toward the heavenly bodies. From this, a worshipper should reflect on the meanings of the postures in prayer. A Persian verse says: “Every veil He draws has a path within it.” In standing, one leaves attachment to the natural world and follows no passing desire; one rises from worldly life, stands before God, and affirms the oneness of His actions. In bowing, one lowers oneself, surrenders, and stretches out one’s neck as if ready to be struck, submitting to God’s words: “God has bought the believers’ lives in exchange for the garden”—the garden of attributes or the garden of the divine essence. The one who bows affirms the oneness of God’s attributes. In prostration, one’s existence disappears and is erased completely, affirming the oneness of God’s essence and passing away fully in Him. — س قده

[^V07-164]: That is, God formed the body and also shaped the inward soul in all forty-eight familiar forms. Some thinkers of illumination say that the body, like the soul, is impressed with the forms of things. — س قده

[^V07-165]: People are kept from this knowledge by turning away from divine philosophy and mathematical sciences, especially astronomy, the noblest of them. They know the heavens only as a color and the stars only as glints and flashes. In truth, they know nothing of the heavens: the color they see belongs to vapour and smoke. They have no understanding of what the heavens are, whether they exist, why they exist, what matter, form, and the fifth nature mean, or what their individual and universal souls are. They have shown no sign of understanding because their concerns are food, sex, and power over people in the natural world. They have not reflected on the creation of the heavens or themselves, or on their greatness, which would make worldly things seem small. They have not reflected on the lasting realm of the spirit, or how short a thousand years of bodily life is compared with the soul’s continued life—whether it is blessed and delighted by meeting God or tormented and deprived. A finite period cannot be compared with what has no end. The same is true when you compare the greatness of your holy soul with the greatness of the world: a person who has never seen a mountain thinks a camel is enormous, while someone who sees the camel beside the mountain knows how small it is.

    Raise your luminous imagination to the spheres and look with astonishment at the quarter of the earth that lies uncovered by water, and at the tiny creatures crawling over it. That is how created beings look from above. Why do people cling to the earth and boast of owning it? How small it is, and how small are those who control it, compared with the vast spheres and their countless stars. Many stars are each larger than the whole earth. Look inwardly at their immense sizes, not as they appear to your outward sight—as small, dim points of light. Notice their varied movements: some travel westward against the usual order, others eastward with it; their speeds differ too. Some complete a circuit in a year, others in twelve years, others in thirty years. The slowest takes twenty-five thousand two hundred years, while the fastest completes its circuit in twenty-four hours. Others take different lengths of time. Notice how the fixed stars keep their positions in relation to one another, while the wandering stars continually change position. They are manifestations of God’s enduring life and displays of His power and care.

    Then rise in your mind to the world of their individual souls, the imagination of the Great Human, whose body is the spheres and all they contain; then to their universal souls, which perceive universal and separate realities; and then to the universal intellects and overpowering lights, both those in a vertical order and those spread across one level. They are simple beings that encompass all, each a unified whole. Whoever sees them has seen God, for they belong to the divine realm. Divine qualities prevail in them, and the rules of everything other than God are consumed within them: they have no state of waiting or readiness to receive. Then follow the poet’s counsel: “O bird’s egg of possibility, you are both the white and the yolk. Spread your wings and fly beyond this old, worn sky.” Be one of those who swim in the sea of God’s greatness and stand in rows before Him, with steadiness and a firm station. The stages of completion are attachment, taking on divine qualities, and realization—or, if you prefer, a passing thought, a state, a settled trait, and a firm station. “Remain upright, as you have been commanded.” — س قده

[^V07-166]: This means the commanding lights cast upon dark beings, which are natural bodies. It draws on the noble hadith: “God created creation in darkness, then sprinkled His light upon them.” Or “His light” means existence given to beings and essences that are dark in themselves. — س قده

[^V07-167]: This refers to what the author says in *Keys to the Unseen* and elsewhere: every seven thousand years, the world of generation and decay is governed by one of the wandering stars, alone and in combination with the others. There are seven such periods; adding a thousand years to account for fractions and leap years in the astronomical year gives fifty thousand years, which is one of the days of the Resurrection. — س قده

[^V07-168]: The author will say that love is broader than longing: God loves Himself, and the intellects love themselves, even when they feel no longing. Longing applies only when there is a lack. The love placed in each thing preserves its being, like its first perfection; the longing placed in it seeks what it lacks, like its second perfection; and movement puts that search into action until it reaches what it seeks. In beings that can act, movement is the means by which the agent reaches its goal. It is like a necessary step toward a goal: needed to get there, but not the goal itself. — س قده

[^V07-169]: There is a question about restricting change in size to plants and animals. The philosophers also allowed a kind of interpenetration, which they counted among the movements of the heavenly spheres, even though it is not plant or animal growth. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-170]: The first point gives every being a beloved; the second says that all existence is one reality, so that the beloved of all beings is God. Their existence is His manifestation and belongs to the same order as His. This also establishes that the cause completes its effect. Each being longs for its perfection, and its perfection belongs to the same order as itself: strong whiteness completes weak whiteness, for example, not blackness; strong light and heat complete weak light and heat, not their opposites. We have established that existence does not consist of wholly separate realities. — س قده

[^V07-171]: This may be a slip of the pen. Certainty is knowledge that something must be so and that its opposite cannot be so. It is general and lasting, and no proof can remove it. A proof may show that something which seemed certain was actually ignorance that only looked like certainty. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-172]: A closer comparison would be an embrace mixed with playful biting and teasing between lover and beloved. — س قده

[^V07-173]: In everyday speech, “love” often means the special attachment between male and female animals: desire for sexual union. In more precise usage, it means a special attachment of a conscious being to beauty as beauty, so that the lover does not want to part from the beloved when they find them and longs for them when they are absent. All beauty, goodness, and happiness return to existence, just as their opposites return to non-existence. This kind of attachment is found between every level of existence and what is above it, between a being and itself, and between a being and the perfections that follow from it. So love must run through all beings, whether or not we say that life and awareness run through them too. This shows that love does not mean knowledge or awareness, though the two may be found together. The author’s claim that love without life or awareness is only a name may therefore need review. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-174]: The author has proved that knowledge goes with existence apart from matter. Matter, and material things such as bodies, cannot be present to themselves in this way, so they are not directly knowing subjects or objects of knowledge. For knowledge to run through all beings and for bodies to be called knowing means that their imaginal and intellectual forms—their actual perfections—know themselves and other things. Knowing them means knowing them through their imaginal and intellectual forms. The same account applies to power: active power means that something can produce effects. In material things, this means that their imaginal and intellectual forms are the sources of their effects, since bodily power is only the possibility of acting, not its full activity. If knowledge and power run through all beings, so does life, which means that a being can know and act. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-175]: The wording would be clearer as “its different kinds come to be through forms,” which is probably how the original read. If “it becomes kinds” is retained, then if this means bodily forms, these are general kinds arranged in levels; if it means species-forms, these are specific kinds at the same level. In either case matter becomes different kinds through forms. — س قده

[^V07-176]: The forms of the four elements and the heavenly sphere; the number becomes six when we include the specific form found in compound things. — س قده

[^V07-177]: When the subject changes and disappears, its accidents follow it into non-existence, while their opposites take their place. Logicians call this an “opposite” whether or not it exists as a thing. The author may instead mean an opposite in the terminology of the philosophers; this follows from the fact that an accident ceases to exist when its subject does, since the subject is one of the things that make it what it is. — س قده

[^V07-178]: First, the connection described here is a special one: it is attachment to perfection and completion, as we have indicated. Accidents are levels of a substance’s existence and complete it, so their attachment to their subject is attachment to perfection, or love. Second, the things Ibn Sina calls necessary attributes of essences are abstract notions and do not exist on their own. Relations and connections, if they are abstract notions, are like these attributes. If they are real relations of existence, they are like the other kinds of existence in which we find love. All of them are dependent existences in relation to the Necessary Being, as every effect is in relation to its cause. If Ibn Sina means relations in the formal sense, they are real accidents. To object to his claim that love runs through accidents by citing surfaces and edges, when he himself says that love runs through them, is to rely on a claim the other side does not accept. That is no proof. Nor is it self-evident; in fact, the objection applies to the one making it as well. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-179]: Unless the author means love indirectly, as he says. — س قده

[^V07-180]: That is, there is nothing separate from God, standing apart from Him. All existence is His manifestation, its link to Him, and complete dependence on Him. He exists through Himself, for Himself, and by Himself. “People, you are the ones who need God; God is the Self-Sufficient, the Praiseworthy.” — س قده

[^V07-181]: You might object that preserving effects through the love of their cause requires the higher cause to attend to the lower effect. Then bringing an effect into being must require this attention too, as must giving it love. The answer is that all this flows from God’s generosity. God acts through care, not through an additional purpose. The loves joined to the worlds and their inhabitants are expressions of His will and love. All things bow before the Living, the Self-Sustaining. New things also arise through His love for Himself, the fixed realities’ love for His names, essences’ love for their existence, and beings’ love for their perfection. Their perfection is necessary existence itself: when love is complete, it is God. — س قده

[^V07-182]: Just as the cause loves the effect’s perfection through its love for itself, the effect also loves the cause indirectly, because whoever loves what something requires also loves the thing itself. The effect’s perfection is preserved by love in this way: the cause loves itself. The effect’s being is preserved through the love that follows from that first love. Another reading is that every effect is preserved by the effect’s love for its cause, because a thing is what it is through its completeness and perfection. As the author will say, loving its cause means that its being is linked to the cause. This real being is the bond of existence, its connection to the cause, and the outpouring of existence from the cause to it. This link is a form of love: the author calls it will, connection, and love, as in the saying, “I loved to be known.” Love, will, and connection are one. Through this dependent existence, the effect keeps its identity, since it is nothing but a relation. Without this link, it would have no identity. If the lower being did not love the higher—that is, depend on it—it would vanish. If “love” instead means the love the higher being has for the perfection of the lower, that is the higher being’s love for itself, as we have seen. — س قده

[^V07-183]: Since it is better to establish a new point than to repeat the previous one, this sentence does not restate the earlier claim. The earlier point is that love preserves the effect’s basic identity. This point is that it preserves its completion and perfection through love of the cause, which gives it being and is itself the effect’s perfection. The cause’s love is identical with its very being, since there is nothing in it but its own pure being. — س قده

[^V07-184]: The mirror is hidden by the image and disappears into it. If we looked at the mirror itself, it would not be a mirror but an object to be seen. This is why the view that beings are mirrors in which God appears is higher than the view that God is a mirror containing images of beings. — س قده

[^V07-185]: This can mean either that the Active Intellect understands God, or that it understands itself, which is God’s self-disclosure, and that its intelligible forms are also God’s self-disclosures. It also knows itself by knowing its cause. On the first reading, “Himself” refers to the one who appears in the self-disclosure. — س قده

[^V07-186]: The Active Intellect’s self-disclosure of intelligible forms and the intelligible things are one, even if there are a thousand intellects. A thing cannot be repeated as itself. If it were repeated, there would be separate individuals, and material conditions would cause the difference. An intelligible thing is like a center; the intellects and souls are like radii that meet at that center. What the Active Intellect understands and what the soul understands are one, even though the soul understands it with the Active Intellect’s help and light. It is, in truth, what God understands. “They grasp nothing of His knowledge except what He wills.” — س قده

[^V07-187]: This means the saintly and prophetic souls, which are universal and encompass all things. The whole world is like a body for them. They are the universal divine soul, the fourth kind in a report from the leader of the saints, the Commander of the Faithful. He divides the soul into four kinds: the growing plant soul, the sensing animal soul, the speaking rational soul, and the holy universal divine soul. He then describes each one. The report is well known. — س قده

[^V07-188]: This is the idea behind the saying, “Take the ends and leave the beginnings.” Everyone seeks freedom from need, which belongs to God; lasting life, which belongs to God; and oneness, which belongs to God, along with His other qualities. Everyone is a lover, but the perfection lies in recognising what one perceives. A Persian verse says: “Thousands of particles rush about in the sun, unaware of what the sun is.” — س قده

[^V07-189]: This means the cause of leadership, when souls are naturally inclined to have servants and attendants. The philosophers of illumination therefore called the soul the “commanding light.” Souls that stop seeking true leadership seem to leave their original nature and turn from this love toward another, such as love of food and drink, or a false kind of leadership. Even this has the cause the author gives. — س قده

[^V07-190]: Discovering true knowledge is a way of becoming like God in knowledge, as philosophers say: philosophy is becoming like God through knowledge and action. The greatest joy is knowledge of God and His greatest sign, the rational soul, as well as knowledge of the nearby causes. Knowledge is the highest purpose of creation. God says: “I created creation so that I might be known.” — س قده

[^V07-191]: Love can be divided according to the being that receives it, or, since receiving a quality means being affected by it, according to the being that causes it. This is like dividing movement into natural, forced, and chosen movement according to its cause. If it is divided according to what receives it, it is like dividing movement into heavenly, earthly, spiritual, and bodily movement. — س قده

[^V07-192]: Love in animals is subtler and more pleasurable than love in plants because animal powers are joined to desire. The three plant powers therefore act together with desire and longing. The senses and imagination also give animals more ways to reach what they love: they can reach it through its natural presence, through its image in the senses, and through its image in imagination. The first of these is the least subtle. — س قده

[^V07-193]: This means the kind’s guiding cause. The author will give it a separate chapter after this one. — س قده

[^V07-194]: The wording should be “more effects,” followed by the explanation “either in number or in strength,” meaning quantity or quality. — س قده

[^V07-195]: This is said about natural powers, since the purpose of a falling body is simply the point where its movement ends; it does not desire that end. The phrase about achieving particular aims applies to non-rational animals, whose appetite gives them aims, though these are imagined and particular. — س قده

[^V07-196]: God says: “Your creation and your rising again are like the creation and raising of a single soul.” Each material part changes form and receives a new one, because God’s giving never stops and His light never sets. A Persian verse says: “By His power, in the field of timeless dominion, each breath becomes Abraham’s fire, each handful of dust a human being.” Traditions from the Imams say that there is no place on earth that has not been a human being’s grave. This does not conflict with all beings eventually reaching their highest goal at the great Resurrection, when the world changes. That account describes a vertical order, as we see in the complete human being: even the body changes toward its final goal. The elements that once received its light can still continue moving; they are not left without purpose. Each thing has a container suited to it. Since the possible things and their goals are without end, there are many movements, also without end: positional and non-positional, accidental and substantial, sideways and upward, in endless containers. All of them reach their final goal. This does not conflict with the endless flow of God’s gifts. We say that the goals too are without end because the final goal exists as a relation to them. — س قده

[^V07-197]: We gave an explanation of this number shortly before. Now that we have shown the full account, the number should be understood as referring to a kind or cycle. Or it may mean that after each period and cycle ends, the world returns to the beginning, since “the heaven returns” and “the earth splits open.” The positions and their effects, the compounds and their properties, and their customs and laws all change so much that it is like another world. Yet all this follows the unity of divine action: God is God in heaven and on earth, in what has passed and what is to come. His command is one. — س قده

[^V07-198]: They may see no stronger reason than this love to draw the soul away from the desires of the natural world after it has been drawn toward God or has followed religious discipline. That is why Farid al-Din Attar, who was wholly devoted to love, said: “A speck of love from Khurasan is better than love from all the world; a speck of pain is better than all lovers.” The holy ones have love but no pain; only human beings can bear pain. You may object that spiritual teachers must reconcile religious law with the spiritual path, and that the most learned among them hold firmly to this principle. How can it agree with religious law? Perhaps they found permission in its symbols and subtle meanings. Or perhaps they allowed one act to be both commanded and forbidden, as many religious scholars do. For example, leaving a house wrongfully occupied is commanded, but the act also involves using another person’s property without permission. Removing the penis after adultery is commanded, but the act is also forbidden for the same reason. The same act is therefore both forbidden and required from different points of view. A fuller explanation belongs elsewhere. God knows the secrets. — س قده

[^V07-199]: In fact, it is the lovers who withdraw from ordinary tasks. Yet withdrawing from worldly affairs can be better than spending time on things that keep a person from real perfection. The lover may not need to clear away other concerns first; it may be enough to add the better thing. — س قده

[^V07-200]: The bodily powers turn toward the brain in service of the soul, as does the natural warmth that helps the brain act. If this happens too much, the brain dries and burns because it cannot bear all the activity directed toward it. — س قده

[^V07-201]: That is, a bodily balance and similar qualities. Love is like a natural inclination in a body whose nature matches the lover’s own. The soul loves a bodily nature that resembles its own, such as a matching temperament. — س قده

[^V07-202]: “Kind” here means a linguistic kind, not a logical class. — س قده

[^V07-203]: This condition is added because when physical connection is removed but the spiritual bond remains, the true purpose becomes clear: which one did the lover seek? — س قده

[^V07-204]: Even one meeting can create union, but it takes repeated images of the beloved to make that union a settled ability. — س قده

[^V07-205]: Someone might think the verse should say, “We are one spirit living in two bodies.” The answer is that once we are united, wherever one of us is, the other is there too. So do not think that only my spirit is in my body: the beloved’s spirit is there too, because my spirit has become theirs. Nor think that only the beloved’s spirit is in their body: my spirit is there too, because theirs has become mine. Also, we are “two spirits” in the sense that two spirits have entered a body, not that our spiritual natures have become one. — س قده

[^V07-206]: The reason is that what the lover wants is union with the spiritual image in the lover’s own soul, settled there as a lasting state. The goal is the lover’s own transformed soul, not the beloved’s body. Physical closeness cannot achieve it. — س قده

[^V07-207]: The author uses “is” here for what a human being is at the beginning: a being that has the potential and readiness to become complete. In full reality, the perfected human being is the final kind and, in a broad sense, includes all kinds. In the same way, the simple, undivided reality includes all existence. — س قده

[^V07-208]: The passage may be read as if someone asked, “Name another beloved, one closer to loving God.” The answer would be the perfected human being. But the author says that, in truth, the beloved is the greater world. — س قده

[^V07-209]: Ibn al-ʿArabī may be saying explicitly elsewhere, or implying here, what al-Ghazālī said: that no world more beautiful than this one could be made. Since the world is in the image of the Real, it is the most beautiful and splendid thing one could imagine. This second reading is clearer. The human being called Adam, whom God created in His image, can mean both the greater human being—the whole world—and the smaller human being, the perfected person. This is the path of oneness. — س قده

[^V07-210]: The image of God here means the image in His knowledge. God created the greater human being, the world, in the pattern of that image in His knowledge. — س قده

[^V07-211]: The word can be read with either vowel. The point is that we can picture the Real only as He appears to us, according to what our own fixed nature can receive. Every being sees God through the veil of its own limits. — س قده

[^V07-212]: This point was discussed earlier in the treatment of speech and the Book. — س قده

[^V07-213]: We have explained that every being in the world displays one of God’s names, and that name guides and sustains it. The perfected human being is the final kind and contains all kinds. Each part therefore displays a name: sight is linked to the All-Seeing, hearing to the All-Hearing, the other senses to the All-Perceiving, movement and action to the All-Powerful, longing to the Willing, anger to the Overpowering, nourishment to the Provider, growth to the One who determines measure, and the forming power to the Formgiver. The power to reproduce is linked to the One who made humanity a steward on earth. The theoretical intellect is linked to the All-Knowing and All-Wise; the practical intellect to the Just and Powerful. The same applies to all the other connections. — س قده

[^V07-214]: Just as the name “God” includes all the beautiful names, its mirror—the human being—includes all kinds and individuals. This is why the names in the verse “He taught Adam all the names” can mean either God’s names or the names of created things. In either case, the realities of those things are meant. — س قده

[^V07-215]: The passage may instead refer to making the human body extremely small, just as the mountain crumbled when Moses saw God. People ask whether God could put the whole world inside an egg. The answer is that He could place it within the egg of the holy dove. — س قده

[^V07-216]: The common matter of the elements shows what their shared nature can become when it appears in the human being. Its inner capacity to change into four kinds leads to an outward ability: a human being can imitate the actions of other kinds. A horse cannot act or make the sound of a donkey, and a donkey cannot act or make the sound of a horse. The same applies to other kinds. — س قده

[^V07-217]: No kind is ever fully satisfied by its beloved because every kind is naturally made to love lasting life and a settled state, free from change. Lasting life and stability belong to God. — س قده

[^V07-218]: Their Garden is not limited by quantity or quality. It is the Garden of God’s being and qualities, as in the words, “Enter My Garden.” At the least, it is the Garden of God’s creative acts. A garden of outward forms would hide them from the world of meaning. They do not become hidden from God’s beauty by living in His house or garden. A Persian verse says: “Do not think the King’s door is closed to us; the generous make no task hard.” — س قده

[^V07-219]: This may mean the souls within the heavenly spheres, or the world of images, which is the universal imagination of the greater human being. It refers here to natural desires. Desires for things made by craft come from what is in human minds: people first picture them, then make them. This may even apply to some natural things. It is said that a person who imagines a beautiful image during sex may have a beautiful child. — س قده

[^V07-220]: “Being in any individual” means existing as an actual instance, not as a concept considered by itself. The philosopher studies things that exist. In general philosophy, we may say that every being necessary through itself has no definition or parts, and that everything beginningless through itself has no beginning. In theology, we mean the unique Necessary Being, exalted in glory, when we say that He has no definition or parts and is beginningless. The same applies to similar statements. — س قده

[^V07-221]: Here the author follows later logicians, who focus on the surface form of words and set competing expressions against one another. This turns the opposition between affirmation and denial—the most basic and obvious principle—into a difficult theory that a student can understand only with great effort. The author’s heading for the subject, “the opposition between affirmation and denial,” is much clearer than the explanation he adds. The earlier logicians were right: the most basic principle is that affirmation and denial cannot both be true and cannot both be false of the same claim. Put another way, a claim and its denial cannot both be true or both false. In truth, this opposition is between a positive and negative statement. Single terms contradict only because their meanings lead back to a positive or negative statement, such as “Zayd exists” and “Zayd does not exist.” — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-222]: The author’s account implies that denial is the direct opposite of affirmation, but affirmation is not the direct opposite of denial, since affirmation does not itself mean “removing” or “denying.” It is the result of denying a denial. So the opposition would be between a direct opposite and something that follows from the opposite. But it is obvious that affirmation itself directly excludes denial; it does not need to do so by following from a second denial. This is enough to show the weakness of that explanation. Affirmation and denial are directly opposed, so there can be no middle ground between them. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-223]: The author’s account can be challenged. The word does not require that the action be repeated on both sides. It may only mean that each side acts against the other. That reading, however, does not fit the author’s explanation that follows. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-224]: If the opposition had to go both ways, and each term itself were a direct opposite, the word “human” would also have to mean the denial of “not human.” But “not human” is the denial of “human”; “human” is not a denial. They therefore made the broader claim that the denial of anything is its opposite. Then it did not matter that the positive term was not itself a denial, since the opposite term is broader than the denial. — س قده

[^V07-225]: They mean the shared sense of the active and passive forms: denial in the broad sense, including both denying and being denied. — س قده

[^V07-226]: One side is a particular thing: in every case, it is the denial or removal of the other side. The other side is not one particular thing. In different cases it may be a human, a horse, a stone, whiteness, and so on. Its particular identity is neither part of its meaning nor a condition for it. Nor must it itself be a denial. It only has to be something to which the denial applies. — س قده

[^V07-227]: In the first type, the “subject” is the individual to which the predicate applies. It is not itself a member of the kind named by the predicate. In statements using “has,” the subject is something that can remain when the quality is absent. — س قده

[^V07-228]: There is another flaw in the previous account. A negative statement makes no claim; it only removes or cancels a claim. So it cannot be denied in the same way that a positive claim can, unless we treat “not making a claim” as a kind of positive claim. In that case, the denial of a denial is an affirmative statement that follows from the denial. This means that “the denial of a denial contradicts a denial” really means that it contradicts the positive statement that follows from the denial. Every positive statement has a direct opposite, its denial; a denial has no direct opposite of its own, only a positive statement that follows from it. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-229]: When the subject is a broad class, both universal statements can be false. For example, “Every animal is human” and “No animal is human” are both false. Both particular statements can be true: “Some animals are human” and “Some animals are not human.” — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-230]: It may seem that the wording should be “has blackness” and “has non-blackness.” The author has explicitly ruled this out, though it would fit the idea that something can both be black and have another quality. His point is that “has non-blackness” has two possible meanings. It can mean “has some quality other than blackness,” such as movement; this does not contradict blackness, since one thing can be both black and moving. Or it can mean “has no blackness at all.” That does contradict blackness. — س قده

[^V07-231]: He says “like a part” because rationality, for example, is a real part of a human being. A writer’s quality, though, is only part of a class or description, since the person is a substance and writing is a quality. — س قده

[^V07-232]: He says “like something outside” because the reason for the claim is outside the thing itself, even though the relation of defining it as a certain kind is included in the claim. — س قده

[^V07-233]: By “a thing” he means a respect or feature. For example, if we say that the First Intellect is the source of a particular sphere because of its relation to the Necessary Being, that relation is an explanatory respect. If we say that it is the source because it is possible in itself, that is a defining respect. The point is not that the thing itself is a cause or effect; that would make the respect part of the thing. A cause is a cause through what makes it able to cause. In the case of God, who is cause through Himself, causality is His very being. In other causes, their ability to cause comes from a feature that makes them actual causes. That feature, in truth, is the cause. The same applies to an effect: being caused is the very being of what is made. — س قده

[^V07-234]: The author’s account is unusual. In “humanity without condition,” the aspect is not truly a defining feature; it is only a way of speaking. It would be clearer to distinguish three cases. First, “without condition” can mean that we are not considering any condition as part of the subject, as in “humanity in itself is neither existent nor non-existent.” Second, a feature can be part of the subject, as in “a body, insofar as it is flat, is white” or “a body, insofar as it is white, can be seen.” Third, a feature can explain why a claim is true, as in “a human being laughs because they can understand unusual things” and “a person who is surprised laughs.” In the third case, the reason is outside the subject as such, so it does not make the subject more complex. A critic might say that surprise and understanding are themselves qualities that add to a person. They do, if they are treated as defining features. But when they are treated as reasons for a claim about the person, they are explanatory. Another might say that a cause explains the predicate, not the subject. But because the predicate and subject are joined in existence, the cause explains the subject too: being surprised explains why a human being laughs, and understanding unusual things explains why they are surprised. — س قده

[^V07-235]: The truth is that a difference in meaning alone is not a real defining feature, since it does not truly restrict the thing. So it does not challenge the common view that real defining features make a thing more complex. — س قده

[^V07-236]: Defining features are different and can add to the thing. The reasons that support them are also different, whether or not the features themselves add separate parts to the thing. This is what the author means when he says that a feature cannot become real without earlier, different explanatory grounds. For example, a person’s ability to hear has the hearing power as its basis, and their ability to see has the sight power as its basis. The same applies to understanding and intellect, movement and the power to move, and all other qualities and powers. — س قده

[^V07-237]: As for cause and effect, a part of a thing is sometimes said to cause the whole thing to be the kind it is. For example, a defining feature can make the general kind into a particular kind. The parts may have one existence in a compound, but they are still considered separately as its matter and form. The same applies to simple beings in this respect. — س قده

[^V07-238]: This is like the familiar claim that a general kind exists if at least one of its individuals exists, and disappears only when all its individuals disappear. People may have based this on the opposition between “some exist” and “none exist,” but did not address the reverse case, because there can be endlessly many individuals. The truth is that existence and non-existence follow the same rule. Partial non-existence occurs when something is absent in one way; complete non-existence occurs only when it is absent in every way. Likewise, partial existence occurs when it exists in one way; complete and unrestricted existence, such as God’s existence, is present in every way and at every level. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-239]: This means a particular respect and a particular basis for a quality. As explained earlier, having a quality in one respect does not mean having it in every respect. A thing moves because of the power that moves it, and is black because of a particular color and mixture. — س قده

[^V07-240]: The point is that even when we carefully divide a complex thing into parts or powers, one power produces only one kind of effect. In a human being, understanding comes from the power of understanding, each sensation from a sensing power, and each movement from a power that moves. Each quality has its own source. The rule is clear. — س قده

[^V07-241]: The One, God, is the clearest example. His respect, its basis, and the being described by it are one. What makes Him a cause is what makes Him what He is. Nothing in Him exists apart from His own being. The author moves between three ways of describing this, as he has explained. — س قده

[^V07-242]: The rule applies to every thing considered as one in a particular respect, not only to the One who is necessary through Himself. Elsewhere, in the discussion of the soul, the author says it applies only to the latter. — س قده

[^V07-243]: Some careful theologians saw the strength of this principle and did not reject it. They said it was a true conditional claim, but its starting point never occurs because there is no truly simple one. They even said that God has both an essence and existence, or at least additional qualities. — س قده

[^V07-244]: The first term is used by the Illuminationist philosophers, the second by the philosophers in Aristotle’s school. A form is what makes a thing actual. “Dimensions” includes both material and imagined dimensions. The third term refers to separate intellects that rise to the universal intellectual world. They are the final links in the upward chain, corresponding to the universal intellects that begin the downward chain of creation. Opponents reject this insight because they think it requires the existence of something eternal and separate from God. They do not see that the real problem would be an existence separate from every essence and quality, or an independent being other than God. This light, however, belongs to God’s own realm. It is separate because God makes it so, and eternal because it depends on God’s eternity. Recognizing it does not limit God’s power: it is itself God’s power and hand. Its act, and the act of everything in God’s realm, is God’s act. Its coming from God is like the whole world’s coming from God. After the Real, all existences are its shadows, just as it is God’s shadow. “Have you not seen how your Lord extends the shadow?” — س قده

[^V07-245]: This refers to the respect from which “not B” comes. In the earlier example, blackness is absent from Zayd in the respect in which he has knowledge. Here, even if B comes from the simple being, it is absent from the respect in which something other than B comes from it. Since the completely simple being has only one respect—its own being—“B does not come from it at all” would be true, even though B was also said to come from it. That would join contradictions. — س قده

[^V07-246]: Because there is only one respect from which things come, the two contradictory effects would have to come from the same level and in the same situation. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-247]: This follows from the earlier explanation that, in a simple cause, being the source of an effect is the cause’s very nature.

[^V07-248]: If B came from the cause through a different feature, the cause would not be simple. The two arguments are close but not identical: the earlier argument asks what makes an aspect a basis for an effect; the present one asks about the aspect itself, which is also its basis. The difference is clear. The author’s earlier method does not easily allow the idea that the effect comes only in a relative sense; the other method does. — س قده

[^V07-249]: This does not rule out drawing two different ideas from one instance considered as one. The point is that the particular feature that makes B come from the cause is not enough to give us the idea “source of something other than B.” We would also have to consider the production of that other thing. So the two ideas would have different actual grounds. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-250]: The author means that if one of the claims is permanent, the contradiction is clear, because a permanent claim is the opposite of a general, unrestricted claim. Here, Kātibī’s wording is false. Two claims contradict when, if one is true, the other must be false. But the author does not mean that no A comes from the simple being in any respect. He means that A comes from it in one respect, while A does not come from it in another respect—the respect from which C comes. Kātibī missed this and said that the simple being would then have two respects, which would make it composite. The criticism therefore falls back on the logician who raised it. — س قده

[^V07-251]: A universal nature exists when at least one of its individuals exists, while it ceases to exist only when all its individuals are gone. This raises another difficulty the Shaykh did not mention: the infinite would be confined between two limits. — س قده

[^V07-252]: If these two features were identical, the contradiction would not follow, since what makes a thing what it is is not itself an effect. But if they are different, the cause would be composite, with two respects. There would also be a further composition, since an internal defining feature would itself require another feature. Since the effect and what defines its cause are tied to these two respects, “of the respect” may mean that the respect explains them, or that it is the basis on which they depend. As established earlier, a defining respect depends on a further basis, and one or many bases require the same number of dependent features. — س قده

[^V07-253]: This would place two distinct respects within the very essence itself. — س قده

[^V07-254]: The reasoning is this: the respect that makes the cause produce an effect cannot be identical to the feature that defines the cause. If they differ, the effect is simply that feature, so the one cause has produced only one effect—contrary to the claim. If they are identical, the effect is identical with the cause, another contradiction: two respects, and thus two effects, would have to be one. One respect can give rise to only one effect. — س قده

[^V07-255]: Causality and causing are not additional positive qualities. The thing that exists is the cause itself, not a further quality called “being a cause” or “causing”; otherwise those qualities would themselves need causes, leading to an infinite regress. The same applies to being affected and to an effect’s being affected. Those who accept “states” count these among them, since states are neither existent nor nonexistent in their view. — س قده

[^V07-256]: Acceptance is a relation, and so is conceptual rather than real. The author says “for him” because al-Muḥaqqiq held acceptance to be real. This also points to the Imam’s own admission, in his commentary on *The Guide*, that receptivity is a relation. When defining body as a substance receptive to dimensions, receptivity itself is not the definition’s essence; it is a relation. — س قده

[^V07-257]: There is no necessary consequence here because alignments and negations are not real effects. They are relations, and relations are conceptual; negations are even less substantial. An effect, by contrast, truly comes from its cause through a specific real feature, not merely through a general relation. — س قده

[^V07-258]: That is, there are not many distinct negations in it. There is one negation: the absence of deficiency, or the negation of negation. Its basis is its pure existence and necessary being. — س قده

[^V07-259]: The real act of bringing something into being must precede the effect, so how could it be later than the effect, as negations and relations are, since these depend on both terms of the relation? The merely relational sense of “bringing forth” does come after both source and effect. But real bringing forth—the specific feature that is existence itself, also called the Holy Effusion—comes before the effect. As established earlier, bringing something into being is its real existence. — س قده

[^V07-260]: The wording is loose. “Valid” here means that no objection applies. Once the objection from negations is not answered, the more detailed objection described above still applies. The author answers it later, beginning, “The proper answer to this objection…” — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-261]: From eternity, every possible thing is absent from Him, and things that will be absent are also absent from Him. So there are many negations in eternity, all grounded in the One. The proper answer is that He has no many negations. — س قده

[^V07-262]: The plurality arises in two ways: from the many negations, and from negation alongside affirmation. The author aims to answer both forms, though the Imam’s original objection concerns the many negations. — س قده

[^V07-263]: This is really al-Muḥaqqiq Tusi’s answer to a detailed objection raised against his reply. Negations attributed to God are not genuine negations in the full sense: they are negations of absences, and negating an absence affirms existence. They only look like negations. This does not answer the Imam’s original challenge that one thing bears many negations; Tusi answers that by saying a negation depends on something beyond the thing denied, namely what is denied, and therefore is not an effect of one cause. The author objects that this dependence does not always hold, as with deficiencies denied of the Necessary Being: the deficiencies need not exist before they are denied. He replies that these are not true negations but negations of negation, which amount to affirmation. The discussion concerns genuine negations. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-264]: Negation is considered in three ways: as something present in thought, as the absence of a capacity, and as a relation to a subject. In those senses, negations differ from one another. Considered simply as absences, they have no distinctions. — س قده

[^V07-265]: That is, a loss of existence. Negation means absence of existence, unlike the author’s account, where it means negation of negation. — س قده

[^V07-266]: For example, affirming humanity of a human being and denying horsehood of them. As discussed in the claim that “the simple reality is all things,” both concern one subject: existence affirms humanity and denies horsehood. Existence itself is one reality. — س قده

[^V07-267]: That is, an affirmation whose predicate is phrased negatively, such as “the human being is non-horse.” — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-268]: If we consider only the feature of being human, only “human” is true. If we consider the restriction or the absence of horsehood, we can say “human without horsehood,” or “not a horse.” — س قده

[^V07-269]: As noted in the discussion of the three modes of judgment, possibility as a property of an essence is one of those modes and is an affirmative-negative quality: the essence is equally related to existence and nonexistence. Possibility itself, however, consists of two straightforward negations—neither necessary existence nor necessary nonexistence. Those two negations cannot become one negation that serves as the source of effects. So reducing all negations to the single negation of possibility involves a serious looseness. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-270]: The Imam’s objection is not limited to God’s attributes. It applies to anything one that has many attributes; he gives the example of “this man is standing and sitting.” It is better, then, to treat the author’s move from the attributes to their underlying principles as a detailed objection to their reply. The answer that God’s attributes reduce to one quality responds to that detailed objection, not to the Imam’s original challenge, which is not confined to God. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-271]: As with the eternal negations, these claims are true of God in eternity. They are not like relations, whose plurality can be explained by their terms, since nothing else was present when the principles of these attributes are considered. One might say that, properly understood, these principles are one. But the respondent has not taken that route. Why go so far around, and permit plurality among the relations, instead of saying plainly that the relation is one and its source is the one divine essence? — س قده

[^V07-272]: These three relations are called knowing, power, and will. They are consequences of real attributes that have relations to other things: existence in an immaterial being, the agent’s capacity to act if it wills and not to act if it does not, and will. In us, will is a firm desire: an intention following resolve, resolve following decision, decision following inclination, inclination following our judgment about the goal of an action, and that judgment following our conception of it. — س قده

[^V07-273]: The author’s point becomes clear from the earlier discussion and from the view common among the great mystics: existence has levels—God’s own existence, unrestricted existence, and limited existence. God is the first; unrestricted existence is His act, the Holy Effusion; limited existence is its effect. The Shaykh’s true causal activity is God’s own essence as the source of unrestricted existence. This is different from the shadow-like causality by which the first effect comes from God, which is the Holy Effusion itself and the manifestation of the divine essence. The Effusion can be considered in two ways: as the cause of the first effect, in which case its unity is numerical and it is necessary; and as God’s face, manifestation, and active will, in which case its unity is a shadow of God’s own unity and the necessity of all things. — س قده

[^V07-274]: The same difficulty applies to the claim that producing the absolute good necessarily follows from God’s essence. That consequence comes after the essence of the whole, so how can it be God’s own causality? If “the whole” means a collection of many essences, there is no single essence of the whole; then there is no single necessary act of producing it, but many necessary acts producing many effects. These cannot be identical with God. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-275]: God is all existence, and existence is all His; He is all perfection, and all perfection is His. As the author says in his *Sanctifications*, His exaltedness and glory do not depend on His being creator or knower at the level where creatures exist. He is Creator without a created thing and Knower without an object of knowledge, because all existence flows from Him and each being is contained in Him in its highest mode. — س قده

[^V07-276]: As explained earlier, the added feature is not itself an effect. It is the act of producing, like a grammatical relation that is neither subject nor object. In the discussion of cause and effect, the author explicitly says that unrestricted existence is not an effect but God’s true causality. See that discussion. — س قده

[^V07-277]: One might say this must be the right account, since the two things cannot be at the same level: the feature causes the First Intellect, and cause and effect are not at the same level. The author’s point to the master is that he must either accept a contradiction or accept that many things come from the One. Since the First Intellect is assumed to be the first effect, the causal feature must be at its level, or else there is a contradiction. But if one accepts the contradiction, it does not follow that many effects come from the One; instead, there is an infinite regress, because the later necessary consequence is the effect. Or the regress ends in a feature identical with the essence. — س قده

[^V07-278]: It would be better to say that this is merely relational causality, which is not at issue. But the author’s claim is also challenged by God’s additional relational attributes, which everyone accepts are pure relations, and even by the concepts of real attributes: as concepts, they are distinct from the essence in thought, though identical with it in reality. — س قده

[^V07-279]: This is a strange claim. The two respects become one, even though they give rise to one effect. But that is not so: unrestricted, all-embracing existence differs from limited existence; the greatest manifestation differs from what it manifests; the Great Human differs from the spirit of that human—the purely intelligible realm—from the whole of the bodily, angelic, and intelligible realms. Their relation is like that between the simple intellect described in the book on the soul and the Perfect Human. If existence has levels, the First Intellect is one level, while the Great Human is the whole range of created levels. The all-embracing existence is their preserved source, because what distinguishes existences is itself what they share. God’s being the source of unrestricted existence is not the same as His being the source of only the separate intellectual existence; the former is identical with His essence, according to the master, while the latter is not. — س قده

[^V07-280]: The claim is not that all things are the intellect. As explained earlier, the intellect is all things: a separate, simple existence whose unity encompasses everything below it and gathers their actualities and perfections, but not their limits and deficiencies. This is “many within one,” not “one within many.” The higher levels contain all that is in the lower, and the lower is contained in the higher, in an ascending order. In this upward development, the soul does not cast off one form and put on another; it takes on one after another. Since a cause must be akin to its effect, and essences remain through the levels while existence is one reality whose distinguishing and shared features are the same, the higher level can contain the actualities of all lower beings in one simple way. This is possible only if the levels remain distinct; otherwise there would be no first effect followed by later effects, and many principles of philosophy would collapse. — س قده

[^V07-281]: That is, what comes later in the concept, not in reality; and its existence is dependent, not independent. — س قده

[^V07-282]: First, we do not accept that the First Effect’s unity is merely numerical. As noted earlier, it contains everything below it. — س قده

[^V07-283]: This objection applies to the master’s explicit wording, where the causal feature is taken in relation to the whole world, the Great Human, all existence, all good, and similar expressions. These suggest a unity made up of many things, so the author’s objection applies: a composite is more deserving of an essence than a simple thing. But it does not apply if the feature is understood as unrestricted existence, the all-embracing effusion, or another name for the sacred light. Unrestricted existence has no essence of its own; in one respect it is the existence of essences, and in another it is their real bringing into being. It is the manifestation of necessary existence, and there is no second manifestation beside it. — س قده

[^V07-284]: This claim has already been questioned. A caused existence is necessarily limited: something is affirmed of it and something denied. That limit is all we mean by an essence. Causation also always involves possibility, which is said to belong to an essence; every possible thing has a composite aspect of essence and existence. Intellects are simple identities only in relation to what is below them, not in relation to what is above. Absolute simplicity and pure being have no instance but God. So if an essence is denied of an intellect, it is denied only in relation to what is below it, since it is not limited relative to what is below it. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-285]: “As existent” means without requiring bodily extension, dimensions, or multiplicity. In the opening notes on general principles, I explained that the unrestricted subject of metaphysics is unrestricted in just this sense. The reason is that natural and mathematical things depend on extension through space and time and on matter, while being as such does not. — س قده

[^V07-286]: At least two things would follow: existence, along with unity, individuation, and the like. Existence would come from one respect, while something other than existence would come from another. Thus existence would both come and not come from the respect that produces the other thing—a contradiction. — س قده

[^V07-287]: Their principle may be stated as: whenever a truly one being exists, two effects do not come from it. The author’s argument treats unity, thinghood, and similar features as one respect, not as several. It seems the word “nonexistence” may have been added by a copyist. The author’s point is that the rule depends on the possibility of a truly one being, which he thinks impossible. For him it is a true conditional with no instance. — س قده

[^V07-288]: The argument can be put this way: whenever two things come from a truly one being, it has contradictory features; and whenever it has contradictory features, it is composite. — س قده

[^V07-289]: People abandon general principles when a particular case seems to challenge them. Seeing no purpose in a thirsty person choosing one of two cups, they drop the principle that every voluntary act has a determining reason. Seeing that killing seems evil, they abandon the general truth that all existence is good.
