They should examine these cases instead of rejecting what is clear: evil is the absence of life, while all the steps leading to it are actual, good existences. The same approach applies to the first principle and to the rest. — س قده

[^V07-290]: That is, in the example of a human being and the structure of the mind: one might say this is also an external composition, since the general kind and defining feature in external compounds are treated as matter and form. — س قده

[^V07-291]: The phrase “and its being described as having parts that share common limits” should perhaps come before this. It may have been omitted by a copyist. — س قده

[^V07-292]: More consistently with the earlier wording, one should say: “its parts are aligned,” or “it is the shortest line joining two points,” and so on, as defining features of straightness. — س قده

[^V07-293]: The author generalizes “within” to include many defining parts and many attributes, as in a human being with many constituents and qualities such as knowledge, power, and will. “Not in existence or essence” means there is no multiplication in the whole, whether in existence or essence. “Not in the concrete” denies a plurality of matter and form; “not in the mind” denies a plurality of general kind and defining feature in external compounds; “not in thought” denies that same plurality in mental compounds such as accidents. None of these kinds of multiplicity applies to God, who has no essence, matter, form, broader kind, species, or added quality. — س قده

[^V07-294]: One might object that there is no problem: surely everyone who knows is also able, since nature does not give perceptive powers without also giving the motive powers on which ability depends. And how could someone able to act not also will, since an agent acts with awareness and will? The reply is that the claim concerns, for example, every knower through acquired knowledge being able to perform acquired crafts, or every knowing feature being the same as an ability, or every perceptive power being able to act. — س قده

[^V07-295]: This is a reply to the objection, and it contains two answers: the first is the discussion of bodily matter and its mode of existence; the second follows, beginning, “Or because each natural kind has another separate form…” — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-296]: The phrase “except that it knows it through its form” is a side note: the answer that follows is that it knows the matter through its ideal or intellectual form. Calling these forms “taken from” matter is loose. If the forms had the same presence and unity as their source, matter itself would possess that presence and unity. Rather, the forms descend from those higher forms, as the lower descends from the higher. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-297]: Completing the argument by analogy shows that it is not a demonstration. In truth, this argument does not help. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-298]: These are the qualities the author calls “knowledge that is not knowledge,” and so on. The related names are the well-known divine names that theology discusses: Living, Knowing, Willing, Powerful, Hearing, Seeing, and Speaking. — س قده

[^V07-299]: These are the attributes mentioned as “knowledge that is not knowledge,” “life like death,” and so forth: knowledge, life, will, power, hearing, sight, and speech. The Ashʿarites counted them as positive attributes, and many followed them. In truth, the basic attributes are three: knowledge, power, and life; hearing and sight follow from them. Will and speech, like other active attributes, reduce on analysis to real attributes and ultimately to the essential feature, as noted earlier. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-300]: The earlier wording better suits the language of declaring God beyond all likeness. In this usage, however, “likeness” and “the source of sources” are invoked, though nothing resembles Him. — س قده

[^V07-301]: The argument depends on a special feature linking the effect to its cause. A careful commentator says that the earlier formulation better suits the language of declaring God beyond all likeness, while this one uses the words “likeness” and “similarity,” even though nothing resembles the first source. That criticism is right. The wording is awkward: it says that fit is likeness, and likeness is a kind of sameness in quality, ultimately a sameness in reality. If “likeness” and “sameness” are used in their technical senses, the argument would not apply to God or to any simple substantial cause, and the premise is itself disputed. If the words are used loosely, the added statements do no work; it would be enough to say that fit means sameness in reality. The further distinction—that sameness between two qualities is called likeness, while sameness between two subjects is called similarity—only makes the argument less clear. A better proof would say: a true cause must have an essential relation and a special bond to its effect, so that the effect’s existence depends on the cause’s existence. This bond requires a kind of shared nature between them, differing only in strength and weakness. If two things came from a truly simple One, either they would come from one aspect, making the One two, or from two aspects, making it composite. Both are impossible. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-302]: The things that come together here are the knower and the known as descriptions. There is no relation between them, only a simple relation that does not repeat. The two sides of the relation that can be repeated by considering them separately are knowing and being known; these are mental, not external, distinctions. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-303]: This is shown by the fact that reciprocal relations, without further qualification, form a separate class among the four kinds of opposition. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-304]: A reciprocal relation means that each of two essences can be understood only by understanding the other, with the relation running both ways. This requires two distinct things in opposition: one and the same existence cannot belong to two distinct essences. The distinction can be drawn when the relation is between two real essences or between two conceptual ideas. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-305]: That is, the thing itself, considered apart from incidental features surrounding it. — س قده

[^V07-306]: The point is that the very universal form that is part of the cause must, in a non-circular way, depend on matter, so that the two are necessarily linked and their combination gives rise to unity. “Form” here does not mean the universal concept or one of its scattered individuals. It means the natural universal—such as corporeality or a natural kind—which exists through its individuals, not separately. Yet the universal and its individual are considered in different ways, somewhat like a moving present and time. The form as unrestricted nature depends on matter in a general way; as a particular, bounded, shaped form, marked by division, connection, and other material features, it depends on matter specifically, since receptivity belongs to matter and particularization requires a basis. If this form were the first effect, it would itself need existence to cause anything below it, and its existence is that of its material individual, whose particularity depends on matter and its features. Then many things would come from the One. The author points to the need for motion and time because the form also depends on matter to persist: its individuals are maintained through succession, division, connection, and change, all of which belong to matter. — س قده

[^V07-307]: Some copies read “even if its existence in the creative realm comes before material things.” On that reading, its intellectual existence does not depend on matter, but the two are not necessarily linked, so it may not depend on matter at all. — س قده

[^V07-308]: The earlier point established the necessary link between form and matter. Now the author answers the opponent’s appeal to the claim that form is only a part of the cause: if form is in matter, many things would come from the One, as just shown. Its existence is also renewed in itself, so how could it be linked to the unchanging eternal source? And if matter is a partner in the cause, this can only mean that the true intellectual cause’s flow reaches form first and matter second. How, then, could this form be God’s first effect and also a source of what comes below it? — س قده

[^V07-309]: The opponent may be comparing the production of form from the source, taking “as they hold” with “so let it be possible for it to come forth.” Or the comparison may concern matter’s coming through form, taking “as they hold” with “through it.” On the latter reading, none of the author’s objections applies. — س قده

[^V07-310]: That is, the unrestricted celestial motion. By combining the particular motions of the spheres, the source produces particular matter and the distinct elemental forms. — س قده

[^V07-311]: This follows his view that the soul begins as bodily and survives as spiritual: in its first stage it is a natural principle, then moves through substantial change until it reaches an immaterial state with no quantity or quality. On that account, it is not prior in itself, much less in time. — س قده

[^V07-312]: The point is that every material power has a positional existence. The kind of production described must therefore be positional, since production depends on existence and position. But position cannot apply to what does not exist, or to matter itself, which has no position in its own right. — س قده

[^V07-313]: The outermost sphere, which determines directions, did not yet exist. This imagined sphere is contained within another; suppose it is in a high place, so change occurs in the containing sphere without a change in anything that contains it. — س قده

[^V07-314]: If a void were possible, there would be empty space around the contained sphere, to serve as the place of the containing sphere before it existed. That would require a void and would explain the greater by the lesser. — س قده

[^V07-315]: One could argue that the purely immaterial spirit is nobler than the spirit attached to a body; if the latter exists, the former should exist before it. Or one could say that natural kinds in our world exist, and are lower and less noble than their counterparts in the world of intellect, so they too should exist there first. — س قده

[^V07-316]: This issue was discussed earlier in the section on Platonic Forms in the discussion of essences. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-317]: A firmer and shorter proof can be built on this premise: existence is fundamental and graded, differing in strength and weakness, actuality and potentiality, nobility and lowliness. Causation among possible beings, together with the claim that creation concerns existence itself, requires more than two levels of existence. One is necessarily God; the others are possible beings, some nobler than others. A caused existence is dependent and relational: it cannot stand by itself, but is grounded in an independent existence. Thus each essential level is dependent on the level above and supports the one below. In a three-level chain, the lowest is dependent on the middle, the middle independently supports the lowest but depends on the highest, and the highest independently supports all below it. If a lower, less noble level existed without a nobler level before it—whether the nobler level came at the same time, later, or never—then something that depends on a cause would exist without its cause, which is impossible. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-318]: This applies to the First Effect. It does not rule out several effects from later intermediate causes, since they contain many aspects. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-319]: Two further possibilities are that a simultaneous obstacle blocks the nobler being, or that a being of equal rank is an obstacle, even though God is admitted to be its cause. But both are ruled out because the principle is being applied only above the material world, where there is no opposition or obstruction. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-320]: There are two reasons for this condition. First, the lower being must be of the same kind as the higher, as dim light is an imperfect form of strong light. White is not an imperfect form of light, nor is strong white the perfection of weak light; the same goes for intense and weak heat. Second, this makes it easier to establish that the nobler being is possible: if its essence is the same as the lower being’s, and the lower exists without contradiction, then the nobler one is plainly possible. That is less clear if their essences differ. — س قده

[^V07-321]: Existence is graded. In addition to the particular range belonging to each level, it has a range across the different levels. Essences drawn from these levels therefore differ in kind, while essences drawn from individual instances differ as individuals. One might object that many essences follow from many existences, while philosophers say many existences follow from many essences. The answer is that existence is the means by which essences multiply; the reverse claim is a way of proving the point. — س قده

[^V07-322]: The earlier proof applies only where the nobler being causes the lower one and no obstacle intervenes. This holds beyond the world of generation, but in that world nobler beings do not cause the lower ones, and events can coincide with obstacles. So the principle does not apply there. The wording is somewhat loose: the objection applies to perishable material things, but not to the heavenly bodies, which this tradition considers everlasting and beyond generation and decay. Yet the claim that the rule applies only to possible beings whose existence is stable excludes those bodies too, since their substance is in motion and their existence is not stable. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-323]: This points to the principle’s application to the noble, necessary ends of things. In reality, these ends precede the things directed toward them, though they appear later. The author’s account therefore applies it to the First Intellect, the levels of the higher intellects, the universal intellects that govern natural kinds, the imaginal forms, the celestial bodies, and the noble ends that are necessary to existence. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-324]: Dawānī calls the discussion difficult because of his own approach; on the author’s view, it is straightforward. — س قده

[^V07-325]: The two possibilities do not conflict, because a cause need only be possible in relation to its effect, not possible in itself. Nor do the two impossibilities conflict: a cause impossible in itself may still be possible in relation to its effect. The nobler level is impossible in itself, but it is possible in relation to the nobler possible being. It is impossible in itself, but not impossible in relation to that possible being. This is the distinction made by the inquirer and the skeptic. Since relational impossibility is broader than impossibility through another, it also covers their confusion between the two, as in Dawānī’s closing remark. — س قده

[^V07-326]: This is like two things impossible in themselves but with no relation between them: the impossibility of either does not rule out its possibility in relation to the other. — س قده

[^V07-327]: Up to “And we also say”: the point is not that the nobler possible has no existence for the objector, and so has no essence or possibility. That would invite two replies: unrealized possibilities such as the mythical ʿAnqāʾ still have possibility, at least in the mind; and it would create circularity, since recognizing the nobler possible’s essence and possibility would depend on knowing that it exists, while knowing it exists would depend on knowing its essence and possibility. Rather, possibilities depend on essences, essences depend on existences, and existences depend on necessary existence—or are its manifestation. Possibility means being able to receive existence or nonexistence from the Necessary Being, or from a possible being that depends on it. How, then, can the nobler being be possible if it would have to receive existence from a level that is impossible in itself? That is only ordinary imaginative speculation. It is like saying Zayd is possible because he could receive existence from a partner of God. — س قده

[^V07-328]: The author is not denying that a perfection can be impossible for God. He is denying that God lacks a perfection required by a real, existing possible being, as the objector alleges. The objector invents a possible essence that supposedly calls for a nobler feature in God, which God does not have; that is an absurd claim, especially since God is self-sufficient and the source of all needs. Every perfection we can imagine is in Him through necessary existence. As the saying goes, pure existence, than which nothing is more complete, cannot be given a second instance: whatever is imagined as another is that very same existence. — س قده

[^V07-329]: This excludes negative features, limits, and deficiencies from the argument. These are impossible for God and amount to negating a negation, which is affirmation. — س قده

[^V07-330]: If possibility, which is a kind of absence, were in God, He would be equally related to existence and nonexistence, which is impossible. If necessary existence were in Him as a feature, necessity through oneself and impossibility through another would coincide, also impossible. If impossibility in itself were in Him, the two kinds of impossibility would coincide, which was shown earlier to be impossible. The same reasoning applies to existence necessary through another. So existence and nonexistence are each either necessary in themselves or necessary through another. Only an essence can bear both an intrinsic and an extrinsic status: intrinsic possibility alongside necessity or impossibility through another. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-331]: Especially since nobility and baseness are relative: each level is lower than what is above it and higher than what is below it. — س قده

[^V07-332]: There are no many separate individuals, finite or infinite, nor are they all gathered and ordered together. Rather, one immaterial being—the universal intellect—has, say, ten levels. It is also God’s knowledge, power, and greatest manifestation, indeed His fullest manifestation. A similar question and answer arise elsewhere in the author’s account of material development: he says there must be an intermediate stage between forms, such as vapor between water and air, algae between nonliving matter and plants, sponges between plants and animals, transformed creatures between animals and humans, an imaginal form between the command-spirit and the vaporous spirit, and balanced blood between the vaporous spirit and the natural body. One might ask whether this entails infinitely many intermediate stages, since each stage would require another between itself and either endpoint. The answer is that these levels form one continuous whole. The intermediate stages are not separated from the endpoints as the endpoints are separated from one another; all are one thing with levels. — س قده

[^V07-333]: In the downward order, the Principle of the Nobler Possible establishes it. The Principle of the Lower Possible then establishes that it has a soul, so its arrival at the rational destination—the governing intellect—does not involve a leap. Thus, on the upward path, a lower possible being, such as the imaginal form of a simple being, must precede the nobler possible being, the intellect. “Soul” here means the imaginal forms of simple beings; “plant power” means something like it, since plant powers properly belong to composites. “Animal life” and “imaginative power” refer to the next world, which is living and is itself knowledge and awareness. The First Teacher called these the plant word and animal word. The author aims to establish subtle truths within his own system, not familiar examples such as: if an animal has sight, it must also have the lower senses; if it has perception, it must also have the lower power of movement; if it has desire, it must also have the lower active power; and so on. — س قده

[^V07-334]: This is one of the familiar consequences of the principle. — س قده

[^V07-335]: Because its nature has no affinity with itself, being close to the world of divine command. — س قده

[^V07-336]: Matter individuates form, while form contributes as a partner in causing matter to exist. The two absences surrounding nature in motion are its prior potentiality and its later actuality. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-337]: This is priority in essence, though also priority by nature. Form precedes matter as an incomplete cause, not as a complete cause and therefore not by full causal priority. Both are forms of priority by nature, but differ: potentiality precedes what is prepared for it as preparation precedes what it prepares, much like temporal priority, so the potential and its fulfillment cannot coincide. Form’s priority over matter differs because form is a co-agent and an incomplete cause of matter; matter can coexist with it, being before, with, and after it. Matter is called nonbeing because it is a thing’s potentiality, and potentiality as such is not yet a thing. — س قده

[^V07-338]: The explanation might more directly say that nature itself is accompanied by both absences, which are potentiality and matter. The author phrases it this way because potentiality and matter are the sources of past and future absence. — س قده

[^V07-339]: This explains why past and future absence do not taint the soul, as they do things that exist together. In the soul, this admixture concerns its acts, not its essence; in things that coexist, it concerns their very existence. — س قده

[^V07-340]: The soul is stable when considered through its successive stages of motion, but it is also shared in by those stages. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-341]: This is different from the earlier analytic nonbeing, which meant that neither existence nor nonexistence is necessary to a thing’s essence. The author did not mention this type in that discussion, just as he did not mention the nonbeing tied to continuous extension, although nature is in motion; an attentive reader can infer the missing parts. The fourth kind—nonbeing required by spatial extension—applies specifically to quantitative form, which is receptive to dimensions in itself. — س قده

[^V07-342]: Aristotle called them the “three beginnings.” When some philosophers say nonbeing is a principle, they mean prior potentiality, without which a generated thing could not come to be as a generated thing. — س قده

[^V07-343]: That is, matter that exists before the form and bears its potentiality. — س قده

[^V07-344]: For example, the matter of a sperm: potentiality comes from it and is in it. A potentiality can belong to something, as the sperm’s body does; or it can be a potentiality for something, as the sperm’s specific form is. The thing being prepared, the capacity in it, and what gives it that capacity are distinct. The last determines what it is prepared to become; without it, the potentiality would not be directed toward a definite outcome. It varies by kind, and the potentiality varies accordingly: the human form prepares for humanity, the egg-form for a chick, and the seed-form for a plant. — س قده

[^V07-345]: That is, in actual existence. — س قده

[^V07-346]: Some of these arguments establish the First Intellect specifically, such as the third and fourth; the others establish an immaterial intellect in general. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-347]: In the two reports, “light” means true light: intellect. Its spiritual nature is through the universal intellect, not an individual one. In Illuminationist language, the universal intellect is the overpowering light. All knowledge is light, since it is manifest in itself and makes other things—objects of knowledge—manifest. Intellect is knowing, knower, and known. Many transmitted reports support this idea. One report describes the higher world as forms without matter or potentiality, shining when God reveals Himself to them and radiating when they contemplate Him. Another, from Kumayl and ʿAlī, places intellect at the center of the individual and universal divine souls; this must mean the universal intellect. A further report from an Arab describes the universal divine soul as a divine power, a simple substance, alive in itself, whose origin is intellect, and then calls intellect a knowing substance that encompasses things, knows them before they exist, and is the cause of existing things and the end of all aims. This plainly supports the point. — س قده

[^V07-348]: This restates the argument given from the beginning of the chapter through the explanatory note. The author means to enumerate and complete the proofs. — س قده

[^V07-349]: As the wording suggests, “intellect” here means intellect in general—a broad category containing different kinds of intellect, each represented by one instance. The proof needs another unstated premise: that there are many intellects arranged in degrees of nobility. The next argument needs the same premise. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-350]: “Kinds” refers to the earlier condition that the Principle of the Nobler Possible applies to higher and lower beings of the same specific kind. The author thinks it is enough that they share the same level or kind of existence. — س قده

[^V07-351]: The intellect “in potential” is a readiness, though it is already actual through its possession of first principles. It is called a capacity because it is what makes further readiness possible; as noted earlier, a readiness and what gives rise to it are inseparable. — س قده

[^V07-352]: Besides the difference in wording, one of these expressions suggests the light of the power itself, the other the light of the sun. In the comparison, the two lights stand for the intellect in act and the Active Intellect; the intellect’s own light is intuition and thought. — س قده

[^V07-353]: One might ask why forms alone are not enough, without the sacred substance serving as matter’s source. There are two reasons. First, the universal form is one in a general sense, while matter is numerically one; an efficient cause should be more unified than its effect, but here it would be less unified, unless form and matter were joint causes. Second, a body affects things through its position, but matter has no position by itself. Matter must therefore be caused by an intellect, which likewise has no position, rather than by an essence or form. This is not a claim that God could produce two changing things. It describes the actual order: essence is below both change and stability, and so this account reconciles the claims that natural kinds change and do not change. — س قده

[^V07-354]: One might object that an intellectual substance is stable, so a changing nature cannot come from it. The reply is that the intellect itself has an essence, and in that respect moves from nonexistence to existence, in a way like motion. It can therefore give rise to nature through its stable aspect, unlike God. — س قده

[^V07-355]: This is not circular. The philosophers’ division of possible beings into those that need only intrinsic possibility to receive existence and those that also need a preparatory possibility was made after they established the separate universal intellects. The argument here does not depend on those intellects being established first. “Completed kinds” means the natural kinds closest to us, considered as requiring an earlier, all-encompassing existence through the Giver’s generosity, not the essences of the intellects. This prior existence is different from the existence of natural kinds and universals themselves: though they too are created directly, they are at the lowest level of the eternal order, as the author says next. The proof can also be stated as follows: logical division requires two kinds of possible things—those whose intrinsic possibility is enough for existence and those that also need preparatory possibility. God is generous and gives without obstruction, granting each thing its due and bringing it to its proper level. This is how the author presents the twelfth proof later, when he divides things into four kinds. — س قده

[^V07-356]: One may object that there is no way to show this sufficiency before proving that separate beings exist. Merely imagining essences of this sort does not prove that such essences exist; until their existence is established, they are only concepts, and their essences are tied to those existences. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-357]: One may also ask why some kinds could not depend on beings that do not exist always or for the most part, as suggested by kinds that have died out. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-358]: This points to another meaning: the differences between natural kinds in this world depend on differences among the higher kinds of intellects. These are the active aspects and levels of God’s power and knowledge; the differences cannot come directly from the One because of His unity and simplicity. — س قده

[^V07-359]: Although the tenth proof resembles the fifth, they differ. The fifth concerns the rational soul and proceeds through motion, its gradual passage from potentiality to actuality; this one concerns the celestial sphere and proceeds through change in general. The second version of the tenth proof differs from the eighth: the latter concerns the ends of natural kinds, directed toward the universal intellects that govern them; this one concerns the ends of the spheres, directed toward the nine higher intellects. The eighth follows the Illuminationist path; this one follows the approach of Aristotle’s followers, or the approach shared by both. — س قده

[^V07-360]: Especially since, in itself, the higher realm is the world of divine command, set against the world of creation, as in the verse, “His are creation and command.” — س قده

[^V07-361]: These forms are immaterial, and philosophers infer from their immateriality that rational souls are immaterial. How much more, then, should their true counterparts in reality be immaterial? — س قده

[^V07-362]: These forms are universal intellectual forms, separate from matter and its features. If they existed only in rational souls or in the Active Intellect, they would depend on and inhere in something else, and would not be intellects themselves. If they exist in themselves, they are universal intellects and luminous forms, as Plato, Socrates, and their followers held: when the intellect grasps universals, it receives them from these forms. — س قده

[^V07-363]: There is a difficulty here. If the Active Intellect contains acquired knowledge, the truth of that knowledge depends on other things in the world that match it. Then those external instances, not the Active Intellect’s knowledge, are what our true ideas really match, and the claim that our ideas match the Active Intellect’s knowledge does no work. If its knowledge is direct or present knowledge, two problems arise: first, it would consist of actual existences that our ideas match whether or not an intellect knew them, so the intellect would not need to exist; second, some truths concern subjects that do not exist, as in “the absence of a cause causes the absence of its effect,” and such things cannot be known through direct presence. A possible reply, drawn from the earlier discussion of why a possible thing’s nonexistence needs a cause, is that the mind first finds existence predicated of itself, then necessarily extends it to the essence drawn from it, and finally to conceptual notions drawn from that essence. Judgments formed in this way can be true without having a match either in the mind or outside it; their match lies in the wider field of what is established, including existence, essence, and conceptual notions. Universal truths of this kind correspond to forms of direct knowledge in the intellectual world. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-364]: This is the basis for the author’s twelfth argument. — س قده

[^V07-365]: That is, in the phrase “the identity flowed from it,” to avoid implying that essence is created. “Identity” could mean essence in the phrase “because it had no identity,” but using it for existence in both places keeps the wording consistent. — س قده

[^V07-366]: This means the level where all things are absorbed into God, with no separate “you” or “I”; no one can grasp this state in ordinary language. At the level where individual things exist, each is truly present with God, since it cannot be separated from Him. — س قده

[^V07-367]: The comparison concerns the Active Intellect and the human intellect. On the illumination account, the Active Intellect’s light shines on the knowing soul as the sun shines on sight. When the two lights meet, the soul sees the universal forms in the Active Intellect themselves; it does not receive new imprints. This resembles the view that rays leave the eye and reflect from a mirror to the object itself. On the “overflow” account, the forms are imprinted in the soul as well. The author’s own view, as indicated earlier, is neither: the soul and the Active Intellect become one, as the soul is absorbed into it. — س قده

[^V07-368]: The difficulty concerns lapses of attention and forgetting. The wording hints at this distinction. — س قده

[^V07-369]: The proposed distinction works for sensory images, but not for intelligible ideas. It assumes that the Active Intellect is a storehouse for the knowing intellect; then false universal ideas must also be there, since we can both lose sight of and forget them. — س قده

[^V07-370]: Perhaps there was not enough time to give a full answer or reveal certain points, as the author will do in his own solution. The great al-Muḥaqqiq Tusi was not incapable of answering. — س قده

[^V07-371]: The reply seems to say that the parts of a false judgment—its subject, predicate, and relation—have counterparts in the Active Intellect, but not the judgment’s affirmation, which is an act of estimation. Yet this would mean that neither true nor false universal affirmations need an intellectual counterpart as affirmations. There is no reason to grant a match only to true affirmations. The argument would then rely only on the conceptual parts and their being remembered or forgotten, which raises two problems: the conclusion would be narrower than the claim it is meant to prove, while the proof itself would apply more broadly than that conclusion. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-372]: If the Active Intellect does not affirm the relation, then it does not judge it. If knowledge means affirming a relation, the Active Intellect would only conceive the terms, not know the proposition. But universal judgments, true or false, are themselves universal. Just as universal concepts are stored in the Active Intellect, so are universal affirmations, including false ones: we can lose sight of and forget false universal judgments. The distinction between momentary inattention and forgetting cannot be drawn without appealing again to the Active Intellect, so the problem remains. A false judgment represented there without assent is a judgment in the primary sense of the term, but not in the ordinary sense. — س قده

[^V07-373]: The wording suggests that Dawānī denied universal relations altogether. In fact, he denied only the special, necessary joining found in false judgments. He explicitly says that the Active Intellect preserves and affirms true judgments. The author’s point is that Dawānī must deny that special joining too: necessary assent to false judgments, and the certainty of someone who is confidently mistaken, also come from the Active Intellect, since it is a unified, universal, immaterial existence. This objection hints at the more precise account the author is about to give. — س قده

[^V07-374]: This is plainly disputable. Not every joining entails identity in existence: two independent existences can be joined, while identity occurs only when one existence depends on another. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-375]: This is true, and the author has explained it in his notes on the knower and the known. But it challenges the basic claim that true judgments match forms imprinted in the substance of the Active Intellect, not only Dawānī’s reply. The answer is that the conceptual knowledge contained in a proposition—both conception and judgment—can match the Active Intellect’s direct knowledge, just as it can match things in the world. The author may be objecting to the usual view that the intellect’s knowledge is conceptual, because its defenders reserve direct knowledge for knowing oneself. He is too insightful to miss that conceptual knowledge can match direct knowledge. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-376]: Another way to answer is that every proposition and every judgment in the Active Intellect is true. A false judgment or a state of compounded ignorance arises only through restriction and narrowing. For example, the claim that the soul is a subtle substance spread through the body like water in a rose or fire in coal contains a truth: it describes the vaporous spirit, which is not separate from the command-spirit. The claim that the soul is a natural principle also contains a truth: nature, formed to obey the command-spirit, is one of its levels; indeed, nature is one level of the soul, which begins bodily and survives as spirit. The same applies to other views. All the true aspects are in the Active Intellect. Perception, inattention, and forgetting come through connection with it or separation from it. A mistaken view is still possible because it restricts or confines the truth, as noted earlier. The author of the *Golden Chain* puts it poetically: “Become a seat for every belief, and see it in all its forms; be the matter of every conviction, and you will escape association and ignorance.” Earlier, the author explained that we perceive universals by contemplating their governing forms from a distance. Even those who are deeply mistaken perceive the universals in their mistaken minds by contemplating these forms. Perceiving universal nonexistence is a faint contemplation of true existence, just as perceiving general existence is contemplating it directly. — س قده

[^V07-377]: This answer resolves the problem of how false knowledge could descend from the Active Intellect, since falsehood is a kind of evil from which the higher intellectual world is free. It does not resolve the problem of there being no actual counterpart there for a false idea. A merely fitting feature is not enough: unless the correspondence is real in some respect, it is not conceptual knowledge. Conceptual knowledge represents something beyond itself; a representation without anything represented, or a path without a destination, makes no sense. How, then, can a false proposition count as knowledge? The answer is that knowledge as such cannot be false, because it inherently represents what lies beyond it. Error in universal knowledge comes from the play of imagination and the mischief of estimation: they put something that is not the subject in place of the subject, or something that is not the predicate in place of the predicate. The details belong elsewhere. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-378]: This question is as important as the question of the world’s coming into being. For religious thinkers, proving that God exists—the foundation of all other questions—depends on it, because they make a thing’s need for a cause depend on its having begun. People who rely on outward appearances are shown only arguments such as “the world is a building, so it must have an ancient builder.” The world’s end, its destruction, and the Last Judgment also depend on its having begun: what is eternal cannot cease to exist. That is why careful philosophers and religious scholars devote so much effort to proving that the world began. But the sound view must bring together religious teaching and proof, while affirming that God’s giving never stops. Among His beautiful names are “the One whose kindness is eternal” and “the One whose hands are open to give.” His words never run out, His light never sets. In short, God’s way of acting cannot be suspended. This can be understood through temporal coming-into-being, inner renewal, and substantial motion, as the author says; or through the “everlasting” coming-into-being taught by the learned Sayyid Dāmād, as we have explained elsewhere. It cannot be understood as the coming-into-being in imaginary time taught by al-Ashʿarī and others, because that would make an effect lag behind its complete cause and would imply that God waits for a certain state, time, or benefit, contrary to the unceasing giving just described. Another account of temporal coming-into-being that brings together religious and philosophical views occurred to me: the world reported by the one who knows it comes into being when that knower does, and passes away when he does. As the Persian verse says: “When a person lays his head down to sleep, / the ropes of his tent come loose.” It is established that matter is not directly perceived in itself. What is directly perceived is something whose existence is also its existence for the one perceiving it. The heavens, earth, and all that lies between them, as pointed to by sense, imagination, or limited intellect, are new. As worldly natural things, all will pass away: the sky will split, the stars scatter, and the sun darken, as happens at death. Take care: this is a subtle point. — س قده

[^V07-379]: If the philosophers had said that the world is eternal, they were looking at God and His qualities, and at existence itself: the light that drives nonexistence away and illuminates the natures of the heavens, earth, and all that lies between. The world, in this view, is only the “thingness” of natures, and these fade into the reality of existence as a shadow disappears in sunlight. Existence belongs first and truly to God, as ʿAlī says: “I saw nothing without seeing God before it.” A Persian verse says: “A heart lit by knowledge and purity / sees God first in everything it sees.” Even when the limited mind attributes existence to particular natures, it must set aside those relations. In the mystics’ vision, the world is filled with God’s splendor, or else has faded away into Him. They see only God, His qualities, and what belongs to His realm, which are eternal. This does not conflict with saying that natures, considered in their changing natural existence, come into being. — س قده

[^V07-380]: The point is that motion is the renewal of a thing, and renewal itself is not an independently existing thing. What exists in its own right is the thing being renewed, such as a changing nature or an accident that changes with that nature. Motion itself is like a grammatical particle: it is not a separate thing. This claim is like saying that coming-to-be is not itself something that comes to be, and that being affected is not itself another affected thing. Otherwise, there would be a motion within motion, a coming-to-be of coming-to-be, and an endless chain. There is no motion of motion; a thing moves. — س قده

[^V07-381]: This discussion was already given in the first journey, in the section on motion. It was also shown there that substantial motion is itself motion and is moved. Keep this in mind. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-382]: It is too strong to reject the claim entirely. It may be correct if it means that natures are always coming into being and renewing themselves. Their substantial motion is like the circular motion of the heavens, not like straight motion from one place to another that begins at rest and ends at rest. — س قده

[^V07-383]: He means that the idea is being taken in place of the actual thing. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-384]: The whole collection is denied eternity because, in the author’s view, it has no existence outside the existence of its individual members. A positive claim needs an existing subject. This differs from a universal nature: although it is denied eternity, it can be said to have begun, because it exists through its individual examples. So one can positively say that a collection has begun only if one believes that the collection itself exists over and above its members. The author denies that view. — س قده

[^V07-385]: “The whole” here means a collection taken as one thing. This does not conflict with the earlier claim that all the individual events began, since that claim treated them one by one, not as one collection. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-386]: This is like saying that each person is one, or that one loaf of bread can satisfy someone. We cannot always move from a claim about each individual to a claim about the whole collection, though it sometimes works for a particular purpose. For example, each possible thing is possible; if an infinite collection of possible things existed as one thing, that collection too would be possible. The claim that each event began works in the same way. — س قده

[^V07-387]: Just as substantial motion is the source of temporal coming-into-being, it is also a higher, “throne-like” source for it. — س قده

[^V07-388]: Size is necessary because the difference between a natural body and a mathematical body is only whether its dimensions are left open or made definite. Position is necessary because, when a body exists as a body—not as a point or a nonbodily thing—it can have lines meeting at right angles. Its parts then stand in an order. This gives rise to the category of position: the relation of the body’s parts to one another and of the whole to what lies outside it. As for place, if it means distance, the point is clear. If it means surface, it means here the natural region occupied by a body. The philosophers in Aristotle’s school say every body has a natural place, which is its position and ordering; the argument would not apply to the outermost sphere, which has no natural place. Time might seem to beg the question, but it does not: what is required here is the category “when,” not time itself. — س قده

[^V07-389]: By “fixed in existence,” he means a broader sense than simple existence, broad enough to include things in space. This supports his point that places and regions do not differ in relation to such a thing. — س قده

[^V07-390]: “The form of change” can mean the change of the form, making the phrase a description of what changes. Or it can mean that substantial motion is the form underlying accidental motions, making it the form belonging to those changes. — س قده

[^V07-391]: Primordial matter cannot exist apart from form, because matter and form are inseparable and God’s giving does not stop, either at the beginning or at the end. — س قده

[^V07-392]: The second should be put in place of the first, and the first in place of the second, as is clear. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-393]: The difference between a nature and matter is that matter’s level is a level of existence: its existence is pure potentiality. A nature’s level, considered apart from opposite qualities, is only what that nature is. In the upward order of beings, matter is the level that can receive every form and is the material source of the natural world, though it has no actual quality—not even the weakest, which is extension. This is why some say that the beginning of the natural world’s receptive side is darkness, emptiness, or the abyss; they mean matter. — س قده

[^V07-394]: By “attribute,” the author means an essential feature, like being human for a human being. Change is part of the real existence of bodies. By “what makes the body up,” he means an inner constituent: change is the very existence of the body’s specific form, one of its parts alongside matter and bodily form. Or the phrase means that change is the basis of the qualities that necessarily follow from a kind of thing; the source of a thing’s effects is part of what makes it what it is. The phrase “or part of the level of its existence” means that change or motion goes with a body’s existence and identity and does not come after them, even though it is not part of the abstract definition of the body. In the first two cases, the feature has some kind of priority over the thing; here it does not. — س قده

[^V07-395]: That is, the motion of the fifth nature belonging to the outermost heaven. Its measure is substantial motion, and both come before positional motion and other accidents. Some things exist along with change and time: the substantial motion of the heavenly natures. Just as the heavens’ positions are always flowing, their natures also flow in their identities. — س قده

[^V07-396]: Natural natures are inside change and surrounded by time, just as a measured body is contained within its measure. A natural body is contained by its mathematical measure, even though time is also in motion, motion is in a subject, and mathematical measure is in a natural body, as every accident is in its subject. — س قده

[^V07-397]: He means that the body and separate intellect are alike in one respect, but not in another. The body’s being “with” time is really its being in time, because it undergoes motion. — س قده

[^V07-398]: No separate thing in the world corresponds to conceptual existence, apart from the actual natures that exist. If existence were a separate thing with its own counterpart, it too would be a basic reality, but that is impossible. — س قده

[^V07-399]: This explains how they fell into the mistake. They treated existence as a concept in the mind and nature as the true reality. Since the nature of a body, in their view, is free of change and its measure, they said change does not belong to the real nature of bodies. They failed to see that real existence is the luminous, flowing reality of a thing, and that change goes with this existence. They saw change only among the body’s accidents. — س قده

[^V07-400]: If “belonging to” is a real relation, then in the outer world the subject must already exist before a quality can belong to it. The same is true when a nature is truly said in the mind to have existence: it would have to undergo endless acts of being given existence, just as in the outside world. We cannot treat the mind’s true claim that a nature exists differently from the claim that existence itself exists, unity itself is one, and so on, because in those cases the subject and quality are the same and the mind creates the relation. Here, however, a nature is a different kind of thing from existence, and the relation is assumed to be real. — س قده

[^V07-401]: There is some imprecision here. Motion is a way of existing and has no separate nature of its own. A better answer is that this view would make a nature itself graded and changeable. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-402]: If this argument worked, it would apply to accidental motion. But substantial motion is not a quality that belongs to a subject from outside; the substance itself is moving. So it would not mean that something is a quality of itself. The author’s statements that motion itself does not move and that there is no motion within motion have also been discussed. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-403]: The form is the nature that is the body’s first source of activity and motion. It is not pure actuality, but neither is it pure potentiality; it lies between them. The source of actual change cannot be either wholly unchanging or wholly fixed. Also, if the form were unchanging, it would be matter: what would make it deserve the name “form” instead of “matter”? If the point is that the concept of form is not itself changing or fixed, that is what the author is trying to prove. This does not prevent its existence from being changeable. — س قده

[^V07-404]: Matter is a subject of motion as the bearer of its potentiality; form is a subject as the substantial reality in which motion exists. The whole made of matter and form is also a subject, since it is nothing beyond matter clothed in form. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-405]: The wording may be reversed. The intended meaning may be that the form is the predicate and the whole is the subject. — س قده

[^V07-406]: The whole depends on its members; if the members are new, the whole is even more clearly new. — س قده

[^V07-407]: The mind’s imagined collection contains only a few individuals and has no meaningful ratio to the infinite collection. A collection conceived by the intellect has one separate, unified existence, like a universal concept, unless it is used to consider the endlessly many individuals as they arise. — س قده

[^V07-408]: Calling the separate forms “forms of knowledge” may mean that they are God’s active knowledge, or that they are the images of His own detailed knowledge, which is one simple awareness that includes all details. The simple reality contains every existence, and all natures and fixed realities are contained within His names and qualities, like a tree contained in a seed. — س قده

[^V07-409]: The disagreement here is about coming into being in time, not about depending on a cause in one’s very existence. The theologians excluded time from the events whose beginning they discuss. They did not say that time itself began at a time, since that would require time to exist before time. Instead, they said that it had no limit either in the past or the future. When they were shown that this would make the world eternal, because time is one of the possible things, some said time was only an imagined idea with no reality. That would undermine all their proofs that the world began in time. Others said time is derived from God’s own essence; but that would make God change. They replied that a derived concept can differ in judgment from the thing it comes from. This distinction between a concept and its reality has no clear meaning here; it leads to sophistry. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-410]: This does not prove what is needed. The desired conclusion is that events have no first moment in time. What follows is only that an unlimited number of determining causes would exist together at one time. It is better to reject this case because it requires an infinite number of causes confined between two limits: God and the new factor that selects the time. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-411]: God is far above additional qualities that come into being, whether the quality itself or its relation to something else is new. Such a change could either make the world new or make God composite, with potentiality and actuality. A new will, for example, would have to be preceded by a readiness to receive it. God is self-sufficient and does not need a condition, a suitable time, an added motive, or anything else. Since He acts by His own essence and by a quality that is His essence, His action never stops. — س قده

[^V07-412]: That is, His state is always the same: before things exist, while they exist, and afterward. His acts do not change, as God says: “You will find no change in God’s way.” — س قده

[^V07-413]: You may wonder how this exception connects to the preceding point. Some theologians did not say that causality and agency are identical with God’s essence, which would make the argument for the world’s eternity even stronger. They instead attributed additional qualities to God. But once they accept causes and effects and say that God’s eternal will causes the world, they must identify a factor that explains why the world began at a particular time. — س قده

[^V07-414]: Put another way: what benefit is there in nonexistence, which is pure evil, instead of existence, which is pure good? Why hold back from giving when a possible nature needs only its own possibility to receive existence, that possibility is eternal, and the source is pure generosity and completely self-sufficient? God’s knowledge of what is beneficial is active, so it cannot be delayed. Also, if God acts to gain a benefit, He is being driven by a motive beyond His essence. — س قده

[^V07-415]: At the very least, the proposed selector must be something different from the time it selects. — س قده

[^V07-416]: You might object that this does not apply only to the Karrāmiyya; it also applies to those who say God has additional qualities. The author has not said that it applies only to the Karrāmiyya. Also, he may not have mentioned it earlier because there was no new thing being received in those other cases, so one might deny that they involve a new receptive state. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-417]: The same place must contain both the wills and the motions caused by them. This resembles our own voluntary actions: each step results from a particular will, and each such will results in a step. — س قده

[^V07-418]: This resembles the view that God’s knowledge of things apart from Himself consists of forms in an intellect or soul, rather than in His essence, to avoid the problems discussed earlier. That view would make God depend on something else for His perfection, as this account of will does. — س قده

[^V07-419]: The same view was quoted earlier, in the general discussion of final causes: it says that will selects a time by its own nature. Remember what we explained there. — س قده

[^V07-420]: He says this because philosophical conclusions must be proved. The philosophers who spoke this way seem to have been arguing against a theologian by accepting the theologian’s own starting point. — س قده

[^V07-421]: This is the view that God eternally willed the world to exist at a future time. Its supporter imagines time as outside the chain of possible things: an endless stretch with a past and a future, over which God’s existence extends forever. God, existing in eternity, willed the world to exist in the future. The author rejects this by pointing out that time is the measure of motion—either the motion of universal nature, in his view, or the motion of the outermost sphere, in another account. In either case, time is possible. If all possible things are set aside, only sheer nonexistence remains. There is no time there with separate moments to which an eternal will could be attached differently. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-422]: It would be more accurate to say “later” or “afterward.” Our will is mixed with potentiality; God’s will is not. — س قده

[^V07-423]: By “the time before it” or “another time,” he means other possible instances of time. They do not exist outside the mind, but the mind can picture them. — س قده

[^V07-424]: The point is that God’s giving to the natural world never stops, even though its parts are continually renewed through substantial motion. This can be true even if some universal kinds endure: according to ancient science, the heavens, stars, the four elements, and composite kinds such as human beings, animals, and plants continue because their individuals replace one another. The argument uses old ideas about astronomy and the order of nature, including the belief that the heavens and their motions last forever and that the four elements are always present beneath the moon. These assumptions have since been disproved by precise experiments: Earth and the other three bodies once called elements are mixtures of different elements; heavenly bodies are also composite and have limited, though long, lifetimes; and the time once measured by the daily movement of the outermost sphere is measured instead by Earth’s rotation and yearly movement around the sun. Still, the author’s argument holds if we replace those old assumptions with modern ones. The world’s nature, through its continuing substantial change, exists in a flowing time and must come into being again and again. There is no problem in saying that God’s giving never ends while forms change, kinds disappear, or a new world arises as the forms of another pass away. The divine outpouring never stops. Several reports from the Imams of the Prophet’s family support this: God has always created one creation after another, one world after another, and will continue to do so. No natural kind that exists in time is eternal and without a first moment. First matter and the universal nature that underlies particular kinds are both indeterminate; temporal kinds give them their specific form. Separate, immaterial beings exist outside time and are not spread across it. It is a mistake to imagine that God, or any immaterial being, exists stretched across an endless span of time. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-425]: This alludes to the verse: “The Jews say, ‘God’s hand is tied up.’ May their hands be tied, and may they be cursed for what they say. Rather, both His hands are open; He gives as He wills.” The author suggests that those who say God withholds His giving resemble the Jews in this respect. His point is that a careful philosopher must affirm God’s eternity and the eternity of what belongs to Him, while also affirming that creation begins from its own side. — س قده

[^V07-426]: That is, the sphere of fire, the sphere of air, and so on. Each sphere is one continuous body, so each kind persists as a single individual. Fire and air themselves, along with the other elements, are kinds with many separate individuals. — س قده

[^V07-427]: Every one of these arguments assumes that time lies outside the realm of possible things. Time must then either be an imagined construct or something drawn from God’s own being and identical with Him—which is impossible. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-428]: The copies consulted here have a line missing: “If that is impossible, the events end at nonexistence; if it is possible …” The point is that neither option gives an infinite series of events. The argument asks whether events fail to reach back into eternity or are found there. A kind exists if at least one of its individuals exists, and ceases only if all its individuals cease. If no event exists in eternity, the series is finite because it ends in nonexistence. If one does exist there, that event is the beginning of the series that continues without end into the future; the series is still finite on that side because it begins with that one event. — س قده

[^V07-429]: A better reply is this: for something to be absent is for its existence to be removed. Its eternal nonexistence is not such a removal, because it could not have existed in eternity and was not meant to exist forever. Its genuine absence is the absence that stands opposite to its existence at the time when it exists. That existence has displaced absence and does not itself admit of nonexistence. What admits of existence or nonexistence is its essence, which in itself has no existence; this is absence that accompanies the thing. By contrast, absence in the past would imply an earlier existence in time. If there was no earlier temporal existence, there was no earlier temporal absence. Necessary existence is neither temporal nor opposed to existence in this way. Also, absence is pure negation, so it cannot be treated as something that is “eternal,” nor can all absences be gathered together in eternity. — س قده

[^V07-430]: If someone says that past events are infinite, how can they also accept that each event’s absence is not preceded by an event? If this means that an absence does not coexist with any event, that is denied. “Preceded” and “followed” here refer to the ends of periods of absence, not to their beginnings, which coincide with the events in question. — س قده

[^V07-431]: Put more clearly: an absence is not paired with a particular event—either the event whose absence it is, or a later event in the endless future. — س قده

[^V07-432]: Eternity, too, is a negative idea: it is like a container with no beginning. Something eternal is simply something that exists in, or belongs to, what has no beginning. So there is no contradiction in saying that both a thing’s existence and its absence are eternal in this sense: both fall within what has no beginning. A further reply is that eternity is not a fixed time or a particular instant that can hold only one thing. It can contain a thing and its removal at different levels, just as the past can contain both Noah’s existence and his absence. More fully, this uses the familiar theologians’ sense of “eternal” as what lies at the beginning of the descending chain from the hidden depths of reality to the visible world, and “everlasting” as what lies at the far end of the ascending chain from outward things to their inner realities. There is also a deeper sense: eternal reality is existence before it is linked to any particular form in any realm, while everlasting reality is that same existence after all forms have been erased by the overpowering One. Once this is understood, there is no conflict between absences in eternity and an endless succession of events in the past, just as all things may come to an end in everlastingness while events continue without end into the future. Their absence is their return to God along the vertical chain of being. Their source lies in the descending chain; their return lies in the ascending chain. Endless changes and stages may unfold across time, with endless relations and destinations, yet all rise along that chain to God, the final end and goal: “To your Lord is the return, and to Him is the end.” — س قده

[^V07-433]: Eternity is not a fixed boundary or a measured time. It is as explained above. — س قده

[^V07-434]: This refers to the earlier claim that the philosophers’ famous doctrine—that kinds are eternal and their essences are preserved through successive individuals—is mistaken. — س قده

[^V07-435]: Perhaps the reason is that a shared universal is abstract and exists only through its individual instances. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-436]: The claim is that the whole is not eternal, not that it begins to exist as a separate thing. It has no existence apart from its parts, or within any one part. A natural universal is different: although it has no existence apart from individuals, it exists through the existence of any one individual. A kind exists when at least one of its individuals exists and ceases only when all of them cease. This is the point the author calls the root of the matter. — س قده

[^V07-437]: Some theologians say that God is not the complete cause of the world’s existence, in order to avoid saying that the world is eternal. They fail to see that the natural world is originated in its very being, even though God is a complete and more-than-complete cause: necessary in Himself and necessary in every respect. Nothing in Him is other than His own pure being. What they say amounts to claiming that God is not rich and sufficient to create the world, but needs something else to become an active cause—even if that means waiting through an endless number of measured periods. They have wrongly compared the whole to one of its parts. A person such as Zayd depends on causes, conditions, and preparations outside himself, so God is not the complete cause of Zayd if the question is only about Zayd’s own power. Yet God needs nothing else to create Zayd: He creates everything on which Zayd’s existence depends. Nothing possible lies outside the world; everything on which the world depends, other than God, belongs to it. So the world depends only on God and His attributes, which are identical with His essence. To ask whether God’s essence is enough to create the whole world, or whether He needs something besides Himself, leaves only two possible answers. To choose the second is an outrageous denial of His self-sufficiency. How can cause and effect be equal in rank? As the author has said repeatedly, the world’s coming to be is essential, not temporal. — س قده

[^V07-438]: The whole is made up of infinitely many groups, each group being like one individual event. The fact that each individual event begins does not prove that the whole series is finite; the same is true of the series of groups. — س قده

[^V07-439]: The difference is that each group of souls consists of parts existing together, while each group of events consists of parts occurring one after another. This is another account of the world’s coming to be. As Shaykh Mahmud Shabistari writes in the *Gulshan-i Raz*: “The whole world, at every moment, becomes nothing, and does not remain for two moments.” — س قده

[^V07-440]: Perhaps this argument appears in the *Matarihat* in the form of a question beginning “I say …”; the author therefore calls it a question. — س قده

[^V07-441]: “In an unusual arrangement.” Even if such an arrangement is rare, that would not make the number of souls finite. It could recur endlessly over endlessly many cycles and revolutions. A rare alignment in one cycle could occur again in other cycles—for example, at the midpoint of another revolution of the sphere of fixed stars. — س قده

[^V07-442]: The author gives four replies. First, divine providence would not allow this kind, the noblest of natural kinds, to disappear when less noble kinds exist from eternity. But providence also takes account of the conditions, causes, and preparations required for other things. It is possible that this kind’s conditions were present for a limited period: it appeared, its individuals lived in succession, it disappeared, and the conditions later returned so that it appeared again. The kind may exist for longer than it is absent, with providence giving each thing its due. The claim that less noble kinds exist forever seems to refer to the four elements and the heavenly bodies, whose supposed eternity has already been challenged. Second, the rule of “the possibility of the nobler” is applied to kinds in general; but that rule depends on proving that the less noble kinds exist, which has not been done. Third, the existence of luminous archetypes that care for their material kinds would mean that no kind is ever absent from existence. But even if such archetypes exist, they are not complete causes: their effects still depend on conditions and readiness. As explained above, these may be present only at some times, not all the time, and may operate intermittently. The author has already discussed the archetypes in the first journey’s treatment of essences. Fourth, some mystics call this form a divine name or a manifestation of the First Reality. But it is not a universal divine name; it is a name belonging to the world. Such worldly names need not persist forever. The same applies to manifestations. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-443]: The lower world is a shadow of the higher one. Since every active intellect is everlasting, each intelligible form there must have an everlasting shadow here. — س قده

[^V07-444]: The author did not conclude that number is eternal. He concluded that multiplicity, which belongs to the category of number, is only a mental construct. This is also the mystics’ view: number is the mind’s repetition of “one” without specifying what kind of one it is, and repeating something in thought does not multiply the thing itself. — س قده

[^V07-445]: “They look united, but their hearts are divided.” These souls do not form one whole with the first, nearest beings, because they have no shared nature with them. This echoes the earlier point in the *Matarihat* that separate souls do not form a whole, since no relation binds them together. — س قده

[^V07-446]: This qualification is needed if the argument is a reductio; it is not needed if it is offered as a direct proof. — س قده

[^V07-447]: This is like the argument about the last effect before the final cause. — س قده

[^V07-448]: You may think this conflicts with the earlier reply to the “divine philosopher,” where the author says that number exists. His point there is that numbers exist as many repeated units. Here he is describing the opponent’s view: that number has no existence at all, because the unit from which number is formed is only a mental construct for him. This reading is supported by the author’s description of number as a weakly existing essence whose form is its own matter. — س قده

[^V07-449]: The act of causing takes place at the instant that separates a period of existence from a period of nonexistence. This is the boundary where something begins to exist: coming to be is existence after nonexistence, just as persistence is existence after existence. The cause acts on existence marked by its coming after nonexistence, at the first instant, not at a second instant; otherwise it would be producing something that already exists. If the effect did not begin in time, there would be no act of causing it. The author discussed this point near the beginning of the first journey. — س قده

[^V07-450]: This argument seems stronger because it establishes an intermediate state between existence and nonexistence. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-451]: Composite making cannot apply to essential attributes. — س قده

[^V07-452]: That is, the effect depends on its agent for its very existence, and the agent acts through its own essential powers. — س قده

[^V07-453]: This may be inferred from the wording of their argument. But since they do not accept continuous substantial motion, their claim that like things replace one another would mean that the body has a different individual essence at every instant from the body in the preceding instant. Common sense rules that out. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-454]: There would be no persistence from one instant to the next. But instantaneous states and a series of separate instants are impossible. The truth is continuous renewal through substantial motion, as explained above. — س قده

[^V07-455]: The fact that something could cease to exist does not mean it did not exist eternally in time. A universal intellect, for example, could possibly cease to exist and yet have existed forever in time. More briefly, one could use the same reply given for motion: if rest is a thing’s second state of being in one place, it must have been preceded by its first state there. — س قده

[^V07-456]: This follows from treating what distinguishes one thing as its defining feature: a distinguishing feature presupposes something shared in essence. — س قده

[^V07-457]: Since coming to be is continual renewal in a thing’s very being, persistence is stability. But a state “between” two limits cannot be stable: at every instant it lies between two boundaries different from those of the instant before and the instant after. It is like a point that keeps moving, held in a continually passing present. — س قده

[^V07-458]: This follows the author’s account of composition as a unity of matter and form. — س قده

[^V07-459]: In debate, showing that the opponent’s reason for denying a claim does not hold does not by itself prove the claim. The opponent may still ask for evidence. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-460]: The middle term in this proof is expressly qualified as “in itself.” It therefore applies to substantial motion and yields the world’s temporal beginning as the author has explained it. The theologians do not make this qualification. They argue that a body is never free of events, and that whatever is never free of events must have begun. Their smaller and larger premises are both unsound. The same problem affects their argument that the world changes and everything that changes must have begun. If they had limited change to what occurs in a thing’s very being, the argument would be valid. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-461]: That is, the thing’s substance and nature continually flow because of substantial motion. — س قده

[^V07-462]: Eternity is not a fixed span of time, as explained above. — س قده

[^V07-463]: A negative statement can be true because there is no subject for it to describe. Eternity is not a particular time. — س قده

[^V07-464]: That is, a motion so called, or what they call “motion in the middle.” Otherwise, each instance of motion would itself be preceded by another instance. — س قده

[^V07-465]: Reward and punishment may be understood as a vertical return to God, so people should not imagine that saying the world is eternal denies the afterlife. Yet the claim still causes a problem: if the world never comes to an end, people do not understand the afterlife or the ascending and descending chains of being, whose final goal is reached by turning toward inner, higher worlds. Even if that account is accepted, it still weakens the proof of the Creator, since people take the world’s coming to be as the reason it needs a cause. — س قده

[^V07-466]: Or one may suppose an endless number of imagined or estimated periods, simply to show that nothing prevents God’s wisdom from acting; nothing can obstruct His judgment. — س قده

[^V07-467]: “Existent” here means existent in the everyday sense: a thing’s essence or what-it-is, not existence itself. Sameness and difference belong to essences. In this sense, the world is the realm of essences. — س قده

[^V07-468]: The word “dominant” can be used broadly, and what counts as dominant depends on how the speaker is looking at things, as in the phrase “the sun and the moon.” For separate beings, time is eternity, or *dahr*. If someone says that the intellects are eternal in time, “time” here means *dahr*, as explained earlier. *Dahr* is the spirit of time, just as everlastingness is the spirit of *dahr*. One may also speak of their time as the time their existence would have, if it were measured; that is enough for this way of speaking. — س قده

[^V07-469]: The author’s words do not mean that bodies existed before time. Nor does the phrase “bodies are eternal in time” necessarily mean that time preceded them. The preposition “in” can indicate the aspect under discussion, as in the expressions “a human being is material in body, immaterial in spirit, actual in existence, and potential in essence.” The intended meaning is that bodies’ existence is temporal, or that they exist along with time. — س قده

[^V07-470]: The mystic did not speak of time and bodies existing together; he spoke of time preceding them. The reply is that the author hints that the mystic’s wording does not establish such temporal priority. At most, it says they exist together, which does not conflict with the essential priority that the mystic himself accepts. — س قده

[^V07-471]: There is no temporal simultaneity here, as between two separate things. Time and what exists in time are bound together, like matter and form. Just as, on the author’s view, one cannot say that form is eternal merely because matter is, as if they were two separate things existing alongside each other, one cannot speak of time and temporal things as two separate things existing together. Their connection has been explained above. — س قده

[^V07-472]: This is a good reading of the mystic’s words. But it leaves a problem: given the dependence between them, it either denies priority in either direction between bodies and time, or gives each priority over the other—bodies because they cause time to exist, and time because it gives bodies their particular form. Yet the mystic denies priority to time and grants it only to bodies. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-473]: As noted in the first journey’s discussion of time, each motion has its own duration: the stretch that belongs to it and is measured by it. There are therefore as many durations as there are motions. The familiar time measured by the daily motion of the heavens is an agreed standard for measuring other motions. Saying that this familiar time makes a thing distinct means that it reveals its distinctness. The time of a body’s movement through one of its accidental features comes only after the body exists and is already distinct. If a body has a time that makes it distinct, that time is the measure of its own substantial motion, which is its flowing existence. — T. M. (editor).

[^V07-474]: This follows from the meaning of necessary existence in itself. Anything necessary in itself must exist under every supposition: whether anything else exists or does not exist. Suppose we consider time and set everything else aside. Everything else is then removed, leaving only time itself. Yet the Necessary Being is not removed; He exists even under the supposition that time alone exists. So God is with every thing, without being joined to it. — T. M. (editor).
